On the dimension of limit sets on ℙ ⁡ ( ℝ 3 ) \mathbb{P}(\mathbb{R}^{3}) via stationary measures: the theory and applications
Jialun Li, Wenyu Pan, Disheng Xu

Abstract
This paper investigates the (semi)group action of SL 3 ​ ( ℝ ) \mathrm{SL}_{3}(\mathbb{R}) on ℙ ⁡ ( ℝ 3 ) \mathbb{P}(\mathbb{R}^{3}) , a primary example of non-conformal, non-linear, and non-strictly contracting action. We study the Hausdorff dimension of a dynamically defined limit set in ℙ ⁡ ( ℝ 3 ) \mathbb{P}(\mathbb{R}^{3}) and generalize the classical Patterson-Sullivan formula using the approach of stationary measures. The two main examples are Anosov representations in SL 3 ​ ( ℝ ) \mathrm{SL}_{3}(\mathbb{R}) and the Rauzy gasket. 1. For Anosov representations in SL 3 ​ ( ℝ ) \mathrm{SL}_{3}(\mathbb{R}) , we establish a sharp lower bound for the dimension of their limit sets in ℙ ⁡ ( ℝ 3 ) \mathbb{P}(\mathbb{R}^{3}) . Coupled with the upper bound in [ PSW22 ] , it shows that their Hausdorff dimensions equal the affinity exponents. The merit of our approach is that it works uniformly for all the components of irreducible Anosov representations in SL 3 ​ ( ℝ ) \mathrm{SL}_{3}(\mathbb{R}) . As an application, it reveals a surprising dimension jump phenomenon in the Barbot component, which is a local generalization of Bowen’s dimension rigidity result [ Bow79 ] . 2. For the Rauzy gasket, we confirm a folklore conjecture about the Hausdorff dimension of the gasket and improve the numerical lower bound to 3 / 2 3/2 . These results originate from a dimension formula of stationary measures on ℙ ⁡ ( ℝ 3 ) \mathbb{P}(\mathbb{R}^{3}) . Let ν \nu be a probability measure on SL 3 ​ ( ℝ ) \mathrm{SL}_{3}(\mathbb{R}) whose support is finite and spans a Zariski dense subgroup. Let μ \mu be the associated stationary measure for the action on ℙ ⁡ ( ℝ 3 ) \mathbb{P}(\mathbb{R}^{3}) . Under the exponential separation condition on ν \nu , we prove that the Hausdorff dimension of μ \mu equals its Lyapunov dimension, which extends [ HS17 ] and [ BHR19 ] to non-conformal and projective settings respectively.



1
Introduction

This paper investigates the (semi)group action of
\mathrm{SL}_{3}(\mathbb{R})
on
\mathbb{P}(\mathbb{R}^{3})
, a primary example of non-conformal, non-linear, and non-strictly contracting action. We focus on the following two important and closely related problems:

1.

(
dimension of the limit sets
) Let
\Gamma
be a discrete subgroup (subsemigroup) of
\mathrm{SL}_{3}(\mathbb{R})
acting on
\mathbb{P}(\mathbb{R}^{3})
. We study how large the limit set
L(\Gamma)
is by determining
the Hausdorff dimension of
L(\Gamma)
.

2.

(
dimension of stationary measures
) Let
\nu
be a finitely supported probability measure on
\mathrm{SL}_{3}(\mathbb{R})
. Let
\mu
be a
\nu
-stationary measure for the action on
\mathbb{P}(\mathbb{R}^{3})
. We study how large
\mu
is by determining the Hausdorff dimension of
\mu
.

1.1
Hausdorff dimension of limit sets

Studying dynamically defined limit sets is a central question in various areas of mathematics, for example, dynamical systems, fractal geometry, and hyperbolic geometry.

In his seminal papers
[
Sul79
]
,
[
Sul84
]
, Sullivan considered a Kleinian group
\Gamma
in
\mathrm{Isom}_{+}(\mathbb{H}^{3})\cong\mathrm{PSL}_{2}(\mathbb{C})
and studied its limit set in the visual boundary
\partial\mathbb{H}^{3}
. The Poincaré series of the group
\Gamma
is defined by

P_{\Gamma}(s)=\sum_{\gamma\in\Gamma}\Big(\frac{\sigma_{2}}{\sigma_{1}}\Big)^{s}(\gamma),

where
\sigma_{i}(\gamma)
are the singular values of
\rho(\gamma)
in decreasing order.
The classical critical exponent
\delta(\Gamma)
of
\Gamma
is defined to be the critical exponent of series
P_{\Gamma}(s)
, i.e.

\delta(\Gamma)={\sup}_{P_{\Gamma}(s)=\infty}\ s={\inf}_{P_{\Gamma}(s)<\infty}\ s.

Sullivan
[
Sul79
]
proved the beautiful equality between the critical exponent
\delta(\Gamma)
, a dynamical invariant, and the Hausdorff dimension of its conic limit set, a geometric invariant. It generalized the earlier work by Patterson for Fuchsian groups
[
Pat76
]
. Furthermore, Sullivan established the deep relation between the Hausdorff dimension of the limit set and
the bottom of the eigenvalue of the Laplace operator and the entropy of the geodesic flow on
\Gamma\backslash\mathbb{H}^{3}
.

In this paper, we consider a discrete subgroup (subsemigroup)
\Gamma
of
\mathrm{SL}_{3}(\mathbb{R})
acting on
\mathbb{P}(\mathbb{R}^{3})
. We
investigate whether an analogous equality is available for the Hausdorff dimension of its limit set. Two main examples are the Anosov representations in
\mathrm{SL}_{3}(\mathbb{R})
and the Rauzy gasket. The group
\mathrm{PSL}_{2}(\mathbb{C})
acts conformally on
\partial\mathbb{H}^{3}
, which plays an essential role in Sullivan’s proof. In contrast,
\mathrm{SL}_{3}(\mathbb{R})
acts on
\mathbb{P}(\mathbb{R}^{3})
non-conformally: circles are mapped to ellipses, which poses an immediate challenge in the estimation of the Hausdorff dimension.

In the following, we usually denote by
\dim\cdot
for the Hausdorff dimension of a set and by
L(\cdot)
for the limit set (see
1.1
) of the projective action of a discrete linear subgroup (subsemigroup).

1.1.1
Anosov representation

The concept of
Anosov representation
was first introduced by Labourie in
[
Lab06
]
to study Hitchin components of the representations of surface groups, which is a natural generalization of convex-cocompact representations in rank one semisimple Lie groups to the higher rank setting. See also
[
GW12
]
,
[
KLP18
]
,
[
GGKW17
]
,
[
BPS19
]
for further study on Anosov representations.

Definition 1.1
.

Let
\Gamma
be a hyperbolic group
1
1

1

In this paper we always consider finitely generated hyperbolic groups.
and
\rho
a representation from
\Gamma
to
\mathrm{SL}_{3}(\mathbb{R})
. Then
\rho
is called an Anosov representation if there exist
C,c>0
such that for any
\gamma\in\Gamma
, we have

\frac{\sigma_{1}(\rho(\gamma))}{\sigma_{2}(\rho(\gamma))}\geqslant\frac{1}{C}e^{c|\gamma|},

where
\sigma_{i}(\rho(\gamma))
are the singular values of
\rho(\gamma)
in decreasing order, and
|\gamma|
is the word length with respect to a fixed symmetric generating set. The limit set
L(\rho(\Gamma))
of
\rho
in
\mathbb{P}(\mathbb{R}^{3})
is defined to be the closure of the set of attracting fixed points of elements in
\rho(\Gamma)
. Let
\mathrm{Hom}(\Gamma,\mathrm{SL}_{3}(\mathbb{R}))
be the set of
\mathrm{SL}_{3}(\mathbb{R})
-representations of the group
\Gamma
, and
\mathrm{HA}(\Gamma,\mathrm{SL}_{3}(\mathbb{R}))
be the subset of
\mathrm{Hom}(\Gamma,\mathrm{SL}_{3}(\mathbb{R}))
consisting of Anosov representations.

Here are some important properties of Anosov representations. If
\rho\in\mathrm{HA}(\Gamma,\mathrm{SL}_{3}(\mathbb{R}))
, then
\rho(\Gamma)
is discrete in
\mathrm{SL}_{3}(\mathbb{R})
. Any Anosov representation is stable under small perturbation in
\mathrm{Hom}(\Gamma,\mathrm{SL}_{3}(\mathbb{R}))
, hence
\mathrm{HA}(\Gamma,\mathrm{SL}_{3}(\mathbb{R}))
is open in
\mathrm{Hom}(\Gamma,\mathrm{SL}_{3}(\mathbb{R}))
.

Affinity exponent.

The concept of
affinity exponent
(or
affinity dimension
) of Anosov representations
was introduced in
[
PSW22
]
, which is essentially a natural extension of the affinity exponent of self-affine fractals due to Falconer
[
Fal88
]
.

For a representation
\rho:\Gamma\to\mathrm{SL}_{3}(\mathbb{R})
, we can similarly define a Poincaré series of the group
\rho(\Gamma)
by

P_{\rho}(s)=\left\{\begin{aligned} &\sum_{\gamma\in\Gamma}\Big(\frac{\sigma_{2}}{\sigma_{1}}\Big)^{s}(\rho(\gamma)),&0<s\leqslant 1;\\
&\sum_{\gamma\in\Gamma}\Big(\frac{\sigma_{2}}{\sigma_{1}}\Big)(\rho(\gamma))\Big(\frac{\sigma_{3}}{\sigma_{1}}\Big)^{s-1}(\rho(\gamma)),&1<s\leqslant 2.\end{aligned}\right.

(1.1)

We denote the critical exponent of
P_{\rho}(s)
by
s_{\mathrm{A}}(\rho)
and call it the affinity exponent (or the affinity dimension). The affinity exponent
s_{\mathrm{A}}(\rho)
is always an upper bound of
\dim L(\rho(\Gamma))\subset\mathbb{P}(\mathbb{R}^{3})
if
\rho:\Gamma\to\mathrm{SL}_{3}(\mathbb{R})
is an Anosov representation
[
PSW22
]
. In this paper, we show
s_{\mathrm{A}}(\rho)
is also a lower bound of
\dim L(\rho(\Gamma))
, which is a first general result in this non-conformal, non-linear, non-strictly contracting setting. Hence, we extend Sullivan’s theorem to
Anosov representations in
\mathrm{SL}_{3}(\mathbb{R})
.

Theorem 1.2
.

Let
\Gamma
be a hyperbolic group and
\rho:\Gamma\rightarrow\mathrm{SL}_{3}(\mathbb{R})
be an irreducible Anosov representation, then the Hausdorff dimension of the limit set
L(\rho(\Gamma))
in
\mathbb{P}(\mathbb{R}^{3})
equals the affinity exponent
s_{\mathrm{A}}(\rho)
.
Moreover, the exponent
s_{\mathrm{A}}(\rho)
is continuous on
\mathrm{HA}(\Gamma,\mathrm{SL}_{3}(\mathbb{R}))
.

When
\Gamma
is a surface group, if
\rho\in\mathrm{Hom}(\Gamma,\mathrm{SL}_{3}(\mathbb{R}))
is in the Hitchin component, then the limit set
L(\rho(\Gamma))
is a
C^{1}
-circle
[
Lab06
]
, and hence its Hausdorff dimension is
1
.
The Hausdorff dimension of limit sets of Anosov representations has been extensively studied, see for example
[
PSW22
]
,
[
PSW21
]
,
[
GMT19
]
,
[
DK22
]
,
[
KMO23
]
. In
[
Duf17
]
, Dufloux studied this problem for the non-conformal action of Schottky groups in
\operatorname{PU}(1,n)
on
\partial\mathbb{H}^{n}_{\mathbb{C}}
, the boundary of the complex hyperbolic
n
-space.

There is a rich literature on studying the dimension theory of non-conformal action. In
[
BHR19
]
, Bárány-Hochman-Rapaport provided a relatively complete study on self-affine contracting Iterated-Functional-Systems (IFS) on
\mathbb{R}^{2}
. Our proof draws inspiration from their method. See also
[
HR21
]
and
[
Rap22
]
for further development, as well as the classical results, for example,
[
Fal88
]
,
[
HL95
]
in this setting. See
[
RS21
]
for the graphs of Weierstrass-type functions,
[
CP10
]
and
[
CPZ19
]
for non-conformal repellers.

1.1.2
Dimension jump

One surprising corollary of
1.2
is a local generalization of Bowen’s dimension rigidity result
[
Bow79
]
.
Specifically, let
\Gamma
be a surface group, and let
\eta_{0}:\Gamma\rightarrow\mathrm{PSL}_{2}(\mathbb{R})
be
a faithful representation onto a cocompact Fuchsian group of
\mathrm{PSL}_{2}(\mathbb{R})
.
We view
\mathrm{PSL}_{2}(\mathbb{R})
as a subgroup of
\mathrm{PSL}_{2}(\mathbb{C})
and let
\iota_{0}
be the natural embedding of
\mathrm{PSL}_{2}(\mathbb{R})
in
\mathrm{PSL}_{2}(\mathbb{C})
, then
\iota_{0}\circ\eta_{0}
is a representation of
\Gamma
to
\mathrm{PSL}_{2}(\mathbb{C})
. The limit set of
\iota_{0}\circ\eta_{0}(\Gamma)
is the smooth circle
\mathbb{P}_{\mathbb{R}}^{1}
in the sphere
\mathbb{P}_{\mathbb{C}}^{1}
.
Let
\rho:\Gamma\to\mathrm{PSL}_{2}(\mathbb{C})
be a representation, which is a small perturbation of
\iota_{0}\circ\eta_{0}.
The limit set
L(\rho(\Gamma))
is known to be homeomorphic to a circle, which implies
\dim L(\rho(\Gamma))\geqslant 1
. Bowen proved that
\dim L(\rho(\Gamma))=1
if and only if
\rho(\Gamma)
is contained in a conjugate of
\mathrm{SL}_{2}(\mathbb{R})
.
2
2

2

The actual result of Bowen is global, not only for small perturbation. The version stated here is a weaker local version.

We extend Bowen’s result in a non-conformal setting: consider the action of
\mathrm{SL}_{3}(\mathbb{R})
on
\mathbb{P}(\mathbb{R}^{3})
. One of our main results is a dimension jump phenomenon, which leads to a local dimension rigidity as follows.

We consider
\mathrm{Hom}(\Gamma,\mathrm{SL}_{3}(\mathbb{R}))
and deform representations here in a way similar to Bowen’s setting.
More precisely, take an arbitrary lift
\rho_{0}:\Gamma\rightarrow\mathrm{SL}_{2}(\mathbb{R})
of
\eta_{0}
.
3
3

3

One can do it by choosing the images of generators in the double cover
\mathrm{SL}_{2}(\mathbb{R})
of
\mathrm{PSL}_{2}(\mathbb{R})
.
Denote by
\iota
the embedding from
\mathrm{SL}_{2}(\mathbb{R})
to the upper left corner of
\mathrm{SL}_{3}(\mathbb{R})
, then the composition
\rho_{1}:=\iota\circ\rho_{0}
is an element in
\mathrm{Hom}(\Gamma,\mathrm{SL}_{3}({\mathbb{R}})).
The action of
\rho_{1}(\Gamma)
preserves
\mathbb{P}(\mathbb{R}^{2})
in
\mathbb{P}(\mathbb{R}^{3})
, which is a smooth circle.
Due to
[
Sul85
]
, if
\rho
in
\mathrm{Hom}(\Gamma,\mathrm{SL}_{3}(\mathbb{R}))
is sufficiently close to
\rho_{1}
, i.e. its images on generators are close to that of
\rho_{1}
, then
\rho(\Gamma)
preserves a unique topological circle close to
\mathbb{P}(\mathbb{R}^{2})
in
\mathbb{P}(\mathbb{R}^{3})
, which turns out to be the limit set
L(\rho(\Gamma))
(see
1.1
).

Theorem 1.3
.

For every
\epsilon>0
, there exists a small neighborhood
O
of
\rho_{1}
in
\mathrm{Hom}(\Gamma,\mathrm{SL}_{3}(\mathbb{R}))
such that for any
\rho
in
O
we have

•

either
\rho(\Gamma)
acts reducibly, i.e. fixing a point or a projective line in
\mathbb{P}(\mathbb{R}^{3})
;

•

or
\rho(\Gamma)
is irreducible and

|\dim L(\rho(\Gamma))-\frac{3}{2}|\leqslant\epsilon.

In other words, once we perturb
\rho_{1}
to any irreducible
\rho
, there is a dimension jump with size about
1/2
, which does not occur in the conformal case. A generic perturbation
\rho
is irreducible because reducible representations form a proper subvariety in
\mathrm{Hom}(\Gamma,\mathrm{SL}_{3}(\mathbb{R}))
(cf.
[
BCLS15
]
).
The representation
\rho_{1}
in Theorem
1.3
is in the Barbot component of
\mathrm{Hom}(\Gamma,\mathrm{SL}_{3}(\mathbb{R}))
. For an irreducible Anosov representation in Barbot’s component, Barbot
[
Bar10
]
proved that the limit set is not Lipschitz. Our result provides new knowledge for Anosov representations in Barbot’s component.

There are non-algebraic settings where a similar dimension jump occurs. For example, see
[
RS21
]
and references therein for results on graphs of Weierstrass-type functions, and
[
BDV95
]
for discontinuity of Hausdorff dimension of hyperbolic sets.

1.1.3
Rauzy gasket

The results (
1.7
) in this paper allow us to study other fractal sets in
\mathbb{P}(\mathbb{R}^{3})
. In the second part of our work with Yuxiang Jiao
[
JLPX
]
,
we study the Rauzy gasket, a self-projective fractal set in
\mathbb{P}(\mathbb{R}^{3})
, and establish the identity between its Hausdorff dimension and its affinity exponent.

The gasket has an interesting history, appearing for the first time in 1991 in the work
of Arnoux and Rauzy
[
AR91
]
in the context of interval exchange transformations. The gasket reemerged in the work of De Leo and Dynnikov
[
DD09
]
in the context of Novikov’s theory
of magnetic fields on monocrystals. It also appeared in the work of Gamburd, Magee and
Ronan
[
GMR19
]
about the asymptotic estimates for integer solutions of the Markov-Hurwitz equations. For more background and history of the Rauzy gasket, please see
[
DHS20
]
and
[
JLPX
]
.

Let
\Delta
be the projectivization of
\{(x,y,z):x,y,z\geqslant 0\}
in
{\mathbb{P}}({\mathbb{R}}^{3}).

Let
\Gamma
be the semigroup generated by

A_{1}=\begin{pmatrix}1&1&1\\
0&1&0\\
0&0&1\end{pmatrix},\ A_{2}=\begin{pmatrix}1&0&0\\
1&1&1\\
0&0&1\end{pmatrix},\ A_{3}=\begin{pmatrix}1&0&0\\
0&1&0\\
1&1&1\end{pmatrix},

and we call it the Rauzy semigroup.
Then as
\Gamma\subset\mathrm{SL}_{3}(\mathbb{R})
, the semigroup
\Gamma
acts on
\mathbb{P}(\mathbb{R}^{3})
. Due to the choice of
\Delta
, the semigroup
\Gamma
preserves
\Delta
. The Rauzy gasket
\mathscr{R}
is the unique attractor of the Rauzy semigroup, which can be defined formally as

\bigcap_{n\rightarrow\infty}\bigcup_{i_{j}\in\{1,2,3\}}(A_{i_{1}}\cdots A_{i_{n}}\Delta).

Figure 1:
Figure in
[
AS13
]

It has received a lot of attention to study the Hausdorff dimension of the Rauzy Gasket
\mathscr{R}
, and the best-known bound is
1.19<\dim\mathscr{R}\leqslant 1.7407
due to
[
GRM20
]
and
[
PS21
]
respectively.
See also the previous works, for example,
[
Lev93
]
,
[
AHS16
]
,
[
Fou20
]
.

The affinity exponent
s_{\mathrm{A}}(\Gamma)
of the Rauzy gasket is the critical exponent of the Poincaré series
P_{\Gamma}(s)
defined in
Eq.

1.1
using the Rauzy semigroup
\Gamma
.
The following theorem confirms a folk-lore conjecture of the Hausdorff dimension of the Rauzy gasket.

Theorem 1.4
.

Let
\mathscr{R},\Gamma
be the Rauzy gasket and Rauzy semigroup respectively,
s_{\mathrm{A}}(\Gamma)
be the associated affinity exponent, then
\dim\mathscr{R}=s_{\mathrm{A}}(\Gamma)
.

We improve the numerical lower bound as an immediate corollary of the result and the proof of Theorem
1.4
.

Corollary 1.5
.

We have
\dim\mathscr{R}\geqslant 3/2
.

The argument is versatile and allows us to deal with a (semi)group that contains a large reducible subsemigroup, which is also the secret sauce of the dimension jump phenomenon in Theorem
1.3
.

Remark 1.6
.

Recently
Natalia Jurga also obtained
1.4
independently.

1.2
Dimension of stationary measures

Stationary measures are a fundamental tool and a central topic in the study of random walks arising from (semi)group actions on metric spaces. Its Hausdorff dimension encodes other important dynamically defined quantities of random walks, such as entropies and Lyapunov exponents.
It provides a powerful tool to estimate the lower bound of the Hausdorff dimension of a fractal set, allowing us to access the rich toolbox of random walks.

1.2.1
Hausdorff dimension and Lyapunov dimension of stationary measures

One key ingredient to prove
1.2
and
1.4
is to study the Hausdorff dimension and
Lyapunov dimension
of stationary measures coming from random walks on
\mathbb{P}(\mathbb{R}^{3})
, which is partially inspired by the methods in
[
BHR19
]
and
[
HS17
]
, which are developed from
[
Hoc14
]
.

Recall that the Hausdorff dimension of a Borel probability measure
\mu
on a metric space
X
is defined as

\dim\mu:=\inf_{A\subset X,\mu(A)=1}\dim A.

The Lyapunov dimension of measures is
an analog of the affinity exponent of sets. For our purpose, we only define it for stationary measures on
\mathbb{P}(\mathbb{R}^{3})
as follows.

In this paper, we call a measure supported on
\mathrm{SL}_{3}(\mathbb{R})

Zariski dense
if the group generated by
\supp\nu
is Zariski dense in
\mathrm{SL}_{3}(\mathbb{R})
. Let
\nu
be a finitely supported Zariski dense probability measure on
\mathrm{SL}_{3}(\mathbb{R})
, and
\mu
be the unique
\nu
-stationary measure (Furstenberg measure) on
\mathbb{P}(\mathbb{R}^{3})
, that is,
\mu
satisfies
\nu*\mu=\mu
, where the convolution is induced by the projective action of
\mathrm{SL}_{3}(\mathbb{R})
on
\mathbb{P}(\mathbb{R}^{3})
. Let
\lambda(\nu)=(\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2},\lambda_{3})
be the Lyapunov vector of
\nu
in decreasing order and
\chi_{i}(\nu)=\lambda_{1}-\lambda_{i+1}
. As
\nu
is Zariski dense, we have
\chi_{2}(\nu)>\chi_{1}(\nu)>0

[
GM89
]
and
[
GR85
]
. Then the Lyapunov dimension of
\mu
can be defined as (following
[
KY79
]
and
[
DO80
]
)

\dim_{\mathrm{LY}}\mu=\left\{\begin{aligned} &\frac{h_{\mathrm{F}}(\mu,\nu)}{\chi_{1}(\nu)},&\text{ if }h_{\mathrm{F}}(\mu,\nu)\leqslant\chi_{1}(\nu);\\
&1+\frac{h_{\mathrm{F}}(\mu,\nu)-\chi_{1}(\nu)}{\chi_{2}(\nu)},&\text{ otherwise,}\end{aligned}\right.

where
h_{\mathrm{F}}(\mu,\nu)
is the Furstenberg entropy (see
Eq.

2.81
for definition).

In
[
LL23b
]
and
[
Rap21
]
, the authors proved that
\dim_{\mathrm{LY}}\mu\geqslant\dim\mu
. Obtaining
\dim_{\mathrm{LY}}\mu\leqslant\dim\mu
is much more challenging. In the one-dimensional setting, the dimension theory and absolute continuity of Bernoulli convolution have attracted a lot of attention since Erdös. Hochman
[
Hoc14
]
made a breakthrough in the dimension theory. See also
[
Var22
]
for the series of progress of Varjú on the absolute continuity.

In this paper, we take a step further and study the action of
\mathrm{SL}_{3}(\mathbb{R})
on
\mathbb{P}(\mathbb{R}^{3})
, which extends
[
HS17
]
and
[
BHR19
]
to non-conformal and projective settings respectively.
Our result is as follows. We pose a mild separation assumption on random walks as in
[
HS17
]
,
[
BHR19
]
,
[
Hoc14
]
, etc.: a finitely supported measure
\nu
on
\mathrm{SL}_{3}(\mathbb{R})
is called satisfying the exponential separation condition, if there exist
C>0
and
N>0
, for all
n\geqslant N
and
(g_{1},\cdots,g_{n}),\ (g_{1}^{\prime},\cdots,g_{n}^{\prime})
in
(\supp\nu)^{\times n}
with
g_{1}\cdots g_{n}\neq g_{1}^{\prime}\cdots g_{n}^{\prime}
, then

d(g_{1}\cdots g_{n},g_{1}^{\prime}\cdots g_{n}^{\prime})>C^{-n},

where
d
is a left
\mathrm{SL}_{3}(\mathbb{R})
-invariant and right
\mathrm{SO}_{3}(\mathbb{R})
-invariant Riemannian metric on
\mathrm{SL}_{3}(\mathbb{R})
. In particular, if
\supp\nu
generates a discrete subgroup (for example,
\nu
is supported on an Anosov representation), then
\nu
satisfies the exponential separation condition.

Theorem 1.7
.

Let
\nu
be a Zariski dense, finitely supported probability measure on
\mathrm{SL}_{3}(\mathbb{R})
that satisfies the exponential separation condition, and
\mu
be its stationary measure on
\mathbb{P}(\mathbb{R}^{3})
. Then we have

\dim\mu=\dim_{\mathrm{LY}}\mu.

For the action of
\mathrm{SL}_{2}(\mathbb{R})
on
\mathbb{P}(\mathbb{R}^{2})
, Ledrappier obtained this result in 1983
[
Led83
]
. Later, Hochman-Solomyak advanced the knowledge in this setting
[
HS17
]
. Recall that the random walk entropy
h_{\mathrm{RW}}(\nu)
of
\nu
is defined by

h_{\mathrm{RW}}(\nu):=\lim_{n\rightarrow\infty}H(\nu^{*n})/n

(1.2)

with
H(\nu^{*n})
the Shannon entropy of a finite set. Hochman-Solomyak obtained
1.7
by replacing the Furstenberg entropy
h_{\mathrm{F}}(\mu,\nu)
by the random walk entropy
h_{\mathrm{RW}}(\nu)
.

Ledrappier-Lessa
[
LL23b
]
proved
1.7
for the case when the measure
\nu
is supported on a Hitchin representation in
\mathrm{PSL}_{n}(\mathbb{R})
for any
n\geqslant 3
. For a Hitchin representation, the associated limit set and hence the support of
\mu
lies in a
C^{1+\beta}
-circle with
\beta>0
, which they heavily rely on.
1.7
is the first general result in a higher dimensional projective space.

1.2.2
Dimension of the projection of stationary measures

Our strategy to show
1.7
is to combine a Ledrappier-Young formula in
[
Rap21
]
and
[
LL23b
]
, and a central result of dimension of projection measures which is a projective version of that in
[
BHR19
]
.

For any one-dimensional subspace
V\subset\mathbb{R}^{3}
, we consider the orthogonal projection from
\mathbb{R}^{3}-V
to
V^{\perp}
, which induces a smooth projection (along projective lines passing through
\mathbb{P}(V)
) from
\mathbb{P}(\mathbb{R}^{3})-\{V\}
to
\mathbb{P}(V^{\perp})
. We denote the smooth projection by
\pi_{V^{\perp}}
. For any probability measure
\nu
on
\mathrm{SL}_{3}(\mathbb{R})
, its inverse
\nu^{-}
is defined by
\nu^{-}(A):=\nu(\{g^{-1}:g\in A\})
with a Borel set
A
. In particular,
\nu
is Zariski dense if and only if so is
\nu^{-}
.

Given
\nu
a Zariski dense, finitely supported probability measure on
\mathrm{SL}_{3}(\mathbb{R})
, let
\mu^{-}
be the stationary measure of
\nu^{-}
on
\mathbb{P}(\mathbb{R}^{3})
. In
[
Rap21
]
and
[
LL23b
]
, they obtain a Ledrappier-Young formula, i.e. for
\mu^{-}
-a.e.
V\in\mathbb{P}(\mathbb{R}^{3})
,

\dim\mu=\dim\pi_{V^{\perp}}\mu+\frac{h_{\mathrm{F}}(\mu,\nu)-\chi_{1}(\nu)\dim\pi_{V^{\perp}}\mu}{\chi_{2}(\nu)};

(1.3)

moreover, they established an upper bound:
\dim\pi_{V^{\perp}}\mu\leqslant\min\{1,h_{\mathrm{F}}(\mu,\nu)/\chi_{1}\}
(see
Section

7.3
for more details).

In the following core theorem, we show that under the assumptions of Theorem
1.7
, the upper bound above is actually also a lower bound. Then Theorem
1.7
is a direct corollary of the following theorem, (
1.3
) and the general fact that
h_{\mathrm{F}}(\mu,\nu)\leqslant h_{\mathrm{RW}}(\nu)
.

Theorem 1.8
.

Let
\nu
be a Zariski dense, finitely supported probability measure on
\mathrm{SL}_{3}(\mathbb{R})
that satisfies the exponential separation condition, and
\mu
be its stationary measure on
\mathbb{P}(\mathbb{R}^{3})
. Then for
\mu^{-}
-a.e.
V\in\mathbb{P}(\mathbb{R}^{3})
, we have

\dim\pi_{V^{\perp}}\mu=\min\{1,\frac{h_{\mathrm{RW}}(\nu)}{\chi_{1}}\}.

1.3
Further questions

For the dimension jump phenomenon in Theorem
1.3
, if
\rho\in\mathrm{Hom}(\Gamma,\mathrm{SL}_{3}(\mathbb{R}))
is reducible, the analysis becomes more subtle, and we have several cases. If
\rho
preserves a projective line in
\mathbb{P}(\mathbb{R}^{3})
, then
L(\rho(\Gamma))
is this projective line. Hence
\dim L(\rho(\Gamma))=1
. If
\rho
does not preserve a projective line, then
L(\rho(\Gamma))
is not Lipschitz
[
Bar10
, Theorem 4.4]
. And
L(\rho(\Gamma))
is a graph-like set, and its dual representation
\rho^{\ast}
preserves a projective line which is actually
L(\rho^{\ast}(\Gamma))
.

Question 1.9
.

In the following table, are the entries with question marks true?

If we can have a positive answer to this question, this table shows that a similar dimension jump phenomenon holds. Then one can distinguish different types of
\rho
in
O
using the Hausdorff dimension of limit sets.

Table 1:
Hausdorff dimension of different types of representations

Reducibility type of
\rho\in O

\dim L(\rho(\Gamma))

\dim L(\rho^{\ast}(\Gamma))

Semisimple

1

1

point-irreducible

1

\approx 3/2
(?)

line-irreducible

\approx 3/2
(?)

1

Irreducible (in this paper)

\approx 3/2

\approx 3/2

•

We follow the definition in
[
LLS21
]
. A reducible representation
\rho:\Gamma\to\mathrm{SL}_{3}(\mathbb{R})
is called
point-irreducible
if it does not preserve any point in
\mathbb{P}(\mathbb{R}^{3})
;
line-irreducible
if it does not preserve any projective line in
\mathbb{P}(\mathbb{R}^{3})
;
semisimple
if it is a product of irreducible representations.

A natural further study is to generalize the results in this paper to higher dimensional Lie groups, and among them, a relatively approachable question is to generalize the dimension jump phenomenon in
1.3
.
To some degree, this phenomenon is attributed to the fact that the Hausdorff dimension of the limit set is not equal to the affinity exponent when the representation preserves a plane. For higher dimensional Lie groups, we can similarly define Anosov representations (cf.
[
Can
]
, etc.), associated limit sets, and affinity exponents. We pose the following general question.

Question 1.10
.

Let
\Gamma
be a hyperbolic group. Classify for which
\rho\in\mathrm{HA}(\Gamma,\mathrm{SL}_{n}(\mathbb{R}))
satisfies

\dim L(\rho(\Gamma))<s_{\mathrm{A}}(\rho).

This question is indeed quite delicate even when one seeks a complete answer for
\mathrm{SL}_{4}(\mathbb{R})
analogous to the above table for
\mathrm{SL}_{3}(\mathbb{R})
.

The continuity of the affinity exponent
s_{\mathrm{A}}(\rho)
is essentially due to
[
PS17
]
and
[
BCLS15
]
. For completeness, we provide a proof in
Section

8.2
.
In the setting of Bowen’s result
[
Bow79
]
, it is known that classical critical exponents depend on representations analytically
[
Rue82
]
. It is natural to ask the following question.

Question 1.11
.

Does the affinity exponent
s_{\mathrm{A}}(\rho)
depend on
\rho\in\mathrm{HA}(\Gamma,\mathrm{SL}_{3}(\mathbb{R}))
analytically?

Bowen’s dimension rigidity result
[
Bow79
]
is a global result for all quasi-Fuchsian representations. It is interesting to investigate the following question.

Question 1.12
.

Do all irreducible Anosov representations in Barbot’s component have an affinity exponent greater than
1
?

In works like
[
Sul79
]
, Patterson-Sullivan measures are central in computing the Hausdorff dimension of limit sets. For Anosov representations, we also have Patterson-Sullivan measures on the limit sets
[
Qui02
]
,
[
PSW22
]
. Our current computation of the Hausdorff dimension of limit sets does not use these nice measures. It is interesting to explore the following question.

Question 1.13
.

Is there a Patterson-Sullivan measure that has the same Hausdorff dimension as the limit set?

Convention

Throughout the paper, we use base-
q
logarithm, where
q
is a fixed integer large enough that will be defined in
Section

2.9
.

Organization of the paper.

After some preparations,
the proof of
1.8
will occupy the most part the paper,
Section

3
-
Section

7
. In
Section

8
, we will give the proof of
1.2
from
1.7
by admitting a variational principle proved in the second paper
[
JLPX
]
joint with Yuxiang Jiao.

2
Preliminaries

2.1
Action of
\mathrm{GL}_{n}(\mathbb{R})
on
\mathbb{P}(\mathbb{R}^{n})

Consider the
n
-dimensional Euclidean space
\mathbb{R}^{n}
and denote by
\|\cdot\|
the Euclidean norm. By abuse of the notation, we denote by
\|\cdot\|
the norm on
\wedge^{2}\mathbb{R}^{n}
induced by the one in
\mathbb{R}^{n}
. Any element
g\in\mathrm{GL}_{n}(\mathbb{R})
acts on
\mathbb{R}^{n}
and we denote the operator norm by
\|g\|
. Let
e_{1},\cdots,e_{n}
be the standard orthonormal basis of
\mathbb{R}^{n}
and
e_{1}^{*},\cdots,e_{n}^{*}
be the dual basis of
(\mathbb{R}^{n})^{*}
. Let
E_{i}=\mathbb{R}e_{i}
be the corresponding element in
\mathbb{P}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
with
i=1,\cdots,n
. Throughout the paper, we always consider the following metric on the projective space
\mathbb{P}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
unless otherwise stated.

Definition 2.1
.

The distance
d
on
\mathbb{P}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
is defined by

d(\mathbb{R}v,\mathbb{R}w):=\frac{\|v\wedge w\|}{\|v\|\|w\|}\,\,\,\text{for any}\,\,\,\mathbb{R}v,\mathbb{R}w\in\mathbb{P}(\mathbb{R}^{n}).

(2.1)

When
A,B
are two hyperplanes of
\mathbb{P}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
, we write
d(A,B)
for their Hausdorff distance.

Remark 2.2
.

The metric
d
is bi-Lipschitz equivalent to the standard
\mathrm{SO}(n)
-invariant Riemannian metric
d_{R}
on
\mathbb{P}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
(coming from the double cover of
\mathbb{P}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
by the unit sphere). More precisely, we have
d(\mathbb{R}v,\mathbb{R}w)=\sin(d_{R}(\mathbb{R}v,\mathbb{R}w))
for any
\mathbb{R}v,\mathbb{R}w\in\mathbb{P}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
.

We will frequently consider the following subgroups of
\mathrm{GL}_{n}(\mathbb{R})
:

\displaystyle K:=

\displaystyle\mathrm{O}_{n}(\mathbb{R}),

\displaystyle A:=

\displaystyle\{a=\operatorname{diag}(a_{1},\ldots,a_{n}):a_{i}\neq 0\},

\displaystyle A^{+}:=

\displaystyle\{a=\operatorname{diag}(a_{1},\ldots,a_{n})\in A:a_{1}\geqslant\ldots\geqslant a_{n}\}.

Definition 2.3
.

For
g\in\mathrm{GL}_{n}(\mathbb{R})
, let
\sigma_{1}(g)\geqslant\cdots\geqslant\sigma_{n}(g)
be the singular values of
g
and
g=\tilde{k}_{g}a_{g}k_{g}\in KA^{+}K
be the Cartan decomposition of
g
(singular value decomposition). Let
\epsilon>0
. Set

•

\chi_{i}(g):=\log(\sigma_{1}(g)/\sigma_{i+1}(g)),1\leqslant i\leqslant n-1
;

•

V_{g}^{+}:=\tilde{k}_{g}E_{1}\in\mathbb{P}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
, which is an attracting point of
g
.

•

H_{g}^{-}:=k_{g}^{-1}(E_{2}\oplus\cdots\oplus E_{n})\subset\mathbb{P}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
, which is a repelling hyperplane of
g

(if
\sigma_{1}>\sigma_{2}
, then
V_{g}^{+}
and
H_{g}^{-}
are uniquely defined);

•

b(g^{-},\epsilon):=\{x\in\mathbb{P}(\mathbb{R}^{n}):\ d(x,H_{g}^{-})>\epsilon\}
for any
\epsilon>0
;

•

B(g^{+},\epsilon):=\{x\in\mathbb{P}(\mathbb{R}^{n}):\ d(x,V_{g}^{+})\leqslant\epsilon\}
for any
\epsilon>0
.

The Cartan projection of
g
is defined to be
\kappa(g):=(\log\sigma_{1}(g),\cdots,\log\sigma_{n}(g))
.

We now state some basic contracting properties of the action of
\mathrm{GL}_{n}(\mathbb{R})
on
\mathbb{P}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
.

Lemma 2.4
.

For any
g\in\mathrm{GL}_{n}(\mathbb{R})
and any
\epsilon>0
, we have

•

g
acts on
b(g^{-},\epsilon)
by contraction: for any
x\neq y
in
b(g^{-},\epsilon)
, we have
\frac{d(g(x),g(y))}{d(x,y)}\leqslant\frac{\sigma_{2}}{\epsilon^{2}\sigma_{1}}
;

•

g(b(g^{-},\epsilon))\subset B(g^{+},\frac{\sigma_{2}}{\epsilon^{2}\sigma_{1}})
;

•

when
g\in\mathrm{SL}_{2}(\mathbb{R})
, we have
\frac{\sigma_{1}(g)}{\sigma_{2}(g)}=\|g\|^{2}
.

Please see
[
BQ16
]
or
[
Li22
, Lemma 2.11]
for the proof.

We state another useful lemma
[
BQ16
, Lemma 14.2 ]
for later use.

Lemma 2.5
.

For any
g\in\mathrm{GL}_{n}(\mathbb{R})
and
V=\mathbb{R}v\in\mathbb{P}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
, we have

\displaystyle d(V,H_{g}^{-})\leqslant\frac{\|gv\|}{\|g\|\|v\|}\leqslant d(V,H_{g}^{-})+q^{-\chi_{1}(g)},\quad d(gV,V_{g}^{+})d(V,H_{g}^{-})\leqslant q^{-\chi_{1}(g)}.

Definition 2.6
.

For a bi-Lipschitz map
f
between two metric spaces
(X,d_{X})
and
(Y,d_{Y})
, we say
f
scales by
u>0
with distortion
C>1
if for any
x\neq x^{\prime}\in X
,

\frac{1}{C}\leqslant\frac{d_{Y}(f(x),f(x^{\prime}))}{ud_{X}(x,x^{\prime})}\leqslant C.

(2.2)

We fix an identification between

\mathbb{P}(\mathbb{R}^{2})
and
\mathbb{R}/\mathbb{Z}
:

\displaystyle\iota:\mathbb{P}(\mathbb{R}^{2})

\displaystyle\to\mathbb{R}/\mathbb{Z}

\displaystyle\mathbb{R}(\cos\theta,\sin\theta)

\displaystyle\mapsto\frac{\theta}{\pi}.

(2.3)

When an element
g\in\mathrm{GL}_{2}(\mathbb{R})
acts on
\mathbb{P}(\mathbb{R}^{2})
, we view
g
as a diffeomorphism of
\mathbb{R}/\mathbb{Z}
by conjugating it by
\iota
and denote by
|g^{\prime}x|
and
|g^{\prime\prime}x|
the
1
st and
2
nd derivatives of
\iota g\iota^{-1}
at
\iota x
. The following are estimates of
|g^{\prime}x|
and
|g^{\prime\prime}x|
, which can be found in
[
HS17
, Page 826]
. For later usage, we introduce
\mathrm{SL}_{2}^{\pm}(\mathbb{R})
, the group of two by two matrices with determinant equal to
1
or
-1
.

Lemma 2.7
.

For
g\in\mathrm{SL}_{2}^{\pm}(\mathbb{R})
and
x\in\mathbb{P}(\mathbb{R}^{2})
, we have

\|g\|^{-2}\leqslant|g^{\prime}x|\leqslant\|g\|^{2},\ |g^{\prime\prime}x|\leqslant 4\|g\|^{2}.

Lemma 2.8
.

For all
0<\epsilon<1/3
, the following holds. Let
g
be an element in
\mathrm{SL}_{2}^{\pm}(\mathbb{R})
. Then for action of
g
on
b(g^{-},\epsilon)\subset\mathbb{P}(\mathbb{R}^{2})
, it scales by
\|g\|^{-2}
with distortion
10\epsilon^{-2}
. Actually, we have a more precise estimate: for any
x\in b(g^{-},\epsilon)
,

\displaystyle-2\log\|g\|\leqslant\log|g^{\prime}(x)|

\displaystyle\leqslant-2\log\|g\|-2\log(\epsilon),

\displaystyle|g^{\prime\prime}(x)|

\displaystyle\leqslant\frac{10}{\epsilon^{3}\|g\|^{2}}.

2.2
Projections in
\mathbb{R}^{3}
and
\mathbb{P}(\mathbb{R}^{3})

Definition 2.9
.

For any line
V
and any hyperplanes
W,W^{\prime}
of
\mathbb{R}^{3}
such that
V\not\subset W,W^{\prime}
, and any
g\in\mathrm{SL}_{3}(\mathbb{R})
such that
g^{-1}V\not\subset V^{\perp}
, we denote

•

the linear projection on
\mathbb{R}^{3}
with kernel
V
and image
W
by
\Pi_{V,W}
;

•

the orthogonal projection with kernel
V
by
\Pi_{V^{\perp}}(=\Pi_{V,V^{\perp}})
;

•

the linear projection from
W^{\prime}
to
W
along
V
(i.e. with kernel
V
) by
\Pi(V,W,W^{\prime})
, i.e.
\Pi(V,W,W^{\prime})=\Pi_{V,W}|_{W^{\prime}}
;

•

the projective transformations associated to
\Pi_{V,W}
,
\Pi_{V^{\perp}}
,
\Pi(V,W,W^{\prime})
by
\pi_{V,W}
,
\pi_{V^{\perp}}
,
\pi(V,W,W^{\prime})
respectively;

•

the composition map
\pi_{V^{\perp}}\circ g|_{V^{\perp}}
by
h_{V,g}:\mathbb{P}(V^{\perp})\to\mathbb{P}(V^{\perp})
.

One important observation is the following geometric lemma on a decomposition of the map
\pi_{V^{\perp}}\circ g
, which enables us to change the direction of projection and apply the ergodic theory on projections.

Lemma 2.10
.

For any
g\in\mathrm{SL}_{3}(\mathbb{R})
,
V\in\mathbb{P}(\mathbb{R}^{3})
such that
g^{-1}V\not\subset V^{\perp}
, we have:

\displaystyle\pi_{V^{\perp}}\circ g

\displaystyle{}=h_{V,g}\circ\pi_{{g^{-1}V},V^{\perp}},

(2.4)

\displaystyle\pi_{V^{\perp}}\circ g

\displaystyle{}=h_{V,g}\circ\pi({g^{-1}V},V^{\perp},(g^{-1}V)^{\perp})\circ\pi_{(g^{-1}V)^{\perp}}.

(2.5)

Proof.

It suffices to show the corresponding equations for linear maps in
\mathbb{R}^{3}
. We have

\displaystyle\Pi_{V^{\perp}}\circ g

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle(\Pi_{V^{\perp}}\circ g)|_{V^{\perp}}\circ\Pi_{g^{-1}V,V^{\perp}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle(\Pi_{V^{\perp}}\circ g)|_{V^{\perp}}\circ\Pi({g^{-1}V},V^{\perp},(g^{-1}V)^{\perp})\circ\Pi_{g^{-1}V,(g^{-1}V)^{\perp}}.

The first equality holds because both
\Pi_{V^{\perp}}\circ g
and
(\Pi_{V^{\perp}}\circ g)|_{V^{\perp}}\circ\Pi_{g^{-1}V,V^{\perp}}
are linear maps from
\mathbb{R}^{3}
to
V^{\perp}
. They have the same kernel which is
g^{-1}V
, and their restrictions to
V^{\perp}
, a complement to
g^{-1}V
, are the same.

The second equality is obtained similarly
by analyzing the kernels and images of the linear maps.
∎

2.3
Decomposition of
\mathrm{SL}_{3}(\mathbb{R})
for the composition maps
\pi_{V^{\perp}}g

In this subsection, we introduce a decomposition of the Lie group
\mathrm{SL}_{3}(\mathbb{R})
, which describes the structure of the composition maps
\pi_{V^{\perp}}g
with
V\in\mathbb{P}(\mathbb{R}^{3})
and
g\in\mathrm{SL}_{3}(\mathbb{R})
.

We start with the decomposition for
E_{1}\in\mathbb{P}(\mathbb{R}^{3})
. Let
U,L
be two closed Lie subgroups of
\mathrm{SL}_{3}(\mathbb{R})
defined by

\displaystyle U

\displaystyle{}:=\left\{\begin{pmatrix}\lambda^{2}&x\ \ y\\
0&\lambda^{-1}\mathrm{Id}_{2}\end{pmatrix}:\lambda\in\mathbb{R}^{+},x,y\in\mathbb{R}\right\},

\displaystyle L

\displaystyle{}:=\left\{\begin{pmatrix}\det h&0\\
n&h\end{pmatrix}:n\in\mathbb{R}^{2},h\in\mathrm{SL}_{2}^{\pm}(\mathbb{R})\right\}.

The group
U
is a solvable subgroup, and the group
L
is isomorphic to
\mathrm{SL}_{2}^{\pm}(\mathbb{R})\ltimes\mathbb{R}^{2}
.

For a general
V\in\mathbb{P}(\mathbb{R}^{3})
, we define
U_{V}:=k^{-1}Uk
and
L_{V}:=k^{-1}Lk
, where
k
is any matrix in
\mathrm{SO}_{3}(\mathbb{R})
satisfying
kV=E_{1}
. We also define the projection

\begin{split}\pi_{L_{V}}:U_{V}L_{V}&\to L_{V}\\
u\ell&\mapsto\ell.\end{split}

(2.6)

The following lemma shows that

U_{V},L_{V},\pi_{L_{V}}
are well-defined and explains the roles that
U_{V}
and
L_{V}
play in the composition maps
\pi_{V^{\perp}}g
with
g\in\mathrm{SL}_{3}(\mathbb{R})
.

Lemma 2.11
.

For any
V\in\mathbb{P}(\mathbb{R}^{3})
and any
k\in\mathrm{SO}_{3}(\mathbb{R})
such that
kV=E_{1}
. The followings hold.

1.

The groups
U_{V}
and
L_{V}
are well-defined, i.e. independent of the choice of
k\in\mathrm{SO}_{3}(\mathbb{R})
.

2.

The product
U_{V}L_{V}
equals the set

\{g\in\mathrm{SL}_{3}(\mathbb{R}):g^{-1}V\notin V^{\perp}\},

and it is Zariski open and Zariski dense in
\mathrm{SL}_{3}(\mathbb{R})
.

3.

The map
\pi_{L_{V}}
is well-defined, i.e., if
u\ell=u^{\prime}\ell^{\prime}
in
U_{V}L_{V}
, then
\pi_{L_{V}}(u\ell)=\pi_{L_{V}}(u^{\prime}\ell^{\prime})
.

4.

For the group
U_{V}
, we have

\{g\in\mathrm{SL}_{3}(\mathbb{R}):\pi_{V^{\perp}}g=\pi_{V^{\perp}}id\}=U_{V}\cup U_{V}k^{-1}\begin{pmatrix}1&0\\
0&-\mathrm{Id}_{2}\end{pmatrix}k.

(2.7)

5.

For every
g\in U_{V}L_{V}
, write
g=k^{-1}u\ell k=k^{-1}u\begin{pmatrix}\det h&0\\
n&h\end{pmatrix}k
with
u\in U
,
\ell\in L
,
n\in\mathbb{R}^{2}
and
h\in\mathrm{SL}_{2}^{\pm}(\mathbb{R})
. We have

h_{V,g}=h

as elements in
\mathrm{PGL}_{2}(\mathbb{R})
, where
h_{V,g}
is given as in
2.9
.

6.

Consider the map

\displaystyle\Phi:L_{V}

\displaystyle\to\{\text{maps from}\,\,\mathbb{P}(\mathbb{R}^{3})\,\,\text{to}\,\,\mathbb{P}(V^{\perp})\}

\displaystyle\ell

\displaystyle\mapsto\pi_{V^{\perp}}\ell.

If
\ell\neq\ell^{\prime}\in L
satisfy
\Phi(\ell)=\Phi(\ell^{\prime})
, then
\ell^{\prime}\ell^{-1}=k^{-1}\begin{pmatrix}1&0\\
0&-\mathrm{Id}_{2}\end{pmatrix}k
.

Proof.

1.

For any
V\in\mathbb{P}(\mathbb{R}^{3})
, let
k_{0}
be a matrix in
\mathrm{SO}_{3}(\mathbb{R})
satisfying
kV=E_{1}
. We have

\{k\in\mathrm{SO}_{3}(\mathbb{R}):kV=E_{1}\}=\left\{\begin{pmatrix}1&0\\
0&k_{1}\end{pmatrix}k_{0}:k_{1}\in\mathrm{SO}_{2}(\mathbb{R})\right\}.

Any matrix of the form
\begin{pmatrix}1&0\\
0&k_{1}\end{pmatrix}
with
k_{1}\in\operatorname{SO}_{2}(\mathbb{R})
normalizes the groups
U
and
L
. This shows that

U_{V}
and
L_{V}
are well-defined.

With the first statement available, it suffices to prove the remaining statements for the case
V=E_{1}
.

2.

We prove

UL=\{g\in\mathrm{SL}_{3}(\mathbb{R}):g^{-1}E_{1}\notin E_{1}^{\perp}\}.

(2.8)

The direction
\subset
can be checked by a straightforward computation using the definitions of
U
and
L
.

For the direction
\supset
, given any
g\in\mathrm{SL}_{3}(\mathbb{R})
such that
g^{-1}E_{1}\notin E_{1}^{\perp}
, the entry in the first row and first column of
g^{-1}
is non-zero.
So the 2 by 2 submatrix
g^{\prime}
of
g
in the lower right corner is non-degenerate. We can write
g^{\prime}=\lambda^{-1}h
with
\lambda>0
and
h\in\mathrm{SL}_{2}^{\pm}(\mathbb{R})
, and hence we can find
u\in U
and
\ell\in L
such that
g=u\ell
.

UL
is Zariski open and Zariski dense because (
2.8
) shows that it is the complement of the proper Zariski closed subvariety
\{g\in\mathrm{SL}_{3}(\mathbb{R}):g^{-1}E_{1}\in E_{1}^{\perp}\}
.

3.

Suppose
u\ell=u^{\prime}\ell^{\prime}
in
UL
.
Then
\ell^{\prime}\ell^{-1}=u^{\prime-1}u\in U\cap L=\{\mathrm{Id}_{3}\}
.

4.

We can check that the group
U
is contained in the group (
2.7
) by a straightforward computation using the definition of
U
.

Given any
g\in\mathrm{SL}_{3}(\mathbb{R})
such that
\pi_{E_{1}^{\perp}}g=\pi_{E_{1}^{\perp}}\mathrm{id}
, we have
gE_{1}=E_{1}
. So
g\in UL
by (
2.8
) and we can write
g=u\ell
with
u\in U
and
\ell\in L
. The relation
E_{1}=gE_{1}=u\ell E_{1}
gives
\ell E_{1}=E_{1}
. As ha result, we can write
\ell=\begin{pmatrix}\det h&0\\
0&h\end{pmatrix}
with
h\in\mathrm{SL}_{2}^{\pm}(\mathbb{R})
. So

\pi_{E_{1}^{\perp}}\mathrm{id}=\pi_{E_{1}^{\perp}}g=\pi_{{E_{1}}^{\perp}}\begin{pmatrix}\det h&0\\
0&h\end{pmatrix}=h.

This implies
h=\mathrm{Id}_{2}
or
-\mathrm{Id}_{2}
.

5.

The fifth statement
holds because

\displaystyle\pi_{E_{1}^{\perp}}g|_{E_{1}^{\perp}}=\pi_{E_{1}^{\perp}}\begin{pmatrix}\det h&0\\
n&h\end{pmatrix}\bigg|_{E_{1}^{\perp}}=h.

6.

Since we have

\pi_{E_{1}^{\perp}}(\ell^{\prime}\ell^{-1})=\pi_{E_{1}^{\perp}}(\ell^{\prime})\circ\ell^{-1}=\pi_{E_{1}^{\perp}}(\ell)\circ\ell^{-1}=\pi_{E_{1}^{\perp}}id,

the sixth statement follows from the fourth statement.

∎

A.1
fixes an identification between
\mathbb{P}(V^{\perp})
with
\mathbb{P}(\mathbb{R}^{2})
for
V\in\mathcal{C}=\mathbb{P}(\mathbb{R}^{3})-\mathbb{P}(E_{1}^{\perp})
. This identification provides a unique choice of
k\in\mathrm{SO}_{3}(\mathbb{R})
.

2.4
Linearize the projection of
L
-action

In this part, we consider the point
E_{1}\in\mathbb{P}(\mathbb{R}^{3})
and the corresponding
UL
-decomposition. We fix a left
L
-invariant and right
\operatorname{SO}(2)
-invariant Riemannian metric
d
on
L
.
For
\ell\in L
and
x\in\mathbb{P}(\mathbb{R}^{3})
, we introduce the following notation

[\ell(x)]:=\pi_{E_{1}^{\perp}}\ell(x)\in\mathbb{P}(E_{1}^{\perp})\cong\mathbb{P}(\mathbb{R}^{2}).

We will always write
\ell=\begin{pmatrix}\det h&0\\
n&h\end{pmatrix}
with
n\in\mathbb{R}^{2}
and
h\in\mathrm{SL}_{2}^{\pm}(\mathbb{R})
as the matrix representation of
\ell
. Then

[\ell(x)]=\mathbb{R}(h(b,c)^{t}+an),

(2.9)

for any
x=\mathbb{R}(a,b,c)^{t}\in\mathbb{P}(\mathbb{R}^{3})
.

The matrix
h
acts on
\mathbb{P}(E_{1}^{\perp})\cong\mathbb{P}(\mathbb{R}^{2})
. Let
h=\tilde{k}_{h}a_{h}k_{h}\in KA^{+}K
be the Cartan decomposition of
h
, and let
H_{h}^{-}\in\mathbb{P}(E_{1}^{\perp})
be its repelling point given as in
2.3
.

Observing that by
Eq.

2.4
and
2.11
, for
\ell=\begin{pmatrix}\det h&0\\
n&h\end{pmatrix}
, we have

\pi_{{E_{1}}^{\perp}}\ell=h\circ\pi_{\ell^{-1}E_{1},E_{1}^{\perp}}.

(2.10)

(As in Figure
3
, for any
x
, the projection
\pi_{\ell^{-1}E_{1},E_{1}^{\perp}}x
can
be viewed as the intersection of the projective line joint by
x
and
\pi_{(\ell^{-1}E_{1})^{\perp}}x
with the projective line in red
\mathbb{P}(E_{1}^{\perp})
, and we decompose
\pi_{E_{1}^{\perp}}\ell
as the composition of
h
with the projection
\pi_{\ell^{-1}E_{1},E_{1}^{\perp}}
.)

Figure 2:
\pi_{{E_{1}}^{\perp}}\ell=h\circ\pi_{\ell^{-1}E_{1},E_{1}^{\perp}}

Figure 3:
The
[\ell]
-attracting region

The contracting region of
\pi_{E_{1}^{\perp}}\ell
should be away from the projective plane spanned by
\ell^{-1}{E_{1}}
and
H_{h}^{-}
. At the same time, note that we have the formula for the linear map

\displaystyle\Pi_{\ell^{-1}E_{1},E_{1}^{\perp}}:\mathbb{R}^{3}

\displaystyle\to\mathbb{R}^{2}

\displaystyle(a,b,c)^{t}

\displaystyle\mapsto(b,c)^{t}+ah^{-1}n.

This motivates the following construction.

Let
f_{h}
be a linear form on
E_{1}^{\perp}\cong\mathbb{R}^{2}
with kernel
H_{h}^{-}
of norm
1
. For any non-zero vector
v\in\mathbb{R}^{2}
, we have

|f_{h}(v)|=\|v\|d(\mathbb{R}v,H_{h}^{-}).

(2.11)

Definition 2.12
.

Let
\ell=\begin{pmatrix}\det h&0\\
n&h\end{pmatrix}\in L
.
We define the linear form
f_{\ell}
on
\mathbb{R}^{3}
by

f_{\ell}(v):=f_{h}(\Pi_{\ell^{-1}E_{1},E_{1}^{\perp}}(v))\,\,\,\text{for}\,\,\,v\in\mathbb{R}^{3}.

The kernel of
f_{\ell}
is the plane spanned by
\ell^{-1}{E_{1}}=\mathbb{R}(1,-h^{-1}n)^{t}

4
4

4

We abuse the notation here and
(1,-h^{-1}n)
is actually the row vector
(1,-(h^{-1}n)^{t})
.
and
H_{h}^{-}\subset E_{1}^{\perp}\subset\mathbb{R}^{3}
.

For
\epsilon>0
, we define an
[\ell]
-attracting region
by

b(f_{\ell},\epsilon):=\left\{x=\mathbb{R}v\in\mathbb{P}(\mathbb{R}^{3}):|f_{\ell}(v)|\geqslant\epsilon\|f_{\ell}\|\|v\|\right\}.

(2.12)

Figure
3
shows the region
b(f_{l},\epsilon)
, here the arrows in blue means
h
is repelling near
H_{h}^{-}\in\mathbb{P}(E_{1}^{\perp})
.

We summarize the structure of the rest of this subsection: in
2.13
, we prove the contracting properties of
\pi_{E_{1}^{\perp}}\ell(\cdot)=[\ell(\cdot)]
on the region
b(f_{\ell},\epsilon)
;
2.14
,
2.16
and
2.17
collect the continuity properties of
\ell
,
f_{\ell}
and
b(f_{\ell},\epsilon)
; we also need an estimate of the diameter of a set acted by the projection of
L
-action (
2.18
);
2.20
is the linearization of the map
\pi_{E_{1}^{\perp}}\ell
.

We start with some basic estimates, which will be frequently used. Note that for any
\ell\in L
,

d(\ell^{-1}E_{1},E_{1}^{\perp})=d(\mathbb{R}(1,-h^{-1}n)^{t},E_{1}^{\perp})=1/\|(1,-h^{-1}n)^{t}\|.

(2.13)

Hence

(1+\|h^{-1}n\|^{2})^{1/2}=1/d(\ell^{-1}E_{1},E_{1}^{\perp}).

(2.14)

For
x=\mathbb{R}v
with
v=(a,b,c)^{t}
and
\|v\|=1
, we have

•

\|\Pi_{\ell^{-1}E_{1},E_{1}^{\perp}}(v)\|\leqslant 1/d(\ell^{-1}E_{1},E_{1}^{\perp});

(2.15)

•

\|f_{\ell}\|\in[1,1/d(\ell^{-1}E_{1},E_{1}^{\perp})];

(2.16)

•

if
x\in b(f_{\ell},\epsilon)
, then

\displaystyle\|\Pi_{\ell^{-1}E_{1},E_{1}^{\perp}}(v)\|\geqslant\epsilon\|v\|,

(2.17)

\displaystyle d(x,\ell^{-1}{E_{1}})\geqslant\epsilon d(\ell^{-1}{E_{1}},{E_{1}}^{\perp}).

(2.18)

Eq.

2.15
holds because

\|\Pi_{\ell^{-1}E_{1},E_{1}^{\perp}}(v)\|=\|(b,c)^{t}+ah^{-1}n\|\leqslant\|(1,-h^{-1}n)^{t}\|.

Eq.

2.17
holds because

\|\Pi_{\ell^{-1}E_{1},E_{1}^{\perp}}(v)\|\geqslant|f_{h}(\Pi_{\ell^{-1}E_{1},E_{1}^{\perp}}(v))|\geqslant\epsilon\|f_{\ell}\|\|v\|\geqslant\epsilon\|v\|.

Eq.

2.16
is obtained as follows. For any
v\in E_{1}^{\perp}
,
f_{\ell}(v)=f_{h}(v)
. As the norm of
f_{h}
is
1
,
\|f_{\ell}\|\geqslant 1
. Meanwhile, by definition, we have
\|f_{\ell}\|\leqslant\|f_{h}\|\cdot\|\Pi_{\ell^{-1}E_{1},E_{1}^{\perp}}\|
. So we use
Eq.

2.15
to get the upper bound.

We obtain
Eq.

2.18
as follows:

\displaystyle d(x,\ell^{-1}{E_{1}})

\displaystyle=\frac{\|(a,b,c)^{t}\wedge(1,-h^{-1}n)^{t}\|}{\|(1,-h^{-1}n)^{t}\|}

\displaystyle\geqslant d(\ell^{-1}{E_{1}},E_{1}^{\perp})\|(b,c)^{t}+ah^{-1}n\|=d(\ell^{-1}{E_{1}},E_{1}^{\perp})\|\Pi_{\ell^{-1}E_{1},E_{1}^{\perp}}(v)\|,

where for the inequality we project the vector
(a,b,c)^{t}\wedge(1,-h^{-1}n)^{t}
to the subspace generated by
E_{1}\wedge E_{2},E_{1}\wedge E_{3}
in
\wedge^{2}\mathbb{R}^{3}
and due to
Eq.

2.13
.

Lemma 2.13
.

Let
C_{1},C_{2}>2
. Let
\ell=\begin{pmatrix}\det h&0\\
n&h\end{pmatrix}\in L
be such that

d(\ell^{-1}{E_{1}},E_{1}^{\perp})>1/C_{1}
. Then for
x=\mathbb{R}v
with
\|v\|=1
in the
[\ell]
-attracting region
b(f_{\ell},1/C_{2})
,
we have

\displaystyle\|\Pi_{E_{1}^{\perp}}\ell(v)\|=\|h\circ\Pi_{\ell^{-1}E_{1},E_{1}^{\perp}}(v)\|\geqslant\|h\|/C_{2},

(2.19)

\displaystyle d(\pi_{\ell^{-1}E_{1},E_{1}^{\perp}}(x),H_{h}^{-})\geqslant 1/C_{1}C_{2}.

(2.20)

Moreover, for the action of
\pi_{E_{1}^{\perp}}\ell
on
b(f_{\ell},1/C_{2})
, we have

\frac{d([\ell(x)],[\ell(x^{\prime})])}{d(x,x^{\prime})}\leqslant 2C_{1}C_{2}^{2}/\|h\|^{2}

(2.21)

for any two distinct points
x,x^{\prime}
in
b(f_{\ell},1/C_{2})
, and the inclusion

[\ell(b(f_{\ell},1/C_{2}))]\subset B(h^{+},C_{1}C_{2}/\|h\|^{2}).

(2.22)

Proof.

We write
w=\Pi_{\ell^{-1}E_{1},E_{1}^{\perp}}(v)
. Hence
\mathbb{R}w=\pi_{\ell^{-1}E_{1},E_{1}^{\perp}}(x)
.

Eq.

2.19
holds because

\|hw\|\geqslant\|h\|\|w\|d(\mathbb{R}w,H_{h}^{-})=\|h\||f_{h}(w)|=\|h\||f_{\ell}(v)|\geqslant\|h\|/C_{2}.

Here the first inequality follows from
2.5
; the second one is by
Eq.

2.11
; the last inequality is due to
\|f_{\ell}\|\geqslant 1
(
Eq.

2.16
) and the definition of
b(f_{\ell},1/C_{2})
.

As
\|v\|=1
, by
Eq.

2.15
, we have

\|w\|=\|\Pi_{\ell^{-1}E_{1},E_{1}^{\perp}}(v)\|\leqslant 1/d(\ell^{-1}E_{1},E_{1}^{\perp})<C_{1}.

(2.23)

Eq.

2.20
holds because

d(\mathbb{R}w,H_{h}^{-})=|f_{h}(w)|/\|w\|\geqslant 1/C_{1}C_{2}.

For
Eq.

2.22
, note that
Eq.

2.10
gives

[\ell(x)]=h(\mathbb{R}w).

With
Eq.

2.20
available, we apply
2.4
to the action of
h
and obtain the inclusion relation.

It remains to prove
Eq.

2.21
. Take any point
x^{\prime}\neq x
in
b(f_{\ell},1/C_{2})
. Write
x^{\prime}=\mathbb{R}(a^{\prime},b^{\prime},c^{\prime})^{t}
with
\|(a^{\prime},b^{\prime},c^{\prime})^{t}\|=1
. We have

\displaystyle d([\ell x],[\ell x^{\prime}])=

\displaystyle\frac{\|(h(b,c)^{t}+an)\wedge(h(b^{\prime},c^{\prime})^{t}+a^{\prime}n)\|}{\|h(b,c)^{t}+an\|\|h(b^{\prime},c^{\prime})^{t}+a^{\prime}n\|}

\displaystyle\leqslant

\displaystyle\left(\frac{C_{2}}{\|h\|}\right)^{2}\|(h(b,c)^{t}+an)\wedge(h(b^{\prime},c^{\prime})^{t}+a^{\prime}n)\|,

(2.24)

where we use
Eq.

2.19
to obtain the inequality.

Note that

d(x,x^{\prime})=\|(a,b,c)^{t}\wedge(a^{\prime},b^{\prime},c^{\prime})^{t}\|\geqslant\max\{\|(b,c)^{t}\wedge(b^{\prime},c^{\prime})^{t}\|,\|a(b^{\prime},c^{\prime})^{t}-a^{\prime}(b,c)^{t}\|\}.

(2.25)

Then the numerator of
Eq.

2.24
is equal to

\displaystyle\|h(b,c)^{t}\wedge h(b^{\prime},c^{\prime})^{t}+n\wedge h(a(b^{\prime},c^{\prime})^{t}-a^{\prime}(b,c)^{t})\|

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\|(b,c)^{t}\wedge(b^{\prime},c^{\prime})^{t}+h^{-1}n\wedge(a(b^{\prime},c^{\prime})^{t}-a^{\prime}(b,c)^{t})\|

\displaystyle\leqslant

\displaystyle\|(b,c)^{t}\wedge(b^{\prime},c^{\prime})^{t}\|+\|h^{-1}n\|\cdot\|a(b^{\prime},c^{\prime})^{t}-a^{\prime}(b,c)^{t}\|\leqslant 2C_{1}d(x,x^{\prime}),

(2.26)

where the second line is due to
h\in\mathrm{SL}_{2}^{\pm}(\mathbb{R})
and the third line is due to
\|h^{-1}n\|\leqslant 1/d(\ell^{-1}E_{1},E_{1}^{\perp})\leqslant C_{1}
( see
Eq.

2.14
).
This verifies
Eq.

2.21
.
∎

Lemma 2.14
.

There exist
\epsilon_{1}>0
and
C_{3}>1
such that for
\ell=\begin{pmatrix}\det h&0\\
n&h\end{pmatrix}\,\,\,\text{and}\,\,\,\ell^{\prime}=\begin{pmatrix}\det h^{\prime}&0\\
n^{\prime}&h^{\prime}\end{pmatrix}
in
L
with
d(\ell,\ell^{\prime})\leqslant\epsilon_{1}
, we have

\displaystyle\|id-h^{-1}h^{\prime}\|\leqslant C_{3}d(\ell,\ell^{\prime}),\,\,\|h^{-1}n^{\prime}-h^{-1}n\|\leqslant C_{3}d(\ell,\ell^{\prime}),

\displaystyle\|h^{-1}n-h^{\prime-1}n^{\prime}\|\leqslant C_{3}d(\ell,\ell^{\prime})/d(\ell^{-1}{E_{1}},E_{1}^{\perp}),

(2.27)

\displaystyle\left|\log\|h^{\prime}\|-\log\|h\|\right|\leqslant C_{3}d(\ell,\ell^{\prime}),

\displaystyle d(\ell^{-1}{E_{1}},(\ell^{\prime})^{-1}{E_{1}})\leqslant C_{3}d(\ell,\ell^{\prime})/d(\ell^{-1}{E_{1}},E_{1}^{\perp}).

Moreover, if
\ell,\ell^{\prime}
satisfy
d(\ell^{\prime},\ell)\leqslant\min\{\epsilon_{1},1/C_{3}\}
, then

\displaystyle d((\ell^{\prime})^{-1}E_{1},E_{1}^{\perp})\geqslant d(\ell^{-1}E_{1},E_{1}^{\perp})/2.

Proof.

Since
\ell
is near
\ell^{\prime}
, we have
\det h=\det h^{\prime}
.
For any
\ell,\ell^{\prime}\in L
, as
d
is a left
L
-invariant metric on
L
, we have

d(\ell,\ell^{\prime})=d(id,\ell^{-1}\ell^{\prime})=d\left(id,\begin{pmatrix}1&0\\
h^{-1}n^{\prime}-h^{-1}n&h^{-1}h^{\prime}\end{pmatrix}\right).

The metric
d
and the distance induced by the norm are locally bi-Lipschitz. Hence, we obtain the two inequalities of the first line.

We also have

\displaystyle\|h^{-1}n-h^{\prime-1}n^{\prime}\|=\|h^{-1}n-h^{-1}n^{\prime}+h^{-1}n^{\prime}-h^{\prime-1}n^{\prime}\|

\displaystyle\ll

\displaystyle d(\ell,\ell^{\prime})+\|(h^{-1}h^{\prime}-id)h^{\prime-1}n^{\prime}\|\ll d(\ell,\ell^{\prime})(1+\|h^{\prime-1}n^{\prime}\|).

Using
Eq.

2.14
, we obtain
Eq.

2.27
.

For
\log\|h\|
, we may suppose
\|h\|\geqslant\|h^{\prime}\|
. Then we have

\log\|h\|-\log\|h^{\prime}\|=\log\left(1+\frac{\|h\|-\|h^{\prime}\|}{\|h^{\prime}\|}\right)\leqslant\frac{\|h\|-\|h^{\prime}\|}{\|h^{\prime}\|}\leqslant\frac{\|h-h^{\prime}\|}{\|h^{\prime}\|}\leqslant{\|h^{\prime-1}h-id\|}.

For
d(\ell^{-1}{E_{1}},\ell^{\prime-1}{E_{1}})
, by definition and
Eq.

2.27
, we have

\displaystyle d(\ell^{-1}{E_{1}},\ell^{\prime-1}{E_{1}})=\frac{\|(1,-h^{-1}n)^{t}\wedge(1,-h^{\prime-1}n^{\prime})^{t}\|}{\|(1,-h^{-1}n)^{t}\|\|(1,-h^{\prime-1}n^{\prime})^{t}\|}

The numerator is bounded above by

\displaystyle\|h^{-1}n-h^{\prime-1}n^{\prime}\|+\|h^{-1}n\wedge h^{\prime-1}n^{\prime}\|

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\|h^{-1}n-h^{\prime-1}n^{\prime}\|+\|h^{-1}n\wedge(h^{-1}n-h^{\prime-1}n^{\prime})\|\leqslant(1+\|h^{-1}n\|)\|h^{-1}n-h^{\prime-1}n^{\prime}\|,

which yields the estimate of
d(\ell^{-1}E_{1},\ell^{\prime-1}E_{1})
.

For
d((\ell^{\prime})^{-1}E_{1},E_{1}^{\perp})
,
using
Eq.

2.14
and
Eq.

2.27
, we have

\displaystyle 1/d((\ell^{\prime})^{-1}E_{1},E_{1}^{\perp})

\displaystyle=\|(1,-h^{\prime-1}n^{\prime})\|\leqslant\|(1,-h^{-1}n)\|+\|h^{\prime-1}n^{\prime}-h^{-1}n\|

\displaystyle\leqslant 1/d(\ell^{-1}E_{1},E_{1}^{\perp})+C_{3}d(\ell,\ell^{\prime})/d(\ell^{-1}E_{1},E_{1}^{\perp})\leqslant 2/d(\ell^{-1}E_{1},E_{1}^{\perp}).

∎

We need a lemma about the continuity of Cartan decomposition.
For any
\epsilon>0
, let
\mathcal{O}_{\epsilon}
be the neighborhood of the identity in
\mathrm{SL}_{2}(\mathbb{R})
with radius
\epsilon
. Recall that
A
is the diagonal subgroup and
A^{+}=\{\mathrm{diag}(a_{1},a_{1}^{-1}):a_{1}\geqslant 1\}
. Let
\widetilde{A}^{\delta}=\{\mathrm{diag}(a_{1},a_{1}^{-1}):a_{1}>1+\delta\}
.

Lemma 2.15

(Effective Cartan decomposition,
[
GOS10
]
, Theorem 1.6)
.

Given
\delta>0
, there exist
l_{0},\epsilon_{2}>0
such that the following holds. For any
\epsilon<\epsilon_{2}
and any
g=k_{1}ak_{2}\in K\widetilde{A}^{\delta}K
, we have

\mathcal{O}_{\epsilon}g\mathcal{O}_{\epsilon}\subset(\mathcal{O}_{l_{0}\epsilon}\cap K)k_{1}(\mathcal{O}_{l_{0}\epsilon}\cap A^{+})ak_{2}(\mathcal{O}_{l_{0}\epsilon}\cap K).

Since we will mostly deal with
h\in\mathrm{SL}_{2}^{\pm}(\mathbb{R})
with
\|h\|
large, we fix
\delta=\log 2
and the corresponding constants
l_{0},\epsilon_{2}
in the last lemma.

Lemma 2.16
.

There exists
C_{4}>1
such that the following holds. Let
\ell=\begin{pmatrix}\det h&0\\
n&h\end{pmatrix}\in L
such that
\|h\|>2
. The linear form
f_{\ell}
is continuous with respect to
\ell
: if
d(\ell,\ell^{\prime})\leqslant\min\{\epsilon_{1},\epsilon_{2}/C_{3}\}
, then

\|f_{\ell}-f_{\ell^{\prime}}\|\leqslant C_{4}d(\ell,\ell^{\prime})/d(\ell^{-1}{E_{1}},E_{1}^{\perp}).

Proof.

Write
\ell^{\prime}=\begin{pmatrix}\det h^{\prime}&0\\
n^{\prime}&h^{\prime}\end{pmatrix}\in L
. When
d(\ell,\ell^{\prime})\leqslant\epsilon_{1}
,
2.14
gives that

\|\mathrm{id}-h^{-1}h^{\prime}\|<C_{3}d(\ell,\ell^{\prime})\leqslant\epsilon_{2}.

Let
h=\tilde{k}_{h}a_{h}k_{h}
and
h^{\prime}=\tilde{k}_{h^{\prime}}a_{h^{\prime}}k_{h^{\prime}}
be the Cartan decomposition of
h
and
h^{\prime}
respectively. Then

2.15
gives

d(k_{h},k_{h^{\prime}})\leqslant l_{0}d(h,h^{\prime}).

Combining these two inequalities, we obtain

\|f_{h}-f_{h^{\prime}}\|=\|e_{1}^{*}\circ k_{h}-e_{1}^{*}\circ k_{h^{\prime}}\|\leqslant l_{0}d(h,h^{\prime})\ll d(\ell,\ell^{\prime}).

(2.28)

For any
x=\mathbb{R}(a,b,c)^{t}
with
\|(a,b,c)^{t}\|=1
, by

2.12
, we obtain

\displaystyle|f_{\ell}((a,b,c)^{t})-f_{\ell^{\prime}}((a,b,c)^{t})|=|f_{h}((b,c)^{t}+ah^{-1}n)-f_{h^{\prime}}((b,c)^{t}+a(h^{\prime})^{-1}n^{\prime})|

\displaystyle\leqslant

\displaystyle\|f_{h}-f_{h^{\prime}}\|\cdot\|(b,c)^{t}+ah^{-1}n\|+|f_{h^{\prime}}(ah^{-1}n-a(h^{\prime})^{-1}n^{\prime})|.

To get the stated inequality, we use
Eq.

2.28
and
Eq.

2.15
to estimate the first term, and
Eq.

2.27
for the second term.
∎

We fix a large constant
C_{L}>0
such that

C_{L}>\max\{1/\epsilon_{1},C_{3},C_{3}/\epsilon_{2},4C_{4}\}

(2.29)

where the constants
\epsilon_{1},\epsilon_{2},C_{3}
and
C_{4}
are given as in
2.14
,
2.15
and
2.16
.

Lemma 2.17
.

Fix any
C_{1},C_{2}>2
. Let
\ell=\begin{pmatrix}\det h&0\\
n&h\end{pmatrix}\in L
be such that
\|h\|>2
.
If
d(\ell^{-1}{E_{1}},{E_{1}}^{\perp})>1/C_{1}
, then for any
\ell^{\prime}=\begin{pmatrix}\det h^{\prime}&0\\
n^{\prime}&h^{\prime}\end{pmatrix}\in B(\ell,1/C_{L}C_{1}C_{2})\subset L
, we have

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}\|h\|\leqslant\|h^{\prime}\|

\displaystyle\leqslant 2\|h\|,

\displaystyle b(f_{\ell},1/C_{2})

\displaystyle\subset b(f_{\ell^{\prime}},1/2C_{2}).

\displaystyle d((\ell^{\prime})^{-1}E_{1},E_{1}^{\perp})

\displaystyle>1/(2C_{1})

Proof.

The first line follows from
2.14
.

For the second line, due to
2.16
and
\|f_{\ell}\|\geqslant 1
(
Eq.

2.16
), we have

\left|\|f_{\ell}\|-\|f_{\ell^{\prime}}\|\right|\leqslant 1/4C_{2}\leqslant\|f_{\ell}\|/4C_{2}.

Hence
\|f_{\ell}\|\geqslant\|f_{\ell^{\prime}}\|/(1+1/4C_{2})
. Again, due to
2.16
, for any
\mathbb{R}v\in b(f_{\ell},1/C_{2})
with
\|v\|=1
, we have

|f_{\ell^{\prime}}(v)|\geqslant|f_{\ell}(v)|-1/4C_{2}\geqslant\|f_{\ell}\|/C_{2}-1/4C_{2}\geqslant\|f_{\ell^{\prime}}\|/(C_{2}+1/4)-\|f_{\ell^{\prime}}\|/4C_{2}\geqslant\|f_{\ell^{\prime}}\|/2C_{2}.

The third line follows from
2.14
.
∎

For sets
E\subset L
and
F\subset\mathbb{P}(\mathbb{R}^{3})
, let
[E.F]=\{[\ell(x)],\ \ell\in E,\ x\in F\}
be a subset in
\mathbb{P}(\mathbb{R}^{2})
.

Lemma 2.18
.

Fix any
C_{1},C_{2}>2
.
Let
E
be a subset in
L
such that any
\ell=\begin{pmatrix}\det h&0\\
n&h\end{pmatrix}\in E
satifies
\|h\|>2
and
\diam E<1/C_{L}C_{1}C_{2}
. Suppose there exists
\ell\in E
such that
d(\ell^{-1}{E_{1}},E_{1}^{\perp})>1/C_{1}
. Let
F
be a subset in
[\ell]
-attracting region
b(f_{\ell},1/C_{2})\subset\mathbb{P}(\mathbb{R}^{3})
. Then

\diam([E.F])\leqslant 16C_{L}C_{1}C_{2}^{2}(\diam E+\diam F)\|h\|^{-2}.

Proof.

For any
\ell\in E
and
x,x^{\prime}\in F
,
2.13
gives
d([\ell x],[\ell x^{\prime}])\leqslant 2C_{1}C_{2}^{2}d(x,x^{\prime})\|h\|^{-2}
.

Let
\ell,\ell^{\prime}\in E
and
x\in F
. Write
\ell^{\prime}=\begin{pmatrix}\det h^{\prime}&0\\
n^{\prime}&h^{\prime}\end{pmatrix}
and
x=\mathbb{R}(a,b,c)^{t}
with
\|(a,b,c)^{t}\|=1
. We have

d([\ell x],[\ell^{\prime}x])=\frac{\|(h(b,c)^{t}+an)\wedge(h^{\prime}(b,c)^{t}+an^{\prime})\|}{\|h(b,c)^{t}+an\|\|h^{\prime}(b,c)^{t}+an^{\prime}\|}.

(2.30)

Due to
x\in b(f_{\ell},1/C_{2})
, we obtain from
2.13
that

\|h(b,c)^{t}+an\|\geqslant\|h\|/C_{2}.

2.17
gives that
x\in b(f_{\ell^{\prime}},1/2C_{2})
and

\|h^{\prime}(b,c)^{t}+an^{\prime}\|\geqslant\|h^{\prime}\|/2C_{2}\geqslant\|h\|/4C_{2}.

For the numerator of
Eq.

2.30
, we have

\displaystyle\|(h(b,c)^{t}+an)\wedge(h^{\prime}(b,c)^{t}+an^{\prime})\|

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\|(b,c)^{t}\wedge h^{-1}h^{\prime}(b,c)^{t}+a^{2}h^{-1}n\wedge h^{-1}n^{\prime}+ah^{-1}n\wedge h^{-1}h^{\prime}(b,c)^{t}-ah^{-1}n^{\prime}\wedge(b,c)^{t}\|

\displaystyle\leqslant

\displaystyle\|(b,c)^{t}\wedge h^{-1}h^{\prime}(b,c)^{t}\|+\|a^{2}h^{-1}n\wedge h^{-1}n^{\prime}\|+\|ah^{-1}n\wedge h^{-1}h^{\prime}(b,c)^{t}-ah^{-1}n^{\prime}\wedge(b,c)^{t}\|.

We use
2.14
to estimate the first two terms and get an upper bound
2C_{L}d(\ell,\ell^{\prime})
. For the last term, we also use
2.14

\displaystyle\|ah^{-1}n\wedge h^{-1}h^{\prime}(b,c)^{t}-ah^{-1}n\wedge(b,c)^{t}\|

\displaystyle\leqslant\|h^{-1}n\wedge(h^{-1}h^{\prime}-id)(b,c)^{t}+(h^{-1}n-h^{-1}n^{\prime})\wedge(b,c)^{t}\|

\displaystyle\leqslant C_{1}C_{L}d(\ell^{\prime},\ell)+C_{L}d(\ell^{\prime},\ell).

Collecting all the terms, we obtain the lemma.
∎

Let
\ell=\begin{pmatrix}\det h&0\\
n&h\end{pmatrix}\in L
. We are interested in the map

[\ell(\cdot)]=\pi_{E_{1}^{\perp}}\ell(\cdot)=h\circ\pi(\ell^{-1}E_{1},E_{1}^{\perp},(\ell^{-1}E_{1})^{\perp})\circ\pi_{(\ell^{-1}E_{1})^{\perp}}(\cdot).

(2.31)

Now we are ready to explain how to linearize the map
[\ell(\cdot)]
with
\ell\in L
. We will approximate it by a composition of translation and scaling map.

Definition 2.19
.

For any
t\in\mathbb{R}
, we define the scaling map
S_{t}:\mathbb{R}\to\mathbb{R}
by
S_{t}x=q^{t}x
.

We have the identifications
\mathbb{P}(V^{\perp})\cong\mathbb{P}(\mathbb{R}^{2})
for
V\in\mathbb{P}(\mathbb{R}^{3})
in
A.1
and
\iota:\mathbb{P}(\mathbb{R}^{2})\cong\mathbb{R}/\mathbb{Z}
(
Section

2.1
).
In the followings, for any
\star,\bullet\in\mathbb{P}(V^{\perp})
,
d(\star,\bullet)
means to view
\star,\bullet
as elements in
\mathbb{P}(\mathbb{R}^{2})
and
d
is the distance given as in
2.1
;
\star-\bullet
means to view
\star,\bullet
as elements in
\mathbb{R}/\mathbb{Z}
and
|\star-\bullet|
is to compute their distance using the rotational invariant distance on
\mathbb{R}/\mathbb{Z}
. We have
d(\star,\bullet)=\sin(\pi|\star-\bullet|)
.

Lemma 2.20
.

Fix any
C_{1},C_{2}>20
.
Let
\ell_{0}=\begin{pmatrix}\det h_{0}&0\\
n_{0}&h_{0}\end{pmatrix}\in L
be such that
\|h_{0}\|>2
and
d(\ell_{0}^{-1}E_{1},E_{1}^{\perp})>1/C_{1}
. Then for any
\ell\in B(\ell_{0},1/C_{L}C_{1}^{2}C_{2}^{2})\subset L
, and
x,x_{0}
in the
[\ell_{0}]
-attracting region
b(f_{\ell_{0}},1/C_{2})
with
d(x,x_{0})\leqslant(1/C_{1}C_{2})^{10}
, there exists a scaling map
S_{-t(\ell_{0},x_{0})}:\mathbb{R}\to\mathbb{R}
with

\big|t(\ell_{0},x_{0})-2\log\|h_{0}\|\big|\leqslant 4\log(C_{1}C_{2})

(2.32)

such that

\displaystyle\big|[\ell(x)]-[\ell(x_{0})]-S_{-t(\ell_{0},x_{0})}\left(\pi_{(\ell_{0}^{-1}{E_{1}})^{\perp}}x-\pi_{(\ell_{0}^{-1}{E_{1}})^{\perp}}x_{0}\right)\big|

(2.33)

\displaystyle\leqslant

\displaystyle C_{L}(C_{2}C_{1})^{9}(d(\ell,\ell_{0})+d(x,x_{0}))d(x,x_{0})/\|h_{0}\|^{2}.

Remark 2.21
.

Let us explain the notions in
Eq.

2.33
.
Let
\iota^{\prime}
be the identification between
\mathbb{R}/\mathbb{Z}
and
[-\frac{1}{2},\frac{1}{2})
. Let
\pi_{\mathbb{R}/\mathbb{Z}}:\mathbb{R}\to\mathbb{R}/\mathbb{Z}
be the quotient map. It will be clear from the proof that
\left|\pi_{(\ell_{0}^{-1}{E_{1}})^{\perp}}x-\pi_{(\ell_{0}^{-1}{E_{1}})^{\perp}}x_{0}\right|<\frac{1}{2}
.
By abusing the notation, we denote
\pi_{\mathbb{R}/\mathbb{Z}}\circ S_{-t(\ell_{0},x_{0})}\circ\iota^{\prime}\left(\pi_{(\ell_{0}^{-1}E_{1})^{\perp}}x-\pi_{(\ell_{0}^{-1}E_{1})^{\perp}}x_{0}\right)

by
S_{-t(\ell_{0},x_{0})}\left(\pi_{(\ell_{0}^{-1}E_{1})^{\perp}}x-\pi_{(\ell_{0}^{-1}E_{1})^{\perp}}x_{0}\right)
.
Eq.

2.33
is to subtract elements in
\mathbb{R}/\mathbb{Z}
.

Proof.

Step 1:
Approximate
[\ell_{0}(x)]-[\ell_{0}(x_{0})]
.
We have

\displaystyle[\ell_{0}(x)]-[\ell_{0}(x_{0})]=

\displaystyle h_{0}\circ\pi(\ell_{0}^{-1}E_{1},E_{1}^{\perp},(\ell_{0}^{-1}E_{1})^{\perp})\circ\pi_{(\ell_{0}^{-1}{E_{1}})^{\perp}}x

\displaystyle-h_{0}\circ\pi(\ell_{0}^{-1}E_{1},E_{1}^{\perp},(\ell_{0}^{-1}E_{1})^{\perp})\circ\pi_{(\ell_{0}^{-1}{E_{1}})^{\perp}}x_{0}.

We will use a basic inequality in analysis to approximate the difference gradually. For a
C^{2}
-map
g
on
\mathbb{R}
and two points
z<y
in
\mathbb{R}
, we have

|gz-gy-g^{\prime}y(z-y)|\leqslant|z-y|^{2}\sup\{|g^{\prime\prime}w|:z\leqslant w\leqslant y\}.

(2.34)

We want to apply
Eq.

2.34
to

\displaystyle g:=

\displaystyle h_{0},

\displaystyle z:=

\displaystyle\pi(\ell_{0}^{-1}E_{1},E_{1}^{\perp},(\ell_{0}^{-1}E_{1})^{\perp})\circ\pi_{(\ell_{0}^{-1}{E_{1}})^{\perp}}(x)=\pi_{\ell_{0}^{-1}E_{1},E_{1}^{\perp}}(x),

\displaystyle y:=

\displaystyle\pi(\ell_{0}^{-1}E_{1},E_{1}^{\perp},(\ell_{0}^{-1}E_{1})^{\perp})\circ\pi_{(\ell_{0}^{-1}{E_{1}})^{\perp}}(x_{0})=\pi_{\ell_{0}^{-1}E_{1},E_{1}^{\perp}}(x_{0}).

Due to
x,x_{0}\in b(f_{\ell_{0}},1/C_{2})
,
Eq.

2.20
in
2.13
implies that

y,z\in b(h_{0}^{-},1/C_{1}C_{2}).

(2.35)

Due to
2.8
, we know

|\log h_{0}^{\prime}(y)+2\log\|h_{0}\||\leqslant 2\log(C_{1}C_{2}).

(2.36)

Using
2.7
and
Eq.

2.21
, we obtain

\begin{split}d(z,y)&=\frac{d(h_{0}^{-1}[\ell_{0}(x_{0})],h_{0}^{-1}[\ell_{0}(x)])}{d([\ell_{0}(x_{0})],[\ell_{0}(x)])}\frac{d([\ell_{0}(x_{0})],[\ell_{0}(x)])}{d(x_{0},x)}d(x,x_{0})\\
&\leqslant\|h_{0}\|^{2}\frac{2C_{1}C_{2}^{2}}{\|h_{0}\|^{2}}d(x,x_{0})=2C_{1}C_{2}^{2}d(x,x_{0}).\end{split}

(2.37)

We have
[z,y]\subset b(h_{0}^{-},1/C_{1}C_{2})
due to
Eq.

2.35
,
Eq.

2.37
and the hypothesis that
d(x,x_{0})\leqslant 1/(C_{1}C_{2})^{10}
.
Hence, by locally identifying
\mathbb{P}(\ell_{0}^{-1}E_{1})
with
\mathbb{R}
, we can use
Eq.

2.34
and the estimate for
h_{0}^{\prime\prime}
(
2.8
) to obtain

\displaystyle\big|[\ell_{0}(x)]-[\ell_{0}(x_{0})]-h_{0}^{\prime}(y)(z-y)\big|\leqslant|z-y|^{2}\sup\{|h_{0}^{\prime\prime}w|:z\leqslant w\leqslant y\}

\displaystyle\leqslant

\displaystyle|z-y|^{2}10(C_{1}C_{2})^{3}/\|h_{0}\|^{2}\leqslant 40C_{1}^{5}C_{2}^{7}d(x,x_{0})^{2}/\|h_{0}\|^{2}.

(2.38)

Then we let

\displaystyle\tilde{g}:=

\displaystyle\pi(\ell_{0}^{-1}E_{1},E_{1}^{\perp},(\ell_{0}^{-1}E_{1})^{\perp}),

\displaystyle z^{\prime}:=

\displaystyle\pi_{(\ell_{0}^{-1}{E_{1}})^{\perp}}x,

\displaystyle y^{\prime}:=

\displaystyle\pi_{(\ell_{0}^{-1}{E_{1}})^{\perp}}x_{0}.

We use
Eq.

2.34
to approximate
\tilde{g}(z^{\prime})-\tilde{g}(y^{\prime})
.
Due to
B.1
and

2.7
, we have for any
w\in\mathbb{P}(E_{1}^{\perp})
,

|\tilde{g}^{\prime\prime}(w)|\leqslant 4/d(\ell_{0}^{-1}E_{1},E_{1}^{\perp})\leqslant 4C_{1},\ 1/C_{1}\leqslant|\tilde{g}^{\prime}(w)|\leqslant C_{1}.

(2.39)

We apply
Eq.

2.18
to
x,x_{0}
and then
B.2
(2) yields

d(z^{\prime},y^{\prime})=d(\pi_{(\ell_{0}^{-1}{E_{1}})^{\perp}}x,\pi_{(\ell_{0}^{-1}{E_{1}})^{\perp}}x_{0})\leqslant C_{1}C_{2}d(x,x_{0}).

(2.40)

Therefore, we use
Eq.

2.34
to obtain

|z-y-\tilde{g}^{\prime}(y^{\prime})(z^{\prime}-y^{\prime})|\leqslant|z^{\prime}-y^{\prime}|^{2}(4C_{1})\leqslant 4C_{1}^{3}C_{2}^{2}d(x,x_{0})^{2}.

(2.41)

Combining with
Section

2.4
and
Eq.

2.36
, we obtain

|[\ell_{0}(x)]-[\ell_{0}(x_{0})]-S_{-t(\ell_{0},x_{0})}(\pi_{(\ell_{0}^{-1}{E_{1}})^{\perp}}x-\pi_{(\ell_{0}^{-1}{E_{1}})^{\perp}}x_{0})|\leqslant 44(C_{1}C_{2})^{7}d(x,x_{0})^{2}/\|h\|^{2}.

(2.42)

Here
S_{-t(\ell_{0},x_{0})}=h_{0}^{\prime}(y)\tilde{g}^{\prime}(y^{\prime})
is the contracting action with contracting rate of
h_{0}\circ\tilde{g}
at
y_{0}:=\pi_{(\ell_{0}^{-1}{E_{1}})^{\perp}}x_{0}
, and we obtain the estimate of
t(\ell_{0},x_{0})
(
Eq.

2.32
) using
Eq.

2.36
and (
2.39
).

Before proceeding to the general case, we would like to point out
a corollary of Step 1:
the sign of
[\ell_{0}(x)]-[\ell_{0}(x_{0})]
is the same with the sign of
z^{\prime}-y^{\prime}
as elements in
[-\frac{1}{2},\frac{1}{2})
. The reason is as follows. Note that
\tilde{g}
scales
\mathbb{P}(\mathbb{R}^{2})
by
1
with distortion
C_{1}
(
2.4
). As
d(z^{\prime},y^{\prime})
is small (
Eq.

2.40
), we can deduce that the sign of
z-y
is the same as that of
z^{\prime}-y^{\prime}
. As
[\ell_{0}(x)]=h_{0}(z)
,
[\ell_{0}(x_{0})]=h_{0}(y)
, and
h_{0}
contracts the minor arc
[z,y]
, we obtain the corollary.

Step 2:
We need to distinguish two cases.

Case 1: Suppose that
\ell^{-1}{E_{1}}
and
\ell_{0}^{-1}{E_{1}}
are in the same side of the great circle passing through
x,x_{0}
,
5
5

5

Take representatives
u,u_{0}\in\mathbb{S}^{2}
of
\ell^{-1}{E_{1}}
and
\ell_{0}^{-1}{E_{1}}
with
d_{R}(u_{0},u)<\pi/2
. The two points
\ell^{-1}{E_{1}}
and
\ell_{0}^{-1}{E_{1}}
are in the same side of a great circle actually means
u,u_{0}
are in the same side. In the figure, we write
\ell^{-1}{E_{1}},\ell_{0}^{-1}E_{1}
instead of
u,u_{0}
.
see
Fig.

5
. We write
\ell_{0}=\begin{pmatrix}\det h_{0}&0\\
n_{0}&h_{0}\end{pmatrix}
,
\ell=\begin{pmatrix}\det h&0\\
n&h\end{pmatrix}
,
x_{0}=\mathbb{R}(a_{0},b_{0},c_{0})^{t}
,
x=\mathbb{R}(a,b,c)^{t}
. We have

\displaystyle d([\ell(x)],[\ell(x_{0})])-d([\ell_{0}(x)],[\ell_{0}(x_{0})])=

\displaystyle\frac{\|((b,c)^{t}\wedge(b_{0},c_{0})^{t}+h^{-1}n\wedge(a(b_{0},c_{0})^{t}-a_{0}(b,c)^{t})\|}{\|h(b,c)^{t}+an\|\|h(b_{0},c_{0})^{t}+a_{0}n\|}

\displaystyle-\frac{\|((b,c)^{t}\wedge(b_{0},c_{0})^{t}+h_{0}^{-1}n_{0}\wedge(a(b_{0},c_{0})^{t}-a_{0}(b,c)^{t})\|}{\|h_{0}(b,c)^{t}+an_{0}\|\|h_{0}(b_{0},c_{0})^{t}+a_{0}n_{0}\|}.

The idea is to compare the difference term by term using the formula

\displaystyle\frac{A}{BC}-\frac{A_{0}}{B_{0}C_{0}}=\frac{AB_{0}C_{0}-A_{0}BC}{BCB_{0}C_{0}}=\frac{(A-A_{0})B_{0}C_{0}+A_{0}(B_{0}-B)C_{0}+A_{0}B(C_{0}-C)}{BCB_{0}C_{0}},

with

\displaystyle A=\|((b,c)^{t}\wedge(b_{0},c_{0})^{t}+h^{-1}n\wedge(a(b_{0},c_{0})^{t}-a_{0}(b,c)^{t})\|,

\displaystyle B=\|h(b,c)^{t}+an\|,\,\,\,C=\|h(b_{0},c_{0})^{t}+a_{0}n\|

and
A_{0},B_{0},C_{0}
the corresponding one with
h,n
replaced by
h_{0},n_{0}
. From
Eq.

2.14
, we obtain

A_{0}\leqslant(1+\|h_{0}^{-1}n_{0}\|)d(x,x_{0})\leqslant 2d(x,x_{0})/d(\ell_{0}^{-1}E_{1},E_{1}^{\perp})\leqslant 2C_{1}d(x,x_{0}).

Similarly, we have
A_{0}\leqslant 4C_{1}d(x,x_{0})
.
From
2.17
, we can apply
2.13
to obtain all possible pairs between
\ell,\ell_{0}
and
x,x_{0}
.
Due to
Eq.

2.19
, we obtain

B,B_{0},C,C_{0}\geqslant\frac{1}{4C_{2}}\|h_{0}\|.

Then due to
\|h(b,c)^{t}+an\|\leqslant\|h\|(1+\|h^{-1}n\|)
, using
Eq.

2.14
we obtain

B,B_{0},C,C_{0}\leqslant 4C_{1}\|h_{0}\|.

For the differences, by triangle inequality,
Eq.

2.27
and
Eq.

2.25
, we have

|A-A_{0}|\leqslant\|(h^{-1}n-h_{0}^{-1}n_{0})\wedge(a(b_{0},c_{0})^{t}-a_{0}(b,c)^{t})\|\leqslant\frac{C_{L}d(\ell,\ell_{0})d(x,x_{0})}{d(\ell_{0}^{-1}E_{1},E_{1}^{\perp})}\leqslant C_{L}C_{1}d(\ell,\ell_{0})d(x,x_{0}).

Similarly, using triangle inequality and
2.14

|B-B_{0}|,|C-C_{0}|\leqslant\|h_{0}\|(\|id-h_{0}^{-1}h\|+\|h^{-1}_{0}n-h^{-1}_{0}n_{0}\|)\leqslant 2C_{L}\|h_{0}\|d(\ell,\ell_{0}).

In the end, we obtain from these computations that

|d([\ell(x)],[\ell(x_{0})])-d([\ell_{0}(x)],[\ell_{0}(x_{0})])|\leqslant 2^{16}C_{L}(C_{1}C_{2})^{4}d(x,x_{0})d(\ell,\ell_{0})/\|h_{0}\|^{2}.

(2.43)

By the position of
\ell^{-1}{E_{1}},\ell_{0}^{-1}{E_{1}},x,x_{0}
on
\mathbb{P}(\mathbb{R}^{3})
(see
Fig.

5
) and
Eq.

2.40
,
\pi_{\ell^{-1}E_{1}^{\perp}}x-\pi_{\ell^{-1}E_{1}^{\perp}}x_{0}
and
\pi_{\ell_{0}^{-1}E_{1}^{\perp}}x-\pi_{\ell_{0}^{-1}E_{1}^{\perp}}x_{0}
have the same sign as elements in
[-1/2,1/2)
.
The corollary of Step 1 holds for both
[\ell(x)]-[\ell(x_{0})]
and
[\ell_{0}(x)]-[\ell_{0}(x_{0})]
. Then they have the same sign. We have the relation

\left|([\ell(x)]-[\ell(x_{0})])-([\ell_{0}(x)]-[\ell_{0}(x_{0})])\right|\leqslant\big||[\ell(x)]-[\ell(x_{0})]|-|[\ell_{0}(x)]-[\ell_{0}(x_{0})]|\big|.

(2.44)

Due to
d([\ell(x)],[\ell(x_{0})])\leqslant 1/2
and
|a-b|\leqslant 2|\sin(a)-\sin(b)|
for
a,b\in[0,\pi/3]
, combined with
Eq.

2.43
, we obtain

|([\ell(x)]-[\ell(x_{0})])-([\ell_{0}(x)]-[\ell_{0}(x_{0})])|\leqslant 2^{17}C_{L}(C_{1}C_{2})^{4}d(x,x_{0})d(\ell,\ell_{0})/\|h_{0}\|^{2}.

(2.45)

Combined with
Eq.

2.42
for both
\ell
and
\ell_{0}
, we obtain the lemma due to
C_{1},C_{2}
large.

Figure 5:

Case 2: Suppose
\ell^{-1}{E_{1}}
and
\ell_{0}^{-1}{E_{1}}
are in different sides of the great circle passing through
x,x_{0}
.
By spherical trigonometry of the triangle
(x,\ell^{-1}E_{1},\ell_{0}^{-1}E_{1})
, we have

\sin\angle_{x}(\ell^{-1}{E_{1}},\ell_{0}^{-1}{E_{1}})=\frac{\sin\angle_{\ell^{-1}{E_{1}}}(x_{,}\ell_{0}^{-1}{E_{1}})}{d(x,\ell_{0}^{-1}{E_{1}})}d(\ell^{-1}{E_{1}},\ell_{0}^{-1}{E_{1}})\leqslant\frac{d(\ell^{-1}{E_{1}},\ell_{0}^{-1}{E_{1}})}{d(x,\ell_{0}^{-1}{E_{1}})}.

Due to
2.14
and

Eq.

2.18
, we have

\sin\angle_{x}(\ell^{-1}{E_{1}},\ell_{0}^{-1}{E_{1}})\leqslant\frac{C_{L}d(\ell,\ell_{0})/d(\ell_{0}^{-1}{E_{1}},{E_{1}}^{\perp})}{d(\ell_{0}^{-1}{E_{1}},{E_{1}}^{\perp})/C_{2}}\leqslant C_{L}C_{1}^{2}C_{2}d(\ell,\ell_{0})\leqslant 1/C_{2}.

(2.46)

Again by spherical trigonometry of the triangle
(x,x_{0},\ell^{-1}E_{1})
, we have

d(\pi_{(\ell^{-1}{E_{1}})^{\perp}}x,\pi_{(\ell^{-1}{E_{1}})^{\perp}}x_{0})=\sin\angle_{\ell^{-1}E_{1}}(x,x_{0})=d(x,x_{0})\frac{\sin\angle_{x}(\ell^{-1}{E_{1}},x_{0})}{d(x_{0},\ell^{-1}{E_{1}})}.

Due to the hypothesis of case 2,
we obtain
\sin\angle_{x}(\ell^{-1}{E_{1}},x_{0})\leqslant\sin\angle_{x}(\ell^{-1}{E_{1}},\ell_{0}^{-1}{E_{1}})
(the angle
\angle_{x}(\ell^{-1}{E_{1}},\ell_{0}^{-1}{E_{1}})
must be acute due to
d(x,\ell^{-1}E_{1})\geqslant d(\ell^{-1}E_{1},\ell_{0}^{-1}E_{1})
). Combined with
Eq.

2.46
and
Eq.

2.18
, we obtain

d(\pi_{(\ell^{-1}{E_{1}})^{\perp}}x,\pi_{(\ell^{-1}{E_{1}})^{\perp}}x_{0})\leqslant d(x,x_{0})\frac{\sin\angle_{x}(\ell^{-1}{E_{1}},\ell_{0}^{-1}E_{1})}{d(x_{0},\ell^{-1}{E_{1}})}\leqslant C_{L}C_{1}^{3}C_{2}^{2}d(\ell,\ell_{0})d(x,x_{0}).

The same inequality also holds with
\ell
replaced by
\ell_{0}
.
Combining with
Eq.

2.42
for
\ell
and the triangle inequality, using the fact that
\theta\leqslant 2\sin\theta
for any
\theta\in[0,\pi/2]
, we obtain

\displaystyle\left|[\ell(x)]-[\ell(x_{0})]-S_{-t(\ell_{0},x_{0})}\left(\pi_{(\ell_{0}^{-1}{E_{1}})^{\perp}}x-\pi_{(\ell_{0}^{-1}{E_{1}})^{\perp}}x_{0}\right)\right|

\displaystyle\leqslant

\displaystyle\left|[\ell(x)]-[\ell(x_{0})]-S_{-t(\ell,x_{0})}\left(\pi_{(\ell^{-1}{E_{1}})^{\perp}}x-\pi_{(\ell^{-1}{E_{1}})^{\perp}}x_{0}\right)\right|+2q^{-t(\ell,x_{0})}d(\pi_{(\ell^{-1}{E_{1}})^{\perp}}x,\pi_{(\ell^{-1}{E_{1}})^{\perp}}x_{0})

\displaystyle+

\displaystyle 2q^{-t(\ell_{0},x_{0})}d(\pi_{(\ell_{0}^{-1}{E_{1}})^{\perp}}x,\pi_{(\ell_{0}^{-1}{E_{1}})^{\perp}}x_{0})

\displaystyle\leqslant

\displaystyle 44(C_{1}C_{2})^{7}d(x,x_{0})^{2}/\|h\|^{2}+(q^{-t(\ell,x_{0})}+q^{-t(\ell_{0},x_{0})})C_{L}C_{1}^{3}C_{2}^{2}d(\ell,\ell_{0})d(x,x_{0})

\displaystyle\leqslant

\displaystyle C_{L}(C_{1}C_{2})^{7}(2d(\ell,\ell_{0})+88d(x,x_{0}))d(x,x_{0})/\|h_{0}\|^{2}.\qed

2.5
q
-Adic partitions

The
q
-adic level-
n
partition for
n\in\mathbb{N}
of
[0,1)
is defined by

\mathcal{Q}_{n}=\left\{[\frac{k}{q^{n}},\frac{k+1}{q^{n}}):0\leqslant k<q^{n},\ k\in\mathbb{Z}\right\}.

Using the canonical identification between
[0,1)
and the circle
\mathbb{P}(\mathbb{R}^{2})
, we can regard
\mathcal{Q}_{n}
as a partition of
\mathbb{P}(\mathbb{R}^{2})
. We write
\mathcal{Q}_{t}=\mathcal{Q}_{[t]}
when
t\in\mathbb{R}^{+}
is not an integer.

We also need similar partitions of the group
L
defined in
Section

2.3
. By Theorem 2.1 of
[
KRS12
]
, for
q
large enough, there exists a
collection of Borel sets
\{Q_{n,i}\subset L:n\in\mathbb{N},i\in\mathbb{N}\}
, having the following properties:

1.

L=\cup_{i\in\mathbb{N}}Q_{n,i}
for every
n\in\mathbb{N}
;

2.

Q_{n,i}\cap Q_{m,j}=\emptyset
or
Q_{n,i}\subset Q_{m,j}
for
n,m\in\mathbb{N}
with
n\geqslant m
and any
i,j\in\mathbb{N}
;

3.

There exists a constant
C_{p}>1
(independent of large
q
) such that for every
n\in\mathbb{N}
and
i\in\mathbb{N}
there
exists
\ell\in Q_{n,i}
with

B(\ell,C_{p}^{-1}q^{-n})\subset Q_{n,i}\subset B(\ell,q^{-n}),

(2.47)

where
B(\ell,q^{-n})
is measured using the left
L
-invariant metric on
L
.

For each
n\in\mathbb{N}
, denote by
\mathcal{Q}_{n}^{L}
the partition
\{Q_{n,i}:i\in\mathbb{N}\}
of
L
. We will usually omit the superscript when there is no ambiguity about which space to partition. Fix a left-invariant Haar measure on
L
. In view of (
2.47
), there exists a constant
C>1
independent of
n
such that

1/C\leqslant q^{5n}\times\,\,\,\text{measure of any atom in}\,\,\,\mathcal{Q}_{n}^{L}\leqslant C.

Hence there exists a constant
C^{\prime}>0
such that for every
n
and any atom
Q\in\mathcal{Q}_{n}^{L}
,

\#\{Q^{\prime}\in\mathcal{Q}_{n+1}^{L}:Q^{\prime}\subset Q\}\leqslant C^{\prime}.

2.6
Component measures and random measures

For a probability measure
\theta
on a measure space
X
and a measurable subset
Y
of
X
, define the normalized restriction measure by

\theta_{Y}=\begin{cases}\frac{1}{\theta(Y)}{\theta|_{Y}},\,\,\,&\text{if}\,\,\,\theta(Y)>0,\\
0,\,\,\,&\text{otherwise}.\end{cases}

For a probability measure
\theta
on
\mathbb{P}(\mathbb{R}^{2})
(or
L
) equipped with a partition
\mathcal{Q}_{n}
, we write
\mathcal{Q}_{n}(x)
for the unique partition element containing
x
. We define measure valued random variables
\theta_{x,n}
such that
\theta_{x,n}=\theta_{\mathcal{Q}_{n}(x)}
with probability
\theta(\mathcal{Q}_{n}(x))
.
For integers
n_{2}\geqslant n_{1}
and an event
\mathcal{U}
, we write

\mathbb{P}_{n_{1}\leqslant i\leqslant n_{2}}(\theta_{x,i}\in\mathcal{U})=\frac{1}{n_{2}-n_{1}+1}\sum_{n=n_{1}}^{n_{2}}\mathbb{P}(\theta_{x,n}\in\mathcal{U}).

2.7
Regularity of stationary measures

Let
\Lambda
be a finite set and let
\nu=\sum_{i\in\Lambda}p_{i}\delta_{g_{i}}
be a finitely supported probability measure on
\mathrm{SL}_{3}(\mathbb{R})
such that the group generated by the support of
\nu
,
\langle\supp\nu\rangle
, is Zariski dense in
\mathrm{SL}_{3}(\mathbb{R})
.
We write
\nu^{*n}
for the convolution, that is,
\nu^{*n}
is the law of the product
g_{1}\cdots g_{n}
, with i.i.d.
g_{i}
following the law of
\nu
.

Let
\mu
be the unique associated stationary measure on
\mathbb{P}(\mathbb{R}^{3})
(
[
Fur73
]
), that is to say, we have

\mu=\nu*\mu:=\sum_{i\in\Lambda}p_{i}(g_{i})_{*}\mu,

where
(g_{i})_{*}\mu
is the pushforward measure defined by
(g_{i})_{*}\mu(E)=\mu(g_{i}^{-1}E)
for any measurable set
E\subset\mathbb{P}(\mathbb{R}^{3})
.

There are
3

Lyapunov exponents

\lambda_{1}\geqslant\lambda_{2}\geqslant\lambda_{3}
of
\nu
such that
for any
i=1,2,3
,

\lambda_{i}=\lim_{n\to\infty}\int\frac{1}{n}\log\sigma_{i}(g_{i_{1}}\cdots g_{i_{n}})d\nu(g_{i_{1}})\cdots d\nu(g_{i_{n}}).

Since
\langle\supp\nu\rangle
is Zariski dense, we have
\lambda_{1}>\lambda_{2}>\lambda_{3}
(see for example
[
GR85
]
,
[
GM89
]
). Set
\chi_{i}=\lambda_{1}-\lambda_{i+1}
for
i=1,2
, and
\lambda(\nu)=(\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2},\lambda_{3})
to be the Lyapunov vector.

We will also consider the measure
\nu^{-}
on
\mathrm{SL}_{3}(\mathbb{R})
defined by
\nu^{-}(g)=\nu(g^{-1})
.
Let
\mu^{-}
be the unique associated stationary measure.

The results stated in this section and
Section

2.8
hold under a more general assumption that
\supp\nu
generates a Zariski dense subgroup and that
\nu
is of finite exponential moment. But for the interest of the current paper, we will focus on the case when
\nu
is finitely supported.

Classical references of products of random matrices include
[
Fur63
]
and
[
BL85
]
.
We refer the reader to
[
BQ16
]
. We recall an equidistribution result for
\mu
,
\mu^{-}
(
[
GR85
]
,
[
BL85
, Chapter III, Theorem 4.3]
)

Lemma 2.22
.

For any
x\in\mathbb{P}(\mathbb{R}^{3})
, as
n\rightarrow\infty
we have

\nu^{*n}*\delta_{x}\rightarrow\mu,\ (\nu^{-})^{*n}*\delta_{x}\rightarrow\mu^{-},

and the convergence is uniform with respect to
x
.

We have the Guivarc’h regularity of stationary measures (
[
Gui90
]
,

[
BQ16
, Prop 14.1]
)

Lemma 2.23
.

There exist
C>0,\beta>0
such that for any
n\geqslant 1
and hyperplane
W
in
\mathbb{P}(\mathbb{R}^{3})

\mu\{x\in\mathbb{P}(\mathbb{R}^{3}):\ d(W,x)\leqslant q^{-n}\}\leqslant Cq^{-\beta n}.

In particular, for any
V\in\mathbb{P}(\mathbb{R}^{3})
and
x\in\mathbb{P}(\mathbb{R}^{2})

(\pi_{V^{\perp}}\mu)\left(B(x,q^{-n})\right)\leqslant\mu\{y:d((x,V),y)\leqslant q^{-n}\}\leqslant Cq^{-\beta n},

where
(x,V)
is the projective plane spanned by
x
and
V
.

As a corollary, we have the following result.

Lemma 2.24
.

Let
C_{1}>1
. For any
g\in\mathrm{SL}_{3}(\mathbb{R})
and
V\in\mathbb{P}(\mathbb{R}^{3})
satisfy

d(g^{-1}V,V^{\perp})>1/C_{1},

we have for any for any
x\in\mathbb{P}(V^{\perp})
and
r>0
,

(\pi_{g^{-1}V,V^{\perp}}\mu)(B(x,r))\leqslant CC_{1}^{\beta}r^{\beta}.\,\,\,

where
C,\beta
are defined in Lemma
2.23
.

Proof.

It follows from the proof of Lemma
2.10
that

\pi_{g^{-1}V,V^{\perp}}=\pi(g^{-1}V,V^{\perp},(g^{-1}V)^{\perp})\circ\pi_{(g^{-1}V)^{\perp}}.

2.23
holds for
\pi_{(g^{-1}V)^{\perp}}\mu
. Moreover,
B.1
states that
\pi(g^{-1}V,V^{\perp},(g^{-1}V)^{\perp})
scales by
1
with distortion
C_{1}
. These imply the lemma.
∎

More generally, the Hölder regularity of stationary measures also holds for irreducible representations
(
[
Gui90
]
,
[
BQ16
, Proposition 10.1]
). Let
p_{2}
be the map from
\mathbb{P}(\mathbb{R}^{3})
