to the projective space of the second symmetric power
\mathbb{P}(Sym^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{3}))
, defined by sending
\mathbb{R}v
to
\mathbb{R}v\otimes v
. It is
\mathrm{SL}_{3}(\mathbb{R})
-equivariant. Let
(p_{2})_{*}\mu
be the pushfoward measure on
\mathbb{P}(Sym^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{3}))
. Then
(p_{2})_{*}\mu
is
\nu
-stationary and we have

Lemma 2.25
.

There exist
C>0,\beta>0
such that for any
n\geqslant 1
and hyperplane
W
in
\mathbb{P}(Sym^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{3}))
, we have

(p_{2})_{*}\mu\{x\in\mathbb{P}(Sym^{2}\mathbb{R}^{3}):\ d(W,x)\leqslant q^{-n}\}\leqslant Cq^{-\beta n}.

2.8
Large deviation estimates

We first introduce several LDP (Large Deviation Principle) estimates for random walks on
\mathrm{SL}_{3}(\mathbb{R})
. Set
G=\mathrm{SL}_{3}(\mathbb{R})
in this subsection. The following lemma can be found in
[
LP82
]
and Proposition 14.3, Theorem 13.17 (iii), Theorem 13.11 (iii) in
[
BQ16
]
. Recall that
\nu
is finitely supported and Zariski dense and
\mu
is the
\nu
-stationary measure on
\mathbb{P}(\mathbb{R}^{3})
.

Lemma 2.26
.

For any
c>0
, there exist
C>0
and
\epsilon>0
such that for any
n\in\mathbb{N}
, we have for any hyperplane
W
in
\mathbb{P}(\mathbb{R}^{3})
and any
V=\mathbb{R}v\in\mathbb{P}(\mathbb{R}^{3})
with
\|v\|=1
,

\displaystyle\nu^{*n}\{g\in G:\ d(gV,W)\leqslant q^{-cn}\}\leqslant Cq^{-\epsilon n},

(2.48)

\displaystyle\nu^{*n}\{g\in G:\ d(V_{g}^{+},W)\leqslant q^{-cn}\}\leqslant Cq^{-\epsilon n},

(2.49)

\displaystyle\nu^{*n}\{g\in G:\ d(H_{g}^{-},V)\leqslant q^{-cn}\}\leqslant Cq^{-\epsilon n},

(2.50)

\displaystyle\nu^{*n}\{g\in G:\ d(V_{g}^{+},gV)\geqslant q^{-(\chi_{1}-c)n}\}\leqslant Cq^{-\epsilon n},

(2.51)

\displaystyle\nu^{*n}\{g\in G:\ \|\frac{1}{n}\kappa(g)-\lambda(\nu)\|\geqslant c\}\leqslant Cq^{-\epsilon n},

(2.52)

\displaystyle\nu^{*n}\{g\in G:\ \frac{1}{n}|\log\|gv\|-\log\sigma_{1}(g)|\geqslant c\}\leqslant Cq^{-\epsilon n}.

(2.53)

As a corollary, we have the following lemma as in
[
BQ16
, Lemma 14.11]
.

Lemma 2.27
.

For any
c>0
, there exist
C>0
and
\epsilon>0
such that for any
m\leqslant n
, we have for any hyperplane
W
in
\mathbb{P}(\mathbb{R}^{3})
and any
V\in\mathbb{P}(\mathbb{R}^{3})
,

\displaystyle\nu^{*n}\{g\in G:\ d(gV,W)\leqslant q^{-cm}\}\leqslant Cq^{-\epsilon m},

(2.54)

\displaystyle\nu^{*n}\{g\in G:\ d(V_{g}^{+},W)\leqslant q^{-cm}\}\leqslant Cq^{-\epsilon m},

(2.55)

\displaystyle\nu^{*n}\{g\in G:\ d(V,H_{g}^{-})\leqslant q^{-cm}\}\leqslant Cq^{-\epsilon m}.

(2.56)

Proof.

For
Eq.

2.54
, when
m=n
, this is
Eq.

2.48
. Since

\nu^{*n}\{g\in G:d(gV,W)\leqslant q^{-cm}\}=\int_{G}\nu^{*m}\{g\in G:d(ghV,W)\leqslant q^{-cm}\}d\nu^{*(n-m)}(h),

then case
n\geqslant m
follows.

For
Eq.

2.55
, we use triangle inequality

d(V_{g}^{+},W)\geqslant d(gV,W)-d(V_{g}^{+},gV).

Therefore

\displaystyle\nu^{*n}\{g\in G:\ d(V_{g}^{+},W)\leqslant q^{-cm}\}

\displaystyle\leqslant

\displaystyle\nu^{*n}\{g\in G:\ d(gV,W)\leqslant 2q^{-cm}\}+\nu^{*n}\{g\in G:\ d(V_{g}^{+},gV)\geqslant q^{-cm}\}.

We may assume
c<\chi_{1}/2
. Then
q^{-cm}\leqslant q^{-(\chi_{1}-c)n}
and the second term above can be estimated using
Eq.

2.51
. The first term can be estimated using
Eq.

2.54
. Hence, we obtain
Eq.

2.55
.

For
Eq.

2.56
,
due to
H_{g}^{-}=(V_{(g^{t})}^{+})^{\perp}
, it is equivalent to prove

\nu^{*n}\{g\in G:\ d(V_{(g^{t})}^{+},W)\leqslant q^{-cm}\}\leqslant Cq^{-\epsilon m},

(2.57)

with
W=V^{\perp}
, which is similar to
Eq.

2.55
. Let
\nu^{t}
be the probability measure on
G
defined by
\nu^{t}(g)=\nu(g^{t})
. It is finitely generated and Zariski dense.
Eq.

2.57
holds as we have
Eq.

2.55
for
\nu^{t}
.
∎

Next we use LDP of
\mathrm{SL}_{3}(\mathbb{R})
to obtain LDP for the
\mathrm{SL}_{2}(\mathbb{R})
-part in the
UL
-decomposition. It will allow us to reduce the
\mathrm{SL}_{3}(\mathbb{R})
-action on
\mathbb{P}(\mathbb{R}^{3})
to the induced
\mathrm{SL}_{2}(\mathbb{R})
-action on
\mathbb{P}(\mathbb{R}^{2})
and obtain the convergence of entropy for the induced action.

Recall that we have the projection map
\pi_{L}:UL\to L
(see
Eq.

2.6
).
In the following lemma, it is important that it holds for all
n\gg\log C
, which will be very useful in proving porosity in
Section

4
.

Lemma 2.28
.

There exists
\beta>0
such that the following holds. For any
C>1
, there exists
N_{C}=O(\log C)>0
such that we have for any
n>N_{C}
and
V\in\mathbb{P}(\mathbb{R}^{3})

\displaystyle\nu^{*n}\{g\in G:\ d(g^{-1}V,V^{\perp})\leqslant 1/C\}

\displaystyle\leqslant C^{-\beta},

(2.58)

\displaystyle\nu^{*n}\{g\in G:\ |\chi_{1}(h)-\chi_{1}(g)|\geqslant\log C\}

\displaystyle\leqslant C^{-\beta},

(2.59)

where
h\in\mathrm{SL}_{2}^{\pm}(\mathbb{R})
comes from the
UL
-decomposition of
g
:
\pi_{L}(g)=\begin{pmatrix}\det h&0\\
n&h\end{pmatrix}
with
n\in\mathbb{R}^{2}
.

Proof.

Applying
Eq.

2.54
to the measure
\nu^{-}
and
W=V^{\perp}
, we obtain
Eq.

2.58
.

For any
g\in UL
, write
g=\begin{pmatrix}\lambda^{-2}&x&y\\
0&\lambda&0\\
0&0&\lambda\end{pmatrix}\begin{pmatrix}\det h&0\\
n&h\end{pmatrix}
. We define
\tilde{h}:=\Pi_{E_{1}^{\perp}}g|_{E_{1}^{\perp}}=\lambda h\in\mathrm{GL}(E_{1}^{\perp})
. We have

q^{\chi_{1}(h)}=\|\tilde{h}\|^{2}/|\det\tilde{h}|.

The determinant is given by

\begin{split}|\det\tilde{h}|=\lambda^{2}=|\langle g^{-1}e_{1},e_{1}\rangle|=d(g^{-1}E_{1},E_{1}^{\perp})\|g^{-1}e_{1}\|.\end{split}

The norm of
\|\tilde{h}v\|
for
v\in E_{1}^{\perp}
is given by

\|\tilde{h}v\|=\|\Pi_{E_{1}^{\perp}}gv\|=\|(gv)\wedge e_{1}\|=d(g\mathbb{R}v,E_{1})\|gv\|.

(2.60)

Hence

\|g\|d(gE_{2},E_{1})d(E_{2},H_{g}^{-})\leqslant d(gE_{2},E_{1})\|ge_{2}\|\leqslant\|\tilde{h}\|\leqslant\|g\|.

(2.61)

Therefore,

\frac{(d(gE_{2},E_{1})d(E_{2},H_{g}^{-1})\|g\|)^{2}}{d(g^{-1}E_{1},E_{1}^{\perp})\|g^{-1}e_{1}\|}\leqslant q^{\chi_{1}(h)}=\frac{\|\tilde{h}\|^{2}}{|\det{h}|}\leqslant\frac{\|g\|^{2}}{d(g^{-1}E_{1},E_{1}^{\perp})\|g^{-1}e_{1}\|}.

It follows from
2.5
that

d(E_{1},H^{-}_{g^{-1}})\leqslant\frac{\|g^{-1}e_{1}\|}{\|g^{-1}\|}\leqslant 1.

(2.62)

We also have
\|g\|^{2}/\|g^{-1}\|=\sigma_{1}(g)^{2}/\sigma_{1}(g^{-1})=\sigma_{1}(g)^{2}\sigma_{3}(g)=\sigma_{1}(g)/\sigma_{2}(g)=q^{\chi_{1}(g)}
. Therefore

q^{\chi_{1}(g)}(d(gE_{2},E_{1})d(E_{2},H_{g}^{-1}))^{2}\leqslant q^{\chi_{1}(h)}\leqslant q^{\chi_{1}(g)}\frac{1}{d(g^{-1}E_{1},E_{1}^{\perp})d(E_{1},H_{g^{-1}}^{-})}.

(2.63)

Consider the elements
g\in G
satisfying all the following inequalities:

(i)

d(E_{1},H^{-}_{g^{-1}})\geqslant q^{-C/8}
;

(ii)

d(g^{-1}E_{1},E_{1}^{\perp})\geqslant q^{-3C/4}
;

(iii)

d(E_{2},H_{g}^{-})\geqslant q^{-C/8}
;

(iv)

d(gE_{2},E_{1})\geqslant q^{-3C/8}
.

It follows from
Eq.

2.54
and
Eq.

2.56
, that
\nu^{*n}
-measure of such
g
is greater than
1-C^{-\beta}
for some
\beta>0
. Using (i), (ii) to
Eq.

2.63
, we have
q^{\chi_{1}(h)-\chi_{1}(g)}\leqslant q^{C}
; using (iii), (iv) to
Eq.

2.63
, we have
q^{\chi_{1}(g)-\chi_{1}(h)}\leqslant q^{C}
.
This finishes the proof of
Eq.

2.59
.
∎

We need more LDP estimates of
h
.

Lemma 2.29
.

For any
c>0
, there exist
C>0
and
\epsilon>0
such that for any
x\in\mathbb{P}(E_{1}^{\perp})
and
n\in\mathbb{N}
, we have

\displaystyle\nu^{*n}\{g\in G:\ \frac{1}{n}|\chi_{1}(h)-\chi_{1}n|\geqslant c\}

\displaystyle\leqslant Cq^{-\epsilon n},

(2.64)

\displaystyle\nu^{*n}\{g\in G:\ d(H_{h}^{-},x)\leqslant q^{-cn}\}

\displaystyle\leqslant Cq^{-\epsilon n},

(2.65)

\displaystyle\nu^{*n}\{g\in G:\ d(V^{+}_{h},hx)\geqslant q^{(-\chi_{1}+c)n}\}

\displaystyle\leqslant Cq^{-\epsilon n},

(2.66)

where
h\in\mathrm{SL}_{2}^{\pm}(\mathbb{R})
comes from the
UL
-decomposition of
g
:
\pi_{L}(g)=\begin{pmatrix}\det h&0\\
n&h\end{pmatrix}
with
n\in\mathbb{R}^{2}
.

Proof.

For
Eq.

2.64
,
we compare
\chi_{1}(h)
with
\chi_{1}(g)
using
Eq.

2.59
. Then use
Eq.

2.52
for the LDP of
\chi_{1}(g)
.

For any
g\in UL
, write
g=\begin{pmatrix}\lambda^{-2}&x&y\\
0&\lambda&0\\
0&0&\lambda\end{pmatrix}\begin{pmatrix}\det h&0\\
n&h\end{pmatrix}
. We define
\tilde{h}:=\Pi_{E_{1}^{\perp}}g|_{E_{1}^{\perp}}=\lambda h\in\mathrm{GL}(E_{1}^{\perp})
. Take a unit vector
v
in
x
.
Due to
2.5
, that is

d(H^{-}_{h},x)\geqslant\frac{\|\tilde{h}v\|}{\|\tilde{h}\|}-q^{-\chi_{1}(h)}.

To obtain
Eq.

2.65
, it suffices to estimate the
\nu^{*n}
-measure of
g
such that

(i)

either its corresponding
\tilde{h}
satisfies
\|\tilde{h}v\|\leqslant 4q^{-cn}\|\tilde{h}\|
,

(ii)

or
\chi_{1}(h)\leqslant 2cn
.

We can bound the
\nu^{*n}
-measure of
g
satisfying (ii) by applying
Eq.

2.64
to another sufficiently small constant
c
. For (i), by
Eq.

2.61
,

\|\tilde{h}\|\leqslant\sigma_{1}(g).

So due to
Eq.

2.60
, we have

d(g\mathbb{R}v,E_{1})\frac{\|gv\|}{\sigma_{1}(g)}\leqslant\frac{\|\tilde{h}v\|}{\|\tilde{h}\|}.

We use
Eq.

2.48
and
Eq.

2.53
to estimate the first term and the second term on the left, respectively. This yields the estimate of the
\nu^{*n}
-measure of
g
satisfying (i), and hence we obtain
Eq.

2.65
.

Using
2.5
, we have

d(V^{+}_{h},hx)\leqslant\frac{q^{-\chi_{1}(h)}}{d(x,H^{-}_{h})}.

Then
Eq.

2.66
follows from
Eq.

2.64
and
Eq.

2.65
.
∎

At the end of the subsection, we introduce two kinds of good subsets in
L
:

•

for any
n\in\mathbb{N}
and
c>0
, set

L(n,c):=\{\ell\in L:\ d(\ell^{-1}E_{1},E_{1}^{\perp})\geqslant q^{-cn},\ \chi_{1}(h)\in[\chi_{1}-c,\chi_{1}+c]n\};

(2.67)

•

for any
t\geqslant 1
and
C_{1}>0
, set

L(t,C_{1}):=\{\ell\in L:\ d(\ell^{-1}E_{1},E_{1}^{\perp})>1/C_{1},\ \|h\|\in q^{t/2}[1/C_{p},C_{p}]\},

(2.68)

where
C_{p}
is defined as in
Eq.

2.47
, and
h
is the
\mathrm{SL}_{2}^{\pm}(\mathbb{R})
matrix of
\ell
: we have
\ell=\begin{pmatrix}\det h&0\\
n&h\end{pmatrix}
with
n\in\mathbb{R}^{2}
.
For
\ell\in L(t,C_{1})
, we have
\|h\|\geqslant 2
. Moreover, it follows from
Eq.

2.58
and
Eq.

2.64
that

(\pi_{L}\nu^{*n})(L(n,c)^{c})\leqslant Cq^{-\epsilon(c)n}.

(2.69)

2.9
Coding, partitions of symbolic space and random words

We consider the general coding scheme of the random walk.
Consider the symbolic space
\Lambda^{\mathbb{N}}
, the set of one-sided infinite words over
\Lambda
. Let
\Lambda^{\ast}
be the set of finite words over
\Lambda
and for each
n\in\mathbb{N}
,
\Lambda^{n}
be the set of words of length
n
. For a word
\mathbf{i}=(i_{1},...,i_{n})\in\Lambda^{\ast}
, we define

g_{\mathbf{i}}:=g_{i_{1}}\circ\cdots\circ g_{i_{n}}
.
Similarly, define
p_{\mathbf{i}}:=p_{i_{1}}\cdots p_{i_{n}}
.

The space
\Lambda^{\mathbb{N}}
comes with the natural partitions into level-
n
cylinder sets. However, it will be more convenient to consider general partitions into cylinders of varying lengths.

The partition
\Psi_{n}^{q}
and the definition of
q
.

We consider a collection of words

\Psi_{n}^{q}=\{(i_{1},\cdots,i_{m})\in\Lambda^{\ast}:\chi_{1}(g_{i_{1}\ldots i_{m}})\geqslant n>\chi_{1}(g_{i_{1}\ldots i_{j}})\ \text{ for all }j<m\}.

Different from the setting in
[
BHR19
]
, the exponent
\chi_{1}
here is not monotone with respect to the length of the word
m
. However, note that the measure
\nu
has compact support, and the Cartan projection is subadditive (see for example
[
Kas08
, Lemma 2.3]
). There exists a constant
c_{0}>0
, depending only on the support of
\nu
, such that for every
n\in\mathbb{N}
and for every
\mathbf{i}\in\Psi_{n}^{q}
,

c_{0}q^{-n}\leqslant q^{-\chi_{1}(g_{\mathbf{i}})}\leqslant q^{-n}.

(2.70)

If we take
q>1/c_{0}
, then for any
n\neq k
, we have

\Psi^{q}_{n}\cap\Psi^{q}_{k}=\emptyset.

(2.71)

By the definition of
\Psi_{n}^{q}
, no word in is a prefix of another word. And the law of large numbers holds for
\nu
(
[
Fur63
]
): we have for
\nu^{\otimes\mathbb{N}}
-a.e.
\mathbf{i}\in\Lambda^{\mathbb{N}}
,

\frac{\chi_{1}(g_{i_{1}\ldots i_{m}})}{m}\rightarrow\chi_{1}>0.

(2.72)

Therefore, the set of
\mathbf{i}\in\Lambda^{\mathbb{N}}
such that
\chi_{1}(g_{i_{1}\ldots i_{m}})<n
for all
m
is of measure zero. Consider the map
\Psi_{n}^{q}:\Lambda^{\mathbb{N}}\rightarrow\Lambda^{*}
sending
\mathbf{i}
to its first
(m+1)
coordinates with
m
the least nonnegative integer such that
\chi_{1}(g_{i_{1}\ldots i_{m}})\geqslant n
. It is defined
\nu^{\otimes\mathbb{N}}
- almost everywhere, and it gives a measurable partition of
\Lambda^{\mathbb{N}}
.

Definition 2.30
.

From now on, we fix the choice of an integer
q
with
q>\max\{1/c_{0},C_{L},4C_{p}^{2}\}
(see
Eq.

2.29
and
Eq.

2.47
), and the logarithm is in base
q
,
\log=\log_{q}
.

Definition 2.31

(Random words
\mathbf{U}(n)
,
\mathbf{I}(n)
)
.

For any given
n\in\mathbb{N}
, we introduce the random words
{\mathbf{I}}(n)
and
{\mathbf{U}}(n)
taking values from subsets coming from measurable partitions of
\Lambda^{\ast}
:

•

{\mathbf{I}}(n)
is the random word taking values in
\Psi_{n}(=\Psi_{n}^{q})
according to the probability
\nu^{\otimes\mathbb{N}}.

i.e.

\mathbb{P}({\mathbf{I}}(n)=\mathbf{i})=\begin{cases}p_{\mathbf{i}},\text{ if }\mathbf{i}\in\Psi_{n}\\
0,\text{ otherwise}.\end{cases}

•

{\mathbf{U}}(n)
is the random word taking values from
\Lambda^{n}
according to the probability
\nu^{\otimes n}
.

For each
n\in\mathbb{N}
and for any measurable set
\mathcal{U}\subset G
, we have

\nu^{*n}\{g\in G:g\in\mathcal{U}\}=\mathbb{P}_{i=n}\{{\mathbf{U}}(i):g_{{\mathbf{U}}(i)}\in\mathcal{U}\}.

(2.73)

Due to the stationarity of the measure
\mu
and that
{\mathbf{I}}(n)
is defined by stopping time, using the martingale property, we have for any
n\in\mathbb{N}

\mu=\mathbb{E}_{j=n}(g_{{\mathbf{I}}(j)}\mu).

(2.74)

The following lemma is the relation between
{\mathbf{U}}(i)
and
{\mathbf{I}}(i)
.

Lemma 2.32
.

There exist
C_{I}>1
and
\beta>0
such that the following holds. Let
\mathcal{U}\subset\Lambda^{\ast}
be a set of words. Suppose that for some
\epsilon>0
and
n(\epsilon)\in\mathbb{N}
, we have for every
n\geqslant n(\epsilon)
,

\mathbb{P}_{1\leqslant i\leqslant n}\{{\mathbf{U}}(i)\in\mathcal{U}\}>1-\epsilon.

Then for every
n\geqslant n(\epsilon)
,

\mathbb{P}_{1\leqslant i\leqslant n}\{{\mathbf{I}}(i)\in\mathcal{U}\}>1-C_{I}\epsilon/2-O(q^{-\beta n}).

Proof.

The subtlety of the proof lies in that
\chi_{1}(g_{i_{1}\ldots i_{m}})
is not monotone with respect to the length of the word
m
.

For any
n\geqslant n(\epsilon)
, we want to bound

\mathbb{P}_{1\leqslant i\leqslant n}\{{\mathbf{I}}(i)\in\mathcal{U}^{c}\}.

Set
C^{\prime}>2(1-\log c_{0})/\chi_{1}
and
\mathcal{B}:=\{\mathbf{i}\in\Psi_{i}:1\leqslant i\leqslant n\}
. We have
\Psi_{i}\cap\Psi_{j}=\emptyset
for any
1\leqslant i<j\leqslant C^{\prime}n
(
Eq.

2.71
).

We consider two cases. For any
\mathbf{i}\in\mathcal{B}
with the word length
l(\mathbf{i})\leqslant C^{\prime}n
, by definition,

\mathbf{i}\in{\mathbf{U}}(j)
for some
j\leqslant C^{\prime}n
. So we have

\mathbb{P}_{1\leqslant i\leqslant n}({\mathbf{I}}(i):{\mathbf{I}}(i)\in\mathcal{U}^{c},l({\mathbf{I}}(i))\leqslant C^{\prime}n)\leqslant C^{\prime}\mathbb{P}_{1\leqslant i\leqslant C^{\prime}n}({\mathbf{U}}(i):{\mathbf{U}}(i)\in\mathcal{U}^{c})\leqslant C^{\prime}\epsilon.

(2.75)

Consider any
\mathbf{i}\in\mathcal{B}
with
l(\mathbf{i})>C^{\prime}n
. Take
\epsilon_{1}=\min\{\epsilon,\chi_{1}/2\}
. It follows from
Eq.

2.70
that

\chi_{1}(g_{\mathbf{i}})\leqslant n-\log c_{0}\leqslant C^{\prime}n(\chi_{1}-\epsilon_{1})\leqslant l(\mathbf{i})(\chi_{1}-\epsilon_{1}).

As
\mathbf{i}\in U(j)
for some
j>C^{\prime}n
, the large deviation estimate
Eq.

2.52
yields

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}_{1\leqslant i\leqslant n}\{{\mathbf{I}}(i):l({\mathbf{I}}(i))>C^{\prime}n\}\leqslant

\displaystyle\frac{1}{n}\sum_{j>C^{\prime}n}\mathbb{P}\{{\mathbf{U}}(j):\chi_{1}(g_{{\mathbf{U}}(j)})\leqslant l({\mathbf{U}}(j))(\chi_{1}-\epsilon_{1})\}

\displaystyle\leqslant

\displaystyle\frac{1}{n}\sum_{j\geqslant C^{\prime}n}Cq^{-\beta(\epsilon_{1})j}.

(2.76)

Combining
Eqs.

2.75
and
2.9
, we finish the proof of the lemma.
∎

2.10
Entropy

For a probability measure
\mu
on a metric space
X
and any countable measurable partitions
\mathcal{E},\mathcal{E}^{\prime}
of
X
, define the entropy and conditional entropy by

\displaystyle H(\mu,\mathcal{E}):=

\displaystyle\sum_{E\in\mathcal{E}}-\mu(E)\log\mu(E),

\displaystyle H(\mu,\mathcal{E}^{\prime}|\mathcal{E}):=

\displaystyle H(\mu,\mathcal{E}^{\prime}\vee\mathcal{E})-H(\mu,\mathcal{E}),

where
\mathcal{E}^{\prime}\vee\mathcal{E}
denotes the common refinement of
\mathcal{E}^{\prime}
and
\mathcal{E}
.

We collect some basic properties of entropy.

Lemma 2.33
.

Let
\mathcal{E}
and
\mathcal{F}
be two measurable partitions of
X
. If each atom in
\mathcal{E}
intersects at most
k
atoms in
\mathcal{F}
, then

H(\mu,\mathcal{F})\leqslant H(\mu,\mathcal{E})+O(\log k).

Suppose
X=\mathbb{P}(\mathbb{R}^{2})
or
L
and
\mathcal{Q}_{n}
the
q
-adic partition on
X
.
This lemma implies that for any
m,n\in\mathbb{N}
,

|H(\mu,\mathcal{Q}_{n})-H(\mu,\mathcal{Q}_{m})|=O(|m-n|).

(2.77)

Let
f
and
g
be two continuous maps on
X
, such that

\|f-g\|_{\infty}\leqslant q^{-n}
.
Denote by
f\mu
and
g\mu
the pushforward measures. Then we have

|H(f\mu,\mathcal{Q}_{n})-H(g\mu,\mathcal{Q}_{n})|=O(1)

(2.78)

(see
[
BHR19
, (2.14)]
).

Lemma 2.34

(Lemma 4.3 in
[
HS17
]
)
.

Suppose
f
scales
\supp\mu
by
u>0
with distortion
C>1
(see
2.6
for definition). Then

H(f\mu,\mathcal{Q}_{n-\log u})=H(\mu,\mathcal{Q}_{n})+O(\log C).

The following is about the concavity of entropy. Let
\delta\in[0,1]
, and
\mu_{1},\mu_{2}
be probability measures on
X
and
\mathcal{Q}
be a measurable partition. We have

\begin{split}\delta H(\mu_{1},\mathcal{Q})+(1-\delta)H(\mu_{2},\mathcal{Q})&\leqslant H(\delta\mu_{1}+(1-\delta)\mu_{2},\mathcal{Q})\\
&\leqslant\delta H(\mu_{1},\mathcal{Q})+(1-\delta)H(\mu_{2},\mathcal{Q})+H(\delta),\end{split}

(2.79)

where
H(\delta):=-\delta\log(\delta)-(1-\delta)\log(1-\delta)
. The quantity
H(\delta)
is small if
\delta
is sufficiently close to
0
or
1
.

Lemma 2.35
.

For any
k,n\in\mathbb{N}
, any
\epsilon_{1},\epsilon_{2}\in(0,1)
and any probability measures
\tau,\tau_{1},\tau_{2}
on
X
that satisfy
\tau=(1-\epsilon_{2})\tau_{1}+\epsilon_{2}\tau_{2}
and
\diam(\supp\tau_{1})=O(q^{-k+\epsilon_{1}n})
, we have

\displaystyle\frac{1}{n}H\left(\tau,\mathcal{Q}_{n+k}|\mathcal{Q}_{k}\right)\geqslant

\displaystyle\frac{1}{n}H(\tau,\mathcal{Q}_{n+k})-\left(\epsilon_{2}+\frac{H(\epsilon_{2})}{n}+O(\epsilon_{1})\right),

\displaystyle\frac{1}{n}H\left(\tau,\mathcal{Q}_{n+k}|\mathcal{Q}_{k}\right)\geqslant

\displaystyle\frac{1}{n}H(\tau_{1},\mathcal{Q}_{n+k})-(\epsilon_{2}+O(\epsilon_{1})).

Proof.

Note that the support of the measure
\tau_{1}
intersects at most
O(q^{\epsilon_{1}n})
atoms of
\mathcal{Q}_{k}
. By
Eq.

2.77
and
Eq.

2.79
, we have

\displaystyle\frac{1}{n}H\left(\tau,\mathcal{Q}_{n+k}|\mathcal{Q}_{k}\right)\geqslant(1-\epsilon_{2})\frac{1}{n}H(\tau_{1},\mathcal{Q}_{n+k}|\mathcal{Q}_{k})

\displaystyle\geqslant

\displaystyle(1-\epsilon_{2})\frac{1}{n}H(\tau_{1},\mathcal{Q}_{n+k})-O(\epsilon_{1})\geqslant\frac{1}{n}H(\tau,\mathcal{Q}_{n+k})-(\epsilon_{2}+H(\epsilon_{2})/n+O(\epsilon_{1})).

For the second one, we have

\displaystyle\frac{1}{n}H\left(\tau,\mathcal{Q}_{n+k}|\mathcal{Q}_{k}\right)\geqslant(1-\epsilon_{2})\frac{1}{n}H(\tau_{1},\mathcal{Q}_{n+k}|\mathcal{Q}_{k})

\displaystyle\geqslant

\displaystyle\frac{1}{n}H(\tau_{1},\mathcal{Q}_{n+k})-O(\epsilon_{1})-\epsilon_{2}\frac{1}{n}H(\tau_{1},\mathcal{Q}_{n+k}|\mathcal{Q}_{k})\geqslant\frac{1}{n}H(\tau_{1},\mathcal{Q}_{n+k})-(\epsilon_{2}+O(\epsilon_{1})).\qed

2.11
Entropy dimension of projection measure

A probability measure
\mu
on a metric space
X
is called
exact dimensional
if for
\mu
-a.e.
x
, we have that

\lim_{r\rightarrow 0}\frac{\log\mu(B(x,r))}{\log r}

exists and equals a constant independent of
x
. This constant is called the
exact dimension of the measure
\mu
. Young
[
You82
]
proved that if
\mu
is exact dimensional, then its exact dimension coincides with
\dim\mu
, the Hausdorff dimension of
\mu
, which we defined in the Introduction. Hence, we also use
\dim\mu
to denote the exact dimension of
\mu
.

In the same paper
[
You82
]
, Young proved a fundamental property of exact dimensional measures (see also
[
FLR02
]
).

Lemma 2.36
.

Let
\mu
be an exact dimensional probability measure on
\mathbb{R}
. Then

\dim\mu=\lim_{n\rightarrow\infty}\frac{1}{n}H(\mu,\mathcal{Q}_{n}).

This lemma enables us to use the entropy to compute the dimension of the measure
\mu
. The limit on the right-hand side is usually called the
entropy dimension
.

Let
\mu
be the
\nu
-stationary measure on
\mathbb{P}(\mathbb{R}^{3})
given as in
Section

2.7
. In
[
LL23b
]
and
[
Rap21
]
, it is proved that for
\mu^{-}
-a.e.
V\in\mathbb{P}(\mathbb{R}^{3})
the projection measure
\pi_{V^{\perp}}\mu
is exact dimensional.
Thus we have

Lemma 2.37
.

For
\mu^{-}
-a.e.
V\in\mathbb{P}(\mathbb{R}^{3})
, we have

\dim\pi_{V^{\perp}}\mu=\lim_{n}\frac{1}{n}H(\pi_{V^{\perp}}\mu,\mathcal{Q}_{n}),

and they are of the same value.

See
[
LL23b
, Corollary 6.5]
and
[
Rap21
, Theorem 1.3]
for the proof.

The following lemma is an application of “pseudo-continuity" property of fixed-scale entropy.

Lemma 2.38
.

For any
\epsilon>0
, any
m\geqslant M(\epsilon)
large enough, any
n\geqslant N(\epsilon,m)
, we have

\inf_{V\in\mathbb{P}(\mathbb{R}^{3})}\mathbb{P}\left\{{\mathbf{U}}(n):\alpha-\epsilon\leqslant\frac{1}{m}H(\pi_{(g^{-1}_{\mathbf{U}(n)}V)^{\perp}}\mu,\mathcal{Q}_{m})\leqslant\alpha+\epsilon\right\}>1-\epsilon.

(2.80)

Proof.

By
2.37
, for
\mu^{-}
-almost every
V
,
\pi_{V^{\perp}}\mu
has entropy dimension
\alpha
. We have the equidistribution of random walks (
2.22
), i.e.
\mathbb{E}(\delta_{{A}_{\mathbf{U}(n)}^{-1}V})
weakly converges to
\mu^{-}
as
n
goes to infinity and the convergence is uniform with respect to
V
. Then (
2.80
) can be shown immediately if the scale-
m
entropy were continuous as a function of measure. Since it is not, we need the following argument.

We can replace the entropy
\frac{1}{m}H(\cdot,\mathcal{Q}_{m})
by a continuous variant
F_{m}(\cdot)
at the cost of
O(1/m)
, where
F_{m}
is continuous in the weak
∗
topology. For a possible choice of
F_{m}
, see the end of Section 2.8 in
[
BHR19
]
. Due to
2.37
, for large
m
, we can obtain an open set
E(m)
with
\mu^{-}(E(m))>1-\epsilon/2
such that for
V\in E(m)
, we have
|F_{m}(\pi_{V^{\perp}}\mu)-\alpha|<\epsilon/2
. Applying
2.22
to
E(m)
, for large
n>N_{2}
, we have
Eq.

2.80
with
\frac{1}{m}H(\cdot,\mathcal{Q}_{m})
replaced
F_{m}(\cdot)
.
Then replace
F_{m}
back to
\frac{1}{m}H(\cdot,\mathcal{Q}_{m})
. The resulting
O(1/m)
error can be absorbed in
\epsilon
, which proves the claim.
∎

We recall the notion of Furstenberg entropy, which appears in the statement of our main results.

Definition 2.39
.

Let
\mu
be a
\nu
-stationary measure on
{\mathbb{P}}({\mathbb{R}}^{3})
or
\mathcal{F}({\mathbb{R}}^{3}),
(the flag variety whose precise definition is given in
3.3
) the
Furstenberg entropy
is given by

h_{\mathrm{F}}(\mu,\nu)=\int\log\frac{\mathrm{d}g\mu}{\mathrm{d}\mu}(\xi)\left(\frac{\mathrm{d}g\mu}{\mathrm{d}\mu}(\xi)\right)\mathrm{d}\nu(g)\mathrm{d}\mu(\xi).

(2.81)

The Furstenberg entropy is mysterious and might be difficult to compute. To obtain a more calculable dimension formula, it is expected the Furstenberg entropy is equal to the random walk entropy, which holds in some concrete examples; see our second paper with Jiao
[
JLPX
]
.

3
Non-concentration on arithmetic sequences

One of the main ingredients to show Theorem
1.7
is to prove the
porosity
property of projections of the stationary measure, see
Section

4
. To obtain the porosity of measures, a key step which we show below is to get the
non-concentration at arithmetic sequences
property. We identify
\mathbb{P}(\mathbb{R}^{2})
with
\mathbb{R}/\mathbb{Z}
(see
Section

2.1
).

Definition 3.1
.

A probability measure
\tau
on
\mathbb{P}(\mathbb{R}^{2})
is called
non-concentrated on arithmetic sequences
(across scales)
if for any
\delta>0
, there exist
l,k_{0}\in\mathbb{N}
such that for all
k>k_{0}
, we have

\tau\left(\bigcup_{0\leqslant n<q^{k}}B\left(\frac{n}{q^{k}},\frac{1}{q^{k+l}}\right)\right)\leqslant\delta.

In
[
BHR19
]
, a property called
uniformly continuous across scales
(UCAS) was defined for measures on metric spaces, which plays an important role in the proof of porosity in their setting (Lemma 3.5 in
[
BHR19
]
). This property is quite strong and can imply the uniform Hölder regularity of measures. However, it is not clear whether it holds for a (projection of a) general stationary measure. In our paper, we apply the knowledge of Fourier decay of Furstenberg measures in
[
Li22
]
to obtain the weaker substitution of UCAS, i.e. non-concentration at arithmetic sequences, which implies Lemma
4.3
that plays a role as that of Lemma 3.5 in
[
BHR19
]
.

Recall that the Fourier coefficients of
\tau\in\mathbf{P}(\mathbb{P}(\mathbb{R}^{2}))
are defined as follows: for any
m\in\mathbb{Z}
,

\hat{\tau}(m):=\int_{x\in\mathbb{R}/\mathbb{Z}}e^{2\pi imx}\mathrm{d}\tau(x).

We say
\tau
is a Rajchman measure (or Fourier decay) if
\hat{\tau}(m)\rightarrow 0
as
|m|\rightarrow\infty
.

Lemma 3.2
.

Let
\tau\in\mathbf{P}(\mathbb{P}(\mathbb{R}^{2}))
be a Rajchman measure. Then
\tau
satisfies Definition
3.1
. In particular, we can choose
l,k_{0}\in\mathbb{N}
in
3.1
such that

\hat{\tau}(jq^{k_{0}})\leqslant\delta^{3}/(10^{4}q^{2})\,\,\,\text{ for any}\,\,\,j\neq 0\,\,\,\text{and}\,\,\,10<q^{l}\delta\leqslant 10q.

Proof.

Let
f
be a smooth function supported on
B(0,2/q^{l})\subset\mathbb{R}/\mathbb{Z}
such that it equals to
1
on
B(0,1/q^{l})
and
\|f^{\prime\prime}\|_{L^{1}}\leqslant 4q^{2l}
. Define
F:\mathbb{R}/\mathbb{Z}\to\mathbb{R}
by
F(x)=\sum_{0\leqslant n<q^{k}}f(xq^{k}-n)
. Then

\displaystyle\tau\left(\bigcup_{0\leqslant n<q^{k}}B\left(nq^{-k},q^{-(k+l)}\right)\right)\leqslant\int F\mathrm{d}\tau=\sum_{m\in\mathbb{Z}}\hat{\tau}(m)\hat{F}(-m),

where
\hat{F}(m)=\int_{\mathbb{R}/\mathbb{Z}}F(x)e^{i2\pi mx}dx
.
We have

\displaystyle\hat{F}(m)

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathbb{R}/\mathbb{Z}}\sum_{0\leqslant n<q^{k}}f(q^{k}x-n)e^{i2\pi mx}\mathrm{d}x=\frac{1}{q^{k}}\int_{\mathbb{R}/\mathbb{Z}}\sum_{1\leqslant n\leqslant q^{k}}f(y)e^{i2\pi m(y+n)/q^{k}}\mathrm{d}y

\displaystyle=\frac{\sum_{1\leqslant n\leqslant q^{k}}e^{i2\pi mn/q^{k}}}{q^{k}}\int_{\mathbb{R}/\mathbb{Z}}f(y)e^{i2\pi my/q^{k}}\mathrm{d}y.

It is non-zero only if
m=jq^{k}
. Therefore, we have
\hat{F}(jq^{k})=\hat{f}(j)
and

\tau\left(\cup_{0\leqslant n<q^{k}}B\left(nq^{-k},q^{-(k+l)}\right)\right)\leqslant\sum_{j\in\mathbb{Z}}\hat{\tau}(q^{k}j)\hat{f}(-j).

When
j=0
, we have
\hat{f}(0)=\int f\leqslant 4q^{-l}
. For the sum of the remaining terms, we have

\left|\sum_{j\neq 0}\hat{\tau}(q^{k}j)\hat{f}(j)\right|\leqslant\left(\sup_{j\neq 0}|\hat{\tau}(q^{k}j)|\right)\sum_{j\neq 0}|\hat{f}(j)|.

Since
f
is smooth, we have

\sum_{j\neq 0}|\hat{f}(j)|\leqslant\sum_{j\neq 0}\frac{1}{j^{2}}\|f^{\prime\prime}\|_{L^{1}}\leqslant 4\|f^{\prime\prime}\|_{L^{1}}\leqslant 16q^{2l}<\infty.

Since
\tau
is a Rajchman measure, we have for any
k
large enough,
\sup_{j\neq 0}|\hat{\tau}(q^{k}j)|\leqslant\delta^{3}/(10^{4}q^{2})
. Choose
l\in\mathbb{N}
such that
10<q^{l}\delta\leqslant 10q
. We have

\sum_{j\in\mathbb{Z}}\hat{\tau}(q^{k}j)\hat{f}(j)\leqslant\int f+\left(\sup_{j\neq 0}|\hat{\tau}(q^{k}j)|\right)\sum_{j\neq 0}|\hat{f}(j)|\leqslant 4/q^{l}+(\delta^{3}/10^{4}q^{2})\times 16q^{2l}<\delta.

Therefore
\tau
satisfies Definition
3.1
.
∎

For higher dimensional cases, the Fourier decay of Furstenberg measures on flag varieties and projective spaces is known in
[
Li22
]
, cf.
Appendix

E
for
3
-dimensional case. For our purpose, we only consider the flag variety
\mathcal{F}(\mathbb{R}^{3})
here.

Definition 3.3
.

1.

(Definitions and notations) Let

\mathcal{F}({\mathbb{R}}^{3}):=\left\{(V_{1},V_{2}):V_{1}\subset V_{2},V_{i}\text{ is a linear subspace of }{\mathbb{R}}^{3}\text{ of dimension }i\right\}

be the space of flags in
{\mathbb{R}}^{3}
, which admits a natural
\mathrm{SL}_{3}({\mathbb{R}})
-action. We abbreviate
\mathcal{F}({\mathbb{R}}^{3})
to
\mathcal{F}
in later discussion.

2.

(Metrics on
\mathcal{F}
) For
\eta=(V_{1}(\eta),V_{2}(\eta)),\eta^{\prime}=(V_{1}(\eta^{\prime}),V_{2}(\eta^{\prime}))\in\mathcal{F}
, let

d_{1}(\eta,\eta^{\prime})=d_{\mathbb{P}(\mathbb{R}^{3})}(V_{1}(\eta),V_{1}(\eta^{\prime}))\,\,\,\text{and}\,\,\,d_{2}(\eta,\eta^{\prime})=d_{\mathbb{P}(\mathbb{R}^{3})}(V_{2}(\eta),V_{2}(\eta^{\prime})).

Here
d_{\mathbb{P}(\mathbb{R}^{3})}(V_{2}(\eta),V_{2}(\eta^{\prime}))
is the Hausdorff distance between two projective lines in
(\mathbb{P}(\mathbb{R}^{3}),d)
. Then the metric
d
on
\mathcal{F}
can be defined as the following:

d(\eta,\eta^{\prime}):=\max\{d_{1}(\eta,\eta^{\prime}),d_{2}(\eta,\eta^{\prime})\}.

(3.1)

3.

(Lifting functions from
\mathbb{P}(\mathbb{R}^{3})
)A function
\varphi
on
\mathcal{F}
is called
lifted from
\mathbb{P}(\mathbb{R}^{3})
if
\varphi(\eta)
only depends on the first coordinate
V_{1}(\eta)
.

Recall that
\mu
is the
\nu
-stationary measure on
\mathbb{P}(\mathbb{R}^{3})
. In the following, for any
V\in\mathbb{P}(\mathbb{R}^{3})
, by identifying
\mathbb{P}(V^{\perp})
isometrically with
\mathbb{P}(\mathbb{R}^{2})
, we view
\pi_{V^{\perp}}\mu
as a measure on
\mathbb{P}(\mathbb{R}^{2})
.

Proposition 3.4
.

For any
\delta>0
, there exist
l,k_{0}\in\mathbb{N}
such that for any
V\in\mathbb{P}(\mathbb{R}^{3})
and any
k\geqslant k_{0}

\pi_{V^{\perp}}\mu(\bigcup_{0\leqslant n<q^{k}}B(nq^{-k},q^{-(k+l)}))\leqslant\delta.

(3.2)

Proof.

By the proof of
3.2
, it is sufficient to prove
\widehat{\pi_{V^{\perp}}\mu}(m)\rightarrow 0
uniformly for all
V
as
|m|\rightarrow\infty
. To obtain it our strategy is to apply
E.2
. Recall the constants
\epsilon_{0},\epsilon_{1}>0
from
E.2
. Let
\epsilon_{2}=\epsilon_{0}/8
.
Fix an arbitrary
V\in\mathbb{P}(\mathbb{R}^{3})
and an integer
m\in\mathbb{Z}
such that
|m|^{\epsilon_{2}}>10
.

Step 1: Define, lift and localize
\varphi
.

Consider the map

\displaystyle\varphi:\mathbb{P}(\mathbb{R}^{3})-\{V\}

\displaystyle\to\mathbb{P}(V^{\perp}),

(3.3)

\displaystyle x

\displaystyle\mapsto\pi_{V^{\perp}}x.

Then
\varphi
can be lifted as a map from
\mathcal{F}-\{\eta=(V_{1}(\eta),V_{2}(\eta)):V_{1}(\eta)\neq V\}
. From now to the end of the proof of Proposition
3.4
we only consider the lifting of
\varphi
and we still denote by
\varphi
. By the definition,
\varphi
is neither a well-defined map on whole
\mathcal{F}
nor a well-defined real-valued function, to which we cannot apply Fourier decay (i.e.
E.2
) directly. To overcome this difficulty we localize
\varphi
through partition of unity.

Let
\rho_{m}(t)
be a
C^{1}
-function on
[0,1]
such that
\rho_{m}(t)=0
if
t\leqslant 1/|m|^{\epsilon_{2}}
and
\rho_{m}(x)=1
if
t\geqslant 2/|m|^{\epsilon_{2}}
. We further assume that the Lipchitz constant of
\rho_{m}
satisfies
\Lip(\rho_{m})\leqslant 2|m|^{\epsilon_{2}}
. Let
r_{m}
be a cutoff function on
\mathcal{F}
given by

r_{m}(\eta)=\rho_{m}(d_{\mathbb{P}(\mathbb{R}^{3})}(V_{2}(\eta),V)).

(3.4)

Roughly speaking the support of
r_{m}
consists of points
\eta=(V_{1}(\eta),V_{2}(\eta))
such that
V
is not too close to
V_{2}(\eta)
; in particular,
V
is also not too close to
V_{1}(\eta)
and we have

d(V_{1}(\eta),V)\geqslant 1/|m|^{\epsilon_{2}}\text{ if $r_{m}(\eta)>0$}.

(3.5)

Our idea to localize
\varphi
is to locally identify
\varphi
with smooth real-valued functions
\varphi_{j}
on
\mathcal{F}
such that
\varphi=\varphi_{j}\ \mathrm{mod}\ \mathbb{Z}
. Then the value of the oscillatory integral (i.e.
Eq.

E.3
) remains the same.
Here is how we proceed: take smooth functions
p_{1}
and
p_{2}
on
\mathbb{R}/\mathbb{Z}
as a partition of unity with respect to open intervals
d(z,\mathbb{Z})<1/3
and
d(z,\frac{1}{2}+\mathbb{Z})<1/3
. Let
r_{m,j}(\eta)=r_{m}(\eta)p_{j}(\pi_{V^{\perp}}(V_{1}(\eta)))
with
\eta=(V_{1}(\eta),V_{2}(\eta))
, for
j=1,2
.
Then on a neighbourhood of the support of
r_{m,j}
, the map
\varphi
takes value in a proper subset of
\mathbb{R}/\mathbb{Z}
which can be identified with a subset of
\mathbb{R}
continuously. Therefore on the neighbourhood of the support of
r_{m,j}
,
\varphi
can be identified with smooth real-valued lifted functions
\varphi_{j}
, without changing the integral.

Step 2: Verify
E.3
.

Our plan is to apply
E.2
to
\varphi_{j}
.
Since both
\varphi_{j}
are functions lifted from
\mathbb{P}(\mathbb{R}^{3})
, we only need to verify the conditions in
E.3
. A remarkable fact for
\mathcal{F}
is that for any
\eta\in\mathcal{F}
, its coordinate provides not only its projection
V_{1}(\eta)
to
\mathbb{P}(\mathbb{R}^{3})
, but also an important tangent direction
V_{\alpha_{2}}\subset T_{\eta}\mathcal{F}
(see
Appendix

E
).

1.

(Verify
Eq.

E.4
and
Eq.

E.7
with
C=64m^{4\epsilon_{2}}
). Using the spherical law of sines, we have

\displaystyle\partial_{1}\varphi_{j}(\eta)=\lim_{t\rightarrow 0}\frac{\varphi_{j}(V_{1})-\varphi_{j}(y(t))}{t}=\lim_{t\rightarrow 0}\frac{\angle_{V}(V_{1},y(t))}{d_{R}(V_{1},y(t))}=\lim_{t\rightarrow 0}\frac{\sin(\angle_{V}(V_{1},y(t)))}{d(V_{1},y(t))}=\lim_{t\rightarrow 0}\frac{\sin(\angle_{V_{1}}(V,y(t)))}{d(y(t),V)},

where
y(t)
is a smooth curve on
\mathbb{P}(\mathbb{R}^{3})
which is contained in
V_{2}
with
d_{R}(V_{1},y(t))=t
. The angle
\angle_{V_{1}}(V,y(t))
is equal to angle between two hyperplane
V_{2}=V_{1}\oplus y(t)
and
V_{1}\oplus V
, so

\displaystyle\partial_{1}\varphi_{j}(\eta)=\frac{d(V_{2},(V_{1}\oplus V))}{d(V_{1},V)}=\frac{d(V_{2},V)}{d(V_{1},V)^{2}},

where we use again spherical law of sines to obtain
d(V_{2},(V_{1}\oplus V))=d(V_{2},V)/d(V_{1},V)
6
6

6

The Hausdorff distance
d(V_{2},(V_{1}\oplus V))
equals
\sin(\angle_{V_{1}}(V,y(t)))
. Let
V^{\prime}
be the point in
V_{2}
such that
d(V,V^{\prime})=d(V,V_{2})
. Then
\angle_{V^{\prime}}(V,V_{1})=\pi/2
. Hence by spherical law of sines,
\sin(\angle_{V_{1}}(V,y(t)))/d(V,V_{2})=\sin(\angle_{V_{1}}(V,y(t)))/d(V,V^{\prime})=\sin(\pi/2)/d(V,V_{1})
.
for the last equality. By the choice of
r_{m,j}
, on its support, due to
Eq.

3.4
and
Eq.

3.5
, we have (See
Fig.

6
)

\partial_{1}\varphi_{j}(\eta)\in[1/|m|^{\epsilon_{2}},|m|^{2\epsilon_{2}}],

which implies
Eq.

E.4
and
Eq.

E.7
with
C=64m^{4\epsilon_{2}}
.

Figure 6:
\alpha_{1}
-direction

2.

(Verify
Eq.

E.5
).
Eq.

E.5
can be obtained directly from
B.2
.

3.

(Verify
Eq.

E.6
). We have

\partial_{1}\varphi_{j}(\eta)-\partial_{1}\varphi_{j}(\eta^{\prime})=\frac{d(V_{2},V)}{d(V_{1},V)^{2}}-\frac{d(V_{2}^{\prime},V)}{d(V_{1}^{\prime},V)^{2}}=\frac{d(V_{2},V)d(V_{1}^{\prime},V)^{2}-d(V_{2}^{\prime},V)d(V_{1},V)^{2}}{d(V_{1},V)^{2}d(V_{1}^{\prime},V)^{2}}.

where
V_{i}^{\prime}:=V_{i}(\eta^{\prime})
.
Due to the triangle inequality, the numerator is less than

|(d(V_{2},V)-d(V_{2}^{\prime},V))d(V_{1}^{\prime},V)^{2}+d(V_{2}^{\prime},V)(d(V_{1}^{\prime},V)^{2}-d(V_{1},V)^{2})|\leqslant 2(d(V_{2},V_{2}^{\prime})+d(V_{1},V_{1}^{\prime})).

Due to
Eq.

3.5
, for
\eta,\eta^{\prime}
in the
1/C
-neighborhood of the support of
r
, the denominator is greater than
1/(2|m|^{\epsilon_{2}})^{4}
. Hence, we have

|\partial_{1}\varphi_{j}(\eta)-\partial_{1}\varphi_{j}(\eta^{\prime})|\leqslant 64|m|^{4\epsilon_{2}}d(\eta,\eta^{\prime})

which is
Eq.

E.6
, where
d(\eta,\eta^{\prime})
is defined in
Eq.

3.1
.

4.

(Estimate the Lipschitz constants of
r_{m,j}
).
Recall a basic estimate of the Lipschitz constant of a product of two Lipchitz continuous functions
f,g
:

\Lip(fg)\leqslant\Lip(f)|g|_{\infty}+|f|_{\infty}\sup_{\eta\neq\eta^{\prime},\eta,\eta^{\prime}\in\supp f}\frac{|g(\eta)-g(\eta^{\prime})|}{d(\eta,\eta^{\prime})}.

By this estimate and (2) in
B.2
,

\displaystyle\Lip(r_{m,j})

\displaystyle\leqslant\Lip(r_{m})+\sup_{\eta\neq\eta^{\prime},\eta,\eta^{\prime}\in\supp r_{m}}\frac{|p_{j}(\pi_{V^{\perp}}(V_{1}(\eta)))-p_{j}(\pi_{V^{\perp}}(V_{1}(\eta^{\prime})))|}{d(\eta,\eta^{\prime})}

\displaystyle\leqslant|m|^{\epsilon_{2}}+\Lip(p_{j})|m|^{\epsilon_{2}}\leqslant 4|m|^{\epsilon_{2}}.

Step 3: Estimate of
\widehat{\pi_{V^{\perp}}\mu}(m)
.

From Step 2, each
\varphi_{j}
is
(m^{\epsilon_{0}},r_{m,j})
-good (due to the choice of
\epsilon_{2}
). Then for each
j
and all
m
with
|m|
large enough, by Theorem
E.2
we have

|\int e^{2\pi im\varphi_{j}(\eta)}r_{m,j}(\eta)\mathrm{d}\mu_{\mathcal{F}}(\eta)|\leqslant|m|^{-\epsilon_{1}},

where
\mu_{\mathcal{F}}
is the
\nu
-stationary measure on
\mathcal{F}
, whose projection on
\mathbb{P}(\mathbb{R}^{3})
is
\mu
.
Therefore, combined with
2.23
and
Eq.

3.5
, we obtain

\displaystyle|\widehat{\pi_{V^{\perp}}\mu}(m)|

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle|\int e^{2\pi im\varphi(\eta)}\mathrm{d}\mu_{\mathcal{F}}(\eta)|

\displaystyle\leqslant

\displaystyle\left|\int e^{2\pi im\varphi(\eta)}(r_{m,1}(\eta)+r_{m,2}(\eta))\mathrm{d}\mu_{\mathcal{F}}(\eta)\right|+\left|\int e^{2\pi im\varphi(\eta)}(1-r_{m,1}(\eta)-r_{m,2}(\eta))\mathrm{d}\mu_{\mathcal{F}}(\eta)\right|

\displaystyle\leqslant

\displaystyle\sum_{j}\left|\int e^{2\pi im\varphi_{j}(\eta)}r_{m,j}(\eta)\mathrm{d}\mu_{\mathcal{F}}(\eta)\right|+\mu_{\mathcal{F}}(\{r_{m}\neq 1\})

\displaystyle\leqslant

\displaystyle\sum_{j}\left|\int e^{2\pi im\varphi_{j}(\eta)}r_{m,j}(\eta)\mathrm{d}\mu_{\mathcal{F}}(\eta)\right|+\mu(\{V_{2}(\eta):d(V_{2}(\eta),V)<1/|m|^{\epsilon_{2}}\})\ll|m|^{-\beta},

for some
\beta>0
and uniform for all
V
. The proof is complete.
∎

Proposition 3.5
.

For any
\delta>0
, there exist
l,k_{0}\in\mathbb{N}
such that for any
V\in\mathbb{P}(\mathbb{R}^{3})
, any
k\geqslant k_{0}
and
g\in\mathrm{SL}_{2}(\mathbb{R})
, we have

g\pi_{V^{\perp}}\mu\left(\bigcup_{0\leqslant n<q^{k}}B(nq^{-(k+[\chi_{1}(g)])},q^{-(k+[\chi_{1}(g)]+l)})\right)\leqslant\delta.

(3.6)

Proof.

Abbreviate
\pi_{V^{\perp}}\mu
by
\tau
. It follows from
2.23
that there exists
s\in(0,1)
such that
\tau(b(g^{-},s)^{c})<\delta/10
and
s
only depends on
\mu
and
\delta
. We decompose the measure
\tau
as follows:

\tau=\lambda\cdot\tau_{b(g^{-},s)^{c}}+(1-\lambda)\cdot\tau_{b(g^{-},s)},

where
\lambda=\tau(b(g^{-},s)^{c})
. We denote
\tau_{b(g^{-},s)^{c}}
by
\tau_{I}
to simply to notation.
Set

t=[\chi_{1}(g)-2|\log s|-1]=[\chi_{1}(g)+2\log s-1].

Let
S_{t}:\mathbb{R}\to\mathbb{R},x\mapsto q^{t}x
be the scaling map given as in
2.19
.
2.4
gives that
gb(g^{-},s)\subset B(g^{+},q^{-\chi_{1}(g)}/s^{2})
.
If
t
is a non-negative integer, then
S_{t}
induces a map
\mathbb{R}/\mathbb{Z}\to\mathbb{R}/\mathbb{Z}
, which we still denote by
S_{t}
by abusing the notation.
2.4
gives that
gb(g^{-},s)\subset B(g^{+},q^{-\chi_{1}(g)}/s^{2})
. Hence the restriction of
S_{t}
on
gb(g^{-},s)
is a diffeomorphism. If
t
is negative, then
S_{t}
is a contracting map. Identifying
\mathbb{R}/\mathbb{Z}
(hence
\mathbb{P}(V^{\perp})
) with
[-\frac{1}{2},\frac{1}{2})
such that
V^{+}_{g}=0
, we have that
gb(g^{-},s)\subset(-\frac{1}{2},\frac{1}{2})
is an interval containing
0
. Hence
S_{t}
restricted on
gb(g^{-},s)
is also a diffeomorphism to its image in
gb(g^{-},s)
.

We have

\displaystyle g\tau_{\mathbf{I}}\left(\cup_{n}B\left(\frac{n}{q^{[\chi_{1}(g)]+k}},\frac{1}{q^{[\chi_{1}(g)]+k+\ell}}\right)\right)

\displaystyle=S_{t}(g\tau_{\mathbf{I}})\left(S_{t}(\cup_{n}B\left(\frac{n}{q^{[\chi_{1}(g)]+k}},\frac{1}{q^{[\chi_{1}(g)]+k+\ell}})\right)\right)

\displaystyle=S_{t}g\tau_{\mathbf{I}}\left(\cup_{n}B\left(\frac{n}{q^{[\chi_{1}(g)]+k-t}},\frac{1}{q^{[\chi_{1}(g)]+k-t+\ell}}\right)\right),

where we only sum up
n
’s such that the interval
B\left(\frac{n}{q^{[\chi_{1}(g)]+k}},\frac{1}{q^{[\chi_{1}(g)]+k+\ell}}\right)
intersects the support of
\tau_{\mathbf{I}}
.

We use
2.8
to obtain distortion estimates.
For
y\in b(g^{-},s)
, we have

|(S_{t}g)^{\prime}y|=q^{t}|g^{\prime}y|\in[s^{2}/q,1/q],|(S_{t}g)^{\prime\prime}(y)|=q^{t}|g^{\prime\prime}(y)|\leqslant 10/(qs).

(3.7)

Let
p_{1}
be a cuffoff function which equals
1
on
b(g^{-},s)
and
0
outside
b(g^{-},s/2)
. Let
p_{1}\tau
be the measure on
\mathbb{P}(V^{\perp})
defined by
p_{1}\tau(f)=\int p_{1}\cdot fd\tau
for any measurable function
f
on
\mathbb{P}(V^{\perp})
. Consider the map
\psi:\mathbb{P}(\mathbb{R}^{3})-\{V\}\to\mathbb{P}(V^{\perp})
defined by

\psi(x)=(S_{t}g)\pi_{V^{\perp}}(x)=S_{t}g\varphi(x)

with
\varphi
given as in
Eq.

3.3
. Then
\psi
can be lifted to a function defined on
\mathcal{F}-\{\eta=(V_{1}(\eta),V_{2}(\eta)):V_{1}(\eta)\neq V\}
.

Take any integer
m
with
|m|^{\epsilon_{2}}>4
, where
\epsilon_{2}=\epsilon_{0}/8
with
\epsilon_{0}
given as in
E.2
. Let
r_{m,1}(\eta)=r_{m}(\eta)p_{1}(\pi_{V^{\perp}}(\eta))
where
\eta=(V_{1}(\eta),V_{2}(\eta))
and
r_{m}
is defined as in
Eq.

3.4
.
Here is the key observation: since
S_{t}g
on
b(g^{-},s)
is a bi-Lipschitz map to its image with bounded
C^{2}
-norm (
Eq.

3.7
), if
\varphi
is
(C,r_{m,1})
-good as in
E.3
, then
(S_{t}g)\varphi
is
(C^{\prime},r_{m,1})
-good with
C^{\prime}=(10q/s)^{2}C
.
As
s
is fixed, we can verify as in
3.4
that
\varphi
is
(64m^{\epsilon_{0}/2},r_{m,1})
-good and hence
\psi=(S_{t}g)\varphi
is
(m^{\epsilon_{0}},r_{m,1})
-good for all integer
m
with
|m|
sufficiently large. Then the same argument as in
3.4
yields that for all integer
m
with
|m|
large,

|\widehat{S_{t}g(p_{1}\tau)}(m)|\leqslant|\int e^{2\pi im\psi(\eta)}r_{m,1}(\eta)\mathrm{d}\mu_{\mathcal{F}}(\eta)|+\mu_{\mathcal{F}}(\{r_{m}=1\}^{c})\ll m^{-\beta}.

3.2
implies that there exist
l,k_{0}\in\mathbb{N}
(depending on
\delta
) such that for all
k\in N
with
[\chi_{1}(g)]+k-t>k_{0}
,

\displaystyle S_{t}g\tau_{\mathbf{I}}\left(\cup_{n}B\left(\frac{n}{q^{[\chi_{1}(g)]+k-t}},\frac{1}{q^{[\chi_{1}(g)]+k-t+\ell}}\right)\right)

\displaystyle\leqslant

\displaystyle\frac{1}{1-\lambda}S_{t}g(p_{1}\tau)\left(\cup_{n}B\left(\frac{n}{q^{[\chi_{1}(g)]+k-t}},\frac{1}{q^{[\chi_{1}(g)]+k-t+\ell}}\right)\right)<\delta/2.

Note that
g\tau=(1-\lambda)\cdot g\tau_{\mathbf{I}}+\lambda\cdot g\tau_{b(g^{-},s)^{c}}
with
\lambda<\delta/10
. The proof is complete.
∎

4
Convolution and porosity

Definition 4.1
.

Let
\alpha,\epsilon>0
and
m,i_{1},i_{2}\in\mathbb{N}
with
i_{1}\leqslant i_{2}
. We call a probability measure
\tau
on
\mathbb{R}
or
\mathbb{P}(\mathbb{R}^{2})
is
(\alpha,\epsilon,m)
-
entropy porous
from scale
i_{1}
to
i_{2}
if

\mathbb{P}_{i_{1}\leqslant j\leqslant i_{2}}\left\{\frac{1}{m}H(\tau_{x,j},\mathcal{Q}_{m+j})<\alpha+\epsilon\right\}>1-\epsilon.

The main result of this part is the entropy porosity of projections of stationary measures, which is a key ingredient in the entropy growth argument.

Proposition 4.2
.

For every
\epsilon>0,\,\,m\geqslant M(\epsilon),\,\,k\geqslant K(\epsilon,m),\,\,n\geqslant N(\epsilon,m,k)
,

\inf_{V\in\mathbb{P}(\mathbb{R}^{3})}\mathbb{P}_{1\leqslant i\leqslant n}\left\{\pi_{V^{\perp}}g_{{\mathbf{I}}(i)}\mu\text{ is }(\alpha,\epsilon,m)\text{-entropy porous from scale $i$ to $i+k$}\right\}>1-\epsilon.

(4.1)

4.1
General results on entropy porosity

In this section, we list some general lemmas in
[
BHR19
, Section 3.1]
which show that if a measure
\tau
decomposes as a convex combination of measures
\tau_{i}
whose support has a short length, then many properties of the
\tau_{i}
, and specifically their entropies, are inherited by the
q
-adic components of
\tau
.

In the following lemma, one fixes a scale
k
, a short scale
k+l_{0}
for the length of the support of the measures
\tau_{i}
which make up
\tau
, and an even shorter scale
k+m
at which the entropy appears. The dependence of the parameters is that
l_{0}
is large depending on
\tau
and
\delta
,
m\gg l_{0}
, and
k
is arbitrary.

Lemma 4.3
.

For every
\epsilon>0
, there exists
\delta>0
with the following property. Let
\tau\in\mathbf{P}(\mathbb{P}(\mathbb{R}^{2}))
be written as a convex combination
\tau=(\sum_{i=1}^{N}p_{i}\tau_{i})+p_{0}\tau_{0}
, with
p_{0}<\delta
, and suppose that for
l_{0}=l_{0}(\tau,\delta)
, some
m,k\in\mathbb{N}
and
\alpha>0
,

(1)

\frac{1}{m}H(\tau_{i},\mathcal{Q}_{k+m})>\alpha
for every
i=1,...,N
.

(2)

\diam(\supp\tau_{i})\leqslant q^{-(k+l_{0})}
for every
i=1,\ldots,N
.

(3)

\tau\left(\bigcup_{n}B\left(\frac{n}{q^{k}},\frac{1}{q^{k+l_{0}}}\right)\right)\leqslant\delta.

Then

\mathbb{P}_{j=k}\left(\frac{1}{m}H(\tau_{x,j},\mathcal{Q}_{j+m})>\alpha-\epsilon\right)>1-\epsilon.

We view
\tau_{0}
and
\tau_{i}
with
1\leqslant i\leqslant N
as the
bad
part and the
good
part of
\tau
respectively, see
4.6
in the next subsection.
4.3
can be proved as in
[
BHR19
, Lemma 3.5]
. We replace UCAS condition (
[
BHR19
, Definition 3.11]
) by the current weaker condition
(3)
(
3.1
), which is sufficient for the proof.

As in
[
BHR19
, Lemma 3.7]
, we use the above lemma to deduce the entropy porosity.

Lemma 4.4
.

For every
\epsilon>0
, there exists
\delta>0
with the following property. Let
m,l\in\mathbb{N}
and
n>n(m,l)
be given, and suppose that
\tau\in\mathbf{P}(\mathbb{P}(\mathbb{R}^{2}))
is a measure such that for
(1-\delta)
-fraction of
1\leqslant k\leqslant n
, we can write
\tau=\sum p_{i}\tau_{i}
so as to satisfy the conditions of the previous lemma for the given
\delta
,
l_{0}
,
m
and
k
. Assume further that

|\frac{1}{n}H(\tau,\mathcal{Q}_{n})-\alpha|<\delta.

Then
\tau
is
(\alpha,\epsilon,m)
-entropy porous from scale
1
to
n
.

Lemma 4.5
.

For every
\epsilon>0
, there exists
\delta>0
with the following property. Let
\tau\in\mathbf{P}(\mathbb{P}(\mathbb{R}^{2}))
be written as a convex combination
\tau=(\sum_{1\leqslant i\leqslant N}p_{i}\tau_{i})+p_{0}\tau_{0}
, with
p_{0}<\delta
, and suppose that for
l_{1}=l_{1}(\tau,\delta)
, some
k,p\in\mathbb{N},\,\,m>m(\epsilon,p)
and
\beta>0
,

(1)

\frac{1}{m}H(\tau_{i},\mathcal{Q}_{k+m})<\beta
for every
i=1,\ldots,N
.

(2)

\diam\supp\tau_{i}\leqslant q^{-(k+l_{1})}
for every
i=1,\ldots,N
.

(3)

\tau\left(\bigcup_{n}B\left(\frac{n}{q^{k}},\frac{1}{q^{k+l_{1}}}\right)\right)\leqslant\delta.

(4)

Every interval of length
q^{-k}
intersects the support of at most
p
of the measures
\tau_{i}
.

Then

\mathbb{P}_{j=k}\left\{\frac{1}{m}H(\tau_{x,j},\mathcal{Q}_{j+m})<\beta+\epsilon\right\}>1-\epsilon.

The proof is the same as that of
[
BHR19
, Lemma 3.9]
, where they used almost convexity of entropy to replace the concavity of entropy in the proof of
[
BHR19
, Lemma 3.5]
. Notice that condition
(3)
is changed. See the discussion below
4.3
.

4.2
Decomposition of a general measure

4.3
motivates the following decomposition of measures for the projective action on
\mathbb{P}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
. This decomposition is essential because the projective action is more subtle: it only contracts outside a neighbourhood of a hyperplane (
2.4
). This decomposition allows us to estimate the entropy of measures that are pushed forward by the projective action.

Definition 4.6
.

Let
\tau
be a probability measure on a metric space
X
. For
s,\delta>0
, a convex decomposition
\tau=\theta\tau_{\mathbf{II}}+(1-\theta)\tau_{\mathbf{I}}
is called an
(s,\delta)
-decomposition
of
\tau
, if

\theta\leqslant\delta
and
\mathrm{diam}(\supp\tau_{\mathbf{I}})\leqslant s
. We call
\tau_{\mathbf{I}}
and
\tau_{\mathbf{II}}
the
good
part and the
bad
part of
\tau
, respectively.

Moreover, if the good part
\tau_{\mathbf{I}}
satisfies
\frac{1}{m}H(\tau_{\mathbf{I}},\mathcal{Q}_{m+l})>\alpha
, then we call the decomposition an
(s,\delta)
-decomposition with
\alpha
-entropy concentration at scale
(m,l)
.

We will be most interested in the following decomposition coming from the action of
\mathrm{SL}_{d}(\mathbb{R})
on
\mathbb{P}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
.

Definition 4.7
.

Let
d\geqslant 2
. Let
h\in\mathrm{GL}_{d}(\mathbb{R})
be such that
\sigma_{1}(h)>\sigma_{2}(h)
and
\tau
be a probability measure on
\mathbb{P}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
. The
r
-attracting decomposition of the pair
(h,\tau)
is defined as follows:

{h}\tau=\theta\cdot{h}(\tau_{b(h^{-},r)^{c}})+(1-\theta)\cdot{h}(\tau_{b(h^{-},r)}),

(4.2)

where
\theta=\tau(b(h^{-},r)^{c})
.
We call
{h}(\tau_{b(h^{-},r)^{c}})
(resp.
{h}(\tau_{b(h^{-},r)})
) the
r
-repelling part
(resp.
the
r
-attracting part
) of

(h,\tau)
.

We need a lemma of a more precise estimate of the support.

Lemma 4.8
.

For any
h\in\mathrm{SL}_{2}^{\pm}(\mathbb{R})
with
\sigma_{1}(h)>\sigma_{2}(h)
and any
r\in[q^{-\chi_{1}(h)},1/10)
, we have

B(h^{+},q^{-\chi_{1}(h)}/2r)\subset\supp\ h(\tau_{b(h^{-},r)})\subset B(h^{+},q^{-\chi_{1}(h)}/r^{2}).

(4.3)

Proof.

Due to
2.4
, we obtain the second relation.

From some elementary computations, we can obtain a lower bound of the support. Without loss of generality, we may assume
h=\mathrm{diag}(q^{\chi_{1}(h)/2},q^{-\chi_{1}(h)/2})
. In view of
2.2
, by a straightforward computation, we have that the diameter of
hb(h^{-},r)
is bounded above by
\sin\left(\arctan\left(\frac{q^{-\chi_{1}(h)}}{\tan(\arcsin(r))}\right)\right)
.
∎

The following lemma links the decomposition given in
4.6
and the one given in
4.7
.

Lemma 4.9
.

Let
\tau
be a probability measure on
\mathbb{P}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
. Suppose there exist
C,\beta>0
such that for any
R\geqslant 0
and hyperplane
W
in
\mathbb{P}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
, we have

\tau(B(W,R))\leqslant CR^{\beta}.

(4.4)

Then for any
g\in\mathrm{SL}_{d}(\mathbb{R})
and
0<r\leqslant 1/2
, the
r
-attracting decomposition of
(g,\tau)
is an
(q^{-\chi_{1}(g)}/r^{2},Cr^{\beta})
-decomposition of the pushforward measure
g\tau
.

Proof.

We have
\diam(B(g^{+},q^{-\chi_{1}(g)}/r^{2}))\geqslant\diam(gb(g^{-},r))
(see
2.4
). And by
Eq.

4.4
, we have
\tau(b(g^{-},r)^{c})\leqslant Cr^{\beta}
.
∎

Lemma 4.10
.

Let
g\in\mathrm{SL}_{2}(\mathbb{R})
and
\tau
be a probability measure that satisfies the assumption of

4.9
.
For any
\epsilon>0,\,\,0<r<r(\epsilon)<\frac{1}{2}
and
m\geqslant M(\epsilon,r)
, if
\frac{1}{m}H(\tau,\mathcal{Q}_{m})\geqslant\alpha
, then the
r
-attracting decomposition of
(g,\tau)
is a
(q^{-\chi_{1}(g)}/r^{2},Cr^{\beta})
-decomposition of
g\tau
with
(\alpha-\epsilon)
-entropy concentration at scale
(m,l)
with
l=\chi_{1}(g)
.

Proof.

We use (
2.79
) to obtain

\displaystyle\frac{1}{m}H\left(\tau_{b(g^{-},r)},\mathcal{Q}_{m}\right)

\displaystyle\geqslant\frac{1}{1-\theta}\left(\frac{1}{m}H(\tau,\mathcal{Q}_{m})-\frac{1}{m}H(\theta)-\frac{\theta}{m}H\left(\tau_{b(g^{-},r)^{c}},\mathcal{Q}_{m}\right)\right)

\displaystyle\geqslant\frac{1}{1-\theta}\left(\frac{1}{m}H(\tau,\mathcal{Q}_{m})-\frac{1}{m}H(\theta)-\theta\right).

with
\theta<Cr^{\beta}
by the previous lemma.
Due to
2.8
and
2.34
, we have

\displaystyle\frac{1}{m}H\left(g(\tau_{b(g^{-},r)}),\mathcal{Q}_{m+\chi_{1}(g)}\right)=\frac{1}{m}H\left(\tau_{b(g^{-},r)},\mathcal{Q}_{m}\right)+\frac{1}{m}O(|\log r|).

Combining the above two formulas, we finish the proof by taking
Cr^{\beta}
(hence
\theta
) sufficiently small with respect to
\epsilon
and
m
large with respect to
|\log r|
and
\epsilon
.
∎

4.3
Keeping porosity under projective transformation

Let
\mu
be the
\nu
-stationary measure on
\mathbb{P}(\mathbb{R}^{3})
given as in
Section

2.7
.
In this section, we show the entropy properties of
\pi_{V^{\perp}}\mu
are inherited by the pushforward measure
g\pi_{V^{\perp}}\mu
with
V\in\mathbb{P}(\mathbb{R}^{3})
and
g\in\mathrm{SL}_{2}^{\pm}(\mathbb{R})
, which is a projective version of
[
BHR19
, Lemma 3.10]
.

Proposition 4.11
.

For any
\epsilon>0
, there exists
\epsilon^{\prime}>0
such that for any
m\geqslant m(\epsilon),\,\,\,n\geqslant n(m,\epsilon)
the following holds. For any
n_{1}\in\mathbb{N}
and
V\in\mathbb{P}(\mathbb{R}^{3})
,
if the measure
\pi_{V^{\perp}}\mu
is
(\alpha,\epsilon^{\prime},m)
-entropy porous from scale
n_{1}
to
n_{2}=n_{1}+n
, then for any
g\in\mathrm{SL}_{2}(\mathbb{R})^{\pm}
,
g\pi_{V^{\perp}}\mu
is
(\alpha,\epsilon,m)
-entropy porous from scale
n_{1}+\chi_{1}(g)
to
n_{2}+\chi_{1}(g)
.

Proof.

Abbreviate
\pi_{V^{\perp}}\mu
by
\tau
.
Fix
\epsilon>0
and
g\in\operatorname{SL}_{2}^{\pm}(\mathbb{R})
.
Without loss of generality, we can assume
\epsilon
small such that
\epsilon^{2}<\frac{\epsilon}{100}
. Recall the uniform Hölder constant
C>0
and Hölder exponent of
\beta>0
given in
2.23
.

We fix a few constants:

•

let
\delta>0
be the constant given in
4.5
for
\epsilon^{2}
;

•

let
l_{0},\,k_{0}\in\mathbb{N}
be the constants
l,k_{0}
given in
3.5
with parameter
\delta/10
;

•

fix an
l\gg l_{0}
such that
q^{-(l-l_{0})}\leqslant r^{2}
with
r=(\delta/10C)^{1/\beta}/4
.

These constants actually depend only on
\epsilon
. We will determine the constants
m,n\in\mathbb{N}
later.

We will prove the proposition for
\epsilon^{\prime}=\min\{\epsilon^{4},\frac{\delta^{2}}{10}\}
.
Assume that
\tau
is
(\alpha,\epsilon^{\prime},m)
-entropy porous from scale
n_{1}
to
n_{2}
. By Markov inequality, there are at least
(1-\sqrt{\epsilon^{\prime}})
fraction of levels
n_{1}\leqslant j\leqslant n_{2}
such that

\mathbb{P}\left\{\frac{1}{m}H(\tau_{j,x},\mathcal{Q}_{j+m})<\alpha+\epsilon^{\prime}\right\}>1-\sqrt{\epsilon^{\prime}}.

By taking
n
sufficiently large, which depends on
k_{0},l,\epsilon^{\prime}
, and hence only depends on
\epsilon
, we have that for at least
(1-2\sqrt{\epsilon^{\prime}})
fraction of levels
n_{1}\leqslant j+l\leqslant n_{2}
such that

j\geqslant k_{0}\,\,\,\text{and}\,\,\,\mathbb{P}\left\{\frac{1}{m}H(\tau_{j+l,x},\mathcal{Q}_{j+l+m})<\alpha+\epsilon^{\prime}\right\}>1-\sqrt{\epsilon^{\prime}}.

(4.5)

We call
j

typical
if
j
satisfies both inequalities in
Eq.

4.5
.

Recall
r=(\delta/10C)^{1/\beta}/4
. Applying
2.23
to
\tau
, we have for any
x\in\mathbb{P}(\mathbb{R}^{2})
,

\tau(B(x,4r))\leqslant\frac{\delta}{10}.

In particular, we have

\tau(b(g^{-},4r)^{c})\leqslant\frac{\delta}{10}.

We have
q^{-(j+l)}\leqslant q^{-(l-l_{0})}\leqslant r
, hence for typical
j
, we have

\mathbb{P}\left\{\begin{array}[]{l}x:\frac{1}{m}H(\tau_{j+l,x},\mathcal{Q}_{m+j+l})<\alpha+\epsilon^{\prime},\\
\operatorname{supp}(\tau_{j+l,x})\subset b(g^{-},r)\end{array}\right\}>1-\sqrt{\epsilon^{\prime}}-\frac{\delta}{10}.

(4.6)

We consider the action of
g
on
b(g^{-},r)
. By
2.8
, it scales by
q^{-\chi_{1}(g)}
with distortion
r^{-2}
. Therefore for typical
j
and for
x
in the event given in
Eq.

4.6
,
2.34
yields

\frac{1}{m}H(g(\tau_{j+l,x}),\mathcal{Q}_{m+j+l+\chi_{1}(g)})<\alpha+\epsilon^{\prime}+\frac{O(|\log r|)}{m}\leqslant\alpha+2\epsilon^{\prime}.

(4.7)

The second inequality holds by taking
m
sufficiently large, depending on
r,\epsilon^{\prime}
and hence only depending on
\epsilon
. Combining
Eqs.

4.6
and
4.7
, we have

\mathbb{P}\left\{\begin{array}[]{l}x:\frac{1}{m}H(g(\tau_{j+l,x}),\mathcal{Q}_{m+j+l+\chi_{1}(g)})<\alpha+2\epsilon^{\prime},\\
\operatorname{supp}(\tau_{j+l,x})\subset b(g^{-},r)\end{array}\right\}>1-\sqrt{\epsilon^{\prime}}-\frac{\delta}{10}>1-\frac{\delta}{2},

(4.8)

where the second inequality is due to
\epsilon^{\prime}\leqslant\delta^{2}/10
.
We take out all
\tau_{j+l,x}
in the event in (
4.8
). There are finitely many different measures
\tau_{j+l,x}
, and we denote these measures by
\tau_{1},\ldots,\tau_{N}
and
\theta_{i}=\tau(\operatorname{supp}\tau_{i})
for
1\leqslant i\leqslant N
. We will apply Lemma
4.5
to
g\tau
in the level
\chi_{1}(g)+j
. The convex combination is given by

g\tau=\mathbb{E}g\tau_{j+l,x}=\sum_{1\leqslant i\leqslant N}\theta_{i}\cdot g\tau_{i}+\theta_{0}\cdot g\tau_{0},

where
\tau_{0}
is the convex combinations of
\tau_{j+l,x}
not in the event in (
4.8
). We have
\theta_{0}<\delta/2
.
We check the conditions of Lemma
4.5
:

1.

\frac{1}{m}H(g\tau_{i},\mathcal{Q}_{m+j+\chi_{1}(g)})\leqslant\frac{1}{m}H(g\tau_{i},\mathcal{Q}_{m+j+l+\chi_{1}(g)})<\alpha+2\epsilon^{\prime}<\alpha+\epsilon^{2}
for
1\leqslant i\leqslant N
;

2.

\mathrm{diam}(\supp(g(\tau_{i})))<q^{-\chi_{1}(g)-l_{0}-j}
for
1\leqslant i\leqslant N
. Here we use the fact that the distortion of
g
on
b(g^{-},r)
is
r^{-2}
with
q^{-(l-l_{0})}\leqslant r^{2}
;

3.

g\tau\left(\cup_{n}B\left(\frac{n}{q^{[\chi_{1}(g)]+j}},\frac{1}{q^{[\chi_{1}(g)]+j+l_{0}}}\right)\right)<\delta/10
. This is due to
3.5
and
typical
j\geqslant k_{0}
;

4.

Each interval of length
q^{-[\chi_{1}(g)]-j}
intersects at most
p=q^{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0}l}}
of the support of
g\tau_{i}
, thanks to
\diam(g\tau_{i})\geqslant q^{-\chi_{1}(g)-j-l}
.

Therefore by our choice of
\delta
, it follows from Lemma
4.5
that for typical
j
, for
m\geqslant m(\epsilon,q^{l})
(hence only depends on
\epsilon
),

\mathbb{P}\{\frac{1}{m}H((g\tau)_{j+\chi_{1}(g),x},\mathcal{Q}_{j+\chi_{1}(g)+m})\leqslant\alpha+2\epsilon^{\prime}\}>1-\epsilon^{2}.

Taking account of non-typical
j
, we get that

\mathbb{P}_{n_{1}\leqslant j\leqslant n_{2}}\{\frac{1}{m}H((g\tau)_{j+\chi_{1}(g),x},\mathcal{Q}_{j+\chi_{1}(g)+m})\leqslant\alpha+2\epsilon^{\prime}\}>(1-\epsilon^{2})(1-2\sqrt{\epsilon^{\prime}}).

By our choice of
\epsilon^{\prime}
, we have that
\alpha+2\epsilon^{\prime}\leqslant\alpha+\epsilon
,
(1-\epsilon^{2})(1-2\sqrt{\epsilon^{\prime}})>1-\epsilon
. Therefore,
g\tau
is
(\alpha,\epsilon,m)
-entropy porous from scale
n_{1}+\chi_{1}(g)
to
n_{2}+\chi_{1}(g)
.
∎

4.4
Decomposition of random projection measures

Let’s recall the decomposition given in
Eq.

2.5
: for any
\spadesuit\in\{{\mathbf{U}}(n),{\mathbf{I}}(i)\}
and
V\in\mathbb{P}(\mathbb{R}^{3})
, if
g_{\spadesuit}^{-1}V\not\subset V^{\perp}
, then we have

\displaystyle{}\pi_{V^{\perp}}\circ g_{\spadesuit}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle{}h_{V,g_{\spadesuit}}\circ\pi_{g^{-1}_{\spadesuit}V,V^{\perp}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle{}h_{V,g_{\spadesuit}}\circ\pi(g^{-1}_{\spadesuit}V,V^{\perp},(g^{-1}_{\spadesuit}V)^{\perp})\circ\pi_{(g^{-1}_{\spadesuit}V)^{\perp}},

(4.9)

where
h_{V,g_{\spadesuit}}
is a projective transformation of
\mathbb{P}(V^{\perp})
and we regard it as an element in
\mathrm{SL}_{2}^{\pm}(\mathbb{R})
by identifying
\mathbb{P}(V^{\perp})
with
\mathbb{P}(\mathbb{R}^{2})
. To simply the notations, we will denote
h_{V,g_{\spadesuit}}
by
h_{V,\spadesuit}
and
\pi_{g^{-1}_{\spadesuit}V,V^{\perp}}
by
\pi_{V,\spadesuit}
in the followings.

The focus of this subsection is to study the decomposition of a random projection measure
\pi_{V,\spadesuit}\mu
defined as in
4.6
and
4.7
. The following lemma is a corollary of the large deviation theorem of random walks on projective space.

Lemma 4.12
.

For every
\epsilon>0
, there exist
C_{1}=C_{1}(\epsilon),N_{1}=N_{1}(\epsilon)\geqslant 1
such that for
n\geqslant N_{1}
,
V\in\mathbb{P}(\mathbb{R}^{3})

\mathbb{P}\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}&{\mathbf{U}}(n):d(g_{\mathbf{U}(n)}^{-1}V,V^{\perp})>1/C_{1},\\
&|\chi_{1}(h_{V,{\mathbf{U}}(n)})-\chi_{1}(g_{{\mathbf{U}}(n)})|\leqslant\log C_{1}\end{array}\right\}>1-\epsilon.

(4.10)

Similarly, we have a corresponding inequality for
\mathbf{I}(i)

\inf_{V\in\mathbb{P}(\mathbb{R}^{3})}\mathbb{P}_{1\leqslant i\leqslant n}\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}&{\mathbf{I}}(i):d(g_{\mathbf{I}(i)}^{-1}V,V^{\perp})>1/C_{1}\\
&|\chi_{1}(h_{V,{\mathbf{I}}(i)})-\chi_{1}(g_{{\mathbf{I}}(i)})|\leqslant\log C_{1}\end{array}\right\}>1-\epsilon.

(4.11)

Proof.

Recall the relation between the convolution measure
\nu^{*n}
and the random walk
{\mathbf{U}}(n)

Eq.

2.73
. The first line for the statement of
{\mathbf{U}}(n)
is due to
Eq.

2.58
. The second line is obtained by applying
Eq.

2.59
to the random walk, which is the conjugation of
\nu
by
k\in\mathrm{SO}(3)
with
kV=E_{1}
.

Then the statement of
{\mathbf{I}}(n)
follows from
2.32
and the statement
Eq.

4.10
for
{\mathbf{U}}(n)
with
\epsilon=\epsilon/C_{I}
.
∎

To simplify the notation we denote by
X_{\mathbf{U},n,V}=X_{\mathbf{U},n,V}(C_{1},\epsilon)
the set of
{\mathbf{U}}(n)
considered in (
4.10
). Then we have the following property for
X_{\mathbf{U},n,V}
.

Lemma 4.13
.

For any
\epsilon>0
and
\delta>0
, let
C_{1}=C_{1}(\epsilon)\geqslant 1
and
N_{1}=N_{1}(\epsilon)
be the constants given in
4.12
.
Then there exist constants
r=r(\epsilon,C_{1},\delta)
and
C_{2}=C_{2}(C_{1},r)
such that for any
V\in\mathbb{P}(\mathbb{R}^{3})
and any
n\geqslant N_{1}
, if
\mathbf{U}(n)\in X_{\mathbf{U},n,V}
, then the
r
-attracting decomposition of

(h_{V,{\mathbf{U}}(n)},\pi_{V,\mathbf{U}(n)}\mu)
enjoys the following properties:

1.

it is a
(C_{2}\cdot q^{-\chi_{1}(g_{{\mathbf{U}}(n)})},\delta)
-decomposition of
\pi_{V^{\perp}}{g}_{\mathbf{U}(n)}\mu
.

2.

for any
m\geqslant M_{1}(\epsilon,C_{1},r)
, if

\frac{1}{m}H(\pi_{(g^{-1}_{\mathbf{U}(n)}V)^{\perp}}\mu,\mathcal{Q}_{m})\geqslant\alpha,

then the decomposition is with
(\alpha-\epsilon)
-entropy concentration at the scale
(m,\chi_{1}(g_{{\mathbf{U}}(n)}))
.

Proof.

Recall the decomposition of
\pi_{V^{\perp}}\circ g_{{\mathbf{U}}(n)}
in
Section

4.4
.

Due to
Eq.

4.10
and
2.24
, the measure
\pi_{V,{\mathbf{U}}(n)}\mu
is uniform Hölder regular: for any
x\in\mathbb{P}(V^{\perp})
and any
R>0
, we have

(\pi_{V,{\mathbf{U}}(n)}\mu)(B(x,R))\leqslant CC_{1}^{\beta}R^{\beta}.

Applying
4.9
to the measure
\pi_{V,{\mathbf{U}}(n)}\mu
, we have for any
0<r\leqslant\frac{1}{2}
, the
r
-attracting decomposition
(h_{V,{\mathbf{U}}(n)},\pi_{V,\mathbf{U}(n)}\mu)
is a
(q^{-\chi_{1}(h_{V,{\mathbf{U}}(n)})}/r^{2},CC_{1}^{\beta}r^{\beta})
-decomposition of
\pi_{V^{\perp}}{g}_{\mathbf{U}(n)}\mu
. We choose
r
such that
CC_{1}^{\beta}r^{\beta}\leqslant\delta
and
and replace
\chi_{1}(h_{V,{\mathbf{U}}(n)})
by
\chi_{1}(g_{{\mathbf{U}}(n)})
with bounded loss which is due to
Eq.

4.10
. Therefore, the decomposition is a
(C_{2}\cdot q^{-\chi_{1}(g_{{\mathbf{U}}(n)})},\delta)
-decompostion of
\pi_{V,{\mathbf{U}}(n)}\mu
with
C_{2}=C_{2}(C_{1},r)
.

For the second statement, due to
Eq.

4.10
and
B.1
, the map
\pi(g^{-1}_{{\mathbf{U}}(n)}V,V^{\perp},(g^{-1}_{{\mathbf{U}}(n)}V)^{\perp})
has scale
1
with distortion
C_{1}
. Hence, by
2.34
, we obtain

\frac{1}{m}H(\pi_{V,{\mathbf{U}}(n)}\mu,\mathcal{Q}_{m})\geqslant\alpha-O(\frac{\log C_{1}}{m}).

By taking
m
large, we can use
4.10
to obtain the entropy concentration at scale
(m,\chi_{1}(h_{V,{\mathbf{U}}(n)}))
and use
|\chi_{1}(h_{V,{\mathbf{U}}(n)})-\chi_{1}(g_{{\mathbf{U}}(n)})|\leqslant\log C_{1}
to change the scale and conclude.
∎

Now we show that the good part of projections of typical cylinders has high entropy at smaller scales, i.e. the good part version of Lemma 3.15 of
[
BHR19
]
.

Lemma 4.14
.

For any
\epsilon>0
and
\delta>0
, there exist
r_{0}=r_{0}(\epsilon,\delta),\ C_{0}=C_{0}(\epsilon,\delta)\geqslant 1
such that for
m\geqslant M_{0}(\epsilon,C_{0})
,
n\geqslant N_{0}(\epsilon,m)
, we have

\inf_{V\in\mathbb{P}(\mathbb{R}^{3})}\mathbb{P}\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}&{\mathbf{U}}(n):\text{ the $r_{0}$-attracting decomposition of }(h_{V,{\mathbf{U}}(n)},\pi_{V,{\mathbf{U}}(n)}\mu)\\
&\text{is a}\,\,(C_{0}\cdot q^{-\chi(g_{{\mathbf{U}}(n)})},\delta)\text{-decomposition of }\pi_{V^{\perp}}g_{{\mathbf{U}}(n)}\mu\\
&\text{with }(\alpha-\epsilon)\text{-entropy concentration at the scale}\,\,(m,\chi_{1}(g_{{\mathbf{U}}(n)}))\end{array}\right\}>1-\epsilon.

Proof.

To prove
4.14
, it suffices to estimate the probability of
{\mathbf{U}}(n)
satisfying the conditions in
4.13
.
We choose
\epsilon_{0},\delta_{0}
much smaller than given
\epsilon,\delta
. By
4.12
, there exist
C_{1}(\epsilon_{0})
and
N_{1}(\epsilon_{0})
such that for any
n\geqslant N_{1}(\epsilon_{0})
, the probability of
\mathbf{U}(n)
belonging to the set
X_{\mathbf{U},n,V}=X_{\mathbf{U},n,V}(C_{1}(\epsilon_{0}),\epsilon_{0})
, which is the probability of
{\mathbf{U}}(n)
satisfying the conditions in (
4.10
), is greater than
1-\epsilon_{0}
.

Moreover, Lemma
2.38
yields that for any
m\geqslant M_{2}(\epsilon_{0})
and any
n\geqslant N_{2}(\epsilon_{0},m)
, the probability of
{\mathbf{U}}(n)
satisfying

\frac{1}{m}H(\pi_{(g^{-1}_{\mathbf{U}(n)}V)^{\perp}}\mu,\mathcal{Q}_{m})\geqslant\alpha-\epsilon_{0}

(4.12)

is greater than
1-\epsilon_{0}
.
Consequently, letting
r=r(\epsilon_{0},C_{1},\delta_{0})
be the constant given in
4.13
,
for any
m\geqslant\max\{M_{1}(\epsilon_{0},C_{1},r),M_{2}(\epsilon_{0})\}
and any
n\geqslant\max\{N_{1}(\epsilon_{0}),N_{2}(\epsilon_{0},m)\}
, the probability of
{\mathbf{U}}(n)
satisfying the conditions of
4.13
is greater than
1-2\epsilon_{0}
.
∎

We can make a similar statement for
\pi_{V^{\perp}}g_{\mathbf{I}(n)}\mu
,

Lemma 4.15
.

For any
\epsilon>0
and
\delta>0
, there exist
r_{1}=r(\epsilon,\delta),C_{1}=C_{1}(\epsilon,\delta)\geqslant 1
such that for
m\geqslant M_{1}(\epsilon,C_{1})
,
n\geqslant N_{1}(\epsilon,m)
, we have

\inf_{V\in\mathbb{P}(\mathbb{R}^{3})}\mathbb{P}_{1\leqslant i\leqslant n}\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}&{\mathbf{I}}(i):\text{ the $r_{1}$-attracting decomposition of}\,\,(h_{V,{\mathbf{I}}(i)},\pi_{V,{\mathbf{I}}(i)}\mu)\\
&\text{is a }(C_{1}\cdot q^{-i},\delta)\text{-decomposition of }\pi_{V^{\perp}}g_{\mathbf{I}(i)}\mu\\
&\text{with }(\alpha-\epsilon)\text{-entropy concentration at the scale}\,\,(m,i)\end{array}\right\}>1-\epsilon.

(4.13)

Proof.

Recall
C_{I}>1
from
2.32
. Given any
\epsilon>0
and
\delta>0
, let
\epsilon_{0}=\epsilon/C_{I}
,
r_{0}=r_{0}(\epsilon_{0}/2,\delta)
,
C_{0}=C_{0}(\epsilon_{0}/2,\delta)
and
M_{0}(\epsilon_{0}/2,C_{0})
be the constants given in
4.14
. For any
m\geqslant M_{0}
and any
V\in\mathbb{P}(\mathbb{R}^{3})
, we define the subset
\mathcal{U}_{V}
of the set of words
\Lambda^{\ast}
as follows:

\mathcal{U}_{V}:=\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}&\mathbf{i}\in\Lambda^{\ast}:\text{ the $r_{0}$-attracting decomposition of }(h_{V,\mathbf{i}},\pi_{V,\mathbf{i}}\mu)\\
&\text{is a }(C_{0}\cdot q^{-\chi_{1}(g_{\mathbf{i}})},\delta)\text{-decomposition of }\pi_{V^{\perp}}g_{\mathbf{i}}\mu\\
&\text{with $(\alpha-\epsilon_{0}/2)$-entropy concentration at the scale }\,\,(m,{\chi_{1}(g_{\mathbf{i}})})\end{array}\right\}.

By
4.14
, for any
n\geqslant N_{0}(\epsilon_{0}/2,m)
, we have

\inf_{V\in\mathbb{P}(\mathbb{R}^{3})}\mathbb{P}\{{\mathbf{U}}(n):{\mathbf{U}}(n)\in\mathcal{U}_{V}\}>1-\epsilon_{0}/2.

Hence, it follows that for any sufficiently large
n
depending on
\epsilon_{0}
,

\inf_{V\in\mathbb{P}(\mathbb{R}^{3})}\mathbb{P}_{1\leqslant i\leqslant n}\{{\mathbf{U}}(i):{\mathbf{U}}(i)\in\mathcal{U}_{V}\}>1-\epsilon_{0}.

Lemma
2.32
implies for any
n\geqslant N(\epsilon_{0},m)
,

\inf_{V\in\mathbb{P}(\mathbb{R}^{3})}\mathbb{P}_{1\leqslant i\leqslant n}\{{\mathbf{I}}(i):{\mathbf{I}}(i)\in\mathcal{U}_{V}\}>1-C_{I}\epsilon_{0}=1-\epsilon.

For any
V\in\mathbb{P}(\mathbb{R}^{3})
and any
1\leqslant i\leqslant n
, if
{\mathbf{I}}(i)\in\mathcal{U}_{V}
, we actually have that the
r_{0}
-attracting decomposition of
(h_{V,{\mathbf{I}}(i)},\pi_{V,{\mathbf{I}}(i)}\mu)
is a
(C_{1}\cdot q^{-i},\delta)
-decomposition of
\pi_{V^{\perp}}g_{{\mathbf{I}}(i)}\mu
with
(\alpha-\epsilon_{0})
-entropy concentration at the scale
(m,i)
. Here is how we obtain the last statement: Due to
Eq.

2.70
, we replace

C_{0}\cdot q^{-\chi_{1}(g_{{\mathbf{I}}(i)})}

by
C_{1}\cdot q^{-i}
, and replace the scale

(m,\chi_{1}(g_{{\mathbf{I}}(i)}))
by
(m,i)
by choosing
m
large enough if necessary.
∎

In the later proof, we need an immediate corollary of
4.15
, which relaxes the condition on the parameter
r
.

Lemma 4.16
.

For any
\epsilon>0
and
\delta>0
, there exist
r_{1}=r(\epsilon,\delta),C_{1}=C_{1}(\epsilon,\delta)\geqslant 1
such that for
m\geqslant M_{1}(\epsilon,C_{1})
,
n\geqslant N_{1}(\epsilon,m)
and
r<r_{1}
, we have

\inf_{V\in\mathbb{P}(\mathbb{R}^{3})}\mathbb{P}_{1\leqslant i\leqslant n}\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}&{\mathbf{I}}(i):\text{ the $r$-attracting part $\mathfrak{m}_{r}$ of}\,\,(h_{V,{\mathbf{I}}(i)},\pi_{V,{\mathbf{I}}(i)}\mu)\\
&\text{satisfies }\frac{1}{m}H(\mathfrak{m}_{r},\mathcal{Q}_{m+i})\geqslant\alpha-\epsilon-2\delta\end{array}\right\}>1-\epsilon.

(4.14)

Proof.

We have the following general result.

Claim
.

Let
\beta,\delta\in(0,1)
and
m,i\in\mathbb{N}
. Let
\tau_{1},\tau_{2}\in\mathbf{P}(\mathbb{P}(\mathbb{R}^{2}))
such that
\tau_{1}
is a restriction of
\tau_{2}
with
\tau_{2}((\supp\tau_{1})^{c})\leqslant\delta
.
If the measure
\tau_{1}
satisfies

\frac{1}{m}H(\tau_{1},\mathcal{Q}_{m+i})\geqslant\beta,

then

\frac{1}{m}H(\tau_{2},\mathcal{Q}_{m+i})\geqslant\beta-\delta.

Due to the hypothesis, we can write
\tau_{2}=(1-\eta)\tau_{1}+\eta\tau^{\prime}
with
\eta\leqslant\delta
. Then the claim follows directly from the concavity of entropy
Eq.

2.79
.

For each
{\mathbf{I}}(i)
belonging to the set in the formula
Eq.

4.13
,
we consider
\tau_{1}=\mathfrak{m}_{r_{1}}
(the
r_{1}
-attracting part) and
\tau_{2}=\mathfrak{m}_{r}
. Due to
r\leqslant r_{1}
, the measure
\tau_{1}
is a restriction of
\tau_{2}
. Since the
r_{1}
-attracting decomposition of
\,\,(h_{V,{\mathbf{I}}(i)},\pi_{V,{\mathbf{I}}(i)}\mu)\text{is an }(C_{1}\cdot q^{-i},\delta)\text{-decomposition of }\pi_{V^{\perp}}g_{\mathbf{I}(i)}\mu
, we have
\tau_{2}((\supp\tau_{1})^{c})\leqslant\frac{\tau(b(h^{-},r)^{c})}{\tau(b(h^{-},r_{1}))}\leqslant\frac{\delta}{(1-\delta)}\leqslant 2\delta
. As the
r_{1}
-decomposition has
(\alpha-\epsilon)
-entropy concentration, that is
\frac{1}{m}H(\tau_{1},\mathcal{Q}_{m+i})\geqslant\alpha-\epsilon
, we can apply the claim to
\tau_{1}
and
\tau_{2}
to finish the proof.
∎

4.5
Proof of
4.2

We are ready to prove
4.2
.
In the following proposition, we fix a point
V\in\mathbb{P}(\mathbb{R}^{3})
such that the projection measure
\pi_{V^{\perp}}\mu
has entropy dimension
\alpha
.

Proposition 4.17
.

For any
\epsilon>0
,
m\geqslant M(\epsilon)
and
n\geqslant N(\epsilon,m,V)
, the projection measure
\pi_{V^{\perp}}\mu
is
(\alpha,\epsilon,m)
-entropy porous from scale
1
to
n
.

Proof.

Write
\tau=\pi_{V^{\perp}}\mu
. Let
\epsilon>0
be as given. We fix a few other constants:

•

let
\delta^{\prime}(\epsilon/2)
be the constant given in
4.3
for
\epsilon/2
and
\delta^{\prime\prime}(\epsilon)
be the one given in
4.4
for
\epsilon
. Fix
\delta<\min\{\delta^{\prime}(\epsilon/2),\delta^{\prime\prime}(\epsilon)\}
.

•

choose constants
\epsilon_{0},\delta_{0}
much smaller than
\epsilon,\delta
respectively.

•

let
C_{1}=C_{1}(\epsilon_{0},\delta_{0})
be the constant given in
4.15
. By Proposition
3.4
, there exist
l_{0}=l_{0}(\delta_{0}),k_{0}
which are independent of
V
such that for any
k\geqslant k_{0}
large we have

\tau\left(\cup_{n}B\left(\frac{n}{q^{k}},\frac{1}{q^{k+l_{0}}}\right)\right)<\delta_{0}.

(4.15)

We fix an
l
such that
C_{1}\cdot q^{-l}\leqslant q^{-l_{0}}
.

We want to apply
4.4
to
\tau
. We first construct convex combinations of
\tau
which satisfy the conditions in
4.3
.

By
4.15
, for
m\geqslant M_{1}(\epsilon_{0},C_{1})
,
n\geqslant N_{1}(\epsilon_{0},m)
, we have

\mathbb{P}_{1\leqslant i\leqslant n}\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}&{\mathbf{I}}(i+l):\pi_{V^{\perp}}g_{\mathbf{I}(i+l)}\mu\,\,\text{has a}\,\,(C_{1}\cdot q^{-(i+l)},\delta_{0})\text{-decomposition }\\
&\text{with $(\alpha-\epsilon_{0})$-entropy concentration at the scale }(m,i+l)\end{array}\right\}>1-2\epsilon_{0}.

(4.16)

We replace the index
i
in
Eq.

4.13
by
i+l
in
Eq.

4.16
. This is possible by taking
n
large enough.
Applying Markov’s inequality, we find that for at least a
(1-\sqrt{2\epsilon_{0}})
-fraction of levels
1\leqslant k\leqslant n
, we have

\mathbb{P}_{i=k}\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}&{\mathbf{I}}(i+l):\pi_{V^{\perp}}g_{\mathbf{I}(i+l)}\mu\,\,\text{has a}\,\,(C_{1}\cdot q^{-(i+l)},\delta_{0})\text{-decomposition}\\
&\text{with $(\alpha-\epsilon_{0})$-entropy concentration at the scale }(m,i+l)\end{array}\right\}>1-\sqrt{2\epsilon_{0}}.

(4.17)

Since we can take
n
arbitrarily large, we can assume for at least a
(1-2\sqrt{\epsilon_{0}})
fraction of levels
1\leqslant k\leqslant n
satisfies both (
4.17
) and (
4.15
). In the following discussion, we fix any such
k
.

Note that we have
\tau=\mathbb{E}_{i=k}(\pi_{V^{\perp}}g_{{\mathbf{I}}(i+l)}\mu)
due to
Eq.

2.74
. As a result, we can use
Eq.

4.17
to construct a convex combination of
\tau
:

\tau=\sum_{1\leqslant j\leqslant N}p_{j}\tau_{j}+p_{0}\tau_{0},

where for
1\leqslant j\leqslant N
,
\tau_{j}
comes from the good part of
\pi_{V^{\perp}}g_{{\mathbf{I}}(k+l)}\mu
with
{\mathbf{I}}(k+l)
belonging to the event in
Eq.

4.17
;
\tau_{0}
is the sum of the bad parts of
\pi_{V^{\perp}}g_{{\mathbf{I}}(k+l)}\mu
with
{\mathbf{I}}(k+l)
belonging to the event in
Eq.

4.17
and those
\pi_{V^{\perp}}g_{{\mathbf{I}}(k+l)}\mu
with
{\mathbf{I}}(k+l)
not belonging to the event in
Eq.

4.17
. This construction gives
p_{0}\leqslant\sqrt{2\epsilon_{0}}+\delta_{0}
.
We continue to check that this convex combination satisfies the conditions of
4.3
:

1.

for
1\leqslant j\leqslant N
, due to
\tau_{j}
the good part of
\pi_{V^{\perp}}g_{{\mathbf{I}}(k+l)}\mu
with
{\mathbf{I}}(k+l)
in
Eq.

4.17
, we have

\frac{1}{m}H(\tau_{j},\mathcal{Q}_{k+m})\geqslant\frac{1}{m}H(\tau_{j},\mathcal{Q}_{k+l+m})-O(\frac{l}{m})\geqslant\alpha-2\epsilon_{0},

where the second inequality is possible by taking
m
large enough depending on
l,\epsilon_{0}
.

2.

for
1\leqslant j\leqslant N
, we have
\mathrm{diam}(\supp\tau_{j})\leqslant C_{1}\cdot q^{-(k+l)}\leqslant q^{-(k+l_{0})}
.

3.

\tau\left(\cup_{n}B\left(\frac{n}{q^{k}},\frac{1}{q^{k+l_{0}}}\right)\right)<\delta_{0}
by
Eq.

4.15
.

We can choose
\epsilon_{0},\delta_{0}
such that
\sqrt{2\epsilon_{0}}+\delta_{0}<\delta
and
\epsilon_{0}<\frac{\epsilon}{2}
, etc. Then
4.3
implies that

\mathbb{P}_{i=k}\left\{\frac{1}{m}H(\tau_{x,i},\mathcal{Q}_{i+m})>\alpha-\epsilon\right\}>1-\epsilon.

Since
\tau
has entropy dimension
\alpha
, we can take
n
large such that
|\frac{1}{n}H(\tau,\mathcal{Q}_{n})-\alpha|<\delta
(in view of
2.37
, here is the only place that
n
depends on
V
). Therefore, we can apply
4.4
to
\tau
and conclude that for any large
n
,
\tau
is
(\alpha,\epsilon,m)
-entropy porous from scale
1
to
n
.
∎

Lemma
2.37
states that there exists a
\mu^{-}
-full measure set
E_{0}
such that for every
V\in E_{0}
,
\pi_{V^{\perp}}\mu
has entropy dimension
\alpha
. Hence
4.17
holds for every
V\in E_{0}
.
In the following corollary, we get rid of the dependency of
n
on
V\in\mathbb{P}(\mathbb{R}^{3})
.

Corollary 4.18
.

For every
\epsilon>0,m>M_{1}(\epsilon)
and
n>N_{1}(\epsilon,m)
, there exists an open set
E=E(\epsilon,m,n)\subset\mathbb{P}(\mathbb{R}^{3})
of measure
\mu^{-}(E)>1-\epsilon
such that for every
V\in E
, the projection measure
\pi_{V^{\perp}}\mu
is
(\alpha,\epsilon,m)
-entropy porous from scale
1
to
n
.

Recall that in
A.1
,we let
\mathcal{C}=\mathbb{P}(\mathbb{R}^{3})-\mathbb{P}(E_{1}^{\perp})
. For each
V\in\mathcal{C}
, the identification between
\mathbb{P}(V^{\perp})
and
\mathbb{P}(\mathbb{R}^{2})
, denoted by
\mathcal{I}_{V}
, is to identify
\pi_{V^{\perp}}V_{0}
with
\mathbb{R}(1,0)
.
The maps
\mathcal{I}_{V}
are continuous with respect to
V\in\mathcal{C}
, which is part of the reason why we can obtain
4.18
.
The proof of
4.18
relies on
A.2
and
A.3
.

Proof of
4.18
.

Let
m>M_{1}(\epsilon)=\max\{M(\epsilon),M_{2}(\epsilon)\}
with
M(\epsilon)
and
M_{2}(\epsilon)
given as in
4.17
and
A.3
respectively. For
n>N(\epsilon,m)
sufficiently large,
the existence of measurable set
E_{0}(\epsilon,m,n)
satisfying
4.18
is a direct consequence of
4.17
. It remains to show that we can find an open set
E(\epsilon,m,n)
with large measure.

Let
V\in E_{0}
. We find a small open neighborhood
B_{\epsilon,m,n}(V)
of
V
in
\mathbb{P}(\mathbb{R}^{3})
that has the following property: for any
W\in B_{\epsilon,m,n}(V)
, identifying
\mathbb{P}(W^{\perp})
with
\mathbb{P}(\mathbb{R}^{2})
by
\mathcal{I}_{W}
, we have any scale
1\leqslant i\leqslant n
, any
x\in\mathbb{P}(\mathbb{R}^{2})
and any sub-interval
I
of
\mathcal{Q}_{i+m}
,

|(\pi_{V^{\perp}}\mu)_{x,i}(I)-(\pi_{W^{\perp}}\mu)_{x,i}(I)|<q^{-3m}

(4.18)

The existence of
B_{\epsilon,m,n}(V)
is guaranteed by
A.2
. We continue to use Lemma
A.3
to obtain that for any component measure
(\pi_{V^{\perp}}\mu)_{x,i}
, if

\frac{1}{m}H((\pi_{V^{\perp}}\mu)_{x,i},\mathcal{Q}_{i+m})<\alpha+\epsilon
, then
\frac{1}{m}H((\pi_{W^{\perp}}\mu)_{x,i},\mathcal{Q}_{i+m})<\alpha+2\epsilon
for any
W\in B_{\epsilon,m,n}(V)
. Based on these, as
\pi_{V^{\perp}}\mu
is
(\alpha,\epsilon,m)
-entropy porous from scale
1
to
n
,
\pi_{W^{\perp}}\mu
is also
(\alpha,2\epsilon,m)
-entropy porous from scale
1
to
n
for any
W\in B_{\epsilon,m,n}(V)
.

As a result, for each
V\in E_{0}
, we can find an open neighbourhood of
V
such that the entropy porosity still holds. Take the union as the set
E(\epsilon,m,n)
.
∎

We are ready to prove the main proposition of this section.

Proof of
4.2
.

Let
\epsilon>0
be as given.
Recall the decompositions given in
Section

4.4
for any
\spadesuit\in\{{\mathbf{U}}(n),{\mathbf{I}}(i)\}
and
V\in\mathbb{P}(\mathbb{R}^{3})
such that
g^{-1}_{\spadesuit}V\notin V^{\perp}
.

Let
\epsilon_{0}=\min\{\epsilon^{\prime}(\epsilon/10),\epsilon/(2C_{I}+1)\}
, where
\epsilon^{\prime}(\epsilon/10)
is the constant given in
4.11
for
\epsilon/10
. For
m\geqslant M(\epsilon_{0})
and
k\geqslant K(m,\epsilon_{0})
, let
E(\epsilon_{0},m,k)
be the open set in
\mathbb{P}(\mathbb{R}^{3})
given in
4.18
. Using the equidistribution of
g_{{\mathbf{U}}(n)}^{-1}V
(
2.22
), we have that for any large
n
and every
V\in\mathbb{P}(\mathbb{R}^{3})
, we have

\mathbb{P}\{{\mathbf{U}}(n):g_{{\mathbf{U}}(n)}^{-1}V\in E(\epsilon_{0},m,k)\}>1-2\epsilon_{0}.

Using
4.18
and
2.32
, we have for any
n\geqslant N(\epsilon_{0},m,k)

\inf_{V\in\mathbb{P}(\mathbb{R}^{3})}\mathbb{P}_{1\leqslant i\leqslant n}\{{\mathbf{I}}(i):\pi_{(g_{{\mathbf{I}}(i)}^{-1}V)^{\perp}}\mu\,\,\text{is}\,\,(\alpha,\epsilon_{0},m)\text{-entropy porous from scale}\,\,1\,\,\text{to}\,\,k\}>1-2C_{I}\epsilon_{0}.

(4.19)

Meanwhile,
4.12
states that there exist
C_{1}(\epsilon_{0})
and
N_{1}(\epsilon_{0})\geqslant 1
such that for any
n\geqslant N_{1}(\epsilon_{0})
, we have

\inf_{V\in\mathbb{P}(\mathbb{R}^{3})}\mathbb{P}_{1\leqslant i\leqslant n}\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}&{\mathbf{I}}(i):d(g_{\mathbf{I}(i)}^{-1}V,V^{\perp})>1/C_{1}\\
&|\chi_{1}(h_{V,{\mathbf{I}}(i)})-\chi_{1}(g_{{\mathbf{I}}(i)})|\leqslant\log C_{1}\end{array}\right\}>1-\epsilon_{0}.

(4.20)

Eqs.

4.19
and
4.20
give that for any
n\geqslant\max\{N(\epsilon_{0},m,k),N_{1}(\epsilon_{0})\}
,

\inf_{V\in\mathbb{P}(\mathbb{R}^{3})}\mathbb{P}_{1\leqslant i\leqslant n}=\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}&{\mathbf{I}}(i):{\mathbf{I}}(i)\,\,\text{satisfies both the conditions in}\\
&\,\,\lx@cref{creftype\lx@tilde refnum}{eqn:proof prop 4.2 1}\,\,\text{and}\,\,\lx@cref{creftype\lx@tilde refnum}{eqn:proof prop 4.2 2}\end{array}\right\}>1-(2C_{I}+1)\epsilon_{0}.

To continue, we will use the decomposition

\pi_{V^{\perp}}g_{{\mathbf{I}}(i)}=h_{V,{\mathbf{I}}(i)}\circ\pi(g^{-1}_{{\mathbf{I}}(i)}V,V^{\perp},(g^{-1}_{{\mathbf{I}}(i)}V)^{\perp})\circ\pi_{(g_{{\mathbf{I}}(i)}^{-1}V)^{\perp}}.

Due to
\epsilon_{0}\leqslant\epsilon^{\prime}(\epsilon/10)
,
it follows from Proposition
4.11
that for
m\geqslant m(\epsilon^{\prime})
,
n\geqslant n(m,\epsilon^{\prime})
, any
V\in\mathbb{P}(\mathbb{R}^{3})
and any
\tilde{g}\in\mathrm{SL}_{2}(\mathbb{R})^{\pm}
, if
\pi_{(g^{-1}_{{\mathbf{I}}(i)}V)^{\perp}}\mu
is
(\alpha,\epsilon_{0},m)
-entropy porous from scale
1
to
k
, then
\tilde{g}\pi_{(g^{-1}_{{\mathbf{I}}(i)}V)^{\perp}}\mu
is

(\alpha,\epsilon/10,m)
-entropy porous from scale
\chi_{1}(\tilde{g})+1
to
\chi_{1}(\tilde{g})+k
. In particular, we consider the words
{\mathbf{I}}(i)
satisfying both the conditions in
Eq.

4.19
and
Eq.

4.20
and let

\tilde{g}=h_{V,{\mathbf{I}}(i)}\circ\pi(g^{-1}_{{\mathbf{I}}(i)}V,V^{\perp},(g^{-1}_{{\mathbf{I}}(i)}V)^{\perp}).

We get
\pi_{V^{\perp}}g_{{\mathbf{I}}(i)}\mu
is
(\alpha,\epsilon/10,m)
-entropy porous
from scale
\chi_{1}(\tilde{g})+1
to
\chi_{1}(\tilde{g})+k
from the decomposition of
\pi_{V^{\perp}}g_{{\mathbf{I}}(i)}\mu
.
To finish, we estimate
\chi_{1}(\tilde{g})=\chi_{1}(h_{V,{\mathbf{I}}(i)}\circ\pi(g^{-1}_{{\mathbf{I}}(i)}V,V^{\perp},(g^{-1}_{{\mathbf{I}}(i)}V)^{\perp}))
. Using
Eq.

2.70
,
Eq.

4.20
, and
B.1
, we have that
\chi_{1}(\tilde{g})
equals
i
, up to an additive constant
C_{2}=C_{2}(\epsilon_{0})
. Therefore,
\pi_{V^{\perp}}g_{{\mathbf{I}}(i)}\mu
is

(\alpha,\epsilon/10+O(C_{2}/k),m)
-entropy porous from scale
i
to
i+k
. By taking
k
large enough and due to
(2C_{I}+1)\epsilon_{0}\leqslant\epsilon
, we obtain the statement of
4.2
.
∎

5
Entropy growth

The main result of this section is
5.7
, which is a projective version of
[
BHR19
, Theorem 4.1]
. The main difficulty in the current setting comes from the non-uniform contraction and non-linearity of the projective action. To deal with the non-uniform contraction of the action, we rely on the decomposition introduced in
4.6
