, which makes it possible to analyze the change of the entropy. To deal with the non-linearity of the action, we repeatedly use
Eq.

2.5
.

5.1
Entropy growth under convolutions: Euclidean case

For
\alpha,\beta\in\mathbf{P}(\mathbb{R})
, we denote by
\alpha*\beta
the additive convolution of
\alpha,\beta
. For every bounded support
\alpha,\beta\in\mathbf{P}(\mathbb{R})
, we have the following lower bound from the concavity of entropy (cf.
[
Hoc14
, Corollary 4.10]
):

\frac{1}{m}H(\alpha*\beta,\mathcal{Q}_{m})\geqslant\frac{1}{m}H(\beta,\mathcal{Q}_{m})-O(\frac{1}{m}).

(5.1)

One may expect that
\frac{1}{m}H(\alpha*\beta,\mathcal{Q}_{m})
is close to
\frac{1}{m}H(\alpha,\mathcal{Q}_{m})+\frac{1}{m}H(\beta,\mathcal{Q}_{m})
, but in general this is not the case. The following modification of
[
BHR19
, Theorem 4.1]
shows a sufficient condition which implies the presence of non-trivial entropy growths. See also Theorem 2.8 in
[
Hoc14
]
.

Theorem 5.1
.

For every
\epsilon\in(0,\frac{1}{10})
, there exists
\delta>0
such that for
m\geqslant m(\epsilon,\delta)
,
C\geqslant 1
and
n>N(\epsilon,\delta,m)\log C
, the following holds.

Let
k\in\mathbb{N}
and
\theta,\tau\in\mathbf{P}(\mathbb{R})
, and suppose that

•

\theta
and
\tau
are supported on intervals of length less than
Cq^{-k}
,

•

\tau
is
\left(1-\frac{5\epsilon}{2},\frac{\epsilon}{2},m\right)
-entropy porous from scale
k
to
k+n
,

•

\frac{1}{n}H(\theta,\mathcal{Q}_{k+n})>3\epsilon
.

Then we obtain the growth of the entropy of scale
(k,n)
:

\frac{1}{n}H(\theta*\tau,\mathcal{Q}_{k+n})\geqslant\frac{1}{n}H(\tau,\mathcal{Q}_{k+n})+\delta.

Proof.

[
BHR19
, Theorem 4.1]
was stated for the case when
C=1
. Set
k^{\prime}=k-\log C
and
\epsilon^{\prime}=2\epsilon
. Choose
n
large enough so that
\frac{2\log C}{n}<\epsilon
. Then
k^{\prime}
and
\theta,\tau
satisfy the hypothesis
[
BHR19
, Theorem 4.1]
. More precisely, we have

•

\theta
and
\tau
are supported on intervals of length less than
q^{-k^{\prime}}
,

•

\tau
is
\left(1-\epsilon^{\prime},\frac{\epsilon^{\prime}}{2},m\right)
-entropy porous from scale
k^{\prime}
to
k^{\prime}+n
,

•

\frac{1}{n}H(\theta,\mathcal{Q}_{k^{\prime}+n})>\epsilon^{\prime}
.

Hence, there exists
\delta=\delta(\epsilon^{\prime})>0
such that

\frac{1}{n}H(\theta*\tau,\mathcal{Q}_{k^{\prime}+n})\geqslant\frac{1}{n}H(\tau,\mathcal{Q}_{k^{\prime}+n})+\delta.

It follows that

\frac{1}{n}H(\theta*\tau,\mathcal{Q}_{k+n})\geqslant\frac{1}{n}H(\theta*\tau,\mathcal{Q}_{k^{\prime}+n})\geqslant\frac{1}{n}H(\tau,\mathcal{Q}_{k^{\prime}+n})+\delta\geqslant\frac{1}{n}H(\tau,\mathcal{Q}_{k+n})+\delta-\frac{\log C}{n}.

By taking
n
large enough, we obtain the result.
∎

5.2
Convolution of measures on
\mathbb{P}(\mathbb{R}^{3})
and measures on
L
: preparation

Recall the subgroup
L=L_{E_{1}}\simeq\mathrm{SL}_{2}(\mathbb{R})^{\pm}\ltimes\mathbb{R}^{2}
in
\mathrm{SL}_{3}(\mathbb{R})
defined in the
UL
-decomposition for
E_{1}\in\mathbb{P}(\mathbb{R}^{3})
(see
Section

2.3
). We are interested in the maps
\pi_{E_{1}^{\perp}}\ell
from
\mathbb{P}(\mathbb{R}^{3})
to
\mathbb{P}(\mathbb{R}^{2})
with
\ell\in L
. Note that for each
\ell\in L
,
\pi_{E_{1}^{\perp}}\ell
is not defined at
\ell^{-1}E_{1}
.
We define a bad locus of
L\times\mathbb{P}(\mathbb{R}^{3})
by

\mathcal{B}:=\{(\ell,\ell^{-1}E_{1}):\ell\in L\},

(5.2)

which is a closed subset of
L\times\mathbb{P}(\mathbb{R}^{3})
.

Let
\theta\in\mathbf{P}(L)
. Let
\tau\in\mathbf{P}(\mathbb{P}(\mathbb{R}^{3}))
be atom-free (for example the stationary measure
\mu
on
\mathbb{P}(\mathbb{R}^{3})
is atom-free).
Let

[\theta.\tau]
be the projection of the convolution measure on
\mathbb{P}(\mathbb{R}^{2})
. More precisely, let
[\ell x]=\pi_{E_{1}^{\perp}}\ell x
, then the measure
[\theta.\tau]
is defined as follows: for any continuous function
f
on
\mathbb{P}(\mathbb{R}^{2})
,

\int_{\mathbb{P}(\mathbb{R}^{2})}f(y)\mathrm{d}[\theta.\tau](y):=\int_{\mathbb{P}(\mathbb{R}^{3})}\int_{L}f([\ell x])\mathrm{d}\theta(\ell)\mathrm{d}\tau(x).

Since we suppose that
\tau
is atom-free, we have
\theta\otimes\tau(\mathcal{B})=0
. The convolution is well-defined.

5.2.1
Linearization of projection of convolution measures

We first need a linearization lemma. Due to non-linearity, for later application of the entropy growth argument, we need to locally replace convolution in the sense of
L
-action by the additive convolution on
\mathbb{R}
, without large cost on entropy.

Identifying
\mathbb{P}(\mathbb{R}^{2})
with
\mathbb{R}/\mathbb{Z}
, for
v\in\mathbb{P}(\mathbb{R}^{2})
, let
T_{v}
be the translation on
\mathbb{P}(\mathbb{R}^{2})
given by
T_{v}(x)=x-v
.
For any
\theta\in\mathbf{P}(L)
and any
x_{0}\in\mathbb{P}(\mathbb{R}^{3})
, we set
[\theta.x_{0}]:=[\theta.\delta_{x_{0}}]
, which is the projection of the convolution of
\theta
with Dirac measure
\delta_{x_{0}}
.

Lemma 5.2

(Linearization of measures)
.

For any
\epsilon>0
,
0<r<1/2
,
C_{1}>20
,
k>K(\epsilon)
,
t\geqslant 1
, we have the followings. Let
\ell_{0}\in L(t,C_{1})
(
\subset L
defined in
Eq.

2.68
).
For any
x_{0}\in b(f_{\ell_{0}},r/2)
, any
\rho<q^{-k}(r/C_{1})^{8}
, any measure
\theta\in\mathbf{P}(L)
with
\supp\theta\subset B(\ell_{0},\rho)
and
\tau\in\mathbf{P}(\mathbb{P}(\mathbb{R}^{3}))
with
\supp\tau\subset B(x_{0},\rho)\subset b(f_{\ell_{0}},r)
, we have

\displaystyle\left|\frac{1}{k}H([\theta.\tau],\mathcal{Q}_{k+t-\log\rho})-\frac{1}{k}H\left((S_{t(\ell_{0},x_{0})}T_{[\ell_{0}(x_{0})]}[\theta.x_{0}])*(\pi_{(\ell_{0}^{-1}E_{1})^{\perp}}\tau),\mathcal{Q}_{k-\log\rho}\right)\right|

(5.3)

\displaystyle<

\displaystyle\epsilon+\frac{4\log(C_{1}/r)+O(1)}{k},

with
t(\ell_{0},x_{0})
the number given as in
2.20
and

|t(\ell_{0},x_{0})-t|\leqslant 8\log(C_{1}/r).

Proof.

Let
C_{2}=1/r
and
y=\pi_{(\ell_{0}^{-1}E_{1})^{\perp}}x_{0}
. Write
\ell_{0}=\begin{pmatrix}\det h_{0}&0\\
n_{0}&h_{0}\end{pmatrix}\in L
.

Under the assumption of this lemma, the condition of
2.20
is satisfied. Using
2.20
and the definition of the set
L(t,C_{1})
, we obtain

|t(\ell_{0},x_{0})-t|\leqslant|t(\ell_{0},x_{0})-2\log\|h_{0}\||+|2\log\|h_{0}\|-t|\leqslant 8\log(C_{1}/r).

Step 1.
We first prove

|\frac{1}{k}H([\theta.\tau],\mathcal{Q}_{k+t-\log\rho})-\frac{1}{k}H([\theta.x_{0}]*(S_{-t(\ell_{0},x_{0})}T_{y}\pi_{(\ell_{0}^{-1}E_{1})^{\perp}}\tau),\mathcal{Q}_{k+t-\log\rho})|<\epsilon.

Let
f,h_{z_{0}}
with
z_{0}=(\ell_{0},x_{0})
be two maps from
L\times\mathbb{P}(\mathbb{R}^{3})-\mathcal{B}
to
\mathbb{P}(\mathbb{R}^{2})
given by

f(\ell,x)=[\ell(x)],\ h_{z_{0}}(\ell,x)=[\ell(x_{0})]+S_{-t(\ell_{0},x_{0})}(\pi_{(\ell_{0}^{-1}E_{1})^{\perp}}x-y).

The push-forward measure
h_{z_{0}}(\ell,x)(\theta,\tau)
on
\mathbb{P}(\mathbb{R}^{2})

is exactly the convolution measure
[\theta.x_{0}]*(S_{-t(\ell_{0},x_{0})}T_{y}\pi_{(\ell_{0}^{-1}E_{1})^{\perp}}\tau)
.

Due to
2.20
and
\rho\leqslant q^{-k}(r/C_{1})^{8}
, we have

\left\|(f-h_{z_{0}})|_{B(\ell_{0},\rho)\times B(x_{0},\rho)}\right\|\leqslant 2C_{L}(C_{1}/r)^{8}\rho^{2}\|h_{0}\|^{-2}\leqslant 2C_{L}C_{p}^{2}\rho q^{-k-t}.

Here
C_{L},C_{p}
are large fixed constants and we take
K(\epsilon)\gg\log(C_{L}C_{p}^{2})
.
Using the continuity of entropy

Eq.

2.78
and
Eq.

2.77
, we conclude Step 1.

Step 2.

Consider the following measure on
\mathbb{P}(\mathbb{R}^{2})

\nu=(T_{[\ell_{0}(x_{0})]}[\theta.x_{0}])*S_{-t(\ell_{0},x_{0})}T_{y}(\pi_{(\ell_{0}^{-1}E_{1})^{\perp}}\tau).

Observe that the support of
[\theta.x_{0}]
is near
[\ell_{0}(x_{0})]
. So the support of
T_{[\ell_{0}(x_{0})]}[\theta.x_{0}]
is near zero. Originally,
S_{t}
is a scaling map defined on
\mathbb{R}
. Comparing
\nu
with the target measure that appears in
Eq.

5.3
, the strategy is to show that the support of
\nu
is small enough. Then the restriction map
S_{t}:\supp\,\,\nu\to S_{t}(\supp\,\,\nu)
is a diffeomorphism, and hence we can use the invariance of entropy under the scaling map to change the scale in the partition

H(\nu,\mathcal{Q}_{t+k})=H(S_{t}\nu,\mathcal{Q}_{k})+O(1).

(5.4)

We estimate the support of
\nu
.

•

(
\supp[\theta.x_{0}]
) Observe that
\supp[\theta.x_{0}]\subset[\supp\theta.\{x_{0}\}]
. Let
E=\supp\theta
and
F=\{x_{0}\}
. Under the assumption of this lemma, we can
apply
2.18
to
E=\supp\theta
and
F=\{x_{0}\}
and obtain

\diam(\supp[\theta.x_{0}])\leqslant 16C_{L}\frac{C_{1}}{r^{2}}\diam(\supp\theta)/\|h_{0}\|^{2}\leqslant 16C_{L}C_{p}^{2}\frac{C_{1}}{r^{2}}\rho q^{-t}.

(5.5)

•

(
\supp(\pi_{(\ell_{0}^{-1}E)^{\perp}}\tau)
)
Note that
\supp\tau\subset b(f_{\ell_{0}},r)
. Then for any
y,z\in\supp\tau
, using
Eq.

2.18
, we obtain
d(y,\ell^{-1}_{0}E_{1})>r/C_{1},\,\,\,d(z,\ell^{-1}_{0}E_{1})>r/C_{1}
. So by
B.2
(2),

d(\pi_{(\ell_{0}^{-1}E)^{\perp}}y,\pi_{(\ell_{0}^{-1}E)^{\perp}}z)\leqslant\frac{C_{1}}{r}d(y,z).

Therefore

\ \diam(\supp(\pi_{(\ell_{0}^{-1}E)^{\perp}}\tau))\leqslant\diam(\supp\tau)C_{1}/r\leqslant C_{1}\rho/r.

•

We want to deduce from the above two diameter estimates that

\diam(\supp\nu)\leqslant q^{-t(\ell_{0},x_{0})}/2,

which is sufficient to have

16C_{L}C_{p}^{2}\frac{C_{1}}{r^{2}}\rho q^{-t}+q^{-t(\ell_{0},x_{0})}C_{1}\rho/r\leqslant q^{-t(\ell_{0},x_{0})}/2.

(5.6)

This is possible because we have
|t(\ell_{0},x_{0})-t|\leqslant 4\log(C_{1}/r)
and
\rho\leqslant q^{-k}(C_{1}/r)^{8}
. Using the assumption
k\geqslant K(\epsilon)\geqslant\log(64C_{L}C_{p}^{2})
, we get
Eq.

5.6
.

Therefore, we obtain the restriction of the scaling map
S_{t(\ell_{0},x_{0})}:\supp\nu\to S_{t(\ell_{0},x_{0})}(\supp\nu)
is a diffeomorphism.

Recall that we use the convention
\mathcal{Q}_{t}=\mathcal{Q}_{[t]}
for the partition. If
t(\ell_{0},x_{0})
is an integer, we can apply directly
Eq.

5.4
and obtain

H(S_{t(\ell_{0},x_{0})}\nu,\mathcal{Q}_{k-\log\rho+t-t(\ell_{0},x_{0})})=H(\nu,\mathcal{Q}_{k-\log\rho+t})+O(1).

(5.7)

Otherwise, we can use
[
HS17
, (4.1)]
and still obtain
Eq.

5.7
.

Comparing
S_{t(\ell_{0},x_{0})}\nu
with the target measure
S_{t(\ell_{0},x_{0})}T_{[\ell_{0}(x_{0})]}[\theta.x_{0}]*(\pi_{(\ell_{0}^{-1}E_{1})^{\perp}}\tau)
in
Eq.

5.3
, the only difference is the shift map
T_{y}
. But the shift map only changes the entropy by
O(1)
. So combining with Step 1, we obtain

\left|\frac{1}{k}H([\theta.\tau],\mathcal{Q}_{k+t-\log\rho})-\frac{1}{k}H\left((S_{t(\ell_{0},x_{0})}T_{[\ell_{0}(x_{0})]}[\theta.x_{0}])*(\pi_{(\ell_{0}^{-1}E_{1})^{\perp}}\tau),\mathcal{Q}_{k-\log\rho+t-t(\ell_{0},x_{0})}\right)\right|<\epsilon+\frac{O(1)}{k}.

To finish, we replace
\mathcal{Q}_{k-\log\rho+t-t(\ell_{0},x_{0})}
by
\mathcal{Q}_{k-\log\rho}
, and it brings the error term
4\log(C_{1}/r)/k
by
Eq.

2.77
.
∎

5.2.2
Decompose projection of convolution measures

5.3
uses the concavity of entropy to decompose the projection of the convolution into measures with small support without losing too much entropy. It is called the multi-scale formula for entropy.

We recall and introduce a few notions.

•

For each
j\in\mathbb{N}
, let
\mu_{\varphi(j,\mathbf{i})}
be the random measure
{g_{\mathbf{i}}}\mu
with
\mathbf{i}
in the symbolic space
\mathbf{I}(j)
given as in
2.31
.

•

In
4.7
, we introduced the
r
-attracting decomposition of the pair
(g_{\mathbf{i}},\mu)
:

\mu_{\varphi(j,\mathbf{i})}=g_{\mathbf{i}}\mu:=\epsilon\cdot g_{\mathbf{i}}(\mu_{b(g_{\mathbf{i}}^{-},r)^{c}})+(1-\epsilon)\cdot g_{\mathbf{i}}(\mu_{b(g_{\mathbf{i}}^{-},r)}),

(5.8)

where
\epsilon=\mu(b(g_{\mathbf{i}}^{-},r)^{c})
. Denote

(\mu_{\varphi(j,\mathbf{i})})_{\mathbf{I}}:=g_{\mathbf{i}}(\mu_{b(g_{\mathbf{i}}^{-},r)}),\,\,\,(\mu_{\varphi(j,\mathbf{i})})_{\mathbf{II}}:=g_{\mathbf{i}}(\mu_{b(g_{\mathbf{i}}^{-},r)^{c}}).

4.9
states that the decomposition of
\mu_{\varphi(j,\mathbf{i})}
in
Eq.

5.8
gives a decomposition satisfying
4.6
.

•

Let

\displaystyle\mathcal{E}_{0}:=\{(\ell,\mathbf{i})\in L\times\Lambda^{*}:\ V_{g_{\mathbf{i}}}^{+}\in b(f_{\ell},r/4)\},

(5.9)

where
b(f_{\ell},r)\subset\mathbb{P}(\mathbb{R}^{3})
is given as in
2.12
.

The constant
r
is the parameter for both
\mathcal{E}_{0}
where
b(f_{\ell},r)
is used to define
\mathcal{E}_{0}
, and
r
-attracting decomposition in
Eq.

5.8
.

Proposition 5.3
.

For any
C_{1}>1
and
t\geqslant 1
, let
\theta\in\mathbf{P}(L)
be such that
\supp(\theta)\subset L(t,C_{1})
. Then any pair of natural numbers
n\geqslant k
, we consider the
r
-attracting decomposition of
(g_{\mathbf{i}},\mu)
with
r=q^{-\sqrt{k}/10}
. We have

\frac{1}{n}H([\theta.\mu],\mathcal{Q}_{t+n})\geqslant\mathbb{E}_{1\leqslant j\leqslant n,\ell\sim\theta}\left(\frac{1}{k}H([\theta_{\ell,j}.(\mu_{\varphi(j,\mathbf{i})})_{\mathbf{I}}],\mathcal{Q}_{t+j+k}),\ \mathcal{E}_{0}\right)-O\left(r^{\beta}+\frac{k}{n}+\frac{\log(C_{1}/r^{4})}{k}\right),

where
\mathbb{E}(\ ,\mathcal{E}_{0})
is the restriction to the subset
\mathcal{E}_{0}
and
\beta>0
is from
2.23
.

Proof.

The proof consists of two steps. Firstly, we use the identity

[\theta.\mu]=\mathbb{E}_{j=i}\left([\theta_{\ell,j}.\mu_{\varphi(j,\mathbf{i})}]\right),

which is valid for any
i\in\mathbb{N}
. Let
s
be the integer part of
n/k
. Similar to the proof of Lemma 4.3 in
[
BHR19
]
, we have for
for every residue
0\leqslant p<k
,

\begin{split}H([\theta.\mu],\mathcal{Q}_{t+n})&=\sum_{m=0}^{s-2}H\left([\theta.\mu],\mathcal{Q}_{t+(m+1)k+p}\mid\mathcal{Q}_{t+mk+p}\right)\\
&\;\;\;\;+H([\theta.\mu],\mathcal{Q}_{t+p})+H([\theta.\mu],\mathcal{Q}_{t+n}|\mathcal{Q}_{t+(s-1)k+p})\\
&\geqslant\sum_{m=0}^{s-2}H\left([\theta.\mu],\mathcal{Q}_{t+(m+1)k+p}\mid\mathcal{Q}_{t+mk+p}\right)\\
&\geqslant\sum_{m=0}^{s-2}\mathbb{E}_{j=mk+p}\left(H\left([\theta_{\ell,j}.\mu_{\varphi(j,\mathbf{i})}],\mathcal{Q}_{t+k+j}\mid\mathcal{Q}_{t+j}\right)\right).\end{split}

(5.10)

where in the last line we used concavity of entropy.
Now by averaging over
0\leq p\leq k-1
and dividing by
n
, and recalling that
s/n\leqslant 1/k
, we get

\displaystyle\frac{1}{n}H([\theta.\mu],\mathcal{Q}_{t+n})\geqslant\frac{1}{n}\sum_{p=0}^{k-1}\sum_{m=0}^{s-2}\mathbb{E}_{j=mk+p}\left(\frac{1}{k}H\left([\theta_{\ell,j}.\mu_{\varphi(j,\mathbf{i})}],\mathcal{Q}_{t+k+j}\mid\mathcal{Q}_{t+j}\right)\right)-O(\frac{s}{n})

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}_{1\leqslant j\leqslant n}\left(\frac{1}{k}H\left([\theta_{\ell,j}.\mu_{\varphi(j,\mathbf{i})}],\mathcal{Q}_{t+k+j}\mid\mathcal{Q}_{t+j}\right)\right)-O(\frac{k}{n}+\frac{1}{k}),

(5.11)

Secondly, we drop the conditional part
\mathcal{Q}_{t+j}
using the support of measures
\theta_{\ell,j}.\mu_{\varphi(j,\mathbf{i})}
, which is similar to
[
HS17
, Lemma 5.3]
. We restrict to
\mathcal{E}_{0}
and have

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{1\leqslant j\leqslant n}\left(\frac{1}{k}H([\theta_{\ell,j}.\mu_{\varphi(j,\mathbf{i})}],\mathcal{Q}_{t+j+k}|\mathcal{Q}_{t+j})\right)\geqslant\mathbb{E}_{1\leqslant j\leqslant n}\left(\frac{1}{k}H([\theta_{\ell,j}.\mu_{\varphi(j,\mathbf{i})}],\mathcal{Q}_{t+j+k}|\mathcal{Q}_{t+j}),\ \mathcal{E}_{0}\right)

Recall from
Eq.

5.8
the
r
-attracting decomposition of
\mu_{\varphi(j,\mathbf{i})}
:

\displaystyle\mu_{\varphi(j,\mathbf{i})}

\displaystyle=\epsilon\cdot g_{\mathbf{i}}(\mu_{b(g_{\mathbf{i}}^{-},r)^{c}})+(1-\epsilon)\cdot g_{\mathbf{i}}(\mu_{b(g_{\mathbf{i}}^{-},r)})

\displaystyle=\epsilon\cdot(\mu_{\varphi(j,\mathbf{i})})_{\mathbf{II}}+(1-\epsilon)\cdot(\mu_{\varphi(j,\mathbf{i})})_{\mathbf{I}}.

(5.12)

We have
\epsilon=\mu(b(g_{\mathbf{i}}^{-},r)^{c})\leqslant Cr^{\beta}
with
the inequality due to
2.23
.

By the definition of
\mathbf{I}(j)
(
Eq.

2.70
), we have
\chi_{1}(g_{\mathbf{i}})\geqslant j
for
\mathbf{i}\in{\mathbf{I}}(j)
.
Combining with
2.4
, we obtain the following lemma.

Lemma 5.4
.

For any
j\in\mathbb{N}
and for pair
(\ell,\mathbf{i})\in\mathcal{E}_{0}\cap(L\times{\mathbf{I}}(j))
, we have

\diam(\supp((\mu_{\varphi(j,\mathbf{i})})_{\mathbf{I}}))\leqslant q^{-j}/r^{2}.

(5.13)

Moreover, if
j\geqslant\log(4/r^{3})
, then

\supp((\mu_{\varphi(j,\mathbf{i})})_{\mathbf{I}})\subset B(V_{g_{\mathbf{i}}}^{+},q^{-j}/r^{2})\subset b(f_{\ell},r/2).

(5.14)

To continue, suppose
j\geqslant\log(2C_{L}C_{1}/r)
. We can apply
2.18
to
C_{2}=1/r
,

E:=\supp\theta_{\ell,j}
and
F:=\supp(\mu_{\varphi(j,\mathbf{i})})_{\mathbf{I}}
. This is because we have
\diam E=\diam\mathcal{Q}_{j}(\ell)\leqslant q^{-j}\leqslant r/(C_{L}C_{1})
and
5.4
. For
\ell\in\supp\theta\subset L(t,C_{1})
, write
\ell=\begin{pmatrix}\det h&0\\
n&h\end{pmatrix}
. We have
\|h\|\geqslant q^{t/2}/C_{p}
.
Hence, we obtain

\displaystyle\diam(\supp[\theta_{\ell,j}.(\mu_{\varphi(j,\mathbf{i})})_{\mathbf{I}}])

\displaystyle\leqslant 16C_{L}C_{1}(\diam(\supp\theta_{\ell,j})+\diam(\supp(\mu_{\varphi(j,\mathbf{i})})_{\mathbf{I}}))/(r^{2}\|h\|^{2})

\displaystyle\leqslant 32C_{L}C_{p}^{2}C_{1}q^{-t-j}/r^{4}.

Therefore, the support of
\supp[\theta_{\ell,j}.(\mu_{\varphi(j,\mathbf{i})})_{\mathbf{I}}]
is of size
O(q^{-(t+j)+\log(C_{1}/r^{4})})
. Applying
2.35
to
[\theta_{\ell,j}.\mu_{\varphi(j\mathbf{i})}]
at the scale
(n,k)=(k,t+j)
, we obtain

\begin{split}\frac{1}{k}H([\theta_{\ell,j}.\mu_{\varphi(j,\mathbf{i})}],\mathcal{Q}_{t+j+k}|\mathcal{Q}_{t+j})\geqslant\frac{1}{k}H([\theta_{\ell,j}.(\mu_{\varphi(j,\mathbf{i})})_{\mathbf{I}}],\mathcal{Q}_{t+j+k})-\frac{1}{k}O(\log(C_{1}/r^{3}))-\epsilon.\end{split}

(5.15)

Consequently, we have

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{1\leqslant j\leqslant n}\left(\frac{1}{k}H([\theta_{\ell,j}.(\mu_{\varphi(j,\mathbf{i})})],\mathcal{Q}_{t+j+k}|\mathcal{Q}_{t+j}),\ \mathcal{E}_{0}\right)

\displaystyle\geqslant

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{1\leqslant j\leqslant n}\left(\frac{1}{k}H([\theta_{\ell,j}.(\mu_{\varphi(j,\mathbf{i})})_{\mathbf{I}}],\mathcal{Q}_{t+j+k}),\ \mathcal{E}_{0}\right)-Cr^{\beta}-(\frac{1}{k}+\frac{1}{n})O(\log(C_{1}/r^{4})).

(5.16)

Here is how we get the inequality: when
j\geqslant\max\{\log(2/r^{2}),\log(2C_{L}C_{1}/r)\}
, we use
Eq.

5.15
to obtain a lower bound with an error term
O(\log(C_{1}/r^{3}))/k+\epsilon
, which is less than
O(\log(C_{1}/r^{4}))/k+Cr^{\beta}
; otherwise, we use the trivial bound of entropy
H([\theta_{\ell,j}.(\mu_{\varphi(j,\mathbf{i})})],\mathcal{Q}_{t+j+k}|\mathcal{Q}_{t+j})

and the definition of
\mathbb{E}_{1\leqslant j\leqslant n}
to obtain an error term
O(\log(C_{1}/r^{4}))/n
.
∎

By the same proof as in Lemma 4.4 in
[
BHR19
]
, we get

Lemma 5.5
.

For any
\theta\in\mathbf{P}(L)
and any pair of natural numbers
n\geqslant k
, we have

\frac{1}{n}H(\theta,\mathcal{Q}_{n})=\mathbb{E}_{1\leqslant j\leqslant n}\left(\frac{1}{k}H(\theta_{\ell,j},\mathcal{Q}_{j+k})\right)+O\left(\frac{k}{n}+\frac{\log(1+R)}{n}\right),

where
R=\diam(\supp(\theta))
.

5.2.3
General projection of convolution of measures

Let
\theta\in\mathbf{P}(L)
and
x\in\mathbb{P}(\mathbb{R}^{3})
. We define the measure
\theta.x
on
\mathbb{P}(\mathbb{R}^{3})
by
\theta.x:=\int_{\mathrm{SL}_{3}(\mathbb{R})}\delta_{gx}\mathrm{d}\theta(g)
. Previously, we treated the measure
[\theta.x]
which is
\pi_{E_{1}^{\perp}}(\theta.x)
.
More generally, for any
V\in\mathbb{P}(\mathbb{R}^{3})
, we consider
the measure
\pi_{V^{\perp}}(\theta.x)
.

Lemma 5.6
.

For every
C_{1}>1
, there exists a compact neighborhood
Z
of the identity in
L
such that for any
V\in\mathbb{P}(\mathbb{R}^{3})
with
d(V,E_{1}^{\perp})>1/C_{1}
, any
\theta\in\mathbf{P}(L)
such that
\supp(\theta)\subset Z
, and any
k,i\in\mathbb{N}
, we have

\mu\left\{x\in\mathbb{P}(\mathbb{R}^{3}):\ \frac{1}{k}H(\pi_{V^{\perp}}(\theta.x),\mathcal{Q}_{k+i})\geqslant\frac{1}{Ck}H(\theta,\mathcal{Q}_{k+i})-\frac{C}{k}\right\}\geqslant\frac{1}{C},

where
C>1
is a constant depending on
Z
and
C_{1}
.

The proof is given in
Appendix

C
.

5.3
Entropy growth under convolutions: projective case

We are ready to prove a projective version of Theorem 4.6 in
[
BHR19
]
. Recall that
\alpha
is the value of
\dim\pi_{V^{\perp}}\mu
for
\mu^{-}
-a.e.
V
defined in
2.37
.

Theorem 5.7
.

Suppose
0<\alpha<1
. For every
\epsilon>0
and
C_{1}>1
, there exists
\delta(\alpha,\epsilon,C_{1})>0
such that the following holds for any
n\geqslant N(\alpha,\epsilon,C_{1})
.

Let
t\geqslant 1
and let
\theta
be a probability measure on
L
satisfies

•

\diam(\supp(\theta))<C_{p}
,

•

\supp(\theta)\subset L(t,C_{1})
(
Eq.

2.68
),

•

\frac{1}{n}H(\theta,\mathcal{Q}_{n})>\epsilon
.

Then

\frac{1}{n}H([\theta.\mu],\mathcal{Q}_{t+n})>\alpha+\delta.

We introduce a parameter
k
, a large integer less than
n
that will be defined at the end of the proof of Theorem
5.7
. We take
r
to be
\exp(-\sqrt{k}/10)
.
Recall
\mathcal{E}_{0}
from
Eq.

5.9
with parameter
r
.

The idea of the proof is similar to
[
BHR19
, Section 4]
and
[
HS17
]
. In
Section

5.3.1
, we find measures with small support and positive entropy (
5.8
). In
Section

5.3.2
, we apply the linearization technique to the convolution to obtain euclidean convolution (
Eq.

5.23
). In
Section

5.3.3
, we continue to modify the small measures, which is to replace
\mathfrak{m}_{1}

Eq.

5.25
by
\mathfrak{m}_{2}

Eq.

5.26
, upgrading
Eq.

5.23
to
Eq.

5.28
. Finally, in
Section

5.3.4
, we use
4.2
to verify that
\mathfrak{m}_{2}
is entropy porous, hence
5.1
implies entropy growth.

5.3.1
Find measures with small support that have positive entropy

By
5.5
, we have

\mathbb{E}_{1\leqslant j\leqslant n}\left(\frac{1}{k}H(\theta_{\ell,j},\mathcal{Q}_{j+k})\right)=\frac{1}{n}H(\theta,\mathcal{Q}_{n})-O(\frac{k}{n}+\frac{\log(1+C_{p})}{n})>\epsilon/2,

where the second inequality holds under the assumption that
k/n
and
\log(1+C_{p})/n
are small compare to
\epsilon
. As a direct consequence, we have

\mathbb{P}_{1\leqslant j\leqslant n}\left(\frac{1}{k}H(\theta_{\ell,j},\mathcal{Q}_{j+k})\geqslant\frac{\epsilon}{4}\right)\geqslant\frac{\epsilon}{4}.

(5.17)

Lemma 5.8
.

There exists
C_{5}=C_{5}(C_{1})>0
such that for
k
large compared to
C_{1}
and
1/\epsilon
,

\mathbb{E}_{1\leqslant j\leqslant n}\left(\mu\left(x:\frac{1}{k}H([\theta_{\ell,j}.x],\mathcal{Q}_{j+k+t})\geqslant\frac{\epsilon}{C_{5}}\right)\right)\geqslant\frac{\epsilon}{C_{5}}.

Proof.

Let
C_{6}=C_{6}(C_{1})>1
be the constant from
5.6
.

Since
5.6
only works for measures supported in a neighbourhood of identity, we first translate the measures. Due to left-invariance of the metric on
L
and
Eq.

2.47
, we have

\frac{1}{k}H(\theta_{\ell,j},\mathcal{Q}_{j+k})=\frac{1}{k}H(\ell^{-1}\theta_{\ell,j},\mathcal{Q}_{j+k})+O(\frac{1}{k}).

Then
\ell^{-1}\theta_{\ell,j}
is a measure supported on a neighbourhood of the identity of radius
O(q^{-j})
.

Suppose
j
is large enough. Then
\ell^{-1}\theta_{\ell,j}
is supported on
Z
, the neighborhood of the identity given in
5.6
. As
\supp\theta\subset L(t,C_{1})
, we get
d(\ell^{-1}E_{1},E_{1}^{\perp})\geqslant 1/C_{1}
. Hence we can apply
5.6
to
V=\ell^{-1}E_{1}
and obtain

\mu\left(x:\ \frac{1}{k}H(\pi_{(\ell^{-1}E_{1})^{\perp}}(\ell^{-1}\theta_{\ell,j}.x),\mathcal{Q}_{j+k})\geqslant\frac{1}{C_{6}k}H(\theta_{\ell,j},\mathcal{Q}_{j+k})-\frac{C_{6}}{k}\right)\geqslant\frac{1}{C_{6}}.

(5.18)

Using
2.10
, we rewrite

[\ell(\ell^{-1}\theta_{\ell,j}.x)]=F(\pi_{(\ell^{-1}E_{1})^{\perp}}(\ell^{-1}\theta_{\ell,j}.x)),

where we write
\ell=\begin{pmatrix}\det h&0\\
n&h\end{pmatrix}
and
F=h\pi({\ell^{-1}(E_{1})},E_{1}^{\perp},(\ell^{-1}E_{1})^{\perp})
is a diffeomorphism from
\mathbb{P}((\ell^{-1}E_{1})^{\perp})
to
\mathbb{P}(E_{1}^{\perp})
.
The map
F
satisfies

\frac{d(F(y),F(y^{\prime}))}{d(y,y^{\prime})}\geqslant q^{-t}/(C_{1}C_{p})^{2}

(5.19)

for
y\neq y^{\prime}
in
\mathbb{P}((\ell^{-1}E_{1})^{\perp})
, because the part
\pi({\ell^{-1}(E_{1})},E_{1}^{\perp},(\ell^{-1}E_{1})^{\perp})
gives a contraction at most
C_{1}
(
B.1
) and the part
h
at most
\|h\|^{-2}
.
For
\mathcal{F}=\mathcal{Q}_{j+k}
and
\mathcal{E}=F^{-1}\mathcal{Q}_{j+k+t}
, we have each atom of
\mathcal{E}
intersects at most
O((C_{1}C_{p})^{2})
atoms of
\mathcal{F}
due to
Eq.

5.19
. We can apply
2.33
. Therefore for
x
satisfying (
5.18
), we have

\begin{split}\frac{1}{k}H([\theta_{\ell,j}.x],\mathcal{Q}_{j+k+t})&=\frac{1}{k}H(F\pi_{(\ell^{-1}E_{1})^{\perp}}(\ell^{-1}\theta_{\ell,j}.x),\mathcal{Q}_{j+k+t})\\
&\geqslant\frac{1}{k}H(\pi_{(\ell^{-1}E_{1})^{\perp}}(\ell^{-1}\theta_{\ell,j}.x),\mathcal{Q}_{j+k})-\frac{O(\log(C_{1}C_{p}))}{k}\\
&\geqslant\frac{1}{C_{6}k}H(\theta_{\ell,j},\mathcal{Q}_{j+k})-\frac{C_{6}+O(\log(C_{1}C_{p}))}{k}.\end{split}

(5.20)

For each
\theta_{\ell,j}
in the set of
Eq.

5.17
and
x
satisfying
Eq.

5.20
, we have

\frac{1}{k}H([\theta_{\ell,j}.x],\mathcal{Q}_{j+k+t})\geqslant\frac{1}{C_{6}k}H(\theta_{\ell,j},\mathcal{Q}_{j+k})-\frac{C_{6}+O(\log C_{1})}{k}\geqslant\frac{\epsilon}{4C_{6}}-\frac{C_{6}+O(\log C_{1})}{k}\geqslant\frac{\epsilon}{8C_{6}},

if
k
is large enough. Hence setting
C_{5}=32C_{6}
, we have

\mathbb{E}_{j}\left(\mu\left(x:\frac{1}{k}H([\theta_{\ell,j}.x],\mathcal{Q}_{j+k+t})\geqslant\frac{\epsilon}{C_{5}}\right)\right)\geqslant\frac{1}{C_{6}}\mathbb{P}_{j}\left(\frac{1}{k}H(\theta_{\ell,j},\mathcal{Q}_{j+k})\geqslant\frac{\epsilon}{4}\right).

The proportion of
j
between
1
and
n
such that the ball
B(id,q^{-j})
is not contained in
Z
goes to zero as
n\rightarrow\infty
. The proof is complete by
Eq.

5.17
.
∎

5.3.2
Apply linearization

We take a small constant
\epsilon_{1}>0
, which will be chosen small compared to fixed constants
\epsilon^{\prime}
and
\delta(\epsilon^{\prime})
defined later.

Let
K(\epsilon_{1})
be the constant given in
5.2
. We may assume
k>K(\epsilon_{1})
. Consider any
j\in\mathbb{N}
such that

q^{-j}\leqslant q^{-k}(r/C_{1})^{8}

and set
\rho=q^{-j}
.
We apply
5.2
to

•

the constants
\epsilon_{1}
,
k
and
r=q^{-\sqrt{k}/10}
;

•

the convolution measure
[\theta_{\ell,j}.(\mu_{\varphi(j,\mathbf{j})})_{\mathbf{I}}]
at any point
x\in\supp(\mu_{\varphi(j,\mathbf{j})})_{\mathbf{I}}
with
(\ell,\mathbf{i})\in\mathcal{E}_{0}
.

We check the conditions of
5.2
:

•

\supp(\theta_{\ell,j})\subset B(\ell,q^{-j})=B(\ell,\rho)
;

•

due to
j\geqslant\log(4/r^{3})
, by
5.4
, we have
\supp(\mu_{\varphi(j,\mathbf{i})})_{\mathbf{I}}\subset B(V_{g_{\mathbf{i}}}^{+},\rho)\subset b(f_{\ell},r/2)
. Hence, for any
x\in\supp(\mu_{\varphi(j,\mathbf{i})})_{\mathbf{I}}
, we have
\supp(\mu_{\varphi(j,\mathbf{i})})_{\mathbf{I}}\subset B(x,2\rho)\subset b(f_{\ell},r)
.

Therefore,
5.2
yields for any
x\in\supp(\mu_{\varphi(j,\mathbf{i})})_{\mathbf{I}}

\displaystyle\frac{1}{k}H([\theta_{\ell,j}.(\mu_{\varphi(j,\mathbf{i})})_{\mathbf{I}}],\mathcal{Q}_{k+t+j})

(5.21)

\displaystyle\geqslant

\displaystyle\frac{1}{k}H(S_{t(\ell,x)}T_{[\ell(x)]}[\theta_{\ell,j}.x]*\pi_{(\ell^{-1}E_{1})^{\perp}}(\mu_{\varphi(j,\mathbf{i})})_{\mathbf{I}},\mathcal{Q}_{k+j})-O\left(\frac{\log(C_{1}/r)}{k}\right)-\epsilon_{1},

with

|t(\ell,x)-t|\leqslant 8\log(C_{1}/r).

(5.22)

Hence, combining with
5.3
, we have

\begin{split}&\frac{1}{n}H([\theta.\mu],\mathcal{Q}_{t+n})\geqslant\mathbb{E}_{1\leqslant j\leqslant n,\ell\sim\theta}\left(\frac{1}{k}H([\theta_{\ell,j}.(\mu_{\varphi(j,\mathbf{i})})_{\mathbf{I}}],\mathcal{Q}_{t+j+k}),\ \mathcal{E}_{0}\right)-\textit{error term}\\
&\geqslant\mathbb{E}_{1\leqslant j\leqslant n,\ell\sim\theta}\left(\left(\int\frac{1}{k}H((S_{t(\ell,x)}T_{[\ell(x)]}[\theta_{\ell,j}.x])*\pi_{(\ell^{-1}E_{1})^{\perp}}(\mu_{\varphi(j,\mathbf{i})})_{\mathbf{I}},\mathcal{Q}_{j+k})\mathrm{d}(\mu_{\varphi(j,\mathbf{i})})_{\mathbf{I}}(x)\right),\mathcal{E}_{0}\right)\\
&\ \ -\textit{error term}\ \ \text{(integrating \lx@cref{creftype\lx@tilde refnum}{equ:con m1 before integral} for $x$ with the measure $(\mu_{\varphi(j,\mathbf{i})})_{\mathbf{I}}$)}.\end{split}

(5.23)

5.3.3
Replace measures by their good parts

For any
\mathbf{i}\in{\mathbf{I}}(j)
and
V\in\mathbb{P}(\mathbb{R}^{3})
,
Section

4.4
states that

\displaystyle{}\pi_{V^{\perp}}\circ g_{\mathbf{i}}={}h_{V,g_{\mathbf{i}}}\circ\pi_{g^{-1}_{\mathbf{i}}V,V^{\perp}}.

(5.24)

Recall we denote
h_{V,g_{\mathbf{i}}}
by
h_{V,\mathbf{i}}
and
\pi_{g^{-1}_{\mathbf{i}}V,V^{\perp}}
by
\pi_{V,\mathbf{i}}
for simplification.

Recall that
\mu_{\varphi(j,\mathbf{i})}=g_{\mathbf{i}}\mu
. So far, we have considered the measure

\mathfrak{m}_{1}:=\pi_{(\ell^{-1}E_{1})^{\perp}}((\mu_{\varphi(j,\mathbf{i})})_{\mathbf{I}}),

(5.25)

which is the pushforward of the
r
-attracting part of
(g_{\mathbf{i}},\mu)
by the projection
\pi_{(\ell^{-1}E_{1})^{\perp}}
. To continue, we will switch the order and consider the following measure

\mathfrak{m}_{2}(\ell,j,\mathbf{i}):=\text{$r^{5}$-attracting part of the pair $(h_{\ell^{-1}E_{1},\mathbf{i}},\pi_{\ell^{-1}E_{1},\mathbf{i}}\mu)$}

(5.26)

(see
4.7
for the construction of the attracting part).

The measure
\mathfrak{m}_{2}(\ell,j,\mathbf{i})
is a random measure with respect to the random variables
\ell
(
\sim\theta
) and
\mathbf{i}
(
\in{\mathbf{I}}(j)
).
We will frequently write
\mathfrak{m}_{2}(\ell,j,\mathbf{i})
as
\mathfrak{m}_{2}
to simplify the notation.
The advantage of considering
\mathfrak{m}_{2}
is that is
\mathfrak{m}_{2}
is a restriction of
\pi_{(\ell^{-1}E_{1})^{\perp}}\mu_{\varphi(j,\mathbf{i})}
and we can apply to it the porosity result
4.2
.

To relate
\mathfrak{m}_{1}
and
\mathfrak{m}_{2}
, we introduce the following subset:

\mathcal{E}_{0}^{\prime}(j)=\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}&(\ell,\mathbf{i})\in\mathcal{E}_{0}\cap(L\times{\mathbf{I}}(j)):\ d(\pi_{(\ell^{-1}E_{1})^{\perp}}V_{g_{\mathbf{i}}}^{+},{h^{+}_{\ell^{-1}E_{1},\mathbf{i}}})\leqslant q^{-j}/r^{2},\\
&\ |\chi_{1}(h_{\ell^{-1}E_{1},\mathbf{i}})-\chi_{1}(g_{\mathbf{i}})|\leqslant|\log r|,\ d(g_{\mathbf{i}}^{-1}\ell^{-1}E_{1},(\ell^{-1}E_{1})^{\perp})\geqslant r\end{array}\right\}.

(5.27)

We will upgrade
Eq.

5.23
in Step 2 to the following version.

Proposition 5.9
.

For any
n\geqslant k\geqslant K(1)
, we have

\begin{split}&\frac{1}{n}H([\theta.\mu],\mathcal{Q}_{t+n})\\
&\geqslant\mathbb{E}_{1\leqslant j\leqslant n,\ell\sim\theta}\left(\int\frac{1}{k}H((S_{t(\ell,x)}T_{[\ell(x)]}[\theta_{\ell,j}.x])*\mathfrak{m}_{2}(\ell,j,\mathbf{i}),\mathcal{Q}_{j+k})\mathrm{d}(\mu_{\varphi(j,\mathbf{i})})_{\mathbf{I}}(x),\mathcal{E}_{0}^{\prime}(j)\right)\\
&-\epsilon_{1}-O(\frac{k}{n}+r^{\beta}+\frac{\log(C_{1}/r)}{k}),\end{split}

(5.28)

where
r=q^{-\sqrt{k}/10}
.

We start the proof of
5.9
by comparing the support of
\mathfrak{m}_{1}
and
\mathfrak{m}_{2}
.

Lemma 5.10
.

For any
k\geqslant K(1)\geqslant 1
and
(\ell,\mathbf{i})\in\mathcal{E}_{0}^{\prime}(j)
, we have:

1.

If
j\geqslant 6|\log r|
, then

B({h^{+}_{\ell^{-1}E_{1},\mathbf{i}}},c_{0}q^{-j}/2r^{4})\subset\supp\mathfrak{m}_{2}\subset B({h^{+}_{\ell^{-1}E_{1},\mathbf{i}}},q^{-j}/r^{11}),

(5.29)

where
0<c_{0}<1
is the constant given as in
Eq.

2.70
.

2.

If
j\geqslant\log(4/r^{3})
, we can write

\mathfrak{m}_{2}=(1-\delta)\mathfrak{m}_{1}+\delta\mathfrak{m}^{\prime},

(5.30)

with
\mathfrak{m}^{\prime}
a probability measure on
\mathbb{P}((\ell^{-1}E_{1})^{\perp})
and
\delta<Cr^{\beta}
.

3.

We can write

\pi_{(\ell^{-1}E_{1})^{\perp}}\mu_{\varphi(j,\mathbf{i})}=\lambda\mathfrak{m}^{\prime\prime}+(1-\lambda)\mathfrak{m}_{2}(\ell,j,\mathbf{i}),

where
\mathfrak{m}^{\prime\prime}
is a probability measure on
\mathbb{P}((\ell^{-1}E_{1})^{\perp})
. It is a
(q^{-j}/r^{11},Cr^{\beta})
-decomposition of
\pi_{(\ell^{-1}E_{1})^{\perp}}\mu_{\varphi(j,\mathbf{i})}
(see
4.6
).

4.

For any pair
j^{\prime},j\in\mathbb{N}
with
j^{\prime}-j\geqslant 4|\log r|
, the equality of the component measures

(\pi_{(\ell^{-1}E_{1})^{\perp}}\mu_{\varphi(j,\mathbf{i})})_{x,j^{\prime}}=(\mathfrak{m}_{2}(\ell,j,\mathbf{i}))_{x,j^{\prime}}

(5.31)

holds at a set of
x
with
\pi_{(\ell^{-1}E_{1})^{\perp}}\mu_{\varphi(j,\mathbf{i})}
-measure greater than
1-O(r^{\beta})
.

Proof.

1.

Using the definition of the random variable
{\mathbf{I}}(j)
(
Eq.

2.70
), we have
\chi_{1}(g_{\mathbf{i}})\in[j,j+|\log c_{0}|]
. Combined with
Eq.

5.27
and
k\geqslant K(1)
, we have
\chi_{1}(h_{\ell^{-1}E_{1},\mathbf{i}})\in j+[-|\log r|,|\log r|+|\log c_{0}|]
.
For
\mathfrak{m}_{2}
, it is the
r^{5}
-attracting part of the pair
(h_{\ell^{-1}E_{1},\mathbf{i}},\pi_{\ell^{-1}E_{1},\mathbf{i}}\mu)
and we also have
q^{-\chi_{1}(h_{\ell^{-1}E_{1},\mathbf{i}})}\leqslant r^{5}
as
j\geqslant 6|\log r|
. Hence by

Eq.

4.3
, we have

B({h^{+}_{\ell^{-1}E_{1},\mathbf{i}}},c_{0}q^{-j}/2r^{4})\subset\supp\mathfrak{m}_{2}\subset B({h^{+}_{\ell^{-1}E_{1},\mathbf{i}}},q^{-j}/r^{11}).

2.

As
j\geqslant\log(4/r^{3})
, we can apply

Eq.

5.14
to
(\mu_{\varphi(j,\mathbf{i})})_{{\mathbf{I}}}
and have

\supp\mathfrak{m}_{1}=\pi_{(\ell^{-1}E_{1})^{\perp}}\supp(\mu_{\varphi(j,\mathbf{i})})_{\mathbf{I}}\subset\pi_{(\ell^{-1}E_{1})^{\perp}}B(V_{g_{\mathbf{i}}}^{+},q^{-j}/r^{2})\subset\pi_{(\ell^{-1}E_{1})^{\perp}}b(f_{\ell},r).

As
(\ell,\mathbf{i})\in\mathcal{E}_{0}
, by the definition of
\mathcal{E}_{0}
(
Eq.

5.9
), we have
V^{+}_{g_{\mathbf{i}}}\in b(f_{\ell},r)
. Using
Eq.

2.18
, we have
d(V_{g_{\mathbf{i}}}^{+},\ell^{-1}E_{1})>r/C_{1}
. Applying (2) in
B.2
to
B(V_{g_{\mathbf{i}}}^{+},q^{-j}/r^{2})
, we obtain

\supp\mathfrak{m}_{1}\subset B(\pi_{(\ell^{-1}E_{1})^{\perp}}V_{g_{\mathbf{i}}}^{+},C_{1}q^{-j}/r^{3}).

It follows from the definition of
\mathcal{E}_{0}^{\prime}(j)
that
d({h^{+}_{\ell^{-1}E_{1},\mathbf{i}}},\pi_{(\ell^{-1}E_{1})^{\perp}}V_{g_{\mathbf{i}}}^{+})\leqslant q^{-j}/r^{2}
. Therefore, we have
\supp\mathfrak{m}_{1}\subset\supp\mathfrak{m}_{2}
.

From the definitions of
\mathfrak{m}_{2}
and
Eq.

5.24
, we know that
\mathfrak{m}_{2}
is a restriction of
\pi_{(\ell^{-1}E_{1})^{\perp}}(\mu_{\varphi(j,\mathbf{i})})
. So we can write
\mathfrak{m}_{2}=\pi_{(\ell^{-1}E_{1})^{\perp}}((g_{\mathbf{i}}\mu)_{S})
with
S=\pi_{(\ell^{-1}E_{1})^{\perp}}^{-1}\supp\mathfrak{m}_{2}
. So the fact that
\supp\mathfrak{m}_{1}\subset\supp\mathfrak{m}_{2}
yields the inclusion
S\supset S^{\prime}
with
S^{\prime}=\supp((\mu_{\varphi(j,\mathbf{i})})_{\mathbf{I}})
. Since the measure
(\mu_{\varphi(j,\mathbf{i})})_{\mathbf{I}}
is the restriction of
\mu_{\varphi(j,\mathbf{i})}
on
S^{\prime}
, we can write

(\mu_{\varphi(j,\mathbf{i})})_{S}=(1-\delta)(\mu_{\varphi(j,\mathbf{i})})_{\mathbf{I}}+\delta\mu^{\prime},

(5.32)

with
\mu^{\prime}
a probability measure on
\mathbb{P}(\mathbb{R}^{3})
, and

\delta=1-(\mu_{\varphi(j,\mathbf{i})})_{S}(S^{\prime})\leqslant 1-\mu_{\varphi(j,\mathbf{i})}(S^{\prime})\leqslant Cr^{\beta},

where the last inequality is due to
Eq.

5.12
.
Applying
\pi_{(\ell^{-1}E_{1})^{\perp}}
to
Eq.

5.32
, we obtain
Eq.

5.29
.

3.

By the same argument as in
4.13
, using
4.9
and
2.24
, we obtain the third statement.

4.

With the third statement available, it remains to show that the two atoms of
\mathcal{Q}_{j^{\prime}}
containing the endpoints of the support of
\mathfrak{m}_{2}
have small measure. As we denote
g_{\mathbf{i}}\mu
by
\mu_{\varphi(j,\mathbf{i})}
, we have

\displaystyle\pi_{(\ell^{-1}E_{1})^{\perp}}g_{\mathbf{i}}\mu(B(x,q^{-j^{\prime}}))=h_{\ell^{-1}E_{1},\mathbf{i}}\pi_{\ell^{-1}E_{1},\mathbf{i}}\mu(B(x,q^{-j^{\prime}}))

\displaystyle\leqslant\sup_{y}\pi_{\ell^{-1}E_{1},\mathbf{i}}\mu(B(y,q^{-(j^{\prime}-j)}/r))\leqslant Cr^{\beta},

where the first inequality is due to the definition in
Eq.

5.27
the second inequality is due to
2.24
.

∎

We will prove
5.9
in the remaining Step 3. Then in Step 4, we will estimate the integrand in the right-hand side of
Eq.

5.28
.

Proof of
5.9
.

We only consider
(\ell,\mathbf{i})\in\mathcal{E}_{0}^{\prime}(j)
,
x\in\supp(\mu_{\varphi(j,\mathbf{i})})_{\mathbf{I}}
and
j\geqslant 6|\log r|
. The part of expectation with
j\leqslant 6|\log r|
is bounded by
\frac{6|\log r|}{n}\times\frac{k+6|\log r|}{k}=O(\frac{|\log r|}{n})
.

Due to
Eq.

5.30
and
Eq.

2.79
(inverse of the concavity of entropy), we obtain

\displaystyle\frac{1}{k}H(S_{t(\ell,x)}T_{[\ell(x)]}[\theta_{\ell,j}.x]*\mathfrak{m}_{1},\mathcal{Q}_{j+k})\geqslant

\displaystyle\frac{1}{1-\delta}(\frac{1}{k}H(S_{t(\ell,x)}T_{[\ell(x)]}[\theta_{\ell,j}.x]*\mathfrak{m}_{2},\mathcal{Q}_{j+k})

\displaystyle-2\delta\frac{1}{k}H(S_{t(\ell,x)}T_{[\ell(x)]}[\theta_{\ell,j}.x]*\mathfrak{m}^{\prime},\mathcal{Q}_{j+k}))-\frac{2}{k}H(\delta).

We give an upper bound to the second term on the right-hand side. Note that by
2.33
, for any
\tau\in\mathbb{P}(\mathbb{P}(\mathbb{R}^{2}))
, we have

H(\tau,\mathcal{Q}_{j+k})=H(\tau,\mathcal{Q}_{j+k}|\mathcal{Q}_{j})+H(\tau,\mathcal{Q}_{j})\leqslant k+O(\log(\diam(\supp\tau)q^{j})).

To apply this to the convolution measure, we estimate the diameter of the convolution measure.
In Step 2, we already verified that we can apply
5.2
to
(\theta_{\ell,j},x)
for any
j\in\mathbb{N}
satisfying
q^{-j}\leqslant q^{-k}(r/C_{1})^{8}
. In particular,

Eq.

5.5
in
5.2
holds for
[\theta_{\ell,j}.x]
. Combining with
Eq.

5.22
, we have

\diam(\supp S_{t(\ell,x)}T_{[\ell(x)]}[\theta_{\ell,j}.x])\leqslant 16C_{L}C_{p}^{2}C_{1}^{9}q^{-j}/r^{10}.

(5.33)

The support of
\mathfrak{m}^{\prime}
is controlled by
Eq.

5.29
, that is
O(q^{-j}/r^{7})
. So we have

\frac{1}{k}H(S_{t(\ell,x)}T_{[\ell(x)]}[\theta_{\ell,j}.x]*\mathfrak{m}^{\prime},\mathcal{Q}_{j+k}))\leqslant 1+\frac{O(\log(C_{1}/r))}{k}.

Hence, we have

\frac{1}{k}H(S_{t(\ell,x)}T_{[\ell(x)]}[\theta_{\ell,j}.x]*\mathfrak{m}_{1},\mathcal{Q}_{j+k})\geqslant\frac{1}{k}H(S_{t(\ell,x)}T_{[\ell(x)]}[\theta_{\ell,j}.x]*\mathfrak{m}_{2},\mathcal{Q}_{j+k})-O(\delta\log(C_{1}/r))/k-2H(\delta)/k,

which holds for every
(\ell,\mathbf{i})\in\mathcal{E}_{0}^{\prime}(j)
.

Notice that
\mathfrak{m}_{1}=\pi_{(\ell^{-1}E_{1})^{\perp}}(\mu_{\varphi(j,\mathbf{i})})_{\mathbf{I}}
. We replace
\mathfrak{m}_{1}
by
\mathfrak{m}_{2}
in
Eq.

5.23
and obtain

\displaystyle\frac{1}{n}H([\theta.\mu],\mathcal{Q}_{t+n})\geqslant\mathbb{E}_{1\leqslant j\leqslant n,\ell\sim\theta}\left(\int\frac{1}{k}H((S_{t(\ell,x)}T_{[\ell(x)]}[\theta_{\ell,j}.x])*\mathfrak{m}_{2}(\ell,j,\mathbf{i}),\mathcal{Q}_{j+k})\mathrm{d}(\mu_{\varphi(j,\mathbf{i})})_{\mathbf{I}}(x),\mathcal{E}_{0}^{\prime}(j)\right)

\displaystyle\ \ \ -\textit{error term}.\qed

Let
\mathbb{P}_{1\leqslant j\leqslant n,\ell\sim\theta}
be the product measure
\mathbb{P}_{1\leqslant j\leqslant n}\otimes\theta
for the random variable
(\mathbf{i},\ell)
. For any
\ell\in L
and any
j\in\mathbb{N}
, let
\mathcal{E}_{0}^{\prime}(j)(\ell)=\{\mathbf{i}\in{\mathbf{I}}:\ (\ell,\mathbf{i})\in\mathcal{E}_{0}^{\prime}(j)\}
.

Lemma 5.11
.

For every
\epsilon>0
and
n\geqslant k\geqslant K(\epsilon)
, we have for any
\ell\in\supp\theta
,

\mathbb{P}_{1\leqslant j\leqslant n}(\mathcal{E}_{0}^{\prime}(j)(\ell))\geqslant 1-\epsilon

and hence

\mathbb{P}_{1\leqslant j\leqslant n,\ell\sim\theta}(\mathcal{E}_{0}^{\prime}(j))\geqslant 1-\epsilon.

Proof.

Fix any
\ell\in\supp\theta
and let
V=\ell^{-1}E_{1}\in\mathbb{P}(\mathbb{R}^{3})
.

By
D.1
, we have

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}_{1\leqslant j\leqslant n}(\mathcal{E}_{0}^{\prime}(j)(\ell))

\displaystyle\geqslant

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}_{1\leqslant j\leqslant n}\left\{\mathbf{i}\in{\mathbf{I}}(j):V_{g_{\mathbf{i}}}^{+}\in b(f_{\ell},2r),\,\,\,d(V^{\perp},H_{g_{\mathbf{i}}}^{-})>2r,\,\,\,|\chi_{1}(h_{V,\mathbf{i}})-\chi_{1}(g_{\mathbf{i}})|\leqslant|\log r|/2,d(g_{\mathbf{i}}^{-1}V,V^{\perp})>r\right\}.

Denote the set in the right-hand side by
\mathcal{U}
. In view of
2.32
, to estimate
the probability
\mathbb{P}_{1\leqslant j\leqslant n}
of the above set for
{\mathbf{I}}
, it suffices to prove for
{\mathbf{U}}
.

We apply the large deviation estimate
Eq.

2.55
to
V_{g_{\mathbf{i}}}^{+}
and the kernel of the linear form
f_{\ell}
(
2.12
); apply
Eq.

2.56
to
H_{g_{\mathbf{i}}}^{-}
and any point
V_{1}\in V^{\perp}
(which is stronger than the Hausdorff distance
d(V^{\perp},H^{-}_{g_{\mathbf{i}}})
); for the third and fourth condition in
\mathcal{U}
, apply
Eq.

2.59
(which is stated for
V=E_{1}
, but due to equivalence, it is also true for general
V
) and
Eq.

2.58
. Therefore, we obtain for
n\geqslant k
,

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}_{1\leqslant j\leqslant n}(\mathbf{U}(j)\in\mathcal{U}^{c})

\displaystyle=\frac{|\log r|/c}{n}\mathbb{P}_{1\leqslant j<|\log r|/c}(\mathbf{U}(j)\in\mathcal{U}^{c})+\frac{n-|\log r|/c}{n}\mathbb{P}_{|\log r|/c\leqslant j\leqslant n}(\mathbf{U}(j)\in\mathcal{U}^{c})

\displaystyle<\frac{|\log r|/c}{n}+Cr^{\beta}\leqslant C(r^{\beta}+1/\sqrt{k}),

where the constant
c>0
comes from
Eq.

2.55
and the last inequality is due to
r=\exp(-\sqrt{k}/10)
,
k\leqslant n
.
Using
2.32
, we have for
n\geqslant k

\mathbb{P}_{1\leqslant j\leqslant n}(\mathbf{I}(j)\in\mathcal{U}^{c})\ll r^{\beta}+1/\sqrt{k}+e^{-\beta n}.

(5.34)

The proof is complete by taking
k
large.
∎

5.3.4
Apply entropy growth of the Euclidean case

Let

\epsilon^{\prime}=\min\{\frac{\epsilon}{10C_{5}},\frac{1-\alpha}{10}\},

(5.35)

where
C_{5}
comes from
5.8
.
Let
\delta_{1}=\delta(\epsilon^{\prime}/2)
from
5.1
and let
m=M(\epsilon^{\prime}/4)
from
4.2
. Take
\epsilon_{1}>0
to be a constant
independent of
\epsilon^{\prime},\delta_{1},m
, which will be determined at the end of the proof.
We introduce the following sets for
j,k\in\mathbb{N}

\displaystyle\mathcal{E}_{1}(j)

\displaystyle=\left\{(\ell,\mathbf{i},x)\in\mathcal{E}_{0}^{\prime}(j)\times\mathbb{P}(\mathbb{R}^{3}):\,\,\,x\in\supp(\mu_{\varphi(j,\mathbf{i})})_{{\mathbf{I}}},\,\,\,\frac{1}{k}H(S_{t(\ell,x)}T_{[\ell(x)]}[\theta_{\ell,j}.x],\mathcal{Q}_{j+k})\geqslant\frac{\epsilon}{C_{5}}\right\},

\displaystyle\mathcal{E}_{2}(j)

\displaystyle=\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}&(\ell,\mathbf{i},x)\in\mathcal{E}_{0}^{\prime}(j)\times\mathbb{P}(\mathbb{R}^{3}):\mathfrak{m}_{2}(\ell,j,\mathbf{i})\text{ is $(1-\epsilon^{\prime},\epsilon^{\prime}/2,m)$-entropy porous}\\
&\text{from scale $j$ to $j+k$ }\end{array}\right\},

\displaystyle\mathcal{E}_{3}(j)

\displaystyle=\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}&(\ell,\mathbf{i},x)\in\mathcal{E}_{0}^{\prime}(j)\times\mathbb{P}(\mathbb{R}^{3}):\,\,\,x\in\supp(\mu_{\varphi(j,\mathbf{i})})_{{\mathbf{I}}},\\
&\frac{1}{k}H(S_{t(\ell,x)}T_{[\ell(x)]}[\theta_{\ell,j}.x]*\mathfrak{m}_{2}(\ell,j,\mathbf{i}),\mathcal{Q}_{j+k})\geqslant\frac{1}{k}H(\mathfrak{m}_{2}(\ell,j,\mathbf{i}),\mathcal{Q}_{j+k})+\delta_{1}/2\end{array}\right\},

\displaystyle\mathcal{E}_{4}(j)

\displaystyle=\left\{(\ell,\mathbf{i},x)\in\mathcal{E}_{0}^{\prime}(j)\times\mathbb{P}(\mathbb{R}^{3}):\ \frac{1}{k}H(\mathfrak{m}_{2}(\ell,j,\mathbf{i}),\mathcal{Q}_{j+k})\geqslant\alpha-\epsilon_{1}\right\}.

We denote by
\pi_{2}
the projections from
L\times\Lambda^{*}\times\mathbb{P}(\mathbb{R}^{3})
to
L\times\Lambda^{*}
.
We want to estimate

\mathbb{E}_{1\leqslant j\leqslant n,\ell\sim\theta}\left(\int\frac{1}{k}H((S_{t(\ell,x)}T_{[\ell(x)]}[\theta_{\ell,j}.x])*\mathfrak{m}_{2}(\ell,j,\mathbf{i}),\mathcal{Q}_{j+k})\mathrm{d}(\mu_{\varphi(j,\mathbf{i})})_{\mathbf{I}}(x),\mathcal{E}_{0}^{\prime}(j)\right)

in
Eq.

5.28
. The rough idea is to estimate the measure of each set above and it is achieved in
1
to
4
.

Claim 1
.

For
k>K(\epsilon^{\prime},\delta_{1})
, we have

\mathcal{E}_{1}(j)\cap\mathcal{E}_{2}(j)\subset\mathcal{E}_{3}(j).

Proof.

For any
(\ell,\mathbf{i},x)\in\mathcal{E}_{1}(j)\cap\mathcal{E}_{2}(j)
, we want to apply
5.1
to
S_{t(\ell,x)}T_{[\ell(x)]}[\theta_{\ell,j}.x]
and
\mathfrak{m}_{2}(\ell,j,\mathbf{i})

to obtain growth of entropy at the scale
(k,n)=(j,k)
. In view of the definitions of
\mathcal{E}_{1}(j)
and
\mathcal{E}_{2}(j)
,
it remains to estimate the size of the support of these two measures.

It follows from
Eq.

5.29
that

\supp\mathfrak{m}_{2}(\ell,j,\mathbf{i})\subset B({h^{+}_{\ell^{-1}E_{1},\mathbf{i}}},q^{-j}/r^{11}).

And
Eq.

5.33
yields that

\diam\left(\supp S_{t(\ell,x)}T_{[\ell(x)]}[\theta_{\ell,j}.x]\right)\leqslant 16C_{L}C_{p}^{2}C_{1}^{9}q^{-j}/r^{10}.

Take the constant
C
in
5.1
to be
16C_{L}C_{p}^{2}C_{1}^{9}/r^{12}
. Then

\frac{\log C}{k}=\frac{\log(16C_{L}C_{p}^{2})+9\log C_{1}-12\log r}{k}\leqslant\frac{\log(16C_{L}C_{p}^{2})+9\log C_{1}+\sqrt{k}}{k},

which goes to
0
as
k
goes to infinity. So we have
k>N(\epsilon^{\prime},\delta_{1},m)\log C
. Therefore,
5.1
yields

\frac{1}{k}H(S_{t(\ell,x)}T_{[\ell(x)]}[\theta_{\ell,j}.x]*\mathfrak{m}_{2}(\ell,j,\mathbf{i}),\mathcal{Q}_{j+k})\geqslant\frac{1}{k}H(\mathfrak{m}_{2}(\ell,j,\mathbf{i}),\mathcal{Q}_{j+k})+\delta_{1}.\qed

Claim 2
.

For
k\geqslant K(C_{1},\epsilon)
, we have

\mathbb{E}_{1\leqslant j\leqslant n,\ell\sim\theta}\left(\int 1_{\mathcal{E}_{1}(j)}(\ell,\mathbf{i},x)\mathrm{d}(\mu_{\varphi(j,\mathbf{i})})_{\mathbf{I}}(x)\right)\geqslant\frac{\epsilon}{4C_{5}}.

Proof.

For any
j\in\mathbb{N}
, we have the equality
\mu=\mathbb{E}_{j}(\mu_{\varphi(j,\mathbf{i})})
. Hence, using (
5.12
), we have

\sup_{A\text{ Borel }}|\mu(A)-\mathbb{E}_{j}((\mu_{\varphi(j,\mathbf{i})})_{\mathbf{I}})(A)|\leqslant\mathbb{E}_{j}(|\mu_{\varphi(j,\mathbf{i})}(A)-(\mu_{\varphi(j,\mathbf{i})})_{\mathbf{I}}(A)|)\ll r^{\beta}.

Let
A(\ell,j)=\{x:\ \frac{1}{k}H([\theta_{\ell,j}.x],\mathcal{Q}_{j+k+t})\geqslant\frac{\epsilon}{C_{5}}\}
. Then for any
1\leqslant j\leqslant n
,

\mathbb{E}_{j,\ell\sim\theta}\left((\mu_{\varphi(j,\mathbf{i})})_{\mathbf{I}}(A(\ell,j))\right)=\mathbb{E}_{\ell\sim\theta}\mathbb{E}_{j}\left((\mu_{\varphi(j,\mathbf{i})})_{\mathbf{I}}(A(\ell,j))\right)\geqslant\mathbb{E}_{\ell\sim\theta}\left(\mu(A(\ell,j))\right)-O(r^{\beta}),

where the inequality is due to the above inequality. Summing over
1\leqslant j\leqslant n
and combining with
5.8
, we obtain

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{1\leqslant j\leqslant n,\ell\sim\theta}\left((\mu_{\varphi(j,\mathbf{i})})_{\mathbf{I}}\left\{x:\frac{1}{k}H([\theta_{\ell,j}.x],\mathcal{Q}_{j+k+t})\geqslant\frac{\epsilon}{C_{5}}\right\}\right)

(5.36)

\displaystyle\geqslant\mathbb{E}_{1\leqslant j\leqslant n,\ell\sim\theta}\left(\mu\left\{x:\frac{1}{k}H([\theta_{\ell,j}.x],\mathcal{Q}_{j+k+t})\geqslant\frac{\epsilon}{C_{5}}\right\}\right)-O(r^{\beta})

\displaystyle\geqslant\frac{\epsilon}{C_{5}}-O(r^{\beta})\geqslant\frac{\epsilon}{2C_{5}}.

Then we can use
Eq.

5.22
,
|t(\ell,x)-t|\leqslant 8\log C_{1}/r
(
Eq.

5.22
) to replace
\frac{1}{k}H([\theta_{\ell,j}.x],\mathcal{Q}_{j+k+t})
by
\frac{1}{k}H(S_{t(\ell,x)}T_{[\ell(x)]}[\theta_{\ell,j}.x],\mathcal{Q}_{j+k})
, and it produces
an error
O(\log C_{1}/r)\leqslant\epsilon^{\prime}/2
.

Comparing
Eq.

5.36
with Claim 2, we
use
5.11
to add the restriction of
\mathcal{E}_{0}^{\prime}(j)
. The proof is complete.
∎

Claim 3
.

For
k>K(\epsilon^{\prime})
and
n>N(\epsilon^{\prime},k)
, we have

\mathbb{P}_{1\leqslant j\leqslant n,\ell\sim\theta}(\pi_{2}(\mathcal{E}_{2}(j)))\geqslant 1-\epsilon^{\prime}.

Proof.

We fix any
\ell\in\supp\theta
, we will show that

\mathbb{P}_{1\leqslant j\leqslant n}\left\{\mathbf{i}\in\mathcal{E}_{0}^{\prime}(j)(\ell):\ \mathfrak{m}_{2}(\ell,j,\mathbf{i})\text{ is }(1-\epsilon^{\prime},\epsilon^{\prime}/2,m)\text{-entropy porous}\text{ from scale $j$ to $j+k$}\right\}>1-\epsilon^{\prime}.

Then the claim follows by integrating
\ell
using the measure
\theta
.

It is good to recall that that

\mathfrak{m}_{2}(\ell,j,\mathbf{i})
is a restriction of
\pi_{(\ell^{-1}E_{1})^{\perp}}g_{\mathbf{i}}\mu
from
Eq.

5.26
and
5.10
.

4.2
yields

\mathbb{P}_{1\leqslant j\leqslant n}\left\{\mathbf{i}\in{\mathbf{I}}(j):\pi_{(\ell^{-1}E_{1})^{\perp}}g_{\mathbf{i}}\mu\text{ is }(\alpha,\epsilon^{\prime}/4,m)\text{-entropy porous}\text{ from scale $j$ to $j+k$}\right\}>1-\epsilon^{\prime}/4.

(5.37)

We take
k
large enough such that for
r=\exp(-\sqrt{k}/10)
, we have
r^{\beta}<\epsilon^{\prime}/4
and
|\log r|/k=1/\sqrt{k}\leqslant\epsilon^{\prime}/4
.

For
\mathbf{i}\in\mathcal{E}_{0}^{\prime}(j)(\ell)
, if
\mathbf{i}
belongs to the set defined in
Eq.

5.37
, then
Eq.

5.31
implies that
\mathfrak{m}_{2}(\ell,j,\mathbf{i})
is

(\alpha,\epsilon^{\prime}/2,m)
-entropy porous from scale
j
to
j+k
. (Actually,
Eq.

5.31
only allows us to obtain the entropy porosity of
\mathfrak{m}_{2}(\ell,j,\mathbf{i})
from scale
j+4|\log r|
to
j+k
. Since
|\log r|/k
is sufficiently small, we obtain the result.)

Combined with
5.11
for the measure of
\mathcal{E}_{0}^{\prime}(j)(\ell)
, we obtain the first inequality in the proof and finish the proof of the claim.
∎

Claim 4
.

For
k>K(\epsilon_{1})
and
n>N(\epsilon_{1},k)

\mathbb{P}_{1\leqslant j\leqslant n,\ell\sim\theta}(\pi_{2}(\mathcal{E}_{4}(j)))\geqslant 1-\epsilon_{1}.

Proof.

Fix any
\ell\in\supp\theta
. Recall from
Eq.

5.26
that
\mathfrak{m}_{2}(\ell,j,\mathbf{i})
is the
r^{5}
-attracting part of the pair
(h_{\ell^{-1}E_{1},\mathbf{i}},\pi_{\ell^{-1}E_{1},\mathbf{i}}\mu)
.
Let
\epsilon=\delta_{1}=\epsilon_{1}/8
. If
r^{5}\leqslant r_{1}(\epsilon_{1})
, then we can apply
4.16
to
\mathfrak{m}_{2}
with
V=\ell^{-1}E_{1}
and
(m,i)=(k,j)
. This gives

\mathbb{P}_{1\leqslant j\leqslant n}\left(\frac{1}{k}H(\mathfrak{m}_{2}(\ell,j,\mathbf{i}),\mathcal{Q}_{k+j})\geqslant\alpha-\epsilon_{1}/2\right)\geqslant 1-\epsilon_{1}/4.

(5.38)

Observed that
\pi_{2}(\mathcal{E}_{4}(j))(\ell)=\{\mathbf{i}:\ (\ell,\mathbf{i})\in\pi_{2}(\mathcal{E}_{4}(j))\}
is the intersection of the set of
Eq.

5.38
with
\mathcal{E}_{0}^{\prime}(j)(\ell)
. Combined with
5.11
, we finishes the proof of the claim.
∎

Proof of
5.7
.

With
5.9
available, it remains to estimate

\mathbb{E}_{1\leqslant j\leqslant n,\ell\sim\theta}\left(\int\frac{1}{k}H((S_{t(\ell,x)}T_{[\ell(x)]}[\theta_{\ell,j}.x])*\mathfrak{m}_{2}(\ell,j,\mathbf{i}),\mathcal{Q}_{j+k})\mathrm{d}(\mu_{\varphi(j,\mathbf{i})})_{\mathbf{I}}(x),\mathcal{E}_{0}^{\prime}(j)\right).

Here we abuse the notation, for a set
F
in
L\times\Lambda^{*}\times\mathbb{P}(\mathbb{R}^{3})
, we use
\mathbb{P}_{1\leqslant j\leqslant n}(F)
to denote

\mathbb{P}_{1\leqslant j\leqslant n}(F)=\mathbb{E}_{1\leqslant j\leqslant n,\ell\sim\theta}\left(\int 1_{F}(\ell,\mathbf{i},x)\mathrm{d}(\mu_{\varphi(j,\mathbf{i})})_{\mathbf{I}}(x)\right).

Under this notation, due to the sets
\mathcal{E}_{2}(j)
and
\mathcal{E}_{4}(j)
independent of
x
, we have
\mathbb{P}_{1\leqslant j\leqslant n}(\mathcal{E}_{2}(j))=\mathbb{P}_{1\leqslant j\leqslant n,\ell\sim\theta}(\pi_{2}(\mathcal{E}_{2}(j)))
, similarly for
\mathcal{E}_{4}(j)
.
2
,
3
and
4
are the estimates of
\mathbb{P}_{1\leqslant j\leqslant n}(\mathcal{E}_{1}(j))
,
\mathbb{P}_{1\leqslant j\leqslant n}(\mathcal{E}_{2}(j))
and
\mathbb{P}_{1\leqslant j\leqslant n}(\mathcal{E}_{4}(j))
.

Therefore, we have

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{1\leqslant j\leqslant n,\ell\sim\theta}\left(\int\frac{1}{k}H(S_{t(\ell,x)}T_{[\ell(x)]}[\theta_{\ell,j}.x]*\mathfrak{m}_{2}(\ell,j,\mathbf{i}),\mathcal{Q}_{j+k})\mathrm{d}(\mu_{\varphi(j,\mathbf{i})})_{\mathbf{I}}(x),\mathcal{E}_{0}^{\prime}(j)\right)

\displaystyle\geqslant(\delta_{1}/2+\alpha-\epsilon_{1})\mathbb{P}_{1\leqslant j\leqslant n}(\mathcal{E}_{3}(j)\cap\mathcal{E}_{4}(j))+(\alpha-\epsilon_{1}-O(1/k))\mathbb{P}_{1\leqslant j\leqslant n}(\mathcal{E}_{4}(j)-\mathcal{E}_{3}(j))

\displaystyle\geqslant(\delta_{1}/2)\mathbb{P}_{1\leqslant j\leqslant n}(\mathcal{E}_{3}(j)\cap\mathcal{E}_{4}(j))+(\alpha-\epsilon_{1})\mathbb{P}_{1\leqslant j\leqslant n}(\mathcal{E}_{4}(j))-O(1/k)

where on
\mathcal{E}_{3}(j)\cap\mathcal{E}_{4}(j)
, we use their definition; on
\mathcal{E}_{4}(j)-\mathcal{E}_{3}(j)
, we estimate the integrand using the trivial lower bound
Eq.

5.1
.

For
\mathbb{P}_{1\leqslant j\leqslant n}(\mathcal{E}_{4}(j))
, we use
4
. For
\mathbb{P}_{1\leqslant j\leqslant n}(\mathcal{E}_{3}(j)\cap\mathcal{E}_{4}(j))
, we use
1
,
2
,
3
and
4
to obtain

\mathbb{P}_{1\leqslant j\leqslant n}(\mathcal{E}_{3}(j)\cap\mathcal{E}_{4}(j))\geqslant\mathbb{P}_{1\leqslant j\leqslant n}((\mathcal{E}_{1}(j)\cap\mathcal{E}_{2}(j))\cap\mathcal{E}_{4}(j))\geqslant\frac{\epsilon}{4C_{5}}-\epsilon^{\prime}-\epsilon_{1}\geqslant\frac{\epsilon}{10C_{5}}-\epsilon_{1},

where
\epsilon^{\prime}<\epsilon/10C_{5}
due to
Eq.

5.35
.
Therefore

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{1\leqslant j\leqslant n,\ell\sim\theta}\left(\int\frac{1}{k}H(S_{t(\ell,x)}T_{[\ell(x)]}[\theta_{\ell,j}.x]*\mathfrak{m}_{2}(\ell,j,\mathbf{i}),\mathcal{Q}_{j+k})\mathrm{d}(\mu_{\varphi(j,\mathbf{i})})_{\mathbf{I}}(x),\mathcal{E}_{0}^{\prime}(j)\right)

\displaystyle\geqslant(\delta_{1}/2)(\frac{\epsilon}{10C_{5}}-\epsilon_{1})+(\alpha-\epsilon_{1})(1-\epsilon_{1})-O(1/k)=\alpha+\frac{\delta_{1}\epsilon}{20C_{5}}-\epsilon_{1}(\alpha+\delta_{1})-O(1/k).

Then from
Eq.

5.28
, we obtain

\frac{1}{n}H([\theta.\mu],\mathcal{Q}_{t+n})\geqslant\alpha+\delta_{1}\epsilon/5C-\epsilon_{1}-O(\frac{k}{n}+r^{\beta}+\frac{\log(C_{1}/r)}{k}),

where
\delta_{1}\epsilon/C
is constant and other terms can be made arbitrarily small by taking
\epsilon_{1}
small, then
k
and
n/k
large and
1/r=\exp(\sqrt{k}/10)
.
∎

6
Exponential separation

Recall the exponential separation condition for
\nu
supported on
\mathrm{SL}_{3}(\mathbb{R})
: there exist
C>0
and
n_{C}\in\mathbb{N}
such that for any
n\geqslant n_{C}
any
g\neq g^{\prime}
in
\supp\nu^{*n}
, we have

d(g,g^{\prime})>1/C^{n},

where
d
is the left-invariant Riemannian metric on
\mathrm{SL}_{3}(\mathbb{R})
. Notice that from
2.11
, for any
g\in\mathrm{SL}_{3}(\mathbb{R})
such that
g^{-1}V\notin V^{\perp}
, the action of
\pi_{V^{\perp}}g
from
\mathbb{P}(\mathbb{R}^{3})
to
\mathbb{P}(V^{\perp})
can be identified by an element
\ell
in
L_{V}
.

Lemma 6.1
.

For a fixed
g\in\mathrm{SL}_{3}(\mathbb{R})
, the set

S(g):=\{V\in\mathbb{P}(\mathbb{R}^{3}):\ g^{-1}V\in V^{\perp}\}

is a subvariety of
\mathbb{P}(\mathbb{R}^{3})
whose image under
p_{2}

is contained in a hyperplane in
\mathbb{P}(Sym^{2}\mathbb{R}^{3})
, where
p_{2}
is defined in
2.25
.

Proof.

The set of such
V
is given by the zero locus of the equation

\langle g^{-1}v,v\rangle=0,

where
v
is a non-zero vector in
V
. For
v,w\in\mathbb{R}^{3}
let
f(v,w)=\langle g^{-1}v,w\rangle+\langle g^{-1}w,v\rangle
, which is non-trivial. Then due to
f(w,v)=f(v,w)
, the linear form
f
on
\mathbb{R}^{3}\otimes\mathbb{R}^{3}
induces a linear form on
Sym^{2}\mathbb{R}^{3}
. Since
\langle g^{-1}v,v\rangle=0
is equivalent to
f(v,v)=0
, we obtain the lemma.∎

For any
V\in\mathbb{P}(\mathbb{R}^{3})
, recall that we have a left-invariant Riemannian metric on
L_{V}
. The action of
\mathrm{SL}_{3}(\mathbb{R})
on row vectors
W
is by right multiplication. We say
W\in\mathbb{P}(V^{\perp})
if
W\cdot V=0
, where
W\cdot V
is the product of a unit row vector in
W
with a unit column vector in
V
. Recall from
Eq.

2.6
that
\pi_{L_{V}}
is a map from a dense open set of
\mathrm{SL}_{3}(\mathbb{R})
to
L_{V}
.

Lemma 6.2
.

There exists
c^{\prime}>0
such that for any
C>0
large enough, any
g\neq g^{\prime}
in
\mathrm{SL}_{3}(\mathbb{R})
with
d(g,g^{\prime})>1/C
, the set

\{V\in\mathbb{P}(\mathbb{R}^{3}):\ d(\pi_{L_{V}}g,\pi_{L_{V}}g^{\prime})<1/C^{4}\}

(6.1)

has diameter less than
c^{\prime}\|g\|^{6}/C
.

The following three lemmas are preparations for the proof of Lemma
6.2
.

Lemma 6.3
.

1.

There exists
\epsilon>0
such that for any
V
, we have for any
u\in U_{V}
and
\ell\in Z_{V}:=B(id,\epsilon)\subset L_{V}
,

d(\pi_{L_{V}}u\ell,\pi_{L_{V}}id)=d(\pi_{L_{V}}\ell,\pi_{L_{V}}id)\simeq\sup_{W\in\mathbb{P}(V^{\perp})}d(W\ell,W)=\sup_{W\in\mathbb{P}(V^{\perp})}d(Wu\ell,W).

Here the first two
d
are the left-invariant distance in
L_{V}
, and the last two
d
are the spherical distance on the projective space.

2.

There exists
\epsilon_{1}>0
independent of
V
such that for any
g\in B(\mathrm{id},\epsilon_{1})\subset\mathrm{SL}_{3}(\mathbb{R})
,

d(\pi_{L_{V}}g,\pi_{L_{V}}id)\simeq\sup_{W\in\mathbb{P}(V^{\perp})}d(Wg,W).

3.

There exists
\epsilon_{2}>0
independent of
V
such that for any
g\in\mathrm{SL}_{3}(\mathbb{R})
if

\sup_{W\in\mathbb{P}(V^{\perp})}d(Wg,W)\leqslant\epsilon_{2}

then

d(\pi_{L_{V}}g,\pi_{L_{V}}id)\ll\sup_{W\in\mathbb{P}(V^{\perp})}d(Wg,W).

Proof.

Without loss of generality, we can assume
V=E_{1}
. Recall that the element
\ell\in L
can be written as
\ell=\begin{pmatrix}\det h&0\\
n&h\end{pmatrix}
. For
\ell
close to identity, we have
h\in\mathrm{SL}(2,\mathbb{R})
. For
(0,w_{1},w_{2})
a unit row vector in
W\in V^{\perp}
, we denote
(w_{1},w_{2})
by
\mathbf{w}
. Then we have

d(W\ell,W)=\frac{\|(0,\mathbf{w})\ell\wedge(0,\mathbf{w})\|}{\|(0,\mathbf{w})\ell\|\|(0,\mathbf{w})\|}.

(6.2)

Since
\ell
is close to the identity, the vector
(0,\mathbf{w})\ell
has length close to 1. So we have

\displaystyle d(W\ell,W)

\displaystyle\simeq\|(0,\mathbf{w})\ell\wedge(0,\mathbf{w})\|\simeq\|\mathbf{w}\cdot n\|+\|\mathbf{w}h\wedge\mathbf{w}\|

\displaystyle\simeq\|\mathbf{w}\cdot n\|+d(\mathbb{R}(\mathbf{w}h),\mathbb{R}(\mathbf{w})).

Notice that the metric induced by the norm and the Riemannian distance are bi-Lipschitz equivalent in a neighborhood of the identity in
L
.
Therefore we have
d(W\ell,W)\leqslant\|n\|+\|h-id\|\simeq d(\ell,id)
.

On the other hand, we have

\sup_{\mathbf{w}}\|\mathbf{w}\cdot n\|=\|n\|.

For any triple of unit vectors
\mathbf{w}_{i},i=1,2,3
which are
1/10
separated,

d_{\mathrm{SL}_{2}(\mathbb{R})}(h,id)\ll\sup_{i=1,2,3}(d(\mathbb{R}(\mathbf{w}_{i}h),\mathbb{R}(\mathbf{w}_{i})))\leqslant\sup_{\mathbf{w}}d(\mathbb{R}(\mathbf{w}h),\mathbb{R}(\mathbf{w}))\leqslant\sup_{W\in\mathbb{P}(V^{\perp})}d(W\ell,W).

Here we use the fact that the map
h\mapsto(\mathbf{w}_{1}h,\mathbf{w}_{2}h,\mathbf{w}_{3}h),\mathrm{PSL}_{2}(\mathbb{R})\to\mathbb{P}(\mathbb{R}^{2})^{3}
is a smooth injection and bi-Lipschitz to its image on a small neighborhood of identity (see, for example Page 827 in
[
HS17
]
). Therefore

\sup_{W\in\mathbb{P}(V^{\perp})}d(W\ell,W)\gg d_{\mathrm{SL}_{2}(\mathbb{R})}(h,id)+\|n\|\gg d(\ell,id).

(6.3)

Since the group
U_{V}
acts trivially on the row vector on
V^{\perp}
, the first statement follows easily from
Eq.

6.3
.

The second statement is due to the fact that the image of the product map from
U_{V}\times Z_{V}
to
\mathrm{SL}_{3}(\mathbb{R})
contains a neighborhood of the identity.

For the last statement, we write
g=u\ell
.
The condition and
Eq.

6.2
imply that for any unit vector
\mathbf{w}
in
V^{\perp}

\|\mathbf{w}h\wedge\mathbf{w}\|+\|\mathbf{w}\cdot n\|\leqslant\epsilon_{2}\|(0,\mathbf{w})\ell\|\leqslant\epsilon_{2}(\|\mathbf{w}h\|+\|\mathbf{w}\cdot n\|).

(6.4)

Hence

\sup_{W\in V^{\perp}}d(W,Wh)\leqslant\epsilon_{2}

(6.5)

for the
h
part in
\pi_{L_{V}}g
. We can obtain the
h
close to
id
as follows: for any
\mathbb{R}v_{1},\mathbb{R}v_{2}\in V^{\perp}
with
d(\mathbb{R}v_{1},\mathbb{R}v_{2})\geqslant 4\epsilon_{2}
,
Eq.

6.5
gives “
\simeq
” in the following equation

1\simeq\frac{d(\mathbb{R}v_{1}h,\mathbb{R}v_{2}h)}{d(\mathbb{R}v_{1},\mathbb{R}v_{2})}=\frac{\|v_{1}\|\|v_{2}\|}{\|v_{1}h\|\|v_{2}h\|};

by taking unit vectors
v_{1}
such that
\|v_{1}h\|=\|h\|
, we obtain that
\|h\|
close to
1
; lastly, use Cartan decomposition to obtain
h
close to
id
. Using
Eq.

6.4
, we obtain that
\|n\|
is close to zero. If
\epsilon_{2}
is sufficiently small such that
\ell\in B(\mathrm{id},\epsilon)\subset L
, then we can use the first statement to conclude about
g
.
∎

Lemma 6.4
.

There exists a constant
c>0
such that the following holds. For any
V\in\mathbb{P}(\mathbb{R}^{3})
,
g\in\mathrm{SL}_{3}(\mathbb{R})
and
\epsilon>0
which is sufficiently small, if
d(id,g)>\epsilon^{1/3}
and
d(\pi_{L_{V}}id,\pi_{L_{V}}g)\leqslant\epsilon
, then for any
V^{\prime}
with
d(V^{\prime},V)\geqslant c\epsilon^{1/3}
, we have
d(\pi_{L_{V^{\prime}}}id,\pi_{L_{V^{\prime}}}g)>\epsilon
.

Proof.

Suppose that our lemma does not hold, i.e.
d(\pi_{L_{V^{\prime}}}id,\pi_{L_{V^{\prime}}}g)\leqslant\epsilon
for some
V^{\prime}
with
d(V^{\prime},V)\geqslant c\epsilon^{1/3}
, where the constant
c
will be defined at the end of the proof of the lemma. For simplicity, we can assume
V=E_{1}
and
V^{\prime}=\mathbb{R}(e_{1}+\delta e_{2})
where
|\delta|<1
and
\delta\geqslant d(V,V^{\prime})\geqslant c\epsilon^{1/3}
.

Consider the
UL
decomposition
g=u\ell
of
g
. We write
u=\begin{pmatrix}\lambda^{2}&x&y\\
0&\lambda^{-1}&0\\
0&0&\lambda^{-1}\end{pmatrix}.

The idea is to use the condition
d(\pi_{L_{V^{\prime}}}id,\pi_{L_{V^{\prime}}}g)\leqslant\epsilon
to obtain that the
u
part is also small, which contradicts the hypothesis
d(id,g)>\epsilon^{1/3}
.

We apply Lemma
6.3
(2) to
V^{\prime}=\mathbb{R}(e_{1}+\delta e_{2})
and
W=\mathbb{R}(\delta e_{1}^{t}-e_{2}^{t}),\ \mathbb{R}(\delta e_{1}^{t}-e_{2}^{t}+e_{3}^{t})\in(V^{\prime})^{\perp}
. Combined with
d(\pi_{L_{V^{\prime}}}id,\pi_{L_{V^{\prime}}}g)\leqslant\epsilon
, we obtain

\begin{split}d(\delta e_{1}^{t}-e_{2}^{t},(\delta e_{1}^{t}-e_{2}^{t})g)&\leqslant c_{2}\epsilon,\\
d(\delta e_{1}^{t}-e_{2}^{t}+e_{3}^{t},(\delta e_{1}^{t}-e_{2}^{t}+e_{3}^{t})g)&\leqslant c_{2}\epsilon.\end{split}

(6.6)

Due to
d_{L}(\ell,id)\leqslant\epsilon
, we have
d_{\mathrm{SL}_{3}(\mathbb{R})}(\ell,id)\leqslant O(\epsilon)
. Hence
\ell
moves any vector in
\mathbb{P}(\mathbb{R}^{3})
of distance at most
c_{3}\epsilon
for some
c_{3}>0
if
\epsilon
is small enough. Therefore by triangle inequality and
Eq.

6.6
we obtain a similar inequalities as
Eq.

6.6
for
u
:

\displaystyle d(\delta e_{1}^{t}-e_{2}^{t},\delta e_{1}^{t}+(-\lambda^{-3}+\delta x\lambda^{-2})e_{2}^{t}+\delta y\lambda^{-2}e_{3}^{t})

(6.7)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle d(\delta e_{1}^{t}-e_{2}^{t},(\delta e_{1}^{t}-e_{2}^{t})u)

\displaystyle\leqslant

\displaystyle d(\delta e_{1}^{t}-e_{2}^{t},(\delta e_{1}^{t}-e_{2}^{t})g)+d((\delta e_{1}^{t}-e_{2}^{t})u\ell,(\delta e_{1}^{t}-e_{2}^{t})u)

\displaystyle\leqslant

\displaystyle c_{2}\epsilon+c_{3}\epsilon\leqslant c_{4}\epsilon

for some constant
c_{4}>0
. Similarly, we get

d(\delta e_{1}^{t}-e_{2}^{t}+e_{3}^{t},\delta e_{1}^{t}+(-\lambda^{-3}+\delta x\lambda^{-2})e_{2}^{t}+(\delta y\lambda^{-2}+\lambda^{-3})e_{3}^{t})\leqslant c_{4}\epsilon.

(6.8)

We consider the following elementary geometric lemma.

Lemma 6.5
.

There exists
c_{5}>0
such that for any
\delta\geqslant 6c_{4}\epsilon
with
\epsilon
small enough, if
w,w^{\prime}\in\mathrm{Span}(e_{2}^{t},e_{3}^{t})
such that
1\leqslant\|w\|\leqslant 2
and
d_{\mathbb{P}(\mathbb{R}^{3})}(\delta e_{1}^{t}+w,\delta e_{1}^{t}+w^{\prime})<c_{4}\epsilon
, then

\|w-w^{\prime}\|\leqslant c_{5}\cdot\frac{\epsilon}{\delta}.

Proof.

Consider the triangle
A_{1}A_{2}A_{3}
contained in
\mathbb{R}^{3}
, where
A_{1}=0\in\mathbb{R}^{3}
,
A_{2}=\delta e_{1}^{t}+w
,
A_{3}=\delta e_{1}^{t}+w^{\prime}
, then by the sine rule we have

\frac{\|A_{2}A_{3}\|}{\|A_{1}A_{2}\|}=\frac{|\sin\angle A_{2}A_{1}A_{3}|}{|\sin\angle A_{1}A_{3}A_{2}|}.

(6.9)

By our assumption of
\epsilon,\delta
,

1\leqslant\|A_{1}A_{2}\|\leqslant 2,~~\|A_{2}A_{3}\|=\|w-w^{\prime}\|,~~|\sin\angle A_{2}A_{1}A_{3}|\leqslant c_{4}\epsilon.

From the third one and
w,w^{\prime}\in\mathrm{Span}(e_{2}^{t},e_{3}^{t})
we have

\delta\|w-w^{\prime}\|\leqslant\|(\delta e_{1}^{t}+w)\wedge(\delta e_{1}^{t}+w^{\prime})\|\leqslant c_{4}\epsilon\|\delta e_{1}^{t}+w\|\|\delta e_{1}^{t}+w^{\prime}\|\leqslant c_{4}\epsilon\cdot 3(1+\|w^{\prime}\|).

Therefore

(1-\frac{3c_{4}\epsilon}{\delta})\|w^{\prime}\|\leqslant\|w-w^{\prime}\|-\frac{3c_{4}\epsilon}{\delta}\|w^{\prime}\|+\|w\|\leqslant\frac{3c_{4}\epsilon}{\delta}+2.

This implies
\|w^{\prime}\|\leqslant 6,\text{ and }

|\sin\angle A_{1}A_{3}A_{2}|=\frac{\|(\delta e_{1}^{t}+w^{\prime})\wedge(w-w^{\prime})\|}{\|\delta e_{1}^{t}+w^{\prime}\|\|w-w^{\prime}\|}\geqslant\frac{\delta\|w-w^{\prime}\|}{\|\delta e_{1}^{t}+w^{\prime}\|\|w-w^{\prime}\|}\geqslant\delta/7.

Therefore by
Eq.

6.9

\|w-w^{\prime}\|\leqslant c_{5}\cdot\frac{\epsilon}{\delta}

for some
c_{5}>0
. ∎

Letting
(w,w^{\prime})
be
(-e_{2}^{t},(-\lambda^{-3}+\delta x\lambda^{-2})e_{2}^{t}+\delta y\lambda^{-2}e_{3}^{t})
in (
6.7
) and
(-e_{2}^{t}+e_{3}^{t},(-\lambda^{-3}+\delta x\lambda^{-2})e_{2}^{t}+(\delta y\lambda^{-2}+\lambda^{-3})e_{3}^{t})
in (
6.8
), applying Lemma
6.5
, we get

\displaystyle\|(1-\lambda^{-3}+\delta x\lambda^{-2})e_{2}^{t}+\delta y\lambda^{-2}e_{3}^{t}\|

\displaystyle\leqslant c_{5}\frac{\epsilon}{\delta},

(6.10)

\displaystyle\|(1-\lambda^{-3}+\delta x\lambda^{-2})e_{2}^{t}+(-1+\delta y\lambda^{-2}+\lambda^{-3})e_{3}^{t}\|

\displaystyle\leqslant c_{5}\frac{\epsilon}{\delta}.

(6.11)

Due to
\delta\geqslant c\epsilon^{1/3}
, by taking
c
large enough (compared to
100c_{i},i=2,\cdots,5
) and comparing the coefficients of
e_{3}^{t}
in
Eq.

6.10
and
Eq.

6.11
, we obtain
|\delta y\lambda^{-2}|,|1-\lambda^{-3}|\leqslant 2c_{5}\frac{\epsilon}{\delta}
, which implies
|\lambda-1|\leqslant\epsilon^{2/3}/10
and
|\delta y|\leqslant\epsilon^{2/3}/10
. From coefficients of
e_{2}^{t}
in
Eq.

6.10
and
Eq.

6.11
, we obtain
|\delta x|\leqslant\epsilon^{2/3}/10
. Therefore we obtain
|x|,|y|\leqslant\epsilon^{1/3}/10
and
|\lambda-1|\leqslant\epsilon^{1/3}/10
. Then we obtain a contradiction from
d(id,g)>\epsilon^{1/3}
if
\epsilon
is small enough.
∎

Proof of
6.2
.

By left-invariance of
d
on
\mathrm{SL}_{3}(\mathbb{R})
, we have
d(g,g^{\prime})=d(id,g^{-1}g^{\prime})
. Fix any
V
in the set defined in (
6.1
), we plan to obtain the information of
\pi_{L_{(g^{-1}V)}}g^{-1}g^{\prime}
and hence obtain
6.2
.
We consider the
U_{V}L_{V}
decomposition of
g,g^{\prime}
respectively:
g=u\ell
and
g^{\prime}=u^{\prime}\ell^{\prime}
. Then by (
6.1
) we have

d(\pi_{L_{V}}g,\pi_{L_{V}}g^{\prime})=d_{L_{V}}(\ell,\ell^{\prime})=d_{L_{V}}(id,\ell^{-1}\ell^{\prime})\leqslant 1/C^{4}.

This means the element
\ell^{-1}\ell^{\prime}
is close to
id
in both
L_{V}
and
\mathrm{SL}_{3}(\mathbb{R})
. Since we assume that
C
is large enough, we can assume
\ell^{-1}\ell^{\prime}
moves point on
\mathbb{P}(\mathbb{R}^{3})
with distance not greater than
C^{-7/2}
. For any row vector
W\in(\ell^{-1}V)^{\perp}
, which means
0=W\cdot(\ell^{-1}V)=(W\ell^{-1})\cdot V
, we have
W\ell^{-1}\in V^{\perp}
. Due to
g^{-1}g^{\prime}=\ell^{-1}u^{-1}u^{\prime}\ell^{\prime}
and the left action of
U_{V}
on column vectors in
\mathbb{P}(V^{\perp})
being trivial, so

\sup_{W\in(\ell^{-1}V)^{\perp}}d(W,Wg^{-1}g^{\prime})=\sup_{W\in(\ell^{-1}V)^{\perp}}d(W,W\ell^{-1}\ell^{\prime})<1/C^{7/2}.

(6.12)

(This is the main advantage of considering row vectors.) Due to the previous estimate (
Eq.

6.12
), we can apply
6.3
(3) to
g^{-1}g^{\prime}
and
\ell^{-1}V
and obtain

d(\pi_{L_{\ell^{-1}V}}id,\pi_{L_{\ell^{-1}V}}g^{-1}g^{\prime})\leqslant 1/C^{3}.

(6.13)

Then we can apply
6.4
to obtain that the set of
\ell^{-1}V=g^{-1}V
such that
Eq.

6.13
holds has radius less than
c^{\prime}/C
, where
c^{\prime}=2c
and
c
is the constant we get in
6.4
. So the set of
V
such that
Eq.

6.13
holds for
g^{-1}V
has radius less than
c^{\prime}\|g\|^{6}/C
, since the Lipschitz constant of
g
on projective space is bounded by
\|g\|^{6}
(See for example (13.2) in
[
BQ16
]
).
∎

Once
6.2
is available, we use an argument similar to that in
[
BHR19
]
,
[
HS17
]
and
[
Hoc14
]
to obtain the following main proposition of this section.

Proposition 6.6
.

Suppose that
\nu
satisfies the exponential separation condition. Then there exist
C>0
and a subset
Y\subset\mathbb{P}(\mathbb{R}^{3})
with
\mu^{-}
-measure zero such that for every
V\in\mathbb{P}(\mathbb{R}^{3})-Y
, there exists
N_{V}\in\mathbb{N}
such that for any
n\geqslant N_{V}
, for any
g\neq g^{\prime}\in\supp\nu^{*n}

d(\pi_{L_{V}}g,\pi_{L_{V}}g^{\prime})>C^{-n}.

Proof.

For
\mathbf{i},\mathbf{j}
in
\Lambda^{n}
with
g_{\mathbf{i}}\neq g_{\mathbf{j}}
, let

E_{\mathbf{i},\mathbf{j}}(C):=\{V\in\mathbb{P}(\mathbb{R}^{3}):\ d(\pi_{L_{V}}g_{\mathbf{i}},\pi_{L_{V}}g_{\mathbf{j}})<1/C^{n}\}.

We consider the set

E_{1}(C):=\bigcap_{m\in\mathbb{N}}\bigcup_{n\geqslant m}\bigcup_{\mathbf{i},\mathbf{j}\in\Lambda^{n}}E_{\mathbf{i},\mathbf{j}}(C).

Let
S
be the countable union of subvarieties
S(g)
given by
6.1
for
g\in\cup_{n\geqslant 1}\supp\nu^{*n}
. Then
S
has
\mu^{-}
-measure
0
.
For any
V
not in
E_{1}(C)\cup S
, there exists
N(V)\geqslant 1
such that for any
n\geqslant N(V)
and
g\neq g^{\prime}\in\supp(\nu^{*n})
, the elements
\pi_{L_{V}}g,\pi_{L_{V}}g^{\prime}
are well-defined, and
d(\pi_{L_{V}}g,\pi_{L_{V}}g^{\prime})\geqslant C^{-n}
. Hence we only need to show that
E_{1}(C)
has
\mu^{-}
measure
0
when
C
is large enough.

Guivarc’h theorem (
2.23
) gives Hölder regularity of
\mu^{-}
. Hence by the Frostman lemma, there exists
s(\mu^{-})>0
such that any subset of
\mathbb{P}(\mathbb{R}^{3})
with positive
\mu^{-}
-measure set has Hausdorff dimension at least
s(\mu^{-})
. Then it suffices to show that for any
s>0
, we can choose
C
large enough such that
\dim(E_{1}(C))\leqslant s
. We apply
6.2
to estimate the Hausdorff dimension of
E_{1}(C)
.

Fix an
s>0
small enough and write
\mathcal{H}^{s}_{\infty}
for the
s
-dimensional Hausdorff content on
\mathbb{P}(\mathbb{R}^{3})
. By exponential separation condition of
\nu
, for
n
large, we have
C^{\prime}
such that for any
g\neq g^{\prime}\in\supp\nu^{*n}
,
d(g,g^{\prime})>(C^{\prime})^{-n}
. Then for
C\gg C^{\prime}
we get

\displaystyle\mathcal{H}^{s}_{\infty}(E_{1}(C))

\displaystyle\leqslant

\displaystyle\lim_{N\to\infty}\sum_{n=N}^{\infty}\sum_{\mathbf{i},\mathbf{j}\in\Lambda^{n}}(\mathrm{diam}\{E_{\mathbf{i},\mathbf{j}}(C)\})^{s}

\displaystyle\leqslant

\displaystyle\lim_{N\to\infty}\sum_{n=N}^{\infty}\sum_{\mathbf{i},\mathbf{j}\in\Lambda^{n}}\left(\frac{\|g_{\mathbf{i}}\|^{6}c^{\prime}}{C^{n/4}}\right)^{s}

\displaystyle(\text{ apply \lx@cref{creftype\lx@tilde refnum}{lem:pi V bad} with $C$ large such that }d(g_{\mathbf{i}},g_{\mathbf{j}})>C^{-n/4})

\displaystyle\leqslant

\displaystyle\lim_{N\to\infty}\sum_{n=N}^{\infty}\sum_{\mathbf{i},\mathbf{j}\in\Lambda^{n}}(\frac{b}{C^{1/4}})^{sn}\text{ (here $b:=\sup_{g\in\supp\nu}\|g\|^{6}\cdot c^{\prime}$)}

\displaystyle\leqslant

\displaystyle\lim_{N\to\infty}\sum_{n=N}^{\infty}D^{2n}(\frac{b}{C^{1/4}})^{sn}\text{ (here $D:=\#\{\supp\nu\}$)}

\displaystyle\leqslant

\displaystyle 0\text{ (by taking $C$ large enough compare to $b,D$)}.

Therefore the Hausdorff dimension of
E_{1}(C)
is not greater than
s
for
C
sufficiently large.
∎

7
Proofs of Theorem
1.7
and Theorem
1.8

In this section, after preparations (
7.1
,
7.2
and
7.4
), we first apply Theorem
5.7
to show
1.8
, then combine it with a Lerappier-Young formula recently shown in
[
LL23a
]
and
[
Rap21
]
to conclude Theorem
1.7
.

7.1
Preparations: estimates for the entropy

Recall
\mathcal{Q}^{L_{V}}_{n}
is the
q-
adic partition of the group
L_{V}
. In the case that there is no ambiguity, we may write it as
\mathcal{Q}_{n}
. Consider the set

R:=\{V\in\mathbb{P}(\mathbb{R}^{3}):g^{-1}V\notin V^{\perp},\forall g\in\cup_{n\geqslant 0}\supp\nu^{\ast n}\}.

By
2.25
and
6.1
,
R
is a
\mu^{-}
-full measure set. Recall that
\pi_{L_{V}}
is a projection from
U_{V}L_{V}
to
L_{V}
defined in
Eq.

2.6
. Then for
\nu^{*n},n\geqslant 1
on
\mathrm{SL}_{3}(\mathbb{R})
and
V\in R
, the measure
\pi_{L_{V}}\nu^{*n}
is a well-defined measure on
L_{V}
. From the exponential separation condition, we have

Lemma 7.1
.

There exists
C>1
such that for
\mu^{-}
-almost every
V\in\mathbb{P}(\mathbb{R}^{3})
,

\lim_{n\rightarrow\infty}\frac{1}{n}H(\pi_{L_{V}}\nu^{*n},\mathcal{Q}^{L_{V}}_{Cn})=h_{\mathrm{RW}}(\nu).

Proof.

By Proposition
6.6
, there exists
C>0
such that for
V
in a full
\mu^{-}
-measure set, distinct atoms of
\pi_{L_{V}}\nu^{*n}
have distance at least
q^{-Cn/2}
from each other for any
n
large enough. On the other hand, the size of atoms of the partition
\mathcal{Q}_{Cn}
is about
q^{-Cn}
. So for
n
large, the atoms of
\pi_{L_{V}}\nu^{*n}
are located in different atoms in
\mathcal{Q}_{Cn}
. Therefore

\lim_{n\rightarrow\infty}\frac{1}{n}H(\pi_{L_{V}}\nu^{*n},\mathcal{Q}_{Cn})=\lim_{n\rightarrow\infty}\frac{1}{n}H(\pi_{L_{V}}\nu^{*n})=\lim_{n\rightarrow\infty}\frac{1}{n}H(\nu^{*n})=h_{\mathrm{RW}}(\nu),

where the second equality is due to the injectivity of the map
\pi_{L_{V}}
on
\supp\nu^{*n}
given in
6.6
.
∎

Lemma 7.2
.

Let
C>1
and
\tau>0
. Then for
\mu^{-}
-a.e.
V\in\mathbb{P}(\mathbb{R}^{3})
,

\limsup_{n\to\infty}\pi_{L_{V}}\nu^{*n}\{\ell\in L_{V}:\frac{1}{Cn}H(\pi_{V^{\perp}}\ell\mu,\mathcal{Q}_{(C+\chi_{1})n})>\alpha-\tau\}=1.

Proof.

Recall that for
g\in U_{V}L_{V}
,
\pi_{V^{\perp}}((\pi_{L_{V}}g)\mu)=\pi_{V^{\perp}}g\mu
, and the measure
\nu^{*n}
is the law of
{\mathbf{U}}(n)
. So it suffices to show that for any
\epsilon>0
, for
\mu^{-}
-almost every
V
,

\limsup_{n\to\infty}\mathbb{P}\left\{{\mathbf{U}}(n):\frac{1}{Cn}H(\pi_{V^{\perp}}g_{{\mathbf{U}}(n)}\mu,\mathcal{Q}_{(C+\chi_{1})n})>\alpha-\tau\right\}\geqslant 1-\epsilon.

(7.1)

Fix a small
\epsilon>0
in (
7.1
).
By
4.12
,
4.13
(let
\delta
in
4.13
to be
\tau/4
) and
Eq.

2.52
, there exists some small
r=r(\epsilon,\tau)>0
such that for any
n
large, we can construct a set
X_{n,\epsilon}
of
{\mathbf{U}}(n)
such that

1.

\mathbb{P}({\mathbf{U}}(n)\in X_{n,\epsilon})>1-\epsilon/2
;

2.

For any
{\mathbf{U}}(n)\in X_{n,\epsilon}
,
|\chi_{1}(g_{{\mathbf{U}}(n)})-\chi_{1}n|\leqslant\tau n/10
;

3.

For any
{\mathbf{U}}(n)\in X_{n,\epsilon},n\geqslant N(r)
, the
r
-attracting part
(\pi_{V^{\perp}}g_{{\mathbf{U}}(n)}\mu)_{{\mathbf{I}}}
of the pair
(h_{V,{{\mathbf{U}}(n)}},\pi_{V,{\mathbf{U}}(n)}\mu)

satisfies

\frac{1}{Cn}H((\pi_{V^{\perp}}g_{{\mathbf{U}}(n)}\mu)_{{\mathbf{I}}},\mathcal{Q}_{Cn+\chi_{1}(g_{{\mathbf{U}}(n)})})\geqslant\frac{1}{Cn}H(\pi_{(g_{{\mathbf{U}}(n)}^{-1}V)^{\perp}}\mu,\mathcal{Q}_{Cn})-\frac{\tau}{10},

(7.2)

and
\pi_{V^{\perp}}g_{{\mathbf{U}}(n)}\mu=(1-\tilde{\delta})(\pi_{V^{\perp}}g_{{\mathbf{U}}(n)}\mu)_{\mathbf{I}}+\tilde{\delta}(\pi_{V^{\perp}}g_{{\mathbf{U}}(n)}\mu)_{{\mathbf{I}}{\mathbf{I}}}
with
\tilde{\delta}\leqslant\tau/4
.

By the 2nd condition of
X_{n,\epsilon}
and
Eq.

2.77
, for
{\mathbf{U}}(n)\in X_{n,\epsilon}
,
replacing
\chi_{1}(g_{{\mathbf{U}}(n)})
by
\chi_{1}n
in LFS of
Eq.

7.2
changes the value of LFS less than
\tau/5
. So we obtain

\frac{1}{Cn}H((\pi_{V^{\perp}}g_{{\mathbf{U}}(n)}\mu)_{{\mathbf{I}}},\mathcal{Q}_{(C+\chi_{1})n})\geqslant\frac{1}{Cn}H(\pi_{(g_{{\mathbf{U}}(n)}^{-1}V)^{\perp}}\mu,\mathcal{Q}_{Cn})-\frac{\tau}{2}.

Then by the concavity of entropy, for
{\mathbf{U}}(n)\in X_{n,\epsilon}

\displaystyle\frac{1}{Cn}H(\pi_{V^{\perp}}g_{{\mathbf{U}}(n)}\mu,\mathcal{Q}_{(C+\chi_{1})n})

\displaystyle\geqslant

\displaystyle(1-\tilde{\delta})\frac{1}{Cn}H((\pi_{V^{\perp}}g_{{\mathbf{U}}(n)}\mu)_{{\mathbf{I}}},\mathcal{Q}_{(C+\chi_{1})n})

(7.3)

\displaystyle\geqslant

\displaystyle(1-\tilde{\delta})(\frac{1}{Cn}H(\pi_{(g_{{\mathbf{U}}(n)}^{-1}V)^{\perp}}\mu,\mathcal{Q}_{Cn})-\frac{\tau}{2})

\displaystyle\geqslant

\displaystyle\frac{1}{Cn}H(\pi_{(g_{{\mathbf{U}}(n)}^{-1}V)^{\perp}}\mu,\mathcal{Q}_{Cn})-\frac{3\tau}{4},

where the last inequality is due to
\tilde{\delta}\leqslant\tau/4
.

On the other hand, we can show the following lemma, which is similar to Lemma 6.2 in
[
BHR19
]
.

Lemma 7.3
.

Let
\tau>0
,
\epsilon>0
. For
\mu^{-}
-a.e.
V\in\mathbb{P}(\mathbb{R}^{3})
, there exists a sequence
n_{k}\to\infty
such that

\mathbb{P}\left\{{\mathbf{U}}(n_{k}):\frac{1}{Cn_{k}}H(\pi_{(g_{{\mathbf{U}}(n_{k})}^{-1}V)^{\perp}}\mu,\mathcal{Q}_{Cn_{k}})>\alpha-\tau/10\right\}>1-\epsilon/10.

Proof.

Let
f_{n}(\omega,V)
be a function on
\Lambda^{\mathbb{N}}\times\mathbb{P}(\mathbb{R}^{3})
defined by

f_{n}(\omega,V)=\begin{cases}1,\ \text{ if }\frac{1}{Cn}H(\pi_{V^{\perp}}\mu,\mathcal{Q}_{Cn})>\alpha-\tau/10\\
0,\ \text{ otherwise.}\end{cases}

By exact dimensionality of
\pi_{V^{\perp}}\mu
,
f_{n}
converges
\nu^{\otimes\mathbb{N}}\otimes\mu^{-}
-a.e. to
1
as
n\to\infty
(
2.37
).

We define a skew-product transformation
T
on
\Lambda^{\mathbb{N}}\times\mathbb{P}(\mathbb{R}^{3})
by

T(\omega,V)=(T\omega,g_{\omega_{1}}^{-1}V),

where
T\omega
is the shift map given by
(T\omega)_{j}=\omega_{j+1}
. Due to stationarity of
\mu^{-}
under
\nu^{-}
, the measure
\nu^{\otimes\mathbb{N}}\otimes\mu^{-}
is
T
-invariant. Moreover, because
\mu^{-}
is the unique
\nu^{-}
-stationary measure,
\nu^{\otimes\mathbb{N}}\otimes\mu^{-}
is also
T
-ergodic, see for example
[
BQ16
, Proposition 2.23]
.

Therefore by Maker’s ergodic theorem, for
\nu^{\otimes\mathbb{N}}\otimes\mu^{-}
-a.e.
(\omega,V)
, we have

\frac{1}{N}\sum_{n=1}^{N}f_{n}(T^{n}(\omega,V))\to 1\,\,\text{as}\,\,N\to\infty.

Integrating the above equation over the
\omega
-component, by dominated convergence theorem, we get for
\mu^{-}
-a.e.
V
,

\frac{1}{N}\sum_{n=1}^{N}\mathbb{P}\left\{{\mathbf{U}}(n):\frac{1}{Cn}H(\pi_{(g_{{\mathbf{U}}(n)}^{-1}V)^{\perp}}\mu,\mathcal{Q}_{Cn})>\alpha-\tau/10\right\}\to 1\,\,\text{as}\,\,N\to\infty.

Therefore, we can find a sequence
n_{k}\to\infty
such that

\mathbb{P}\left\{{\mathbf{U}}(n_{k}):\frac{1}{Cn_{k}}H(\pi_{(g_{{\mathbf{U}}(n_{k})}^{-1}V)^{\perp}}\mu,\mathcal{Q}_{Cn_{k}})>\alpha-\tau/10\right\}>1-\epsilon/10.\qed

Combine Lemma
7.3
with (
7.3
).
Let
\{n_{k}\}
be the infinite sequence given in
7.3
. For all large
n_{k}
,
\frac{1}{Cn}H(\pi_{V^{\perp}}g_{{\mathbf{U}}(n)}\mu,\mathcal{Q}_{(C+\chi_{1})n})>\alpha-\tau
holds for a set of
{\mathbf{U}}(n_{k})
with measure greater than
1-\epsilon
. Hence

\mathbb{P}\left\{{\mathbf{U}}(n_{k}):\frac{1}{Cn_{k}}H(\pi_{V^{\perp}}g_{{\mathbf{U}}(n_{k})}\mu,\mathcal{Q}_{(C+\chi_{1})n_{k}})\geqslant\alpha-\tau\right\}>1-\epsilon.\qed

Lemma 7.4
.

For
\mu^{-}
-a.e.
V\in\mathbb{P}(\mathbb{R}^{3})
, we have

\lim_{n\rightarrow\infty}\frac{1}{n}H(\pi_{L_{V}}\nu^{*n},\mathcal{Q}_{1}^{L_{V}})\leqslant\alpha\chi_{1}(\nu).

Proof.

By
2.37
, for
\mu^{-}
- a.e.
V
we have

\lim_{n\to\infty}\frac{1}{\chi_{1}n}H(\pi_{V^{\perp}}\mu,\mathcal{Q}_{\chi_{1}n})\rightarrow\alpha.

(7.4)

Fix any
V
for which
Eq.

7.4
holds. By conjugating with an element in
\mathrm{SO}_{3}(\mathbb{R})
, we may assume
V=E_{1}
.

Let
X_{i}
be matrix-valued i.i.d. random variables with the law
\nu
. Denote by
Z_{n}(\omega):=X_{1}(\omega)\cdots X_{n}(\omega),\omega\in\Lambda^{\times\mathbb{N}}
. By Furstenberg’s theorem (see for example
[
BQ16
, Proposition 4.7]
), the following Furstenberg boundary map is well-defined for
\nu^{\otimes\mathbb{N}}
-a.e.
\omega

\xi:\Lambda^{\times\mathbb{N}}\to\mathbb{P}(\mathbb{R}^{3}),\,\,\xi(\omega)\text{ is the unique point such that }\delta_{\xi(\omega)}=\lim_{n\to\infty}Z_{n}(\omega)_{\ast}\mu,

and satisfies that
\mu=\mathbb{E}(\delta_{\xi(\omega)})
and
\xi(\omega)=X_{1}(\omega)\xi(T\omega),\nu^{\otimes\mathbb{N}}
-a.e.

\omega
, where
T:\Lambda^{\times\mathbb{N}}\to\Lambda^{\times\mathbb{N}}
is the shift map.

For each element
g\in\mathrm{SL}_{3}(\mathbb{R})
, we consider its
UL
-decomposition:
g=u\ell=u\begin{pmatrix}\det h&0\\
n&h\end{pmatrix}
with
u\in U
and
\ell\in L
.
Recall
h^{+}
is the contracting point of the
h
part of
\ell
in
\mathbb{P}({E_{1}}^{\perp})
. Let
v_{n}(\omega)=h(Z_{n}(\omega))^{+}
. Then we can state the following exponential convergence lemma.

Lemma 7.5
.

For any
\epsilon>0
, there exists
c(\epsilon)>0
such that

\mathbb{P}\{\omega\in\Lambda^{\times\mathbb{N}}:d_{\mathbb{P}(\mathbb{R}^{2})}(\pi_{{E_{1}}^{\perp}}\xi(\omega),v_{n})<q^{-(\chi_{1}-\epsilon)n}\}=1-O(e^{-c(\epsilon)n}).

(7.5)

Proof.

Fix any
x\in\mathbb{P}(E_{1}^{\perp})
and let
w_{n}(\omega)=Z_{n}(\omega)x
in
\mathbb{P}(\mathbb{R}^{3})
.

Claim
.

\mathbb{P}\{\omega\in\Lambda^{\times\mathbb{N}}:d_{\mathbb{P}(\mathbb{R}^{3})}(\xi(\omega),w_{n})<q^{-(\chi_{1}-\epsilon/10)n}\}=1-O(e^{-c(\epsilon)n}).

Proof.

Due to the equivariance of
\xi
, we have

d(\xi(\omega),w_{n})=d(Z_{n}(\omega)\xi(T^{n}\omega),Z_{n}(\omega)x).

By
2.4
, if we have

\chi_{1}(Z_{n}(\omega))>(\chi_{1}-\epsilon/20)n,\ d(\xi(T^{n}\omega),H_{Z_{n}(\omega)}^{-})>q^{-\epsilon n/40}\,\,\text{and}\,\,d(x,H_{Z_{n}(\omega)}^{-})>q^{-\epsilon n/40},

(7.6)

then we have

d(Z_{n}(\omega)\xi(T^{n}\omega),Z_{n}(\omega)x)<q^{-\chi_{1}(Z_{n}(\omega))+\epsilon n/20}\leqslant q^{-(\chi_{1}-\epsilon/10)n}.

Notice that the random variable
Z_{n}(\omega)
has the law
\nu^{*n}
. Then for fixed
\xi(T^{n}w)
, by
Eqs.

2.52
and
2.50
we know
Eq.

7.6
holds with
\nu^{*n}
-probability greater than
1-O(e^{-cn})
. Since
T^{n}\omega
and
Z_{n}(\omega)
are independent, we obtain the claim by applying a Fubini argument.
∎

We return to the proof of
Eq.

7.5
. Observe that

d_{\mathbb{P}(\mathbb{R}^{2})}(\pi_{{E_{1}}^{\perp}}\xi(\omega),v_{n})\leqslant d_{\mathbb{P}(\mathbb{R}^{2})}(\pi_{{E_{1}}^{\perp}}\xi(\omega),\pi_{E_{1}^{\perp}}w_{n})+d_{\mathbb{P}(\mathbb{R}^{2})}(\pi_{E_{1}^{\perp}}w_{n},v_{n}).

(7.7)

It suffices to show for each term on RHS of (
7.7
), the probability that it is less than
\frac{1}{2}q^{-(\chi_{1}-\epsilon)n}
is at least
