(1-O(e^{-c(\epsilon)n}))
.

For the term
d_{\mathbb{P}(\mathbb{R}^{2})}(\pi_{{E_{1}}^{\perp}}\xi(\omega),\pi_{E_{1}^{\perp}}w_{n})
, note that the distribution of
\xi(\omega)
is
\mu
. It follows from
2.23
that we have
d(\xi(\omega),E_{1})>q^{-\epsilon n/10}
with probability at least
(1-O(e^{-c(\epsilon)n}))
. Combining the claim with
B.2
, we get that with probability greater than
(1-O(e^{-c(\epsilon)n}))
,

d_{\mathbb{P}(\mathbb{R}^{2})}(\pi_{{E_{1}}^{\perp}}\xi(\omega),\pi_{E_{1}^{\perp}}w_{n})<q^{-(\chi_{1}-\epsilon/10)n}\cdot q^{\epsilon n/10}<\frac{1}{2}q^{-(\chi_{1}-\epsilon)n},\text{ for $n$ large enough.}

For the term
d_{\mathbb{P}(\mathbb{R}^{2})}(\pi_{E_{1}^{\perp}}w_{n},v_{n})
, notice that
v_{n}=h(Z_{n}(\omega))^{+}
and
\pi_{{E_{1}}^{\perp}}w_{n}=\pi_{E_{1}^{\perp}}Z_{n}(\omega)x=h(Z_{n}(\omega))x
(use the
UL
-decomposition and the assumption
x\in\mathbb{P}(E_{1}^{\perp})
). Then by
Eq.

2.66
, with probability greater than
(1-O(e^{-c(\epsilon)n}))
,

d_{\mathbb{P}(\mathbb{R}^{2})}(v_{n},\pi_{{E_{1}}^{\perp}}w_{n})<q^{-(\chi_{1}-\epsilon/10)n}<\frac{1}{2}q^{-(\chi_{1}-\epsilon)n},\text{ for $n$ large enough,}

which completes the proof of Lemma
7.5
.∎

Now we go back to the proof of Lemma
7.4
. By (
7.4
), Lemma 4.4 of
[
HS17
]
and
Eq.

7.5
, we have

|\frac{1}{\chi_{1}n}H(\tau_{n},\mathcal{Q}_{\chi_{1}n})-\alpha|\to 0,

(7.8)

here the measure
\tau_{n}
is the distribution of
v_{n}
on
\mathbb{P}(E_{1}^{\perp})
.

We define a map
\Phi
from
L
to
\mathbb{P}(E_{1}^{\perp})\cong\mathbb{P}(\mathbb{R}^{2})
by mapping
\ell=\begin{pmatrix}\det h&0\\
n&h\end{pmatrix}
to
h^{+}
. (If
h\in\mathrm{SO}_{2}(\mathbb{R})
, we take
h^{+}=E_{2}
.) Then
\Phi(\pi_{L}\nu^{*n})
is the distribution of
v_{n}
and
\tau_{n}=\Phi(\pi_{L}\nu^{*n})
.
Recall that the set
L(n,\epsilon)\subset L
is defined in
Eq.

2.67
. Then we have

Lemma 7.6
.

For any atom
E
in the partition
\mathcal{Q}_{\chi_{1}n}
of
\mathbb{P}(\mathbb{R}^{2})
,
\Phi^{-1}(E)\cap L(n,\epsilon)
intersects at most
O(q^{10\epsilon n})
number of atoms in
\mathcal{Q}_{1}^{L}
.

Proof.

From the definition of
L(n,\epsilon)
, for an atom
E
in
\mathcal{Q}_{\chi_{1}n}
, either
L(n,\epsilon)\cap\Phi^{-1}E=\emptyset
or we could pick a block diagonal element
\ell=\begin{pmatrix}1&0\\
0&h\end{pmatrix}\in L(n,\epsilon)\cap\Phi^{-1}E
.
Our lemma is trivial if
L(n,\epsilon)\cap\Phi^{-1}E=\emptyset
. Suppose
L(n,\epsilon)\cap\Phi^{-1}E\neq\emptyset
, then for any
7
7

7

For
\ell^{\prime}=\begin{pmatrix}-1&0\\
n^{\prime}&h^{\prime}\end{pmatrix}
, we can find some element
\ell=\begin{pmatrix}-1&0\\
0&h\end{pmatrix}
and do the same argument.
\ell^{\prime}=\begin{pmatrix}1&0\\
n^{\prime}&h^{\prime}\end{pmatrix}\in L(n,\epsilon)\cap\Phi^{-1}E

we have

\ell^{-1}\ell^{\prime}=\begin{pmatrix}1&0\\
h^{-1}n^{\prime}&h^{-1}h^{\prime}\end{pmatrix}.

Take the Cartan decomposition of
h,h^{\prime}
,
h=k_{h}a_{h}l_{h}
and
h^{\prime}=k_{h^{\prime}}a_{h^{\prime}}l_{h^{\prime}}
. Since
h^{+},(h^{\prime})^{+}\in E
, we have

d(k_{h}^{-1}k_{h^{\prime}},id)=d(h^{+},(h^{\prime})^{+})\leqslant q^{-\chi_{1}n}.

By definition of
L(n,\epsilon)
,
\chi_{1}(h),\chi_{1}(h^{\prime})\leqslant(\chi_{1}+\epsilon)n
,

\|h^{-1}h^{\prime}\|=\|l_{h}^{-1}a_{h}^{-1}(k_{h}^{-1}k_{h^{\prime}})a_{h^{\prime}}l_{h^{\prime}}\|=\|a_{h}^{-1}(k_{h}^{-1}k_{h^{\prime}})a_{h^{\prime}}\|\leqslant q^{\frac{1}{2}(\chi_{1}+\epsilon)n}\cdot O(q^{-\chi_{1}n})\cdot q^{\frac{1}{2}(\chi_{1}+\epsilon)n}\leqslant O(q^{2\epsilon n}).

Recall a basic property of left-invariant and right
\mathrm{SO}(2)
-invariant Riemannian metric on
\mathrm{SL}_{2}(\mathbb{R})
i.e. for diagonal matrix we have
d(\mathrm{diag}(a,a^{-1}),id)=2\log|a|

[
Bry
]
. Combining it with the Cartan decomposition, we get

d_{L}(h^{-1}h^{\prime},id)\leqslant 1+2\log\|h^{-1}h^{\prime}\|\leqslant 4\epsilon n.

We view
h
as the matrix
\begin{pmatrix}1&0\\
0&h\end{pmatrix}
in
L
and similarly for
h^{\prime}
. We then have

\displaystyle d_{L}(\ell^{-1}\ell^{\prime},id)\leqslant d_{L}(\ell^{-1}\ell^{\prime},h^{-1}h^{\prime})+d_{L}(h^{-1}h^{\prime},id)=d_{L}((h^{\prime})^{-1}h\ell^{-1}\ell^{\prime},id)+d_{L}(h^{-1}h^{\prime},id)

\displaystyle=d_{L}(\begin{pmatrix}1&0\\
(h^{\prime})^{-1}n^{\prime}&id_{2}\end{pmatrix},id)+d_{L}(h^{-1}h^{\prime},id)\ll\|(h^{\prime})^{-1}n^{\prime}\|+d(h^{-1}h^{\prime},id)\leqslant O(q^{2\epsilon n}),

where we used
\|(h^{\prime})^{-1}n^{\prime}\|\leqslant 1/d(\ell^{-1}E_{1},E_{1}^{\perp})
(
Eq.

2.14
) and the definition of
L(n,\epsilon)
.
Therefore by our choice of
\ell^{\prime}
,
\ell^{-1}(L(n,\epsilon)\cap\Phi^{-1}E)
is contained in a
neighborhood of
id
in
L
with radius
O(q^{2\epsilon n})
. By left invariance of
d_{L}
and
\dim L=5
, we get
\ell^{-1}(L(n,\epsilon)\cap\Phi^{-1}E)
intersects at most
O(q^{10\epsilon n})
number of atoms in
\mathcal{Q}_{1}^{L}
.
∎

We are ready to prove
7.4
. To simplify the notation, let
\nu_{1}=\pi_{L}\nu^{*n}|_{L(n,\epsilon)},\nu_{2}=\pi_{L}\nu^{*n}|_{L(n,\epsilon)^{c}}
. We take the decomposition
\pi_{L}\nu^{*n}=(1-\delta)\nu_{1}+\delta\nu_{2}
, with
\delta=\pi_{L}\nu^{*n}(L(n,\epsilon)^{c})
. Denote
\Phi\nu_{i}
by
m_{i}
,
i=1,2
, then
\tau_{n}=\Phi(\pi_{L}\nu^{*n})=(1-\delta)m_{1}+\delta m_{2}
. Since
\supp\nu_{1}\subset L(n,\epsilon)
, by
7.6
and
2.33
we obtain

\displaystyle\frac{1}{n}H(\nu_{1},\mathcal{Q}_{1}^{L})

\displaystyle\leqslant\frac{1}{n}H(\nu_{1},\Phi^{-1}\mathcal{Q}_{\chi_{1}n})+O(\epsilon)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{n}H(\Phi\nu_{1},\mathcal{Q}_{\chi_{1}n})+O(\epsilon)=\frac{1}{n}H(m_{1},\mathcal{Q}_{\chi_{1}n})+O(\epsilon).

Combined with
Eq.

2.79
,

\displaystyle H(\pi_{L}\nu^{*n},\mathcal{Q}_{1}^{L})

\displaystyle\leqslant(1-\delta)H(\nu_{1},\mathcal{Q}_{1}^{L})+\delta H(\nu_{2},\mathcal{Q}_{1}^{L})+H(\delta)

\displaystyle\leqslant(1-\delta)H(m_{1},\mathcal{Q}_{\chi_{1}n})+O(n\epsilon)+\delta H(\nu_{2},\mathcal{Q}_{1}^{L})+H(\delta)

\displaystyle\leqslant H(\tau_{n},\mathcal{Q}_{\chi_{1}n})+O(n\epsilon)+\delta H(\nu_{2},\mathcal{Q}_{1}^{L})+H(\delta).

The measure
\nu_{2}
is supported on a ball of radius
q^{C_{\nu}n}
due to compact support of
\nu
, so
H(\nu_{2},\mathcal{Q}_{1}^{L})\leqslant O(C_{\nu}n)
. Due to
\delta=O(q^{-c(\epsilon)n})
(
Eq.

2.69
), we obtain

\frac{1}{n}H(\pi_{L}\nu^{*n},\mathcal{Q}_{1}^{L})\leqslant\frac{1}{n}H(\tau_{n},\mathcal{Q}_{\chi_{1}n})+O(\epsilon+\delta)\leqslant\frac{1}{n}H(\tau_{n},\mathcal{Q}_{\chi_{1}n})+O(\epsilon+q^{-c(\epsilon)n}).

The proof can be completed by
Eq.

7.8
and using the fact
that
\epsilon
can be arbitrarily small.
∎

7.2
Proof of Theorem
1.8

We start with a contradiction hypothesis that for
\mu^{-}
-a.e.
V
we have

\alpha=\dim\pi_{V^{\perp}}\mu<\min\left\{1,\frac{h_{\mathrm{RW}}(\nu)}{\chi_{1}(\nu)}\right\}.

(7.9)

and
\pi_{V^{\perp}}\mu
is of exact dimension (
2.37
).
We fix any
V\in\mathbb{P}(\mathbb{R}^{3})
satisfying
Eq.

7.9
,
7.1
,
7.2
and
7.4
. Without loss of generality, using conjugation with some element
k
in
SO(3)
, that is replacing
\nu
by
k\nu k^{-1}
, we can assume
V=E_{1}
.

Lower bound of entropy: application of entropy growth
5.7
and contradiction hypothesis:

Due to
7.1
and
7.4
, we have that for any large
n
,

\displaystyle\frac{1}{n}H(\pi_{L}\nu^{*n},\mathcal{Q}_{Cn}|\mathcal{Q}_{1})=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{n}(H(\pi_{L}\nu^{*n},\mathcal{Q}_{Cn})-H(\pi_{L}\nu^{*n},\mathcal{Q}_{1}))

\displaystyle\geqslant

\displaystyle(h_{\mathrm{RW}}(\nu)-\alpha\chi_{1}(\nu))/2.

Observe that

\frac{1}{n}H(\pi_{L}\nu^{*n},\mathcal{Q}_{Cn}|\mathcal{Q}_{1})=\mathbb{E}_{i=1}\left(\frac{1}{n}H((\pi_{L}\nu^{*n})_{\ell,i},\mathcal{Q}_{Cn})\right).

By Chebyshev’s inequality, there exists
\epsilon>0
such that for any
n
large enough

\mathbb{P}_{i=1}\left(\frac{1}{n}H((\pi_{L}\nu^{*n})_{\ell,i},\mathcal{Q}_{Cn})>\epsilon\right)>\epsilon.

(7.10)

We fix such
\epsilon
in the proof of Theorem
1.8
. We will see later
Eq.

7.10
is actually the source of positive entropy to apply the entropy growth argument. Denote by
\theta=\pi_{L}\nu^{*n}
and
\mathcal{U}_{0}=\mathcal{U}_{0}(n):=\{\ell\in L:\frac{1}{n}H(\theta_{\ell,1},\mathcal{Q}_{Cn})>\epsilon\}
, then

7.10
is equivalent to
\pi_{L}\nu^{\ast n}(\mathcal{U}_{0}(n))>\epsilon
for
n
large.

Consider the subset
\mathcal{U}_{0}^{\prime}=\mathcal{U}_{0}^{\prime}(n,C_{1}^{\prime})
of
\mathcal{U}_{0}
defined by

\mathcal{U}_{0}^{\prime}(n,C_{1}^{\prime}):=\{\ell\in\mathcal{U}_{0}(n):d(\ell^{-1}E_{1},E_{1}^{\perp})>1/C_{1}^{\prime}\}

The constant
C_{1}^{\prime}
is chosen such that
C_{1}^{\prime-\beta}<\epsilon/8
, where
\beta
is given in
Eq.

2.58
.
Then by
Eq.

2.58
and
\pi_{L}\nu^{\ast n}(\mathcal{U}_{0}(n))>\epsilon
, for any
n
large enough we have

\pi_{L}\nu^{\ast n}(\mathcal{U}_{0}^{\prime}(n,C_{1}^{\prime}))>7\epsilon/8

(7.11)

Now we try to apply
5.7
using
\mathcal{U}_{0}^{\prime}
. For any
\ell\in\mathcal{U}_{0}^{\prime}(n,C_{1}^{\prime})
, for the component measure
\theta_{\ell,1}
we have

•

\diam(\theta_{\ell,1})\leqslant\diam(\mathcal{Q}_{1}(\ell))\leqslant 1<C_{p}
;

•

for any
\ell^{\prime}\in\supp\theta_{\ell,1}\subset\mathcal{Q}_{1}(\ell)
,
|\chi_{1}(h(\ell^{\prime}))-\chi_{1}(h(\ell))|\leqslant 1
by our choice of
q
(and the choice of constants
C_{p},C_{L}
), see
2.30
and
2.14
.

•

for any
\ell^{\prime}\in\supp\theta_{\ell,1}\subset\mathcal{Q}_{1}(\ell)
,

d(\ell^{\prime-1}E_{1},E_{1}^{\perp})\geqslant 1/2d(\ell^{-1}E_{1},E_{1}^{\perp})\geqslant 1/2C_{1}^{\prime}

(7.12)

•

\frac{1}{n}H(\theta_{\ell,1},\mathcal{Q}_{Cn})>\epsilon
since
\ell\in\mathcal{U}_{0}
.

Therefore for
\ell\in\mathcal{U}_{0}^{\prime}
, we can apply
5.7
to
\theta_{\ell,1}
and
\mu
with
C_{1}=2C_{1}^{\prime}
and contracting rate

: for

large enough,

\frac{1}{Cn}H([\theta_{\ell,1}.\mu],\mathcal{Q}_{Cn+\chi_{1}(h(\ell))})>\alpha+\delta,

.

By

2.64
and (

), for any

, for

\pi_{L}\nu^{\ast n}(\mathcal{U}_{0}^{\prime\prime}(n,C_{1}^{\prime},\epsilon_{1}))>3\epsilon/4
. Fix a positive number
\epsilon_{1}
much smaller than
\delta
, then by
Eq.

2.77
, for any
\ell\in\mathcal{U}_{0}^{\prime\prime}
we have

\begin{split}\frac{1}{Cn}H([\theta_{\ell,1}.\mu],\mathcal{Q}_{Cn+\chi_{1}n})\geqslant\frac{1}{Cn}H([\theta_{\ell,1}.\mu],\mathcal{Q}_{Cn+\chi_{1}(h(\ell))})-O(\frac{\epsilon_{1}}{C})>\alpha+\delta-O(\frac{\epsilon_{1}}{C})>\alpha+\delta/2.\end{split}

(7.13)

We set
\mathcal{U}_{1}(n)=\mathcal{U}_{1}:=\{\ell\in L:\,\,\ell\,\,\text{satisfies}\,\,\lx@cref{creftype\lx@tilde refnum}{equ:nu n growth}\}
. Then by the discussion above about
\mathcal{U}_{0}^{\prime\prime}
, for
n
large enough, the
\theta
-measure of
\mathcal{U}_{1}
greater than
\epsilon/2
, and
\mathcal{U}_{1}
consists of atoms of
\mathcal{Q}_{1}
.

Due to
7.2
, for any
\tau>0
, there exists an infinite sequence
\{n_{k}\}
and a sequence of subset
\mathcal{U}_{2}=\mathcal{U}_{2}(n_{k})\subset L
such that for each

,
\mathcal{U}_{2}(n_{k})
has

-probability greater than

, and for any

\frac{1}{Cn}H([\ell\mu],\mathcal{Q}_{(C+\chi_{1})n})>\alpha-\tau.

(7.14)

Take
n
large enough to be some

defined above. Consider

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{i=1}\left(\frac{1}{Cn}H\left([\theta_{\ell,i}.\mu],\mathcal{Q}_{(C+\chi_{1})n}\right)\right)=\int\frac{1}{Cn}H\left([\theta_{\ell,1}.\mu],\mathcal{Q}_{(C+\chi_{1})n}\right)\mathrm{d}\theta(\ell)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\int_{\mathcal{U}_{1}}\frac{1}{Cn}H\left([\theta_{\ell,1}.\mu],\mathcal{Q}_{(C+\chi_{1})n}\right)\mathrm{d}\theta(\ell)+\int_{\mathcal{U}_{1}^{c}}\frac{1}{Cn}H\left([\theta_{\ell,1}.\mu],\mathcal{Q}_{(C+\chi_{1})n}\right)\mathrm{d}\theta(\ell).

For the first term on RHS, we apply
Eq.

7.13
to estimate it. For the second term, since
\mathcal{U}_{1}
consists of atoms of
\mathcal{Q}_{1}
, by the concavity of entropy we obtain

\int_{\mathcal{U}_{1}^{c}}\frac{1}{Cn}H\left([\theta_{\ell,1}.\mu],\mathcal{Q}_{(C+\chi_{1})n}\right)\mathrm{d}\theta(\ell)\geqslant\int_{\mathcal{U}_{1}^{c}}\frac{1}{Cn}H\left([\ell\mu],\mathcal{Q}_{(C+\chi_{1})n}\right)\mathrm{d}\theta(\ell)\geqslant(\alpha-\tau)\theta(\mathcal{U}_{2}-\mathcal{U}_{1}),

where the last inequality is due to

7.14
. Therefore, we have

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{i=1}\left(\frac{1}{Cn}H\left([\theta_{\ell,i}.\mu],\mathcal{Q}_{(C+\chi_{1})n}\right)\right)\geqslant

\displaystyle\frac{\epsilon}{2}(\alpha+\frac{\delta}{2})+(1-\tau-\epsilon/2)(\alpha-\tau)>\frac{\epsilon\delta}{4}+(\alpha-\tau)(1-\tau).

By taking
\tau
small, we obtain

\limsup_{n\rightarrow\infty}\mathbb{E}_{i=1}\left(\frac{1}{Cn}H\left([\theta_{\ell,i}.\mu],\mathcal{Q}_{(C+\chi_{1})n}\right)\right)>\alpha+\epsilon\delta/8.

(7.15)

Upper bound of entropy: application of exact dimension.

Another consequence from the exact dimension of
\pi_{E_{1}^{\perp}}\mu
is the following

\lim_{n\rightarrow\infty}\frac{1}{Cn}H(\pi_{E_{1}^{\perp}}\mu,\mathcal{Q}_{(C+\chi_{1})n}|\mathcal{Q}_{\chi_{1}n})=\alpha.

Note that
\pi_{E_{1}^{\perp}}\mu=\pi_{E_{1}^{\perp}}(\nu^{*n}*\mu)=[(\pi_{L}\nu^{*n}).\mu]=[\theta.\mu]
, so
\pi_{E_{1}^{\perp}}\mu=\mathbb{E}_{i=1}([\theta_{\ell,i}.\mu])
. By the concavity of entropy we have

\limsup_{n\rightarrow\infty}\mathbb{E}_{i=1}\left(\frac{1}{Cn}H([\theta_{\ell,i}.\mu],\mathcal{Q}_{(C+\chi_{1})n}|\mathcal{Q}_{\chi_{1}n})\right)\leqslant\alpha.

(7.16)

We would like to eliminate the conditional entropy in
Eq.

7.16
. This will enable us to compare
Eq.

7.16
with
Eq.

7.15
and obtain a contradiction. The idea is that for most of
\ell
in
\supp\theta
, we can decompose
[\theta_{\ell,1}.\mu]
similar to the
r
-attracting decomposition (
4.7
) as the following claim.

Claim 5
.

There exists
\epsilon_{0}>0
such that for any positive
\epsilon_{1}<\epsilon_{0}
, for
\ell\in L(n,\epsilon_{1})
defined in
Eq.

2.67
, the component
[\theta_{\ell,1}.\mu]
can be decomposed as the sum
[\theta_{\ell,1}.\mu]=(1-\epsilon_{2})\tau_{1}+\epsilon_{2}\tau_{2}
such that

\epsilon_{2}\ll q^{-\beta\epsilon_{1}n}\,\,\,\text{and}\,\,\,\diam(\supp\tau_{1})\leqslant q^{-(\chi_{1}-4\epsilon_{1})n}\text{ for $n$ large enough}.

Proof of
5
.

Consider
\tau=\tau(\ell)=\ell^{-1}\theta_{\ell,1}
on
L
and
{\tau}*\mu
on
\mathbb{P}(\mathbb{R}^{3})
. Then

[\theta_{\ell,1}.\mu]=[\ell({\tau}*\mu)].

And
\tau(\ell)
is supported on a uniformly bounded neighbourhood of the identity in
L
.

Let
m:={\tau}*\mu
,

m_{1}:=m_{b(f_{\ell},q^{-\epsilon_{1}n})}
and
m_{2}:=m_{b(f_{\ell},q^{-\epsilon_{1}n})^{c}}
, where the good region
b(f_{\ell},q^{-\epsilon_{1}n})
is introduced in
2.12
, whose complement is the
q^{-\epsilon_{1}n}
neighborhood of a hyperplane.
Since
\tau
is uniformly bounded supported, there exists
C^{\prime}>0
such that for any
g\in\supp\tau
, we can apply the bound in
2.23
to obtain

g\mu(b(f_{\ell},q^{-\epsilon_{1}n})^{c})\leqslant\mu(b(W,C^{\prime}q^{-\epsilon_{1}n})^{c})\ll(q^{-\epsilon_{1}n})^{\beta},

for some hyperplane
W
. Hence for
\epsilon_{2}
from the decomposition of
m=(1-\epsilon_{2})m_{1}+\epsilon_{2}m_{2}

\epsilon_{2}=\tau*\mu(b(f_{\ell},q^{-\epsilon_{1}n})^{c})=\int g\mu(b(f_{\ell},q^{-\epsilon_{1}n})^{c})\mathrm{d}\tau(g)\ll q^{-\beta\epsilon_{1}n}.

(7.17)

Apply
2.13
we obtain

\diam([\ell m_{1}])\leqslant q^{-(\chi_{1}-4\epsilon_{1})n},\text{ for $n$ large enough.}

By letting
\tau_{1}=[\ell m_{1}]
and
\tau_{2}=[\ell m_{2}]
, we finish the proof of the claim.
∎

Due to
5
, we can apply
2.35
to
\ell\in L(n,\epsilon_{1})
and obtain

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{i=1}\left(\frac{1}{Cn}H\left([\theta_{\ell,i}.\mu],\mathcal{Q}_{(C+\chi_{1})n}|\mathcal{Q}_{\chi_{1}n}\right)\right)

\displaystyle\geqslant

\displaystyle\int_{L(n,\epsilon_{1})}\frac{1}{Cn}H([\theta_{\ell,1}.\mu],\mathcal{Q}_{(C+\chi_{1})n}|\mathcal{Q}_{\chi_{1}n})\mathrm{d}\theta(\ell)

\displaystyle\geqslant

\displaystyle\int_{L(n,\epsilon_{1})}\frac{1}{Cn}H([\theta_{\ell,1}.\mu],\mathcal{Q}_{(C+\chi_{1})n})-\frac{1}{Cn}(O(\epsilon_{2}n)+H(\epsilon_{2})+O(\epsilon_{1}n))\mathrm{d}\theta(\ell)

\displaystyle\geqslant

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{i=1}\left(\frac{1}{Cn}H\left([\theta_{\ell,i}.\mu],\mathcal{Q}_{(C+\chi_{1})n}\right)\right)-\frac{1}{Cn}(O(\epsilon_{2}n)+H(\epsilon_{2})+O(\epsilon_{1}n)+O(q^{-c(\epsilon_{1})n})),

where the last inequality is due to
Eq.

2.69
. By taking
\epsilon_{1}
sufficiently small (and hence
\epsilon_{2}
small by
Eq.

7.17
), as
n
going to infinity, combining with
Eq.

7.16
, we obtain a contradiction to (
7.15
).

Hence we conclude
\dim\pi_{E_{1}^{\perp}}\mu=\min\{1,h_{\mathrm{RW}}(\nu)/\chi_{1}(\nu)\}
.

7.3
A Ledrappier-Young formula and the proof of
1.7
from
1.8
.

We first recall the Ledrappier-Young type formulas of Furstenberg measures recently shown in
[
LL23b
, Corollary 6.5]
and
[
Rap21
]
. (See
[
LL23a
]
for a more general case.)

Proposition 7.7
.

Let
\nu
be a finitely supported probability measure such that
\supp\nu
generates a Zariski dense subgroup in
\mathrm{SL}_{3}(\mathbb{R})
. Then there exist
\gamma_{1},\gamma_{2}\geqslant 0
such that for
\mu^{-}
a.e.
V
we have

1.

the projection measure
\pi_{V^{\perp}}\mu
is of exact dimension
\gamma_{1}
;

2.

consider the disintegration of the measure
\mu
under the projection
\pi_{V^{\perp}}
,

\mu=\int_{\mathbb{P}(V^{\perp})}\mu^{x}_{V}\ \mathrm{d}(\pi_{V^{\perp}}\mu)(x).

For
\pi_{V^{\perp}}\mu
a.e.
x\in\mathbb{P}(V^{\perp})
,
the measure
\mu^{x}_{V}
is of exact dimension
\gamma_{2}
;

3.

we have

h_{\mathrm{F}}(\mu,\nu)=\gamma_{1}\chi_{1}+\gamma_{2}\chi_{2},\ \dim\mu=\gamma_{1}+\gamma_{2},

(7.18)

where
h_{\mathrm{F}}(\mu,\nu)
is the Furstenberg entropy defined by (
2.81
).

Proof.

7.7
is essentially in Corollary 6.5 of
[
LL23b
]
which is presented in a different way. To explain it, we recall some notions from
[
LL23b
]
. For the set
\{1,2,3\}
, we consider the topopogies
T^{2}=(\{1\},\{2,3\},\{3\})\prec T^{1}=(\{1,3\},\{2,3\},\{3\})\prec T^{0}=(\{1,2,3\},\{2,3\},\{3\})
, see Section 1.4 in
[
LL23b
]
. Let
f=(\{0\}=U_{0}\subset U_{1}\subset U_{2}\subset U_{3}=\mathbb{R}^{3})
and
f^{\prime}=(\{0\}=U_{0}^{\prime}\subset U_{1}^{\prime}\subset U_{2}^{\prime}\subset U_{3}^{\prime}=\mathbb{R}^{3})
be two flags in general position, that is to say,
U_{1}\oplus U_{2}^{\prime}=U_{1}^{\prime}\oplus U_{2}=\mathbb{R}^{3}
. For each topology
T=T^{i}
, we define the map
[F_{T}(f,f^{\prime})]_{I}
with
I\in T
by

[F_{T}(f,f^{\prime})]_{I}=\oplus_{i\in I}(U_{i}\cap U_{4-i}^{\prime}).

For example, let
T
be
T^{0}=(\{1,2,3\},\{2,3\},\{3\})
and consider the map
[F_{T^{0}}(f,f^{\prime})]_{I}
with
I\in T^{0}

•

for
I=\{1,2,3\}
, due to flags in general position, we obtain
\mathbb{R}^{3}
;

•

for
I=\{2,3\}
, we obtain
(U_{2}\cap U_{2}^{\prime})\oplus U_{1}^{\prime}=U_{2}^{\prime}
;

•

for
I=\{3\}
, we obtain
U_{1}^{\prime}
.

In summary, we get
[F_{T^{0}}(f,f^{\prime})]=(\mathbb{R}^{3},U_{2}^{\prime},U_{1}^{\prime})
. Similarly we obtain
[F_{T^{2}}(f,f^{\prime})]=(U_{1},U_{2}^{\prime},U_{1}^{\prime})
and
[F_{T^{1}}(f,f^{\prime})]=(U_{1}\oplus U_{1}^{\prime},U_{2}^{\prime},U_{1}^{\prime})
. Therefore we have
projection mappings (in the sense of Page 8
[
LL23b
]
)

(U_{1},U_{2}^{\prime},U_{1}^{\prime})\xrightarrow{\pi_{T^{2},T^{1}}}(U_{1}\oplus U_{1}^{\prime},U_{2}^{\prime},U_{1}^{\prime})\xrightarrow{\pi_{T^{1},T^{0}}}(\mathbb{R}^{3},U_{2}^{\prime},U_{1}^{\prime}).

Let
\mu_{\mathcal{F}},\mu^{-}_{\mathcal{F}}
be the stationary measures of
\nu,\nu^{-}
on
\mathcal{F}
, respectively.
For a topology
T
, consider the pushforward measure
(F_{T})_{*}(\mu_{\mathcal{F}}\otimes\mu_{\mathcal{F}}^{-})
and the disintegration

(F_{T})_{*}(\mu_{\mathcal{F}}\otimes\mu_{\mathcal{F}}^{-})=\int(\mu_{\mathcal{F}})_{T}^{f^{\prime}}\mathrm{d}\mu_{\mathcal{F}}^{-}(f^{\prime}),

where the fiber measure
(\mu_{\mathcal{F}})_{T}^{f^{\prime}}
is supported on
X_{T}^{f^{\prime}}
, the set of
F_{T}(f,f^{\prime})
for all
f
such that
f,f^{\prime}
are in general position.
Since
T^{1}
is finer than
T^{0}
, for
\mu_{\mathcal{F}}^{-}
a.e.
f^{\prime}
, we have

(\pi_{T^{1},T^{0}})_{*}(\mu_{\mathcal{F}})_{T^{1}}^{f^{\prime}}=(\mu_{\mathcal{F}})_{T^{0}}^{f^{\prime}}.

Here
(\mu_{\mathcal{F}})_{T^{0}}^{f^{\prime}}
is supported on
X_{T^{0}}^{f^{\prime}}
which only consists of one point, and
(\mu_{\mathcal{F}})_{T^{1}}^{f^{\prime}}
is supported on
X_{T^{1}}^{f^{\prime}}\simeq\{U_{1}\oplus U_{1}^{\prime},\ (f,f^{\prime})\text{ are in general position}\}
.
Hence, for the disintegration

(\mu_{\mathcal{F}})_{T^{1}}^{f^{\prime}}=\int\mu_{T^{1},T^{0}}^{x^{\prime}}\mathrm{d}(\mu_{\mathcal{F}})_{T^{0}}^{f^{\prime}}(x^{\prime}),

the fiber measure
\mu_{T^{1},T^{0}}^{x^{\prime}}=(\mu_{\mathcal{F}})_{T^{1}}^{f^{\prime}}
. We have the isomorphism
\{U_{1}\oplus U_{1}^{\prime},U_{1}\oplus U_{2}^{\prime}=\mathbb{R}^{3}\}\simeq\mathbb{P}((U_{1}^{\prime})^{\perp})-\{\text{point}\}
.
Hence the fiber measure
\mu_{T^{1},T^{0}}^{x^{\prime}}
can be identified with
\pi_{V^{\perp}}\mu
for
V=U_{1}^{\prime}
following the law
\mu^{-}
. Similarly, the fiber measure
\mu_{T^{2},T^{1}}^{y^{\prime}}=\mu_{V}^{x}
for
y^{\prime}=(U_{1}\oplus U_{1}^{\prime},U_{2}^{\prime},U_{1}^{\prime})\in X_{T_{1}}^{f^{\prime}}
, with
V=U_{1}^{\prime}
and
x=\pi_{V^{\perp}}(U_{1}\oplus U_{1}^{\prime})
.

With all these identifications above,
[
LL23b
, Corollary 6.5]
can be viewed as
Eq.

7.18
.
∎

Proof of
1.7
from
1.8
.

By the exponential separation assumption in
1.7
,
1.8
implies that
\gamma_{1}=\min\{1,\frac{h_{\mathrm{RW}}(\nu)}{\chi_{1}}\}
.

If
\gamma_{1}=1
,
1.7
directly follows the definition of
\dim_{\mathrm{LY}}(\nu)
and
Eq.

7.18
. For the case that
\gamma_{1}=\frac{h_{\mathrm{RW}}(\nu)}{\chi_{1}}<1
, we use the general fact that
h_{\mathrm{F}}(\mu,\nu)\leqslant h_{\mathrm{RW}}(\nu)
, cf.
[
KV83
]
and also
[
Fur02
, Theorem 2.31]
. These results are stated for the discrete groups. If
G_{\nu}=\langle\supp\nu\rangle
is not discrete in
\mathrm{SL}_{3}(\mathbb{R})
, we can give a discrete topology on
G_{\nu}
as an abstract group, denoted by
G_{\nu}^{\prime}
and corresponding measure by
\nu^{\prime}
. Then the space
(\mathbb{P}(\mathbb{R}^{3}),\mu)
is still a
(G_{\nu}^{\prime},\nu^{\prime})
-space. So
h_{\mathrm{F}}(\mu,\nu)\leqslant h_{\mathrm{RW}}(\nu^{\prime})=h_{\mathrm{RW}}(\nu)
. Then we obtain
\dim_{\mathrm{LY}}\mu\leqslant\dim\mu
. Since
\dim_{\mathrm{LY}}\mu
is always an upper bound of
\dim\mu
, we finish the proof.
∎

8
Proof of
1.3
and
1.2

Approximation of affinity exponents

To complete the proof of Theorem
1.2
, it remains to obtain
\dim L(\rho(\Gamma))
from the dimension formula of stationary measures. We need a variational principle type result, i.e. show that the affinity exponent
s_{\mathrm{A}}(\rho)
of
\rho(\Gamma)
can be approximated by the Lyapunov dimension of stationary measures.

Theorem 8.1
.

Let
\Gamma
be a hyperbolic group and
\rho\in\mathrm{HA}(\Gamma,\mathrm{SL}_{3}({\mathbb{R}}))
whose image is Zariski dense. For every
\epsilon>0,
there exists a Zariski dense probability measure
\nu
supported on
\rho(\Gamma)
such that its Furstenberg measure
\mu
satisfies
\dim_{\mathrm{LY}}\mu\geqslant s_{\mathrm{A}}(\rho)-\epsilon.

The proof is given in the second paper
[
JLPX
]
.

8.1
Proof of Theorem
1.2

A direct application of the dimension variation is to compute the dimension of the minimal sets. Once we establish the identity between the Lyapunov dimension and the exact dimension of stationary measures, we can obtain the identity between the affinity exponent and the Hausdorff dimension of minimal sets.

Proof of
1.2
.

Recall that
{\mathbf{G}}
is the Zariski closure of
\rho(\Gamma).
We first consider the case
{\mathbf{G}}=\mathrm{SL}_{3}({\mathbb{R}}).
Note that
\rho(\Gamma)
is a discrete subgroup of
\mathrm{SL}_{3}({\mathbb{R}}).
Then for every
\nu\in\mathscr{P}_{\mathrm{f.s.}}^{\mathbf{G}}(\rho(\Gamma)),

\nu
is a Zariski dense finitely supported probability measure with exponential separation. Hence the unique
\nu
-stationary measure
\mu
on
{\mathbb{P}}({\mathbb{R}}^{3})
satisfying
\dim_{\mathrm{LY}}\mu=\dim\mu
by
1.7
. Moreover,
\mu
is supported on the limit set
L(\rho(\Gamma))\subset{\mathbb{P}}({\mathbb{R}}^{3}).
By
[
You82
]
,
\dim\mu=\dim_{\mathrm{H}}\mu\leqslant\dim_{\mathrm{H}}L(\rho(\Gamma)).
Combining with the upper bound estimate of Hausdorff dimension in
[
PSW21
]
, we obtain

\displaystyle\dim_{\mathrm{H}}L(\rho(\Gamma))

\displaystyle\leqslant s_{\mathrm{A}}(\rho)\leqslant\sup\left\{\dim_{\mathrm{LY}}\mu:\nu\in\mathscr{P}_{\mathrm{f.s.}}^{\mathbf{G}}(\rho)\right\}

\displaystyle=\sup\left\{\dim\mu:\nu\in\mathscr{P}_{\mathrm{f.s.}}^{\mathbf{G}}(\rho)\right\}\leqslant\dim_{\mathrm{H}}L(\rho(\Gamma)).

Then all unequal signs are equal. In particular,
\dim_{\mathrm{H}}L(\rho(\Gamma))=s_{\mathrm{A}}(\rho).

Secondly, we consider the case
{\mathbf{G}}\neq\mathrm{SL}_{3}(\mathbb{R})
.
Since
\rho
is an irreducible Anosov representation, due to
[
BCLS15
, Corollary 2.20]
8
8

8

The statement of Corollary is for projective Anosov representation. In
\mathrm{SL}_{3}(\mathbb{R})
, projective Anosov is equivalent to Anosov, so we can freely use this Corollary.
, the Zariski closure of
\rho(\Gamma)
is semi-simple without compact factor, and its centralizer is contained in
\{\pm\mathrm{Id}\}
. So
\rho(\Gamma)
is the image of an irreducible representation of
\mathrm{SL}_{2}(\mathbb{R})
. We can write
\rho
as the composition of
\rho_{0}:\Gamma\rightarrow\mathrm{SL}_{2}(\mathbb{R})
and an irreducible representation
\iota_{1}:\mathrm{SL}_{2}(\mathbb{R})\rightarrow\mathrm{SL}_{3}(\mathbb{R})
. The representation
\iota_{1}
induces an algebraic map from
\mathbb{P}(\mathbb{R}^{2})
to
\mathbb{P}(\mathbb{R}^{3})
. We only need to compute the Hausdorff dimension of
L(\rho_{0}(\Gamma))
. Due to the classical result of Patterson, Bowen and Sullivan, the Hausdorff dimension of the limit set is also equal to the critical exponent, which is equal to the affinity exponent in this case.

The proof of the continuity of
s_{\mathrm{A}}(\rho)
is postponed to the next subsection.
∎

8.2
Continuity and discontinuity of dimensions: proof of Theorem
1.3
and the continuity part of Theorem
1.2
.

Recall that
\mathrm{HA}(\Gamma,\mathrm{SL}_{3}(\mathbb{R}))
is the space of Anosov represesntations of
\Gamma
inside
\mathrm{Hom}(\Gamma,\mathrm{SL}_{3}(\mathbb{R}))
, which is an open subset.
In the following of this section, we always assume that
\rho
is a representation in
\mathrm{HA}(\Gamma,\mathrm{SL}_{3}({\mathbb{R}})).

Recall that the Lie algebra
\mathfrak{a}=\{\lambda=\mathrm{diag}(\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2},\lambda_{3}):\lambda_{i}\in\mathbb{R},\ \sum\lambda_{i}=0\}
and the positive Weyl chamber
\mathfrak{a}^{+}=\{\lambda\in\mathfrak{a}:\lambda_{1}\geqslant\lambda_{2}\geqslant\lambda_{3}\}
. For an element
g\in\mathrm{SL}_{3}(\mathbb{R})
let
\lambda(g)\in\mathfrak{a}^{+}
be its Jordan projection. Let
\alpha_{1}
and
\alpha_{2}
be two simple roots on
\mathfrak{a}
, given by
\alpha_{i}(\lambda)=\lambda_{i}-\lambda_{i+1}
, which are nonnegative on
\mathfrak{a}^{+}
. For any nonzero
\psi\in\{a\alpha_{1}+b\alpha_{2}:\ a,b\in\mathbb{R}_{\geqslant 0}\}\subset\mathfrak{a}^{*}
, we define

h_{\rho}^{\psi}=\limsup_{T\rightarrow\infty}\frac{1}{T}\log\#\{[\gamma]\in[\Gamma]:\ \text{non torsion},\ \psi(\lambda(\rho(\gamma)))\leqslant T\},

where
[\Gamma]
is the set of conjugacy classes and
[\gamma]
is the conjugacy class of
\gamma
in
\Gamma
. For such
\psi
, we have
h_{\rho}^{\psi}\in(0,\infty)
since
\rho\in\mathrm{HA}(\Gamma,\mathrm{SL}_{3}(\mathbb{R}))
. By
[
PS17
, Cor. 4.9]
or
[
BCLS15
]
, we know that
h_{\rho}^{\psi}
is analytic with respect to
\rho
in
\mathrm{HA}(\Gamma,\mathrm{SL}_{3}(\mathbb{R}))
.

In order to study the affinity exponent, we consider the following function
\psi_{s}
on
\mathfrak{a}
as

\psi_{s}=\left\{\begin{aligned} &s\alpha_{1},&0<s\leqslant 1;\\
&\alpha_{1}+(s-1)(\alpha_{1}+\alpha_{2}),&1<s\leqslant 2.\end{aligned}\right.

(8.1)

Since
\rho
is an Anosov representation, we obtain that
h_{\rho}^{\psi_{s}}\in(0,\infty)
for every
0<s\leqslant 2
.

Lemma 8.2
.

(1)

For any
0<t<1
, we have
h_{\rho}^{\psi_{ts}}>th_{\rho}^{\psi_{ts}}\geqslant h_{\rho}^{\psi_{s}};

(2)

For
0<t<1,0<s\leqslant 1
, we have
th_{\rho}^{\psi_{ts}}=h_{\rho}^{\psi_{s}}
;

(3)

For
0<t<1,s>1
and
ts>1
, we have

h_{\rho}^{\psi_{s}}\geqslant\frac{ts-1}{s-1}h_{\rho}^{\psi_{ts}}.

Proof.

The first two items can be checked by definition. The third one follows from the inequality
\psi_{s}\leqslant\frac{s-1}{ts-1}\psi_{ts}
when
0<t<1,s>1
and
ts>1
.
∎

As a corollary, we have

Lemma 8.3
.

h_{\rho}^{\psi_{s}}
is a strictly decreasing continuous function on
s
.

Lemma 8.4
.

The affinity exponent
s_{\mathrm{A}}(\rho)
is the unique
s
such that
h_{\rho}^{\psi_{s}}=1
.

Proof.

In
[
Sam14
, Corollary 4.4]
, it is proved that for Anosov representation
\rho
, the exponent
h_{\rho}^{\psi_{s}}
is also equal to

\limsup_{T\rightarrow\infty}\frac{1}{T}\log\#\{\gamma\in\Gamma:\ \psi_{s}(\kappa(\rho\gamma))\leqslant T\},

which equals the critical exponent of the series

\sum_{\gamma\in\Gamma}q^{-t\psi_{s}\kappa(\rho\gamma)}

with respect to
t\in\mathbb{R}
. Here
q
is a large integer, which is the base of the logarithm in this article.
Hence, if
h_{\rho}^{\psi_{s}}>1
, then the series
\sum_{\gamma\in\Gamma}q^{-\psi_{s}\kappa(\rho\gamma)}
diverges; if
h_{\rho}^{\psi_{s}}<1
, then the series
\sum_{\gamma\in\Gamma}q^{-\psi_{s}\kappa(\rho\gamma)}
converges. By the definition of affinity exponent, this completes the proof.
∎

We are able to prove the continuity part in
1.2
.

Proposition 8.5
.

The affinity exponent
s_{\mathrm{A}}(\rho)
is continuous on
\mathrm{HA}(\Gamma,\mathrm{SL}_{3}(\mathbb{R}))
.

Proof.

Consider the
\mathbb{R}^{+}
-value map
h_{\rho}^{\psi_{s}}
that maps
(\rho,s)
to
h_{\rho}^{\psi_{s}}
. For a fixed
s
, the aforementioned result (
[
PS17
, Cor. 4.9]
and
[
BCLS15
]
) implies that
h_{\rho}^{\psi_{s}}
is an analytic function on
\rho\in\mathrm{HA}(\Gamma,\mathrm{SL}_{3}({\mathbb{R}}))
. For a fixed
\rho
,
h_{\rho}^{\psi_{s}}
is a strictly decreasing continuous function (
8.3
). A basic analysis lemma says that a two-variable function, which is continuous on
\rho
and strictly monotonic continuous on
s
, satisfies the implicit function theorem. We obtain that
s_{\mathrm{A}}(\rho)
, the solution of
h_{\rho}^{\psi_{s}}=1
(
8.4
), is a continuous function on
\mathrm{HA}(\Gamma,\mathrm{SL}_{3}(\mathbb{R}))
.
∎

We prove
1.3
in a more general setting, that is starting from any convex cocompact representation
\rho_{0}:\Gamma\rightarrow\mathrm{SL}_{2}(\mathbb{R})
.
Recall that
\iota
and
\rho_{1}=\iota\circ\rho_{0}
are defined in the introduction.

Theorem 8.6
.

For every
\epsilon>0
, there exists a small neighborhood
O
of
\rho_{1}
in
\mathrm{Hom}(\Gamma,\mathrm{SL}_{3}(\mathbb{R}))
such that for any
\rho
in
O
we have

•

either
\rho(\Gamma)
acts reducibly on
\mathbb{R}^{3}
, i.e. fixing a line or a plane in
\mathbb{R}^{3}
;

•

or
\rho(\Gamma)
is irreducible and

|\dim L(\rho(\Gamma))-(\dim L(\rho_{1}(\Gamma))+\min\{\dim L(\rho_{1}(\Gamma)),1/2\})|\leqslant\epsilon.

If we start with a surface group
\Gamma
, then the limit set
L(\rho_{1}(\Gamma))
is the full circle of dimension one. Then we obtain
3/2
in
1.3
.

Proof.

By the definition of the embedding
\iota
, for any
\gamma\in\Gamma
we have
\sigma_{1}(\rho_{1}(\gamma))=1/\sigma_{3}(\rho_{1}(\gamma))=\mu_{1}(\rho_{0}(\gamma))=1/\mu_{2}(\rho_{0}(\gamma))
and
\sigma_{2}(\rho_{1}(\gamma))=1
(In order to distinguish the notations, we use
\mu_{1}(\rho_{0}(\gamma))\geqslant\mu_{2}(\rho_{0}(\gamma))
to denote the singular values of
\rho_{0}(\gamma)\in\mathrm{SL}(2,{\mathbb{R}})
). Then

\sum_{\gamma\in\Gamma}\left(\frac{\mu_{2}(\rho_{0}\gamma)}{\mu_{1}(\rho_{0}\gamma)}\right)^{s}=\sum_{\gamma\in\Gamma}\left(\frac{\sigma_{2}(\rho_{1}\gamma)}{\sigma_{1}(\rho_{1}\gamma)}\right)^{2s}=\sum_{\gamma\in\Gamma}\frac{\sigma_{2}(\rho_{1}\gamma)}{\sigma_{1}(\rho_{1}\gamma)}\left(\frac{\sigma_{3}(\rho_{1}\gamma)}{\sigma_{1}(\rho_{1}\gamma)}\right)^{s-1/2}.

(8.2)

The map
\iota
induces an isometric embedding of the limit set
L(\rho_{0}(\Gamma))
to
L(\rho_{1}(\Gamma))
, in particular, they have the same Hausdorff dimension. A classic result in the theory of Fuchsian groups tells us that
\dim L(\rho_{0}(\Gamma))
is the critical exponent of the first series in (
8.2
). Hence

\dim L(\rho_{1}(\Gamma))=\max\{s_{\mathrm{A}}(\rho_{1})/2,s_{\mathrm{A}}(\rho_{1})-1/2\}.

Therefore

s_{\mathrm{A}}(\rho_{1})=\min\{2\dim L(\rho_{1}(\Gamma)),\dim L(\rho_{1}(\Gamma))+1/2\}.

(8.3)

Due to
8.5
, for any irreducible
\rho
in the neighbourhood
O
of
\rho_{1}
, by
1.2
.
and
Eq.

8.3
we have

\dim L(\rho(\Gamma))=s_{\mathrm{A}}(\rho)\geqslant\min\{2\dim L(\rho_{1}(\Gamma)),\dim L(\rho_{1}(\Gamma))+1/2\}-\epsilon.\qed

Appendix A
Auxiliary results

Identification between
\mathbb{P}(V^{\perp})
with
\mathbb{P}(\mathbb{R}^{2})

We will identify
\mathbb{P}(V^{\perp})
with
\mathbb{P}(\mathbb{R}^{2})
through rotation invariant metrics for general
V\in\mathbb{P}(\mathbb{R}^{3})
. A prior, there is no canonical choice of such identification, but different identifications only differ by rotations and orientation. In order to apply certain continuity arguments, we fix a one-dimensional subspace
V_{0}=E_{3}
and let
\mathcal{C}=\mathbb{P}(\mathbb{R}^{3})-\mathbb{P}(E_{1}^{\perp})\subset\mathbb{P}(\mathbb{R}^{3})-\{V_{0}\}
. Since most of our results are on measure-theoretical properties, we can freely delete this single
bad circle

\mathbb{P}(E_{1}^{\perp})\subset\mathbb{P}(\mathbb{R}^{3})
.

Lemma A.1
.

For each point
V\in\mathcal{C}
, we have an identification between
\mathbb{P}(V^{\perp})
and
\mathbb{P}(\mathbb{R}^{2})
and the identification is continuous with respect to
V
.

Proof.

For any
V\neq V_{0}
, the projection
\pi_{V^{\perp}}
is well-defined at
V_{0}
. We take the isometric identification between
\mathbb{P}(V^{\perp})
and
\mathbb{P}(\mathbb{R}^{2})
such that
\pi_{V^{\perp}}V_{0}
is identified with
\mathbb{R}(1,0)
.

For any
V
not in
\mathbb{P}(E_{1}^{\perp})
, we take a representative
v\in\mathbb{S}^{2}\subset\mathbb{R}^{3}
of
V
with positive first coordinate. Take
k\in\mathrm{SO}(3)
such that
kv=(1,0,0).

The orientation of
\mathbb{P}(V^{\perp})
comes from the identification between
V^{\perp}
and
E_{2}\oplus E_{3}
by
k
, which is independent of the choice of
k
.
∎

Under the assumption of this lemma, by identifying
\mathbb{P}(V^{\perp})
with
\mathbb{P}(\mathbb{R}^{2})
, we can regard the linear map
h_{V,g}
in (
2.9
) as an element in
\mathrm{PGL}_{2}(\mathbb{R})
.

Weakly convergence of projections of a measure

Lemma A.2
.

For any
V\in\mathcal{C}
and any interval
I\subset\mathbb{P}(\mathbb{R}^{2})
, we have

1.

\pi_{W^{\perp}}(\mu)(I)
converges to
\pi_{V^{\perp}}(\mu)(I)
if
W
converges to
V
.

2.

(\pi_{W^{\perp}}\mu)_{I}\to(\pi_{V^{\perp}}\mu)_{I}
weakly if
W
converges to
V
.

Proof.

Let
I\subset\mathbb{P}(\mathbb{R}^{2})
be any interval. For any
W\in\mathbb{P}(\mathbb{R}^{3})
, denote the region
(\pi_{W^{\perp}})^{-1}(\mathcal{I}_{W}^{-1}(I))
by
S(I,W)
. So we have

\pi_{W^{\perp}}(\mu)(I)=\mu(S(I,W)).

For the first statement,
it suffices to show
\mu(S(I,W))\to\mu(S(I,V))
when
W\to V
. Due to the definition of
\mathcal{I}_{V}
, for any
\rho>0
, there exists
\delta>0
such that if
d(W,V)<\delta
, then for any
x\in\mathbb{P}(\mathbb{R}^{2})
,

d(\mathcal{I}_{V}^{-1}(x),\mathcal{I}_{W}^{-1}(x))<\rho.

Therefore, the symmetric difference
S(I,W)\Delta S(I,V)
of
S(I,W)
and
S(I,V)
can be covered by the
\rho
neighborhood of
\partial S(I,V)
, that is
\pi_{V^{\perp}}^{-1}\mathcal{I}_{V}^{-1}(\partial I)
. We this denote by
(\partial S(I,V))^{(\rho)}
. Notice that
\partial S(I,V)
is the union of two great circles. Therefore, by Lemma
2.23
, we know that
\mu(S(I,W)\Delta S(I,V))
can be arbitrarily small when
W\to V
.

We prove the second statement. For any sub-interval
J\subset I
,
\pi_{W^{\perp}}(\mu)(J)
converges to
\pi_{V^{\perp}}(\mu)(J)
. Therefore for any open set
U\subset I
, say
U=\cup_{i=1}^{\infty}J_{i}
which is a countable disjoint union of open intervals, we have that for any
n
,
\pi_{W^{\perp}}(\mu)(\cup_{i=1}^{n}J_{i})\to\pi_{V^{\perp}}(\mu)(\cup_{i=1}^{n}J_{i})
. Hence
\liminf_{W\to V}\pi_{W^{\perp}}(\mu)(U)\geqslant\pi_{V^{\perp}}(\mu)(U)
, then by (1) and equivalent condition of weak convergence of measures we get (2).
∎

Sequences of numbers

Lemma A.3
.

For any
\epsilon>0
and any
m\geqslant M(\epsilon)
, the following holds. Given any two finite sequence of numbers
\{a_{i}\}_{i=1}^{q^{m}}
and
\{b_{i}\}_{i=1}^{q^{m}}
in
[0,1]
that satisfy

\sum a_{i}=\sum b_{i}=1\,\,\text{and}\,\,|a_{i}-b_{i}|\leqslant q^{-3m}\,\,\text{for each}\,\,1\leqslant i\leqslant q^{m},

we have

\frac{1}{m}\left|\sum a_{i}\log a_{i}-\sum b_{i}\log b_{i}\right|\leqslant\epsilon.

Proof.

We estimate
|a_{i}\log a_{i}-b_{i}\log b_{i}|
in two ways:

1.

If
a_{i}>q^{-1.5m}
, since
|a_{i}-b_{i}|\leqslant q^{-3m}
, we can assume that
b_{i}>q^{-1.6m}
. Then by Lagrange mean value theorem we have
|\log a_{i}-\log b_{i}|\leqslant q^{1.6m}\cdot q^{-3m}=q^{-1.4m}
. Therefore

\displaystyle|a_{i}\log a_{i}-b_{i}\log b_{i}|

\displaystyle\leqslant

\displaystyle|a_{i}-b_{i}|\cdot|\log a_{i}|+|b_{i}|\cdot|\log a_{i}-\log b_{i}|

\displaystyle\leqslant

\displaystyle q^{-3m}\cdot(1.5m)+q^{-1.4m}\leqslant q^{-1.3m}

if
m
is large enough.

2.

If
a_{i}\leqslant q^{-1.5m}
, since
|a_{i}-b_{i}|\leqslant q^{-3m}
, we can assume that
b_{i}<q^{-1.4m}
and
m
large enough such that
q^{-m}
less than the unique extreme point of
x\log x
in
[0,1]
. Therefore we have

|a_{i}\log a_{i}-b_{i}\log b_{i}|\leqslant 2q^{-1.4m}\cdot(1.4m)\leqslant q^{-1.3m}
if
m
large enough.

Therefore combining two estimates above we get
\frac{1}{m}\left|\sum a_{i}\log a_{i}-\sum b_{i}\log b_{i}\right|\leqslant\frac{1}{m}q^{-0.3m}\leqslant\epsilon

for any
m
large enough.
∎

Appendix B
Spherical geometry

Lemma B.1
.

Let
C>1
.
If
d(V,(E_{1})^{\perp})\geqslant 1/C
, then the map
\pi(V,E_{1}^{\perp},V^{\perp})
has scale
u=1
and distortion bounded by
C
.

Moreover, by the identification in Lemma
A.1
, for
V_{1}\notin V_{2}^{\perp}
, the action
\pi(V_{1},V_{2}^{\perp},V_{1}^{\perp})
can be identified with some element in
\mathrm{PSL}_{2}(\mathbb{R})
of norm
|1/d(V_{1},V_{2}^{\perp})|^{1/2}
.

Proof.

Without loss of generality, we can assume that
V=\mathbb{R}v,v=\frac{e_{1}+\lambda e_{3}}{\sqrt{1+\lambda^{2}}}
with
\sqrt{1+\lambda^{2}}=1/d(V,(E_{1})^{\perp})
. Let
V^{\prime}=\mathbb{R}v^{\prime},v^{\prime}=\frac{-\lambda e_{1}+e_{3}}{\sqrt{1+\lambda^{2}}}
, then
(v,v^{\prime},e_{2})
forms an orthonormal basis of
\mathbb{R}^{3}
. The map
\pi(V,E_{1}^{\perp},V^{\perp})
projects a vector
ae_{2}+bv^{\prime}
to
E_{1}^{\perp}
, along kernel
V
. So the image is

(ae_{2}+bv^{\prime})+b\lambda v=ae_{2}+b\sqrt{\lambda^{2}+1}e_{3}.

Therefore with basis
e_{2},v^{\prime}
and
e_{2},e_{3}
, the transformation
\Pi(V,E_{1}^{\perp},V^{\perp})
can be written as a matrix
\begin{pmatrix}1&0\\
0&\sqrt{1+\lambda^{2}}\end{pmatrix}
. Then the distortion estimate of
\pi(V,E_{1}^{\perp},V^{\perp})
follows from basic estimates of
\mathrm{PSL}_{2}(\mathbb{R})
(
2.7
).

We can find
k\in\mathrm{SO}(3)
such that
V_{2}=kE_{1}
, then

\pi(V_{1},V_{2}^{\perp},V_{1}^{\perp})=k\pi(k^{-1}V_{1},E_{1}^{\perp},(k^{-1}V_{1})^{\perp})k^{-1}.

Then the statement follows from the computation of the first part by noticing
d(k^{-1}V_{1},E_{1}^{\perp})=d(V_{1},V_{2}^{\perp})
.
∎

We will use the following classical result in spherical geometry later. For completeness, we provide a proof.

Lemma B.2
.

Let
x\neq y,V\in\mathbb{P}(\mathbb{R}^{3})
and
C>0
. If we denote by
(xy)
the projective line in
\mathbb{P}(\mathbb{R}^{3})
passing through
x,y
, then we have

1.

If
d(V,(xy))>1/C
, then
d(\pi_{V^{\perp}}x,\pi_{V^{\perp}}y)\geqslant d(x,y)/C
;

2.

If either
d(V,x)\geqslant 1/C
or
\ d(V,y)\geqslant 1/C
, then
d(\pi_{V^{\perp}}x,\pi_{V^{\perp}}y)\leqslant Cd(x,y)
.

Proof.

Let
z=V
. Then
d(\pi_{V^{\perp}}x,\pi_{V^{\perp}}y)=\sin(\angle_{z}(x,y))
(the angle between two projective lines
(xz),(yz)
at vertex
z
). By the spherical law of sines, the definition of
d
on
\mathbb{P}(\mathbb{R}^{3})
and the assumption of the lemma, we have

\frac{d(x,y)}{\sin\angle_{z}(x,y)}=\frac{d(y,z)}{\sin\angle_{x}(y,z)}\text{ and }\frac{d(z,(xy))}{\sin\angle_{x}(y,z)}=\frac{d(x,z)}{\sin(\pi/2)}.

For the first statment, if
d(z,(xy))\geqslant 1/C
, we have

\frac{d(x,y)}{\sin\angle_{z}(x,y)}=\frac{d(y,z)d(x,z)}{d(z,(xy))}\leqslant C,

which implies
d(\pi_{V^{\perp}}x,\pi_{V^{\perp}}y)=\sin\angle_{z}(x,y)\geqslant d(x,y)/C
.

For the second statement, without loss of generality, we assume that
d(V,y)=d(z,y)\geqslant 1/C
. By the spherical law of sines again, we have

\frac{d(x,y)}{\sin\angle_{z}(x,y)}=\frac{d(y,z)}{\sin\angle_{x}(y,z)}\geqslant 1/C,

which implies
d(\pi_{V^{\perp}}x,\pi_{V^{\perp}}y)=\sin\angle_{z}(x,y)\leqslant Cd(x,y)
.
∎

Appendix C
Proof of Lemma
5.6

We consider the following definition, which is a quadratic analogue of
\sigma
-independence in
[
BHR19
]
(also occured in
[
Hoc
]
).

Definition C.1
.

Recall the map
p_{2}
from
2.25
. For
\rho>0
and
V\in\mathbb{P}(\mathbb{R}^{3})
, we call five points
V_{1},\cdots,V_{5}
in
\mathbb{P}(\mathbb{R}^{3})
are
(\rho,V)
-quadratic independent if for every
1\leqslant i\leqslant 5
,
p_{2}(V_{i})
has distance at least
\rho
to the subspace generated by
p_{2}(V)
and
p_{2}(V_{j}),\ j\neq i
in
\mathbb{P}(Sym^{2}\mathbb{R}^{3})
.

By the following lemma, we can always find
(\rho,V)
-quadratic independent sets in a set with a certain
\mu
measure.

Lemma C.2
.

For any
\epsilon>0,
there exists
\rho>0
such that for any
V\in\mathbb{P}(\mathbb{R}^{3})
, any Borel set

A\subset\mathbb{P}(\mathbb{R}^{3}),\mu(A)>(100\epsilon)^{1/5}
, there exists
\{V_{1},\dots,V_{5}\}\subset A
such that
V_{1},\cdots,V_{5}
are
(\rho,V)
-quadratic independent.

Proof.

The proof is similar to that of
[
Hoc
]
. We first need a lemma saying that
\mu
cannot have a big weight on a small neighbourhood of a subvariety. For a subset
W
of a metric space write
W^{(\epsilon)}
for its
\epsilon-
neighborhood. Then
2.25
reads as

Lemma C.3
.

For any
\epsilon>0
, there exists
\rho(\epsilon)>0
such that for any hyperplan
W\subset\mathbb{P}(Sym^{2}\mathbb{R}^{3})
,
(p_{2})_{*}\mu(W^{(\rho)})<\epsilon
.

We go back to the proof of Lemma
C.2
. For any
V_{1},\dots,V_{5}\in\mathbb{P}(\mathbb{R}^{5})
, Llet
\mathcal{C}(V_{1},V_{2},\dots V_{5})
be the subspace generated by
p_{2}(V_{1}),\cdots,p_{2}(V_{5})
in
\mathbb{P}(Sym^{2}\mathbb{R}^{3})
.
Now we fix
V\in\mathbb{P}(\mathbb{R}^{3})
,
\epsilon>0
, and
\rho(\epsilon)
we get from last lemma. Let
X_{1},\dots,X_{5}
be independent
\mathbb{P}(\mathbb{R}^{3})
-valued random variables, each distributed according to
\mu
.
Therefore by
C.3
, if
\mu(A)>(100\epsilon)^{1/5}
,

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}(X_{i}\in A,X_{i}\notin\mathcal{C}(V,X_{j},j\neq i)^{(\rho)})\geqslant\mathbb{P}(X_{i}\in A,\forall i)-100\epsilon

\displaystyle>

\displaystyle\mu(A)^{5}-100\epsilon>0.

Notice that any realization
X_{1},\dots,X_{5}
from the event above are
(\rho,V)
-quadratic independent. The proof is complete.
∎

Recall that
L\simeq\mathrm{SL}_{2}^{\pm}(\mathbb{R})\ltimes\mathbb{R}^{2}
is the group formed by
\{\begin{pmatrix}\det h&\\
n&h\end{pmatrix},n\in\mathbb{R}^{2},h\in\mathrm{SL}_{2}^{\pm}(\mathbb{R})\}
.
Fix
\epsilon=10^{-7}
and
\rho=\rho(\epsilon)
is decided by Lemma
C.2
. Denote by

\displaystyle I(\rho,V)

\displaystyle:=

\displaystyle\{(V_{1},\dots,V_{5})\in\mathbb{P}(\mathbb{R}^{3})^{5}:\ V_{1},\dots,V_{5}\text{ are $(\rho,V)$-quadratic independent}\}

\displaystyle I(\rho)

\displaystyle:=

\displaystyle\{(V_{1},\cdots,V_{5},V)\in\mathbb{P}(\mathbb{R}^{3})^{6}:\ \ (V_{1},\dots,V_{5})\in I(\rho,V),\ d(V,E_{1}^{\perp})\geqslant 1/2C_{1}\},

then
I(\rho,V)
and
I(\rho
) are closed and hence compact.

For any
(\rho,V)
-quadratic independent five points
\{V_{1},\dots,V_{5}\}
, we consider the evaluation map from
L
to
\mathbb{P}(\mathbb{R}^{3})^{5}
,

E_{V_{1},\dots V_{5}}:L\to\mathbb{P}(\mathbb{R}^{3}),\quad E_{V_{1},\dots,V_{5}}(g):=(g(V_{1}),\dots,g(V_{5})).

Define the map

\Phi^{V}_{V_{1},\dots,V_{5}}:L\to\mathbb{P}(\mathbb{R}^{2})^{5},\quad\Phi^{V}_{V_{1},\dots,V_{5}}(g):=\pi^{5}_{V^{\perp}}\circ E_{V_{1},\dots,V_{5}}(g),

here we denoting by
\pi_{V^{\perp}}^{5}
the map
(\pi_{V^{\perp}},\dots,\pi_{V^{\perp}}):\mathbb{P}(\mathbb{R}^{3})^{5}\to\mathbb{P}(\mathbb{R}^{2})^{5}
.

Notice that even though
\pi_{V^{\perp}}^{5}
is not well-defined everywhere, the restriction of
\pi_{V^{\perp}}^{5}
on
(\mathbb{P}(\mathbb{R}^{3})-B(V,\rho/2))^{5}
is
C^{2}
, uniformly on
V
. Then by invariance of
\mathrm{SO}(3)
, let

C^{\prime}_{1}=C^{\prime}_{1}(\rho,V)=\|D\pi_{V^{\perp}}|_{\mathbb{P}(\mathbb{R}^{3})-B(V,\rho/2)}\|<\infty,

which is finite and independent of
V
.

By continuity, there exists a compact neighborhood
Z_{1}
of
\mathrm{id}\in L
such that for any
g\in Z_{1}
and
V_{0}\in\mathbb{P}(\mathbb{R}^{3})
, we have
d_{\mathbb{P}(\mathbb{R}^{3})}(gV_{0},V_{0})\leqslant\rho/2
. Since the action
E_{V_{1},\dots,V_{5}}
is
C^{\infty}
on
L
and all the derivatives continuously depend on
\{V_{1},\dots,V_{5}\}
, hence by compactness of
I(\rho,V)
,

C^{\prime}_{2}:=\sup_{(V_{1},\dots,V_{5})\in I(\rho,V),g_{0}\in Z_{1}}\|D_{g}E_{V_{1},\dots,V_{5}}|_{g=g_{0}}\|

is finite. The definition of
\rho
-quadratic independence also implies
d(V,V_{i})\geqslant\rho
. Therefore by our choice of
Z_{1}
we know that

C^{\prime}_{3}:=\sup_{(V_{1},\dots,V_{5},V)\in I(\rho),g_{0}\in Z_{1}}\|D_{g}\Phi^{V}_{V_{1},\dots,V_{5}}|_{g=g_{0}}\|\leqslant C^{\prime}_{1}C^{\prime}_{2}

(C.1)

is finite.
The following lemma explains the reason why we consider quadratic independence.

Lemma C.4
.

There exist a compact neighborhood
Z\subset Z_{1}
of
\mathrm{id}\in L
and a constant
C_{4}^{\prime}>0
such that

\inf_{(V_{1},\dots,V_{5},V)\in I(\rho),g_{0}\in Z}|\mathrm{Jac}(D_{g}\Phi^{V}_{V_{1},\dots,V_{5}})|_{g=g_{0}}|\geqslant C_{4}^{\prime}.

(C.2)

Proof.

Notice that the function
|\mathrm{Jac}(D_{g}\Phi^{V}_{V_{1},\dots,V_{5}})|
continuously depends on
g_{0}\in Z_{1}
and
(V_{1},\dots,V_{5},V)\in I(\rho)
. Therefore by compacticity
I(\rho)
, to find
Z
and
C_{4}^{\prime}
we only need to show that for all
(V_{1},\dots,V_{5},V)\in I(\rho)

|\mathrm{Jac}(D_{g}\Phi^{V}_{V_{1},\dots,V_{5}})|_{g=\mathrm{id}}|>0.

(C.3)

We show (
C.3
) quite straightforwardly. Apparently any vector in the Lie algebra
\mathrm{Lie}(L)
can be written as
X_{\mathfrak{n},\mathfrak{h}}=\begin{pmatrix}&&\\
n_{1}&h_{11}&h_{12}\\
n_{2}&h_{21}&-h_{11}\end{pmatrix}

with real coefficients.
We write that
V_{i}=\mathbb{R}\cdot(a_{i},b_{i},c_{i})^{t}
and
V=\mathbb{R}\cdot(v_{1},v_{2},v_{3})^{t}
. Then if (
C.3
) does not holds then there exist
(V_{1},\dots,V_{5},V)\in I(\rho)
and
X_{\mathfrak{n},\mathfrak{h}}\in\mathrm{Lie}(L)-\{0\}
such that

(D_{g}\Phi^{V}_{V_{1},\dots,V_{5}})|_{g=\mathrm{id}}(X_{\mathfrak{n},\mathfrak{h}})=0.

(C.4)

Notice that for any
V_{i}\neq V
, the kernel
K_{V_{i}}\subset T_{V_{i}}\mathbb{P}(\mathbb{R}^{3})
of the tangent map
D_{x}\pi_{V^{\perp}}|_{x=V_{i}}:\mathbb{P}(\mathbb{R}^{3})\to\mathbb{P}(\mathbb{R}^{2})
is the tangent space at
V_{i}
of the project line
(VV_{i})
passing through
V,V_{i}
. Therefore if (
C.4
) holds, then

D_{g}E_{V_{1},\dots,V_{5}}|_{g=\mathrm{id}}(X_{\mathfrak{n},\mathfrak{h}})\in K_{V_{1}}\oplus\cdots\oplus K_{V_{5}}.

By the separable form of the evaluation map and the canonical correspondence between Lie algebras and the one-parameter subgroup of Lie groups, we get that the last equation is equivalent to

\frac{d}{dt}|_{t=0}(e^{tX_{\mathfrak{n},\mathfrak{h}}}\cdot V_{i})\in K_{V_{i}}=T_{V_{i}}(VV_{i}),

(C.5)

for
i=1,\cdots,5
.
If we consider the corresponding equation of (
C.5
) in linear space rather than the equation in the tangent space of projective space, we get (here the action is linear action): for
i=1,\cdots,5

\frac{d}{dt}|_{t=0}(e^{tX_{\mathfrak{n},\mathfrak{h}}}\cdot\begin{pmatrix}a_{i}\\
b_{i}\\
c_{i}\end{pmatrix})\in\left\{l\cdot\begin{pmatrix}a_{i}\\
b_{i}\\
c_{i}\end{pmatrix}+k\cdot\begin{pmatrix}v_{1}\\
v_{2}\\
v_{3}\end{pmatrix},(k,l)\in\mathbb{R}^{2}\right\}.

By direct calculation, we have

\frac{d}{dt}|_{t=0}(e^{tX_{\mathfrak{n},\mathfrak{h}}}\cdot\begin{pmatrix}a_{i}\\
b_{i}\\
c_{i}\end{pmatrix})=\begin{pmatrix}&&\\
n_{1}&h_{11}&h_{12}\\
n_{2}&h_{21}&-h_{11}\end{pmatrix}\cdot\begin{pmatrix}a_{i}\\
b_{i}\\
c_{i}\end{pmatrix}=\begin{pmatrix}\\
a_{i}n_{1}+b_{i}h_{11}+c_{i}h_{12}\\
a_{i}n_{2}+b_{i}h_{21}-c_{i}h_{11}\end{pmatrix}.

Therefore

\det\begin{pmatrix}0&a_{i}&v_{1}\\
a_{i}n_{1}+b_{i}h_{11}+c_{i}h_{12}&b_{i}&v_{2}\\
a_{i}n_{2}+b_{i}h_{21}-c_{i}h_{11}&c_{i}&v_{3}\end{pmatrix}=0.

(C.6)

Recall that
\{e_{1},e_{2},e_{3}\}
is a basis of
\mathbb{R}^{3}
and
\{e_{i}e_{j}:\ i\leqslant j\}
is a basis of
Sym^{2}\mathbb{R}^{3}
. Let
\{(e_{i}e_{j})^{*}:\ i\leqslant j\}
be the the dual basis of
\{e_{i}e_{j}:\ i\leqslant j\}
.
Consider the linear form
\varphi
on
Sym^{2}\mathbb{R}^{3}

\displaystyle(n_{2}v_{2}-n_{1}v_{3})(e_{1}e_{1})^{*}+\frac{1}{2}(h_{21}v_{2}-h_{11}v_{3}-n_{2}v_{1})(e_{1}e_{2})^{*}

\displaystyle+

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}(n_{1}v_{1}-h_{12}v_{3}-h_{11}v_{2})(e_{1}e_{3})^{*}-(h_{21}v_{1})(e_{2}e_{2})^{*}+(h_{11}v_{1})(e_{2}e_{3})^{*}+(h_{12}v_{1})(e_{3}e_{3})^{*}.

Then
Eq.

C.6
is just for
i=1,\cdots,5
,
u_{i}=(a_{i},b_{i},c_{i})^{t}

\displaystyle\varphi(u_{i}u_{i})=\varphi(a_{1}^{2}(e_{1}e_{1})+2a_{1}b_{1}(e_{1}e_{2})+2a_{1}c_{1}(e_{1}e_{3})+b_{1}^{2}(e_{2}e_{2})+2b_{1}c_{1}(e_{2}e_{3})+c_{1}^{2}(e_{3}e_{3}))=0.

(C.7)

Due to
v_{1}\neq 0
from the hypothesis of
V
, we see that if
X_{\mathfrak{n},\mathfrak{h}}\neq 0
, then at least one coefficient of the equation is non-zero. Then
W=\mathbb{P}(\ker\varphi)
is a hyperplane in
\mathbb{P}(Sym^{2}\mathbb{R}^{3})
,

By (
C.7
),
W
passes through
p_{2}(V_{i})
for
i=1,\cdots,5
. Observe that in (
C.6
) if let
(a_{i},b_{i},c_{i})=(v_{1},v_{2},v_{3})
then the determinant is
0
. Therefore
W
also passes through
p_{2}(V)
for
V=\mathbb{R}\cdot(v_{1},v_{2},v_{3})^{t}
, which contradicts with the assumption that
V_{1},\dots,V_{5}
are
(\rho,V)
-quadratic independent.
∎

Remark
.

In
C.4
and the next lemma, the condition
v_{1}\neq 0
, i.e.
d(V,E_{1}^{\perp})>0
is a crucial and necessary condition. Otherwise, the group
U_{V}
may intersect
L
non-trivially, which means there is a non-trivial subgroup
S
of
L
such that
\pi_{V^{\perp}}S=\pi_{V^{\perp}}id
.
In this case, we cannot get a bi-Lipschitz map.

As a corollary of Lemma
C.4
, we have

Lemma C.5
.

There exist a compact neighborhood
Z
of identity of
L
and
C^{\prime}_{6}>0
such that for any
(V_{1},\dots,V_{5},V)\in I(\rho)
,
\Phi^{V}_{V_{1},\dots,V_{5}}|_{Z}
is bi-Lipschitz with its image, with Lipschitz constant
C^{\prime}_{6}
.

Proof.

By
C.4
, we get a compact neighborhood
Z_{2}\subset Z_{1}
of identity in
L
and constants
C^{\prime}_{3},C^{\prime}_{4}>0
such that

\sup_{(V_{1},\dots,V_{5},V)\in I(\rho),g_{0}\in Z}\|D_{g}\Phi^{V}_{V_{1},\dots,V_{5}}|_{g=g_{0}}\|\leqslant C^{\prime}_{3},\inf_{(V_{1},\dots,V_{5},V)\in I(\rho),g_{0}\in Z}|\mathrm{Jac}(D_{g}\Phi^{V}_{V_{1},\dots,V_{5}})|_{g=g_{0}}|\geqslant C^{\prime}_{4}

(C.8)

which implies that there exists
C^{\prime}_{5}>0

\sup_{(V_{1},\dots,V_{5},V)\in I(\rho),g_{0}\in Z}\|(D_{g}\Phi^{V}_{V_{1},\dots,V_{5}}|_{g=g_{0}})^{-1}\|\leqslant C^{\prime}_{5}.

Claim 1:

There is a compact neighborhood
Z\subset Z_{2}
of
id
such that
\Phi^{V}_{V_{1},\dots,V_{5}}
is injective restricted on
Z
.

Proof of the Claim 1.

As we showed in
Eq.

C.8
, by taking
g_{0}=id
and applying the inverse function theorem we know for every
(V_{1},\dots,V_{5},V)\in I(\rho)
, there is a neighborhood
Z_{V_{1},\dots,V_{5},V}
of
id
such that
\Phi^{V}_{V_{1},\dots,V_{5}}|_{Z_{V_{1},\dots,V_{5},V}}
is a diffeomorphism to its image. It suffices to show
Z_{V_{1},\dots,V_{5},V}
has a uniform size or an effective estimate of the size of the neighbourhood we got from the inverse function theorem.

The key point is that by shrinking
Z
if necessary we could assume
\Phi^{V}_{V_{1},\dots,V_{5}}|_{Z}
is uniformly
C^{2}
for
(V_{1},\dots,V_{5},V)\in I(\rho)
. Recall that
\Phi^{V}_{V_{1},\dots,V_{5}}=\pi^{5}_{V^{\perp}}\circ E_{V_{1},\dots,V_{5}}
. Notice that
E_{V_{1},\dots,V_{5}}
is uniformly
C^{2}
on any compact set if
(V_{1},\dots,V_{5},V)
take values in the compact set
I(\rho)
, and
\pi^{5}_{V^{\perp}}
is uniformly
C^{2}
on
((\mathbb{P}(\mathbb{R}^{3})-B(V,\rho/2))^{5}
. By definition of quadratic independence,
d(V_{i},V)\geqslant\rho
. Hence if
Z
is small enough then
d(gV_{i},V)>\rho/2
for
i=1,\dots 5
. Consequently by taking
Z
small enough we can assume
\Phi^{V}_{V_{1},\dots,V_{5}}|_{Z}
is uniformly
C^{2}
for
(V_{1},\dots,V_{5},V)\in I(\rho)
. Then by the classical effective estimate (which needs a uniform
C^{2}
estimate) of the size neighbourhood in inverse function theorem (for example, see
[
Lan12
]
Lemma 1.3 Section XIV), we get the proof of the claim.
∎

Back to the proof of the Lemma.
On the one hand, by
Eq.

C.8
and the arguments in the second paragraph of Claim 1, we get that there exists
s>0
such that the family of maps
\Phi^{V}_{V_{1},\dots,V_{5}},(V_{1},\dots,V_{5},V)\in I(\rho)
is uniformly local bi-Lipschitz on any small ball with radius
s
in
Z
, with a uniform local Lipschitz constant
C_{6}^{\prime\prime}
.

On the other hand, by compactness of
I(\rho)
and
Z\times Z-\{(g,g^{\prime}):d(g,g^{\prime})<s\}
and the injectivity we shown in Claim 1, there exists some constant
C_{6}^{\prime\prime\prime}>1
such that

\frac{d_{L}(g,g^{\prime})}{d_{\mathbb{P}(\mathbb{R}^{2})}(\Phi^{V}_{V_{1},\dots,V_{5}}(g),\Phi^{V}_{V_{1},\dots,V_{5}}(g^{\prime}))}\in[1/C_{6}^{\prime\prime\prime},C_{6}],

for any
(V_{1},\dots,V_{5},V)\in I(\rho)
and
g,g^{\prime}\in Z,d(g,g^{\prime})\geqslant s
. Let
C_{6}^{\prime}=\max(C_{6}^{\prime\prime},C_{6}^{\prime\prime\prime})
. Then for each
(V_{1},\dots,V_{5},V)\in I(\rho)
,
\Phi^{V}_{V_{1},\dots,V_{5}}
is uniformly bi-Lipschitz restricted on
Z
to its image with bi-Lipschitz constant
C_{6}^{\prime}
.
∎

Notice that
C^{\prime}_{6}
depends on
Z,I(\rho)
, and
I(\rho)
depends on
C_{1}
by definition, so we emphasize that
C^{\prime}_{6}=C^{\prime}_{6}(Z,C_{1})
.
As a corollary, we have

Lemma C.6
.

There exists a constant
C^{\prime}=C^{\prime}(Z,C_{1})>0
such that if
(V_{1},\dots,V_{5},V)\in I(\rho)
and
\theta
supported on
Z
, then for any
n\in\mathbb{N}

H(\theta,\mathcal{Q}_{n}^{L})\leqslant\sum_{j=1}^{5}H(\pi_{V^{\perp}}(\theta.V_{j}),\mathcal{Q}_{n})+C^{\prime}.

(C.9)

Proof.

Let
\pi_{j}:\mathbb{P}(\mathbb{R}^{2})^{5}\to\mathbb{P}(\mathbb{R}^{2})
be the coordinate projections. Consider
\Phi^{V}_{V_{1},\dots,V_{5}}
restricted on
Z
in Lemma
C.5
, then
\Phi^{V}_{V_{1},\dots,V_{5}}
is a diffeomorphism on
Z
and be bi-Lipschitz to its image
\Phi^{V}_{V_{1},\dots,V_{5}}(Z)
with constant
C_{6}^{\prime}
. Thus by
2.34
there is a constant
C^{\prime}
depending on
Z
,
I(\rho)
(hence
C_{1}
) such that

|H(\theta,\mathcal{Q}_{n}^{L})-H(\Phi^{V}_{V_{1},\dots,V_{5}}\theta,\mathcal{Q}^{\mathbb{P}(\mathbb{R}^{2})^{5}}_{n})|\leqslant C^{\prime}.

Since
\mathcal{Q}^{\mathbb{P}(\mathbb{R}^{2})^{5}}_{n}=\bigvee_{j=1}^{5}\pi_{j}^{-1}\mathcal{Q}^{\mathbb{P}(\mathbb{R}^{2})}_{n}
, this is the same as

|H(\theta,\mathcal{Q}_{n}^{L})-H(\Phi^{V}_{V_{1},\dots,V_{5}}\theta,\bigvee_{j=1}^{5}\pi_{j}^{-1}\mathcal{Q}^{\mathbb{P}(\mathbb{R}^{2})}_{n})|\leqslant C^{\prime}.

The statement now follows by

H(\Phi^{V}_{V_{1},\dots,V_{5}}\theta,\bigvee_{j=1}^{5}\pi_{j}^{-1}\mathcal{Q}^{\mathbb{P}(\mathbb{R}^{2})}_{n})\leqslant\sum_{j=1}^{5}H(\Phi^{V}_{V_{1},\dots,V_{5}}\theta,\pi_{j}^{-1}\mathcal{Q}^{\mathbb{P}(\mathbb{R}^{2})}_{n})=\sum_{i=1}^{5}H(\pi_{V^{\perp}}(\theta.V_{j}),\mathcal{Q}_{n}).

∎

Proof of
5.6
.

Let
C^{\prime}
be as in the last lemma and set

B=\{x\in\mathbb{P}(\mathbb{R}^{3}):\frac{1}{k}H(\pi_{V^{\perp}}(\theta_{\cdot}x),\mathcal{Q}_{i+k})>\frac{1}{5k}H(\theta,\mathcal{Q}_{i+k})-\frac{C^{\prime}}{k}\}.

We claim that
\mu(B)\geqslant 1-(100\epsilon)^{1/5}=0.9
. Otherwise by Lemma
C.2
, for any
V
such that
d(V,E_{1}^{\perp})\geqslant\frac{1}{2C_{1}}
, there exists
(V_{1},\dots,V_{5})
such that
V_{i}\notin B
, and
(V_{1},\dots,V_{5},V)\in I(\rho)
. Hence by applying Lemma
C.6
, we get

H(\theta,\mathcal{Q}^{L}_{i+k})\leqslant\sum_{i=1}^{5}H(\pi_{V^{\perp}}(\theta_{\cdot}V_{i}),\mathcal{Q}_{i+k})+C^{\prime}\leqslant H(\theta,\mathcal{Q}_{i+k}^{L})-4C^{\prime},

which is a contradiction.

Therefore the lemma follows by letting
C=\max\{5,C^{\prime}(Z,C_{1})\}
.
∎

Appendix D
Results in linear algebra

Lemma D.1
.

For any
1/4>r>0
, for
g\in\mathrm{SL}_{3}(\mathbb{R})
and
V\in\mathbb{P}(\mathbb{R}^{3})
with
d(V_{g}^{+},V)>2r
,
d(V^{\perp},H_{g}^{-})>2r
(in the sense of Hausdorff distance) and
|\chi_{1}(h_{V,g})-\chi_{1}(g)|\leqslant|\log r|/2
, then

d(\pi_{V^{\perp}}V_{g}^{+},{h^{+}_{V,g}})\leqslant q^{-\chi_{1}(g)}/r^{2}.

Proof.

From the condition
d(V^{\perp},H_{g}^{-})>2r
, we obtain

\exists x\in\mathbb{P}(V^{\perp}),d(x,H_{g}^{-}),\ d(x,h_{V,g}^{-})>r.

Due to
2.5
, combined with
\ \chi_{1}(h_{V,g})\geqslant\chi_{1}(g)-|\log r|/2
, we have

d(V_{g}^{+},gx)\leqslant\frac{q^{-\chi_{1}(g)}}{d(x,H_{g}^{-})}\leqslant q^{-\chi_{1}(g)}/r,\ d({h_{V,g}^{+}},h_{V,g}x)\leqslant\frac{q^{-\chi_{1}(h_{V,g})}}{d(x,h_{V,g}^{-})}\leqslant q^{-\chi_{1}(g)}/r^{3/2}.

(D.1)

Due to
B.2
and
d(V_{g}^{+},V)>2r
from the hypothesis, we obtain from the first inequality of
Eq.

D.1
that

d(\pi_{V^{\perp}}V_{g}^{+},\pi_{V^{\perp}}gx)\leqslant q^{-\chi_{1}(g)}/2r^{2}.

Combined with
\pi_{V^{\perp}}gx=h_{V,g}x
and the second inequality of
Eq.

D.1
, we obtain the claim if
r<1/4
.
∎

Appendix E
Fourier decay property of stationary measures on flag variety

Recall that the flag variety
\mathcal{F}=\mathcal{F}(\mathbb{R}^{3})
is the space of flags

\mathcal{F}({\mathbb{R}}^{3}):=\left\{(V_{1},V_{2}):V_{1}\subset V_{2},V_{i}\text{ is a linear subspace of }{\mathbb{R}}^{3}\text{ of dimension }i\right\}.

In this section, we recall and explain the result in
[
Li22
]
on the Fourier decay property of stationary measures on
\mathcal{F}
.
Roughly speaking, the Fourier decay property of the stationary measure
\mu
(see Theorem
E.2
for details)is the decay of the oscillatory integral

\int_{\mathcal{F}}e^{i\xi\varphi(\eta)}r(\eta)\mathrm{d}\mu(\eta),

(E.1)

as the real parameter
\xi
tends to infinity, where
\varphi
is a
C^{2}
-function and
r
is a
C^{1}
-function.

The classical van der Corput lemma is stated for
([0,1],\mathrm{Leb})
instead of
(\mathcal{F},\mu)
in
Eq.

E.1
, and it requires a non-degenerate condition of
\varphi^{\prime}
. In our higher dimensional case, a similar non-degeneracy assumption (
E.1
,
E.3
) is also introduced to obtain Fourier decay.

Due to the group action of
\mathrm{SL}_{3}(\mathbb{R})
and the structure of
\mathcal{F}
, not all the tangent directions on
\mathcal{F}
play the same role. For this reason, we introduce the
simple root directions
of the tangent space.

Simple root directions of
T\mathcal{F}
.

Recall
\alpha_{1},\alpha_{2}
are the simple roots of the root system of
\mathrm{SL}_{3}(\mathbb{R})
, that is for
\lambda
in the Lie algebra
\mathfrak{a}=\{\lambda=(\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2},\lambda_{3}):\lambda_{i}\in\mathbb{R},\ \sum\lambda_{i}=0\}
, we have
\alpha_{i}(\lambda)=\lambda_{i}-\lambda_{i+1}
for
i=1,2
.

We define the line bundle
V_{\alpha_{1}}
as the sub-bundle of the tangent bundle
T\mathcal{F}
, whose fiber at any
\eta=(V_{1}(\eta),V_{2}(\eta))\in\mathcal{F}
is the tangent space of the circle
\{(V_{1}^{\prime},V_{2}(\eta)):V_{1}^{\prime}\subset V_{2}(\eta),\dim V_{1}^{\prime}=1\}\subset\mathcal{F}
. Similarly,
V_{\alpha_{2}}
is defined as the sub-line bundle of
T\mathcal{F}
over
\mathcal{F}
, whose fiber at any
\eta\in T_{\eta}\mathcal{F}
is the tangent space of the circle
\{(V_{1}(\eta),V_{2}^{\prime}):V_{1}(\eta)\subset V_{2}^{\prime},\dim V_{2}^{\prime}=2\}\subset\mathcal{F}
. From the definition, both
V_{\alpha_{i}},i=1,2
are
\mathrm{SL}_{3}(\mathbb{R})
-invariant sub-bundles of
T\mathcal{F}
.

For each
\eta\in\mathcal{F}
, let
V_{\alpha_{1}+\alpha_{2}}(\eta)
be the one-dimensional subspace of
T_{\eta}\mathcal{F}
orthogonal to
V_{\alpha_{1}}(\eta)
and
V_{\alpha_{2}}(\eta)
.
For any
g\in\mathrm{SL}_{3}(\mathbb{R})
, the tangent map
Dg:T\mathcal{F}\to T\mathcal{F}
of the action
g:\mathcal{F}\to\mathcal{F}
contracts
V_{\alpha_{i}}
with a speed can be computed using
\alpha_{i}
for
i=1,2
. For a non-zero vector
v
in the direction
V_{\alpha_{1}+\alpha_{2}}(\eta)
, for most of
g
the image vector
Dg(v)
will have non-trivial component in
V_{\alpha_{1}}(g\eta)\oplus V_{\alpha_{2}}(g\eta)
dominating the whole vector.
See Section 2.4 in
[
Li22
]
for more details of these directions and computations of the contraction rates.

Let
Y_{\alpha_{i}}(\eta)
be a unit vector in
V_{\alpha_{i}}(\eta)
at
\eta
, for
i=1,2
, where the metric is from the
\mathrm{SO}(3)
-invariant Riemannian metric on
\mathcal{F}
. We denote by
\partial_{i}\varphi(\eta)=\partial_{Y_{\alpha_{i}}}\varphi(\eta)
the directional derivative at a point
\eta
.
9
9

9

The unit vector
Y_{\alpha_{1}}(\eta)
cannot be continuously extended to the full
\mathcal{F}
, due to the fact that
\mathcal{F}
is non-orientable. But we can always extend it locally and will be used in
Eq.

E.6

(C,r)
-good functions and Fourier decay of stationary measures.

We introduce the
(C,r)
-good condition of
\varphi
for
C\geqslant 1,r\in C^{1}(\mathcal{F}),\varphi\in C^{2}(\mathcal{F})
. Recall the metrics
d,d_{i},i=1,2
on
\mathcal{F}
defined in
3.3
.

Definition E.1

(Definition 4.1 in
[
Li22
]
)
.

Let
C\geqslant 1
,
\varphi\in C^{2}(\mathcal{F})
and
r\in C^{1}(\mathcal{F})
. Denote by
v_{i}:=\sup_{\eta\in\supp r}|\partial_{i}\varphi|
. Let
J
be the
1/C
-neighborhood of
\supp r
. The function
\varphi
is called
(C,r)
-good if:

1.

For
\eta\in\supp r
and
i=1,2

|\partial_{i}\varphi(\eta)|\geqslant v_{i}/C.

2.

For
\eta,\eta^{\prime}\in J
and
d(\eta,\eta^{\prime})<1/C
,

|\varphi(\eta)-\varphi(\eta^{\prime})|\leqslant C\sum_{1\leqslant i\leqslant 2}d_{i}(\eta,\eta^{\prime})v_{i}.

3.

For
\eta,\eta^{\prime}\in J
and
d(\eta,\eta^{\prime})<1/C
,
i=1,2

|\partial_{i}\varphi(\eta)-\partial_{i}\varphi(\eta^{\prime})|\leqslant Cd(\eta,\eta^{\prime})v_{i}.

4.

\sup_{1\leqslant i\leqslant 2}v_{i}\in[1/C,C].

The third condition serves as a similar role to the second derivative. The second condition is due to the fact that we only use two directions
V_{\alpha_{1}}
and
V_{\alpha_{2}}
, and we need to use the derivations on these two directions to control the Lipschitz property.

For Lipschitz-continuous function
r
on a metric space
(X,d_{X})
, its Lipschitz constant is defined by

\Lip(r)=\sup_{x\neq x^{\prime}}\frac{|r(x)-r(x^{\prime})|}{d_{X}(x,x^{\prime})}.

(E.2)

The main result of Fourier decay is as follows.

Theorem E.2

(Theorem 1.7 in
[
Li22
]
)
.

Consider the action of
\mathrm{SL}_{3}(\mathbb{R})
on
\mathcal{F}
. Let
\nu
be a Zariski dense finitely supported probability measure on
\mathrm{SL}_{3}(\mathbb{R})
, and
\mu_{\mathcal{F}}
be the
\nu
-stationary measure on
\mathcal{F}
. Then there exist
\epsilon_{0},\epsilon_{1}>0
only depending on
\nu
such that the following holds.

Let
\xi>1
be large enough. For any pair of real functions
\varphi\in C^{2}(\mathcal{F})
,
r\in C^{1}(\mathcal{F})
if
\varphi
is
(\xi^{\epsilon_{0}},r)
-good,
\|r\|_{\infty}\leqslant 1
and
\Lip(r)\leqslant\xi^{\epsilon_{0}}
, then

\left|\int_{\mathcal{F}}e^{i\xi\varphi(\eta)}r(\eta)\mathrm{d}\mu_{\mathcal{F}}(\eta)\right|\leqslant\xi^{-\epsilon_{1}}.

(E.3)

For its application in
Section

3
, it suffices to consider
\varphi\in C^{2}(\mathcal{F})
lifted from
\mathbb{P}(\mathbb{R}^{3})
(cf. Definition
3.3
). For such a function
\varphi
,
(C,r)
-good condition can be simplified as follows.

Definition E.3
.

Let
C\geqslant 1
,
r\in C^{1}(\mathcal{F})
and
\varphi\in C^{2}(\mathcal{F})
be a function lifted from
\mathbb{P}(\mathbb{R}^{3})
. Let
v_{1}=\sup_{\eta\in\supp r}|\partial_{1}\varphi|
. Let
J
be the
1/C
-neighborhood of
\supp r
. We say that
\varphi
is
(C,r)
-good if:

1.

For
\eta\in\supp r
,

|\partial_{1}\varphi(\eta)|\geqslant v_{1}/C.

(E.4)

2.

For
\eta,\eta^{\prime}\in J
and
d(\eta,\eta^{\prime})<1/C
,

|\varphi(\eta)-\varphi(\eta^{\prime})|\leqslant Cd(V_{1}(\eta),V_{1}(\eta^{\prime}))v_{1}.

(E.5)

3.

For
\eta,\eta^{\prime}\in J
and
d(\eta,\eta^{\prime})<1/C

|\partial_{1}\varphi(\eta)-\partial_{1}\varphi(\eta^{\prime})|\leqslant Cd(\eta,\eta^{\prime})v_{1}.

(E.6)

4.

v_{1}\in[1/C,C].

(E.7)

Claim:

If a lifted function
\varphi\in C^{2}(\mathcal{F})
is
(C,r)
-good in the sense of Definition
E.3
, then it is
(C,r)
-good in the sense of Definition
E.1
.

Proof of the claim.

Since
\varphi
is lifted from
\mathbb{P}(\mathbb{R}^{3})
, the function
\varphi(\eta)=\varphi(V_{1}(\eta),V_{2}(\eta))
is independent of
V_{2}(\eta)
.
Note that
Y_{\alpha_{2}}(\eta)
is a tangent vector to the circle
\{(V_{1}(\eta),V_{2}^{\prime}):V_{1}(\eta)\subset V_{2}^{\prime},\dim V_{2}^{\prime}=2\}
at
\eta
, and
\varphi
is constant on this circle. Therefore
\partial_{2}\varphi(\eta)=\partial_{Y_{\alpha_{2}}}\varphi(\eta)=0
. Then
E.1
can be simplified to
E.3
.
∎
